# Political Theory — Topic 08: Approaches, Behaviouralism and Post-Behaviouralism (Live Learning Session)

## Why this topic is different from every topic before it

Topics 01 to 07 asked **what** political theory should say — about the state, about liberty, about class, about community. This one asks something prior and stranger: **how is anyone entitled to say anything about politics at all?** It is the chapter where the discipline turns round and inspects its own instruments.

That makes it the hinge of the whole module. Every earlier topic was produced by *some* approach; after this topic you will be able to name which one, and say what it could and could not see. ⚠️ And it is the topic most often answered badly, because it is tempting to treat a method as if it were a doctrine — to be "for" behaviouralism or "against" it, as one might be for or against socialism. ❌ That is the single error this session is built to prevent. An approach is a **choice of what to look at**; it is defended or attacked by showing what it reveals and what it hides, not by moral approval.

A second temptation, equally common and equally fatal, is to read the post-behavioural revolution as a revolt against science. ❌ It was not. The chapter's own words are that it **represented no complete departure from behaviouralism** and stood for **consolidating its gains**. Keep that sentence close; it decides a large share of the marks in this topic.

## Lesson 1 — Method and approach are not the same thing

Progress: 1 / 34 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — conceptual hygiene

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§1, 3 and 5 with the Advanced companion's §§1 and 4, read against the chapter's opening section headed "Distinction between Method and Approach" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.108 = printed 94), which supplies the loose-usage complaint, the definition of method, the four examples of method, the definition of approach, Van Dyke's two formulations and the wedding observation.
CA search: "David Easton behaviouralism post-behaviouralism political science approach 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current-affairs item is concept-linked to a 1960s methodological debate in a way that would survive scrutiny, so **no** live anchor is carried into this lesson. 📰 Dated anchors appear only where the chapter itself dates a claim.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem, before any definition

You are asked to "study Indian politics". Two people accept the task. One spends six months with the Constitution and Supreme Court judgments. The other spends six months with survey data on how people voted and why.

Neither is cheating. But they will produce incompatible books — and not because one used statistics and the other did not. They diverged **before** any technique was chosen, at the moment they decided *what would count as the relevant material*. That prior decision is what this chapter calls an **approach**, and the whole topic hangs on seeing that it is a separate thing from a **method**.

### 2. Why the chapter starts by complaining

✅ The chapter's first move is a complaint about usage:

> In the sphere of social sciences the terms 'method' and 'approach' are applied rather loosely, and sometimes even interchangeably. To be precise, as far as possible, in their usage, distinction may be drawn between the two.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.108 (printed 94)

⚠️ Note what kind of claim this is. It is not a discovery about politics; it is a **stipulation** about words, offered because the looseness causes confusion downstream. An answer that opens by reproducing the distinction is reproducing the chapter's own opening move.

### 3. Method

✅ **Method is a more general term which denotes a particular way of doing something.** In a systematic study it is defined precisely:

> method may be defined as the procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge could be obtained and reliable conclusions could be drawn.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.108 (printed 94)

✅ Four examples are given: **scientific method, inductive method, deductive method, comparative method**.

🔑 Two words carry the definition: **procedure**, and **reliable** — twice. A method is judged by whether what comes out of it can be relied on.

### 4. Approach

✅ **Approach is a wider term** which comprehends **not only the method (i.e. how to inquire) but also the focus of our study (i.e. what to inquire)**.

✅ And then the chapter hands the definition to **Vernon Van Dyke** (*Political Science: A Philosophical Analysis*; 1960):

> An approach consists of criteria of selection—criteria employed in selecting the problems or questions to consider and in selecting the data to bring to bear; it consists of standards governing the inclusion and exclusion of questions and data.
>
> — Van Dyke, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.108 (printed 94)

✅ And the compressed restatement, which is the one to memorise:

> In brief, approaches consist of criteria for selecting problems and relevant data, whereas methods are procedures for getting and utilizing data.
>
> — Van Dyke, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.108 (printed 94)

### 5. The visual

```text
   A QUESTION ARRIVES: "study politics in country X"

   +---------------------------------------------------+
   |  APPROACH  -- criteria of SELECTION               |
   |                                                   |
   |   which PROBLEMS or QUESTIONS to consider?  ------+--> INCLUDE / EXCLUDE
   |   which DATA to bring to bear?              ------+--> INCLUDE / EXCLUDE
   |                                                   |
   |   +-------------------------------------------+   |
   |   |  METHOD -- procedures for GETTING and     |   |
   |   |            UTILIZING data                 |   |
   |   |   scientific | inductive | deductive |    |   |
   |   |   comparative                             |   |
   |   +-------------------------------------------+   |
   +---------------------------------------------------+

   NESTING, NOT RIVALRY: the method sits INSIDE the approach.
   The approach has already decided what the method will be
   pointed at.
```

*Caption: the single most common error in this topic is to draw these as two side-by-side boxes. They are concentric. Van Dyke's "standards governing the inclusion and exclusion" happen before the method is switched on.*

### 6. Why the nesting matters for an answer

⚠️ Because it explains why methodological disputes in political science are rarely settled by better technique. If two scholars disagree about *which questions count*, giving both of them better statistics changes nothing. ⚠️ That is the structural reason the behavioural revolution could not simply be refuted by data, and the structural reason the post-behavioural revolution had to be about **what gets studied**, not about whether the studies were competently done. 🔑 Hold that thought until Lesson 23; it is the hinge of the entire second half of the chapter.

### Revision notes

- ✅ The terms are used **rather loosely, and sometimes even interchangeably**; the chapter stipulates a distinction.
- ✅ **Method** = **the procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge could be obtained and reliable conclusions could be drawn**.
- ✅ Four examples of method: **scientific, inductive, deductive, comparative**.
- ✅ **Approach** = **wider**; covers **the method (i.e. how to inquire)** *and* **the focus of our study (i.e. what to inquire)**.
- ✅ Van Dyke (*Political Science: A Philosophical Analysis*; 1960): an approach is **criteria of selection** — **criteria employed in selecting the problems or questions to consider and in selecting the data to bring to bear**.
- ✅ Van Dyke again: it consists of **standards governing the inclusion and exclusion of questions and data**.
- 🔑 Van Dyke compressed: **approaches consist of criteria for selecting problems and relevant data, whereas methods are procedures for getting and utilizing data.**
- 🔑 The relation is **nesting**, not rivalry — selection happens before procedure.
- ⚠️ **Reliable** appears twice in the definition of method; reliability is the method's test.
- ⚠️ Consequence: disputes about approach cannot be settled by improving method, which is why they persist across generations of better data.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two researchers both use rigorous statistical technique on Indian elections, and reach incompatible accounts of Indian politics. Using the chapter's distinction, explain how this is possible and what it shows.

**Model answer:** It is possible because identical **methods** can sit inside different **approaches**, and the approach has already done the decisive work before any technique is applied. ✅ The chapter defines method as **the procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge could be obtained and reliable conclusions could be drawn**, and approach as the **wider** term covering **not only the method (i.e. how to inquire) but also the focus of our study (i.e. what to inquire)**. ✅ Van Dyke makes the point sharper: an approach is **criteria of selection**, consisting of **standards governing the inclusion and exclusion of questions and data**. ⚠️ So two researchers may each apply impeccable statistics while having already excluded different questions and different data — one counting only recorded votes, the other counting also the patterns that never reach the ballot. ⚠️ Their results do not contradict each other in the way two measurements of the same quantity contradict each other; they are answers to differently selected questions. ⚠️ What this shows is the structural point that governs the rest of the chapter: a dispute at the level of approach is **not** adjudicable by better technique, because technique operates only after selection. 🔑 This is why the behavioural revolution had to be argued about rather than tested, and why the post-behavioural correction at Lesson 23 is framed as a demand about **what gets studied** rather than a complaint about how well it was studied.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat method and approach as two parallel choices, as though a researcher first picks "the institutional approach" and then independently picks "the statistical method" from a separate menu. The chapter's own wording makes approach the **wider** term that **comprehends** the method; the relation is containment. ❌ And do not promote the distinction into a claim that approach matters and method does not — the chapter's definition of method insists twice on **reliable**, and the post-behavioural section at Lesson 24 explicitly refuses to trade rigour away for relevance.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why is an approach said to be wider than a method in political science? *(Why — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Because an approach decides what will be inquired into before any procedure is applied. The chapter defines method as the **procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge could be obtained and reliable conclusions could be drawn**, instancing scientific, inductive, deductive and comparative method. An approach **comprehends not only the method (i.e. how to inquire) but also the focus of our study (i.e. what to inquire)**. Van Dyke states it exactly: an approach **consists of criteria of selection** and of **standards governing the inclusion and exclusion of questions and data**, whereas **methods are procedures for getting and utilizing data**. ⚠️ The relation is containment, not rivalry: the method operates inside a selection already made. ⚠️ The consequence is that a disagreement about approach cannot be resolved by improving technique, which is why such disputes survive better data.

**Rubric [10]:** definition of method with **reliable** retained **2** · definition of approach as covering what to inquire **2** · Van Dyke's criteria-of-selection formulation **3** · containment rather than rivalry **2** · the consequence for unresolvable disputes **1**.

## Lesson 2 — Why each approach drags a method behind it

Progress: 2 / 34 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — conceptual hygiene

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§1 and 5 with the Advanced companion's §§1 and 3, read against the closing paragraph of "Distinction between Method and Approach" and its continuation (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.108-109 = printed 94-95), which supply the asymmetry of the wedding relation, the four worked pairings, and the observation that the models of political analysis are really methods inside the empirical approach.
CA search: "political science methodology approach method distinction 2025 2026 India"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem

If approach and method are distinct, can you mix them freely — a philosophical approach with a scientific method, say? The chapter's answer is **no, and not quite yes either**, and the asymmetry is the whole lesson.

### 2. The asymmetry

✅ The chapter's formulation:

> It may, however, be observed that an approach is usually wedded to a particular method while a method is not always wedded to a particular approach. That is why an approach suggests the relevant method also.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.108 (printed 94)

🔑 Read the two halves separately.
- **Approach → method:** *usually* determined. Choose the approach and the method largely follows.
- **Method → approach:** *not always* determined. The comparative method, for instance, can serve several approaches.

⚠️ Note the hedges. **Usually**, and **not always**. The chapter is describing a tendency, not a logical law, and an answer that reports it as a strict entailment overstates it.

### 3. The four worked pairings

| Approach | Wedded method | The chapter's stated reason |
|---|---|---|
| **Behavioural** | **Scientific method** | ✅ **because behaviour of several actors in a political situation is capable of scientific study** |
| **Normative** | **Philosophical method** | ✅ **because norms and values can only be determined philosophically, not through scientific method** |
| **Philosophical** | Philosophical method | ✅ The approach **suggests the use of** the corresponding method, **although they also point to their respective focus of study** |
| **Historical** | Historical method | ✅ Same formula as the philosophical pairing |

🔑 The reasons are not decorative. Each is a claim about **what the subject matter will tolerate**. Behaviour submits to scientific study; norms do not. That is why the wedding holds.

### 4. The twist at the end of the section

✅ The chapter then turns the distinction on the contemporary material itself:

> empirical approach to the study of politics leads us to 'political analysis', and several models of political analysis (e.g. systems analysis, structural-functional analysis and decision-making analysis) in fact point to several methods adopted under this approach (although these are loosely referred to as 'political system approach', 'structural-functional approach' and 'decision-making approach' respectively).
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.109 (printed 95)

🔑 This is a high-value sentence and it is almost always missed. **Systems analysis, structural-functional analysis and decision-making analysis are, strictly, methods inside the empirical approach — not approaches in their own right.** The chapter says plainly that calling them "approaches" is **loose**.

⚠️ Use it with care. The chapter itself goes on to use the loose labels at Lessons 26-31, as does virtually all exam literature. The examinable point is that you **know** the labels are loose and can say why: they are different ways of arranging data within one and the same selection of what counts as data.

### 5. The visual

```text
   APPROACH  ==========>  METHOD          (usually wedded)
   METHOD    ---- ? ---->  APPROACH       (NOT always wedded)

   WORKED PAIRS, with the chapter's REASON:

   BEHAVIOURAL ----> SCIENTIFIC
       reason: "behaviour of several actors in a political
                situation is CAPABLE OF scientific study"

   NORMATIVE ------> PHILOSOPHICAL
       reason: "norms and values CAN ONLY BE determined
                philosophically, NOT through scientific method"

   PHILOSOPHICAL --> philosophical method  \  "although they also
   HISTORICAL -----> historical method     /   point to their
                                               respective FOCUS"

   AND THE STING IN THE TAIL:

        EMPIRICAL APPROACH
                |
        "political analysis"
                |
     +----------+-----------+-------------------+
     |          |           |                   |
   systems  structural-  decision-making   ... these are, strictly,
   analysis  functional     analysis           *METHODS* under ONE
                                                approach --
                                                "LOOSELY referred to"
                                                as approaches.
```

*Caption: the bottom block is the part to carry into an answer. It lets you write "the so-called systems, structural-functional and decision-making approaches are more accurately methods within the empirical approach" — a sentence that signals you have read past the headings.*

### Revision notes

- 🔑 **An approach is usually wedded to a particular method while a method is not always wedded to a particular approach.** The relation runs strongly one way.
- ✅ **That is why an approach suggests the relevant method also.**
- ✅ Behavioural approach → scientific method, **because behaviour of several actors in a political situation is capable of scientific study**.
- ✅ Normative approach → philosophical method, **because norms and values can only be determined philosophically, not through scientific method**.
- ✅ Philosophical and historical approaches **suggest the use of** their corresponding methods, **although they also point to their respective focus of study**.
- 🔑 The **empirical approach** leads to **political analysis**; systems, structural-functional and decision-making analysis are **several methods adopted under this approach**, only **loosely referred to** as approaches.
- ⚠️ Keep the hedges **usually** and **not always**; the chapter asserts a tendency, not a law.
- ⚠️ The comparative method is the natural counter-example to a one-method-one-approach rule.
- ⚠️ Because the four liberal models share one approach, they differ in **arrangement**, not in what counts as data — which is why Marxian analysis at Lesson 32 is a different kind of entry.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says the so-called systems, structural-functional and decision-making "approaches" are loosely named. What is the precise claim, and what follows from it for how the five models should be compared?

**Model answer:** The precise claim is that these three are **methods adopted under** one approach — the empirical approach — and that the familiar labels **'political system approach'**, **'structural-functional approach'** and **'decision-making approach'** are therefore **loose**. ✅ The chapter's route to this is explicit: the **empirical approach to the study of politics leads us to 'political analysis'**, and the models **point to several methods adopted under this approach**. ⚠️ What follows is a precise comparative discipline. Because the three share a single approach, they share the same prior selection of what counts as data — observable political behaviour and process — and therefore they cannot differ about whether politics is a fit object of scientific study. ⚠️ They differ only at the next stage: in how the selected data are **arranged** so as to yield an explanation, which Lesson 25 shows is exactly how the chapter defines a model. ⚠️ So the right comparative question between systems and structural-functional analysis is "which arrangement of the same data explains more?", not "which is the truer view of politics". 🔑 The contrast with Marxian analysis is correspondingly sharper, since the chapter notes the four liberal models are **associated with the liberal view of politics** while Marxist analysis **may lead to very different results** — there the disagreement really is about selection, because Marxian analysis denies that the political system is an autonomous object at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not convert the asymmetry into a rule that a method belongs to exactly one approach. The chapter says a method is **not always** wedded to a particular approach, and the comparative method is the obvious counter-example — it serves institutional, behavioural and structural-functional work alike. ❌ Equally, do not now refuse to use the words "systems approach" or "decision-making approach"; the chapter uses them itself in later sections. The examinable skill is knowing that the usage is loose and being able to say in one sentence what the strict description would be.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Systems analysis and decision-making analysis are approaches to the study of politics." Comment. *(Comment — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** The description is the chapter's own, and the chapter also records that it is loose. It states that the **empirical approach to the study of politics leads us to 'political analysis'**, and that systems analysis, structural-functional analysis and decision-making analysis **in fact point to several methods adopted under this approach**, **although these are loosely referred to as** approaches. ⚠️ The precise claim is that all three share one prior selection of data — observable political behaviour and process — and differ only in how that data is arranged to yield an explanation. ⚠️ The asymmetry behind the usage is that **an approach is usually wedded to a particular method while a method is not always wedded to a particular approach**. ⚠️ A comment should accept the usage while stating the strict description, which matters when Marxian analysis is compared with the other four.

**Rubric [10]:** reproduction of the **methods adopted under this approach** formulation **3** · identification of the usage as **loose** **2** · the shared-selection point **2** · the wedding asymmetry **2** · the forward relevance to Marxian analysis **1**.

## Lesson 3 — Traditional against contemporary: a date, not a verdict

Progress: 3 / 34 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — conceptual hygiene

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§4 and 5 with the Advanced companion's §§2 and 3, read against the chapter's section headed "Traditional versus Contemporary Approaches" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.109 = printed 95), which supplies the dating criterion, Alan Ball's caution, the refusal of comprehensive lists, the abandoned facts-versus-values equation, the roster of four traditional approaches, the identity problem of contemporary political science and the interdisciplinary addition.
CA search: "traditional and contemporary approaches political science 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem

"Traditional" is a word that sounds like a judgment. In ordinary speech it means outdated, superseded, perhaps quaint. If that is how you read it here, you will mis-handle the entire first half of the chapter — and you will write the two sentences the chapter spends a paragraph refuting.

### 2. The criterion is a date

✅ The division is made by **when**, not by **what**:

> Broadly speaking, the approaches which remained largely in vogue till the end of the Second World War (1939-45) are described as traditional approaches while those which were developed thereafter are known to be contemporary approaches.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.109 (printed 95)

⚠️ The extraction prints the war dates as `(1939^5)`, rendered here as **(1939-45)** and catalogued in the Source Ledger. 📰 The chapter dates the dividing line to the end of the Second World War and asserts nothing about the discipline after its own **2009** edition.

### 3. Ball's caution — the sentence that stops the misreading

✅ The chapter immediately disarms the word:

> As Alan Ball (Modern Politics and Government; 1988) has rightly suggested, the use of the label 'traditional' is neither a criticism nor a refutation of the obvious fact that they still play important role in modern political studies.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.109 (printed 95)

✅ And in the chapter's own restatement: the traditional approaches **have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches**.

🔑 That is a precise formulation and worth reproducing exactly. It separates two very different claims: *they stopped working* (denied) and *they stopped being preferred* (asserted).

### 4. No lists, no watertight compartments

✅ Two further hedges:
- **It is not possible to furnish any comprehensive lists of the traditional or contemporary approaches.**
- **Truly speaking, they do not represent watertight compartments, although some of their distinctive features might be identified.**

⚠️ So the four-plus-three roster you are about to learn is explicitly offered as a set of **usual identifications**, not a closed taxonomy. An answer that presents it as exhaustive is stricter than the source.

### 5. The abandoned equation

✅ The chapter then records a view and discards it:

> A few decades ago it was argued that the contemporary approaches focus on facts while traditional approaches focus on values. This view is no longer upheld. Hence the distinction between empirical and normative approaches cannot be treated as coterminous with the distinction between traditional and contemporary approaches.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.109 (printed 95)

🔑 **Coterminous** is the operative word. Lesson 4 is nothing but the demonstration that the two cuts do not coincide.

### 6. What each cluster actually contains

| | Traditional | Contemporary |
|---|---|---|
| **Dating** | ✅ **in vogue till the end of the Second World War** | ✅ **developed thereafter** |
| **Dominated by** | ✅ **the study of philosophy, history, law and institutions** | ✅ **phenomenon of politics as a process as manifested in the behaviour of different actors** |
| **Usual roster** | ✅ **philosophical, historical, legal and institutional** | ✅ **behavioural approach**; **different models of political analysis**; **interdisciplinary approach** |
| **Characteristic problem** | ⚠️ not stated as a problem by the chapter | ✅ **faced with the problem of the identity of the discipline** |
| **Method signature** | ⚠️ borrowed from the parent discipline | ✅ **sought to be studied by scientific method** |

✅ The interdisciplinary addition has its own reason: **contemporary political science seeks to enrich itself by the relevant achievements of other social sciences which leads us to the interdisciplinary approach.**

### 7. The visual

```text
                       1945
   TRADITIONAL           |           CONTEMPORARY
   -----------           |           ------------
   philosophical         |           behavioural
   historical            |           models of political analysis
   legal                 |           interdisciplinary
   institutional         |
                         |
   "dominated by the     |   "focus on phenomenon of politics
    study of philosophy, |    AS A PROCESS as manifested in the
    history, law and     |    BEHAVIOUR of different actors ...
    institutions"        |    studied by SCIENTIFIC METHOD"
                         |
   BALL'S CAUTION  >>>   |   "the label 'traditional' is NEITHER
                         |    A CRITICISM NOR A REFUTATION of the
                         |    obvious fact that they STILL PLAY
                         |    IMPORTANT ROLE in modern political
                         |    studies"
                         |
   >>> "have NOT become all outdated, but they are NO LONGER
        FAVOURED by the champions of the contemporary approaches"

   AND TWO MORE HEDGES, BOTH EXAMINABLE:
     * "NOT POSSIBLE to furnish any comprehensive lists"
     * "they do NOT represent WATERTIGHT COMPARTMENTS"
```

*Caption: the three bracketed warnings are the difference between a safe answer and an over-claiming one. Each is the chapter's own sentence, and each blocks a specific exam error.*

### 8. Why it matters next

⚠️ The identity problem is worth pausing on. ✅ The chapter says contemporary approaches **are faced with the problem of the identity of the discipline** — meaning that once politics is studied as a process permeating society, the boundary of political science becomes unclear, which is precisely why the institutional approach at Lesson 14 is singled out as the one traditional approach that **alone gives independent identity to the systematic study of politics**. 🔑 The identity problem runs from here to Lesson 15 and then into the interdisciplinary tenet at Lesson 21.

### Revision notes

- ✅ Criterion: **in vogue till the end of the Second World War (1939-45)** = traditional; **developed thereafter** = contemporary.
- ✅ **Alan Ball** (*Modern Politics and Government*; 1988): the label **'traditional' is neither a criticism nor a refutation of the obvious fact that they still play important role in modern political studies**.
- 🔑 **Have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches.**
- ✅ **Not possible to furnish any comprehensive lists**; they **do not represent watertight compartments, although some of their distinctive features might be identified**.
- 🔑 The facts/values equation is recorded and discarded: **this view is no longer upheld**, so the empirical/normative distinction **cannot be treated as coterminous with** the traditional/contemporary distinction.
- ✅ Traditional study was **dominated by the study of philosophy, history, law and institutions**; hence the roster **philosophical, historical, legal and institutional**.
- ✅ Contemporary approaches focus on **politics as a process as manifested in the behaviour of different actors**, **sought to be studied by scientific method**.
- ✅ **Behavioural approach** is **a typically contemporary approach**; models of political analysis and the **interdisciplinary approach** also fall within it.
- ✅ Contemporary approaches are **faced with the problem of the identity of the discipline**.
- ⚠️ The interdisciplinary addition is justified because contemporary political science **seeks to enrich itself by the relevant achievements of other social sciences**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Traditional approaches have been superseded by contemporary approaches, which replaced value-laden speculation with factual science." Identify every claim in that sentence the chapter denies, and give the chapter's own formulation in each case.

**Model answer:** Three claims, and the chapter denies all three. ❌ First, **superseded**. ✅ The chapter reports Alan Ball's caution that the label **'traditional' is neither a criticism nor a refutation of the obvious fact that they still play important role in modern political studies**, and gives its own formulation: they **have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches**. ⚠️ The precise correction is that a change in **preference** has been mistaken for a change in **validity**. ❌ Second, **replaced**. ✅ The chapter states that the two clusters **do not represent watertight compartments**, so the relation cannot be a clean substitution; it also refuses to supply **comprehensive lists**, which a replacement claim would require. ❌ Third, and most damagingly, **value-laden speculation** against **factual science**. ✅ The chapter records exactly this view — **a few decades ago it was argued that the contemporary approaches focus on facts while traditional approaches focus on values** — and rules that **this view is no longer upheld**, concluding that the empirical/normative distinction **cannot be treated as coterminous with** the traditional/contemporary one. ⚠️ Lesson 4 supplies the counter-examples in both directions that make the refusal stick. 🔑 The safe formulation is that traditional and contemporary is a **dating** distinction about dominant styles of inquiry, nothing more.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the four traditional and three contemporary headings as a closed classification into which every piece of political scholarship must fall. The chapter explicitly says a comprehensive list is **not possible** and that the compartments are not watertight; it offers the roster as what is **usually identified**. ❌ Equally, do not read "no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches" as the chapter's own preference — it is a report about who favours what, and the chapter's own verdict at Lesson 15 is that **any other approach will also be incomplete without paying due attention to institutions**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** On what basis does the chapter separate traditional from contemporary approaches, and what does the label not mean? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** The basis is a date. Approaches **in vogue till the end of the Second World War (1939-45)** are traditional; those **developed thereafter** are contemporary. ⚠️ The label carries no verdict. Alan Ball holds that **'traditional' is neither a criticism nor a refutation of the obvious fact that they still play important role in modern political studies**, and the chapter's own formulation is that they **have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches** — a change of preference, not of validity. ⚠️ Two hedges limit the classification: **it is not possible to furnish any comprehensive lists**, and the clusters **do not represent watertight compartments**. ⚠️ Decisively, the equation of traditional with values **is no longer upheld**, so the two distinctions are **not coterminous**.

**Rubric [10]:** the dating criterion with the war years **2** · Ball's caution **2** · the preference-not-validity formulation **2** · the two hedges on lists and compartments **2** · the refused facts/values equation **2**.

## Lesson 4 — The equation the chapter refuses

Progress: 4 / 34 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — conceptual hygiene

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§5 and 9 with the Advanced companion's §§2 and 4, read against the opening of the chapter's section headed "Empirical and Normative Approaches" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.110 = printed 96), which supplies the refusal of the coincidence, three traditional thinkers rich in empirical content, four contemporary thinkers rich in normative content, and the broad definitions of the two approaches.
CA search: "empirical normative distinction political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem

Lesson 3 ended with a refusal. This lesson is the proof. The chapter does not merely assert that traditional ≠ normative; it produces **seven named counter-examples**, and they are the most quotable thing in the first half of the chapter.

### 2. The refusal restated

✅ **Although contemporary political science gives prominence to empirical approach and traditional study of politics was dominated by normative approach, it cannot be assumed that the distinction between empirical and normative approaches reflects the distinction between contemporary and traditional approaches.**

✅ The reason: **some features of empirical as well as normative approaches are found both in the traditional and contemporary approaches.**

🔑 Note the structure of the argument. The chapter **concedes the correlation** (traditional work *was* dominated by the normative) and then denies the **identity**. That is a more sophisticated move than a flat denial, and reproducing the concession is what makes an answer look read rather than remembered.

### 3. The counter-examples, both directions

| Direction | Named case | The chapter's verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **Traditional but empirical** | **Aristotle's analysis of the causes of revolution (or rebellion)** | ✅ **rich in empirical content** |
| | **Montesquieu's theory of separation of powers** | ✅ **rich in empirical content** |
| | **Marx's analysis of the exploitation of the working classes** | ✅ **rich in empirical content** |
| **Contemporary but normative** | **Karl Popper's advocacy of incremental change** | ✅ **very rich in their normative content** |
| | **F.A. Hayek's defence of libertarianism** | ✅ **very rich in their normative content** |
| | **C.B. Macpherson's concept of creative freedom** | ✅ **very rich in their normative content** |
| | **Rawls's theory of justice** | ✅ **very rich in their normative content** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **Learn the list as a list.** This is one of the few places in the chapter where a question can be answered almost entirely by accurate recall of names plus one sentence of framing, and the advanced owner's prompt for this topic asks for exactly these cases.

### 4. The broad definitions

✅ With the equation out of the way, the chapter defines:

> Broadly speaking, the empirical approach seeks to discover and describe facts whereas the normative approach seeks to determine and prescribe values.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.110 (printed 96)

⚠️ The extraction garbles **describe facts** as `describe/ac?s`; the reading is secured by the parallel structure and by the summary table at p.112, which gives **Facts** and **Values** as the two chief concerns. The correction is catalogued.

✅ And in terms of the statement produced: the empirical approach aims at **an empirical statement which is concerned with 'is'**, the normative at **a normative statement which is concerned with what 'ought to be' or 'should be'**.

🔑 But — and this is Lesson 5 — ✅ **these forms of expression cannot be followed literally as the criterion of distinction between the two approaches.**

### 5. The visual

```text
          TRADITIONAL                        CONTEMPORARY
   (to 1945, dominated by normative)   (after 1945, prominence to empirical)
   +-----------------------------+     +-----------------------------+
   |                             |     |                             |
   |  >>> BUT RICH IN EMPIRICAL  |     |  >>> BUT RICH IN NORMATIVE  |
   |      CONTENT:               |     |      CONTENT:               |
   |                             |     |                             |
   |   ARISTOTLE                 |     |   POPPER                    |
   |     causes of revolution    |     |     incremental change      |
   |     (or rebellion)          |     |                             |
   |                             |     |   HAYEK                     |
   |   MONTESQUIEU               |     |     defence of              |
   |     separation of powers    |     |     libertarianism          |
   |                             |     |                             |
   |   MARX                      |     |   MACPHERSON                |
   |     exploitation of the     |     |     creative freedom        |
   |     working classes         |     |                             |
   |                             |     |   RAWLS                     |
   |                             |     |     theory of justice       |
   +-----------------------------+     +-----------------------------+
            3 cases                              4 cases

   THE TWO CUTS CROSS EACH OTHER. They are not the same line.

          |  EMPIRICAL content   |  NORMATIVE content
   -------+----------------------+---------------------
   TRAD.  |  Aristotle,          |  (the dominant case)
          |  Montesquieu, Marx   |
   -------+----------------------+---------------------
   CONTEM.|  (the dominant case) |  Popper, Hayek,
          |                      |  Macpherson, Rawls
```

*Caption: the 2x2 at the bottom is the honest picture. Each cell is occupied; the diagonal is merely heavier than the off-diagonal. That is a correlation, not an identity — which is precisely the chapter's claim.*

### 6. Why this is the chapter's most reusable paragraph

⚠️ Because it is a transferable **form of argument**, not just a fact about political science. Any question that offers you a tidy binary — ancient/modern, Eastern/Western, descriptive/prescriptive — can be answered with this shape: concede the correlation, name counter-examples in both directions, and conclude that the two distinctions **are not coterminous**. 🔑 Lesson 34 builds that move into the answer architecture.

### Revision notes

- ✅ The concession first: contemporary political science **gives prominence to empirical approach** and traditional study **was dominated by normative approach**.
- 🔑 The denial second: it **cannot be assumed that the distinction between empirical and normative approaches reflects the distinction between contemporary and traditional approaches**.
- ✅ The ground of denial: **some features of empirical as well as normative approaches are found both in the traditional and contemporary approaches**.
- ✅ Traditional but empirically rich: **Aristotle** (causes of revolution or rebellion), **Montesquieu** (separation of powers), **Marx** (exploitation of the working classes).
- ✅ Contemporary but normatively rich: **Karl Popper** (incremental change), **F.A. Hayek** (libertarianism), **C.B. Macpherson** (creative freedom), **Rawls** (theory of justice).
- ✅ **The empirical approach seeks to discover and describe facts whereas the normative approach seeks to determine and prescribe values.**
- ✅ Empirical statement is concerned with **'is'**; normative with **'ought to be' or 'should be'**.
- 🔑 ⚠️ **These forms of expression cannot be followed literally as the criterion of distinction between the two approaches.**
- ⚠️ The argument form is transferable: concede the correlation, name counter-examples both ways, deny the identity.
- ⚠️ Three traditional cases against four contemporary ones — both off-diagonal cells are occupied.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter name seven thinkers rather than simply asserting that the two distinctions differ? Reconstruct the argument's structure and say what it would take to defeat it.

**Model answer:** Because the claim being attacked is a **universal** one — that traditional work is normative and contemporary work empirical — and a universal claim is defeated by instances, not by counter-assertion. ⚠️ The chapter builds the argument in three moves. ✅ First it **concedes** the correlation: contemporary political science **gives prominence to empirical approach** and the traditional study of politics **was dominated by normative approach**. ⚠️ That concession is strategically important, because it shows the chapter is not denying the obvious historical tendency. ✅ Second it states the ground of denial: **some features of empirical as well as normative approaches are found both in the traditional and contemporary approaches**. ✅ Third it populates both off-diagonal cells — **Aristotle** on the causes of revolution, **Montesquieu** on separation of powers and **Marx** on the exploitation of the working classes are traditional yet **rich in empirical content**; **Popper** on incremental change, **Hayek** on libertarianism, **Macpherson** on creative freedom and **Rawls** on justice are contemporary yet **very rich in their normative content**. ⚠️ To defeat the argument one would have to show that these seven attributions are wrong — that Aristotle's account of revolution is not empirical, or that Rawls's theory is not normative — which is a far harder task than disputing a general claim. 🔑 That is the point: the chapter has converted a methodological dispute into a question of textual fact, where it is much more secure.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not report the seven cases as showing that the traditional/contemporary distinction is **worthless**. The chapter keeps the distinction and uses it to organise the rest of the chapter; what it refuses is only the stronger claim that the distinction is **coterminous** with the empirical/normative one. ❌ And do not transfer the cases carelessly — the chapter says Aristotle's analysis of the **causes of revolution** is empirically rich, not that Aristotle was an empiricist, and it says Marx's analysis of **exploitation of the working classes** is empirically rich while Lesson 32 shows the chapter also treats Marxian analysis as refusing the autonomy of politics. Each attribution is to a specific piece of work.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Show, with named instances, why empirical and normative do not map onto contemporary and traditional. *(Show — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** The chapter concedes the correlation and denies the identity. It grants that contemporary political science **gives prominence to empirical approach** and that traditional study **was dominated by normative approach**, then rules that the distinctions cannot be assumed to reflect one another because **some features of empirical as well as normative approaches are found both in the traditional and contemporary approaches**. ⚠️ Three traditional cases are **rich in empirical content**: **Aristotle's analysis of the causes of revolution (or rebellion)**, **Montesquieu's theory of separation of powers**, and **Marx's analysis of the exploitation of the working classes**. ⚠️ Four contemporary cases are **very rich in their normative content**: **Popper's advocacy of incremental change**, **Hayek's defence of libertarianism**, **Macpherson's concept of creative freedom** and **Rawls's theory of justice**. ⚠️ Both off-diagonal cells are occupied, so the correlation is heavy but not an identity.

**Rubric [10]:** the conceded correlation stated before the denial **2** · three traditional-but-empirical cases named correctly **3** · four contemporary-but-normative cases named correctly **3** · the occupied-off-diagonal conclusion **2**.

## Lesson 5 — Form against content: the test that actually works

Progress: 5 / 34 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — conceptual hygiene

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§3, 5 and 7 with the Advanced companion's §§2 and 4, read against the chapter's paragraphs distinguishing empirical from normative statements (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.110-111 = printed 96-97), which supply the observation/verification criterion, the two definitions of justice, the voting example in both its phrasings, the intrinsic/instrumental pair and the closing rule about content.
CA search: "fact value distinction political science is ought 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem

Everyone is taught the slogan: **"is" statements are empirical, "ought" statements are normative.** The chapter spends two pages showing that the slogan is false in both directions, and supplies the replacement. This is the single most transferable idea in Part I.

### 2. What actually makes a statement empirical

✅ The criterion is triple:

> an empirical statement is concerned with a situation which can be observed by our sense-experience, which can be verified by repeated observation and whose accuracy can be tested.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.110 (printed 96)

🔑 Three tests, not one: **observed by sense-experience** · **verified by repeated observation** · **accuracy can be tested**.

### 3. What actually makes a statement normative

✅ And the counterpart:

> a normative statement tends to express preference for a particular type of order as dictated by a sense of duty or universal need or by commitment to a moral principle or ideal. While strong arguments may be advanced in support of a normative statement, it is not capable of being discovered, described or verified by our sense-experience.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.110 (printed 96)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Do not skip the middle clause.** ✅ **Strong arguments may be advanced in support of a normative statement.** The chapter is denying verifiability, **not** arguability — and Lesson 6 turns that concession into a full reply to Weldon.

### 4. Direction one: "is" that is not empirical

✅ The worked case is the definition of justice. The chapter offers two candidate answers to **what is justice**:

- **justice is treating equals equally and unequals unequally**
- **justice is giving equal freedom and equal opportunity to all provided any departure from equal distribution will prove beneficial to the least advantaged**

✅ And rules:

> Now all such answers purporting to define what is justice express a variety of value preferences; none of them is based on empirical observation or is capable of empirical verification. So in spite of using the 'is' form they are by no means empirical statements.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.110 (printed 96)

⚠️ Note in passing that the second formulation is recognisably Rawlsian in content, and that the chapter has just listed **Rawls's theory of justice** among the normatively rich contemporary cases at Lesson 4. ❌ But the chapter does **not** attribute the sentence to Rawls here, and neither should you. ➡️ Rawls's theory belongs to the justice owner.

### 5. Direction two: "ought" that is empirical

✅ The same example is given in two phrasings, deliberately:

- **everybody ought to vote in election in order to make democracy work** — fulfilment of a purpose
- **if democracy is to work, everybody ought to (or should, or must) vote in election**

✅ And the ruling:

> Now these types of statements are certainly empirical statements in spite of using the 'ought to' form, because their contents can be empirically verified or repudiated.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.110-111 (printed 96-97)

🔑 The reason is exact: **their contents can be empirically verified or repudiated**. Whether universal voting in fact makes democracy work is a question about the world.

### 6. The underlying machinery: intrinsic against instrumental

✅ Why does the "ought" collapse into an empirical claim in one case and not the other? Because of what the required action serves:

| | Serves an **intrinsic value** | Serves an **instrumental value** |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | ✅ **an end-in-itself** | ✅ **a means to some higher end** |
| The chapter's examples | ✅ **the truth, the good, the beautiful** | ✅ **do regular exercise to improve your health**; **grow more trees to reduce environmental pollution** |
| Statement type | **Normative** | **Empirical** |
| Why | The end cannot be verified | ⚠️ The means-end link **can** be tested |

🔑 And then the rule, which is the sentence to carry into every answer on this topic:

> In short, it is the content of a statement, not its form, which makes it empirical or normative.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.111 (printed 97)

### 7. The visual

```text
   THE SLOGAN (FALSE):   "is" = empirical      "ought" = normative

   COUNTER-CASE 1: "IS" that is NOT empirical
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   "Justice IS treating equals equally and unequals unequally"
   "Justice IS giving equal freedom and equal opportunity to all
    provided any departure from equal distribution will prove
    beneficial to the least advantaged"
         |
         +--> expresses "a VARIETY OF VALUE PREFERENCES"
         +--> "NONE of them is based on empirical observation
              or is capable of empirical verification"
         => NORMATIVE, despite the "is"

   COUNTER-CASE 2: "OUGHT" that IS empirical
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   "Everybody OUGHT TO vote in election IN ORDER TO make
    democracy work"
   "IF democracy is to work, everybody OUGHT TO (or should, or
    must) vote in election"
         |
         +--> "their CONTENTS can be empirically VERIFIED or
              REPUDIATED"
         => EMPIRICAL, despite the "ought"

   THE MACHINERY UNDERNEATH
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
        does the required action serve ...
                      |
        +-------------+--------------+
        |                            |
   INTRINSIC value              INSTRUMENTAL value
   "an END-IN-ITSELF"           "a MEANS to some HIGHER END"
   the truth | the good |       exercise -> health
   the beautiful                trees    -> less pollution
        |                            |
   NOT verifiable               means-end link IS testable
        |                            |
   NORMATIVE                    EMPIRICAL

   >>> THE RULE: "it is the CONTENT of a statement, NOT ITS FORM,
       which makes it empirical or normative."
```

*Caption: memorise the two counter-cases as a matched pair. Offering only one of them looks like a half-remembered example; offering both, with the intrinsic/instrumental machinery between them, looks like comprehension.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ Empirical statement: **can be observed by our sense-experience**, **verified by repeated observation**, **accuracy can be tested** — three tests.
- ✅ Normative statement: **expresses preference for a particular type of order as dictated by a sense of duty or universal need or by commitment to a moral principle or ideal**.
- 🔑 ⚠️ **Strong arguments may be advanced in support of a normative statement**, yet it is **not capable of being discovered, described or verified by our sense-experience** — arguability is conceded, verifiability denied.
- ✅ "Is" but normative: **justice is treating equals equally and unequals unequally**, and the least-advantaged formulation.
- ✅ Their verdict: they **express a variety of value preferences** and are **by no means empirical statements**.
- ✅ "Ought" but empirical: **everybody ought to vote in election in order to make democracy work**, in two phrasings.
- ✅ Their reason: **their contents can be empirically verified or repudiated**.
- ✅ **Intrinsic value** = **an end-in-itself** — **the truth, the good, the beautiful**.
- ✅ **Instrumental value** = **a means to some higher end** — exercise → health; trees → less pollution.
- 🔑 **It is the content of a statement, not its form, which makes it empirical or normative.**
- ⚠️ The least-advantaged formulation is Rawlsian in content but is **not** attributed to Rawls here. ➡️ The justice owner.

### Concept check

**Question:** Classify each of the following using the chapter's own test, giving the reason in each case: (i) "Reservation is justified because it corrects historical disadvantage." (ii) "If turnout falls below half, the legislature's claim to represent the electorate weakens." (iii) "Liberty is the absence of unreasonable restraint."

**Model answer:** The test is the chapter's rule that **it is the content of a statement, not its form, which makes it empirical or normative**, applied through the intrinsic/instrumental pair. ⚠️ **(i) Normative.** Despite the causal-sounding "because", the operative word is **justified**, which asserts that the correction of historical disadvantage is worth pursuing — an end treated as good in itself. ✅ It therefore **expresses preference for a particular type of order** and is **not capable of being discovered, described or verified by our sense-experience**. ⚠️ Note that a neighbouring statement — "reservation has in fact reduced the disadvantage" — would be empirical, which shows how narrowly the classification turns on content. ⚠️ **(ii) Empirical.** It is conditional and means-end in structure, exactly like the chapter's own **if democracy is to work, everybody ought to … vote in election**; whether a sub-half turnout weakens a representative claim is a matter whose **contents can be empirically verified or repudiated**, at least in principle. ⚠️ **(iii) Normative.** It has the pure "is" form of a definition, and the chapter's worked case is precisely a definition: answers **purporting to define what is justice express a variety of value preferences** and are **by no means empirical statements**. ⚠️ The word doing the work is **unreasonable**, which cannot be settled by observation. 🔑 In each case the classification was decided by asking what the statement's content commits one to, never by looking for "is" or "ought".

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not replace one slogan with another by concluding that "all definitions are normative". The chapter's claim is narrower: definitions **purporting to define what is justice** are normative because they **express a variety of value preferences**. ❌ A stipulative definition of a technical term — the chapter's own definition of **method**, for instance — carries no such preference. ❌ And do not infer from "strong arguments may be advanced in support of a normative statement" that normative statements are therefore verifiable; the chapter grants arguability while denying verifiability, and Lesson 6 depends on keeping those two apart.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Statements using 'ought' are normative and those using 'is' are empirical." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** The proposition is false in both directions, and the chapter refutes it with matched cases. Definitions of justice — **justice is treating equals equally and unequals unequally** — use the **is** form yet **express a variety of value preferences** and are **by no means empirical statements**, since none is **based on empirical observation or is capable of empirical verification**. ⚠️ Conversely, **everybody ought to vote in election in order to make democracy work** uses the **ought** form yet is empirical **because their contents can be empirically verified or repudiated**. ⚠️ The machinery is the intrinsic/instrumental pair: an **ought** serving **an end-in-itself** is normative, while one serving **a means to some higher end** makes a testable means-end claim. 🔑 Hence: **it is the content of a statement, not its form, which makes it empirical or normative**.

**Rubric [10]:** the 'is' counter-case with its verdict **3** · the 'ought' counter-case with its stated reason **3** · the intrinsic/instrumental machinery **2** · the content-not-form rule quoted **2**.

---

## Lesson 6 — Is the normative approach merely a matter of taste?

Progress: 6 / 34 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — conceptual hygiene

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§5 and 9 with the Advanced companion's §§4 and 7, read against the chapter's paragraphs on objectivity, terminology and validity (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.111 = printed 97), which supply Weldon's charge, the chapter's three-step reply, the true/false versus right/wrong confusion, the tentativeness of scientific principles and Dahl's observation about refusing to decide.
CA search: "T.D. Weldon vocabulary of politics 2026 political theory"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem

Lesson 5 conceded that normative statements cannot be verified. The obvious next move for a critic is: then they are not knowledge at all, merely preference — and there is nothing to discuss. The chapter meets that move head on, and the reply has three distinct steps.

### 2. The charge

✅ The charge is given a name and a book:

> T.D. Weldon, in his Vocabulary of Politics (1953) pointed out that a political philosophy is like a matter of taste; one can only state one's taste and go away—there is no point in arguing.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.111 (printed 97)

✅ And the chapter's verdict, delivered immediately and without hedging: **This is a biased view.**

⚠️ The context is the critics' claim that **the empirical approach is objective whereas the normative approach is subjective**. Weldon is cited as the sharpest statement of that claim.

### 3. Reply step one — disagreement does not entail incommensurability

✅ **While there may exist several schools of thought upholding different interpretations of, say, freedom, equality and justice, a dialogue between these schools can always be opened.**

⚠️ The chapter concedes the plurality of schools and denies the conclusion drawn from it. Plurality is a fact; silence is a choice.

### 4. Reply step two — shared principles of reasoning are constructible

✅ **Those holding different viewpoints can always come together to evolve or arrive at certain basic principles of reasoning by which they can prove or disprove different points.**

🔑 The key word is **evolve**. The chapter is not claiming that shared standards already exist waiting to be found; it claims they can be **built** by the parties. ⚠️ That is a weaker and more defensible claim than moral realism, and an answer that notices the difference is doing real work.

### 5. Reply step three — the disanalogy with taste

✅ **It is not like a matter of individual taste which can be stated but which cannot be defended.**

🔑 This is the decisive sentence, and it returns the concession made at Lesson 5. ✅ There, **strong arguments may be advanced in support of a normative statement**. Here, the point of that concession becomes clear: **defensibility**, not verifiability, is what separates a normative claim from a taste.

### 6. A second front: the vocabulary problem

✅ The chapter then notes that **the terminology adopted by empirical and normative approaches for approval or disapproval of any proposition creates confusion at times** — the categories **'true or false'** and **'right or wrong'** carry different meanings in, say, **mathematical and moral questions**.

✅ The assumption being attacked: that the **empirical approach refers to 'true or false', 'right or wrong' as absolute categories while normative approach treats them as conditional**.

✅ And the attack:

> However, it is now widely accepted that even scientific principles are largely tentative. Nobody can claim to have found the final truth in the realm of matter, not to speak of the realm of mind. Scientific principles in the sphere of nature as well as society can be treated as valid until they are repudiated by some new discovery.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.111 (printed 97)

🔑 The move is elegant: the alleged gap narrows from **both** ends. Normative claims turn out to be arguable; empirical claims turn out to be tentative.

### 7. And a practical consequence

✅ **In politics, particularly, we cannot afford to abstain from acting until a very high level of scientific validity is achieved.** The chapter closes the paragraph with **Robert Dahl** (*Modern Political Analysis*; 1991):

> in politics, 'refusing to decide' is simply deciding to allow others to decide for you
>
> — Dahl, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.111 (printed 97)

⚠️ ⚠️ Mark this sentence. It is the earliest appearance in the chapter of the argument that will become the **post-behavioural** case at Lesson 24: abstention is not neutrality. 🔑 Being able to say that Dahl's line at p.111 anticipates Easton's 1969 case at p.121 is a genuinely high-value observation.

### 8. The visual

```text
   WELDON'S CHARGE (Vocabulary of Politics; 1953)
   "a political philosophy is like a MATTER OF TASTE; one can only
    STATE one's taste and GO AWAY -- there is NO POINT IN ARGUING"
                          |
                 >>> "This is a BIASED view." <<<
                          |
   +----------------------+----------------------+
   |                      |                      |
   STEP 1                 STEP 2                 STEP 3
   plurality != silence   standards can be       taste cannot be
                          BUILT                  DEFENDED
   "several schools ...   "can always come       "It is NOT LIKE a
    a DIALOGUE between     together to EVOLVE     matter of individual
    these schools can      or arrive at certain   taste which can be
    ALWAYS BE OPENED"      BASIC PRINCIPLES OF    STATED but which
                           REASONING by which     CANNOT BE DEFENDED"
                           they can PROVE or
                           DISPROVE"
                          |
   SECOND FRONT: the gap narrows from BOTH ends
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   NORMATIVE side:  arguments CAN be advanced; dialogue CAN open
   EMPIRICAL side:  "even scientific principles are LARGELY
                     TENTATIVE"; valid "UNTIL THEY ARE REPUDIATED
                     BY SOME NEW DISCOVERY"
                          |
   PRACTICAL UPSHOT -- and a forward link to Lesson 24
   "we CANNOT AFFORD TO ABSTAIN FROM ACTING until a very high level
    of scientific validity is achieved"
        DAHL: "in politics, 'REFUSING TO DECIDE' is simply DECIDING
               TO ALLOW OTHERS TO DECIDE FOR YOU"
```

*Caption: the three-step reply is the shape to reproduce. Most answers give only step one — "but people do argue about justice" — which concedes too much, because Weldon can agree that people argue and still deny that the arguing settles anything.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ The critics' framing: **the empirical approach is objective whereas the normative approach is subjective**.
- ✅ **T.D. Weldon**, *Vocabulary of Politics* (1953): a political philosophy is **like a matter of taste; one can only state one's taste and go away—there is no point in arguing**.
- 🔑 The chapter's verdict: **This is a biased view.**
- ✅ Step 1: **a dialogue between these schools can always be opened**, despite **several schools of thought upholding different interpretations of, say, freedom, equality and justice**.
- ✅ Step 2: parties **can always come together to evolve or arrive at certain basic principles of reasoning by which they can prove or disprove different points**.
- ✅ Step 3: it is **not like a matter of individual taste which can be stated but which cannot be defended**.
- ✅ The vocabulary problem: **'true or false'**, **'right or wrong'** mean different things in **mathematical and moral questions**.
- ✅ **Even scientific principles are largely tentative**; they are **valid until they are repudiated by some new discovery**.
- ✅ **In politics, particularly, we cannot afford to abstain from acting until a very high level of scientific validity is achieved.**
- 🔑 **Robert Dahl** (*Modern Political Analysis*; 1991): **in politics, 'refusing to decide' is simply deciding to allow others to decide for you**.
- ⚠️ Dahl's line at p.111 anticipates the post-behavioural case at Lesson 24: abstention is not neutrality.
- ⚠️ Residual: step 2 is asserted, not demonstrated — no worked case of convergence is supplied.

### Concept check

**Question:** Weldon could reply: "I never denied that people argue about justice; I denied that the arguing can settle anything." Does the chapter's reply meet that version of the charge?

**Model answer:** Partly, and the honest answer must say where it does and where it does not. ⚠️ Step one — **a dialogue between these schools can always be opened** — does **not** meet the stronger charge, because Weldon can grant that dialogue occurs and still deny that it converges. ⚠️ Step two does meet it, if it can be made good: the claim is that parties **can always come together to evolve or arrive at certain basic principles of reasoning by which they can prove or disprove different points**. ✅ The operative verbs are **prove** and **disprove**, which is precisely settlement, and the operative verb for the standards is **evolve**, which concedes that they do not pre-exist the dialogue. ⚠️ Step three then states the conclusion: a normative position, unlike a taste, **can be defended**, and defensibility is what Weldon's analogy denies. ⚠️ The residual, which an honest answer should concede, is that the chapter **asserts** the possibility of evolving shared principles rather than demonstrating it — no worked example of two schools converging is supplied. ✅ The supporting move is indirect: since **even scientific principles are largely tentative** and hold only **until they are repudiated by some new discovery**, the contrast between settled empirical knowledge and unsettleable moral opinion is softer than Weldon's analogy requires. 🔑 So the reply shifts the burden convincingly without discharging it, and saying exactly that is stronger than claiming the chapter refutes Weldon outright.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not use Dahl's line as a general argument for activism. ✅ Its stated context is narrow — **we cannot afford to abstain from acting until a very high level of scientific validity is achieved** — so it licenses acting under uncertainty, not acting without evidence. ❌ And do not convert "even scientific principles are largely tentative" into a claim that empirical and normative inquiry are on an equal footing; the chapter has already said normative statements are **not capable of being discovered, described or verified by our sense-experience**, and it never withdraws that.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Assess the chapter's reply to the charge that political philosophy is a matter of taste. *(Assess — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Weldon holds that a political philosophy is **like a matter of taste; one can only state one's taste and go away—there is no point in arguing**; the chapter answers that **this is a biased view** and replies in three steps. First, plurality does not entail silence: **a dialogue between these schools can always be opened**. Second, standards are constructible: parties **can always come together to evolve or arrive at certain basic principles of reasoning by which they can prove or disprove different points**. Third, the disanalogy: it is **not like a matter of individual taste which can be stated but which cannot be defended**. ⚠️ The gap narrows from the other end too, since **even scientific principles are largely tentative**. ⚠️ The residual is that the second step is asserted without a worked case of convergence.

**Rubric [10]:** Weldon's charge in his own terms **2** · the three reply steps distinguished **4** · the tentativeness of scientific principles **2** · the conceded residual about an undemonstrated step **2**.
