## Lesson 7 — Describing and prescribing, in both directions

Progress: 7 / 34 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — conceptual hygiene

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§5 and 7 with the Advanced companion's §§4 and 6, read against the chapter's closing paragraphs on the two approaches (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.111-112 = printed 97-98), which supply the descriptive/prescriptive contrast, the unalterable/alterable law distinction, the two-way traffic between description and prescription, and the worked case in which empirical inquiry defeats a normative prescription.
CA search: "empirical evidence overturning normative premise political theory 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem

If the empirical approach describes and the normative approach prescribes, the two can be kept in separate rooms. The chapter's last move in this section is to show that they constantly trespass on each other — and that one of the trespasses is extremely powerful.

### 2. The headline contrast, and the reason behind it

✅ **The empirical approach remains largely descriptive while the normative approach is mainly prescriptive.** ⚠️ Keep the hedges: **largely** and **mainly**.

✅ The reason is given in terms of what kind of law each is about:

| | Empirical approach | Normative approach |
|---|---|---|
| Kind of law | ✅ **laws that are unalterable** | ✅ **laws and conditions largely created or adopted by human society, which are alterable** |
| Example | ✅ **law of gravitational force** | ✅ **laws governing property and public order** |
| Status | ✅ **beyond man's control; one can discover and describe them** | ✅ one can **examine how far they are morally right or wrong and then prescribe the right course** |

🔑 The argument is that **prescription only makes sense where change is possible**. You cannot sensibly prescribe to gravity. That is why the normative approach attaches to the humanly made.

### 3. The traffic runs both ways

✅ **Incidentally, in the normative approach prescription may be preceded by description.** ✅ The chapter's instance: **Plato and Aristotle had given description of their experience before prescribing their respective solutions.**

✅ **On the other hand, in the empirical approach description may be followed by prescription for the achievement of some obvious goals, such as economy and efficiency, or some specified goals, such as health and stability.**

⚠️ Read the two lists carefully. **Economy and efficiency** are called **obvious goals**; **health and stability** are called **specified goals**. ⚠️ The chapter does not explain the difference, and an answer should not invent one. ⚠️ A defensible reading is that the first pair are taken as unquestioned while the second must be stipulated — but that is a reconstruction, and should be labelled as such if used.

### 4. The most powerful move in Part I

✅ Then the chapter states a stronger claim: **empirical approach can render immense help in examining the grounds of a normative argument.** And it supplies a worked case:

> For example, Aristotle prescribed harsher punishment to a slave than to a freeman for the same crime, on the ground that a slave is less sensitive to punishment. Empirical approach has now established beyond doubt that men are not more or less sensitive to punishment because of their status as freemen or slaves. This is a sufficient reason to reject Aristotle's prescription in this behalf.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.112 (printed 98)

🔑 ⚠️ **Understand exactly what has and has not been shown.** The empirical finding does **not** refute the moral principle "punishment should be proportioned to sensitivity". It refutes the **factual premise** that slaves are less sensitive. The prescription falls because one of its legs was empirical all along.

⚠️ This is the chapter's demonstration that the two approaches are not sealed off: a normative argument almost always contains factual premises, and those premises are fair game for empirical inquiry.

### 5. The visual

```text
   A NORMATIVE ARGUMENT, DISSECTED
   (Aristotle on punishing slaves -- the chapter's own example)

   PREMISE 1 (NORMATIVE):  punishment should be proportioned to
                           the offender's sensitivity to it
                                      |
   PREMISE 2 (EMPIRICAL):  "a slave is LESS SENSITIVE to punishment"
                                      |
   CONCLUSION (PRESCRIPTIVE): "harsher punishment to a slave than
                              to a freeman for the SAME CRIME"

              |                        |
              |                        |
     EMPIRICAL APPROACH CANNOT   EMPIRICAL APPROACH CAN AND DID:
     TOUCH THIS                  "established BEYOND DOUBT that men
                                  are NOT more or less sensitive to
                                  punishment because of their status
                                  as freemen or slaves"
                                            |
                                   "a SUFFICIENT REASON to REJECT
                                    Aristotle's prescription
                                    IN THIS BEHALF"

   >>> THE PRESCRIPTION FALLS -- not because the moral principle
       was refuted, but because ONE LEG OF IT WAS EMPIRICAL
       ALL ALONG and has been knocked away.

   TRAFFIC IN BOTH DIRECTIONS
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   NORMATIVE:  description --> prescription
               "Plato and Aristotle had given DESCRIPTION of their
                experience BEFORE prescribing their respective
                solutions"

   EMPIRICAL:  description --> prescription
               "for the achievement of some OBVIOUS goals, such as
                economy and efficiency, or some SPECIFIED goals,
                such as health and stability"
```

*Caption: the dissection is the reusable part. Any normative claim in any topic can be attacked this way — find the factual premise, and test it. This is the single most portable analytical technique in the whole module.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **The empirical approach remains largely descriptive while the normative approach is mainly prescriptive** — ⚠️ with both hedges kept.
- ✅ Empirical approach seeks **laws that are unalterable** (e.g. **law of gravitational force**).
- ✅ Those laws are **beyond man's control; one can discover and describe them**.
- ✅ Normative approach deals with laws **largely created or adopted by human society, which are alterable** (e.g. **laws governing property and public order**).
- ✅ One may **examine how far they are morally right or wrong and then prescribe the right course**.
- ✅ **In the normative approach prescription may be preceded by description** — **Plato and Aristotle** described before prescribing.
- ✅ In the empirical approach **description may be followed by prescription** for **obvious goals, such as economy and efficiency, or some specified goals, such as health and stability**.
- 🔑 **Empirical approach can render immense help in examining the grounds of a normative argument.**
- 🔑 The worked case: Aristotle's prescription rested on **a slave is less sensitive to punishment**; inquiry **established beyond doubt** otherwise — **a sufficient reason to reject Aristotle's prescription in this behalf**.
- ⚠️ What falls is the prescription, because its **factual premise** fell — not the moral principle above it.
- ⚠️ **In this behalf** limits the rejection to that prescription, not to Aristotle generally.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Gauba's slave example proves that empirical inquiry can refute moral claims." Correct the statement precisely, and then state what the example does prove.

**Model answer:** The statement is wrong as it stands, and the correction is exact rather than verbal. ⚠️ Empirical inquiry did not refute a moral claim; it refuted a **factual premise embedded inside** a moral argument. ✅ The chapter's structure is clear: Aristotle **prescribed harsher punishment to a slave than to a freeman for the same crime, on the ground that a slave is less sensitive to punishment**. ⚠️ That ground is a claim about the world, and it is the only thing empirical inquiry touched when it **established beyond doubt that men are not more or less sensitive to punishment because of their status as freemen or slaves**. ⚠️ The underlying moral principle — that punishment should answer to the offender's sensitivity — was neither tested nor refuted, and would survive unscathed if the factual premise had been true. ✅ What the example does prove is the chapter's stated claim that **empirical approach can render immense help in examining the grounds of a normative argument**, and that such examination can be decisive: the finding is **a sufficient reason to reject Aristotle's prescription in this behalf**. ⚠️ Note how carefully the chapter limits the conclusion with **in this behalf** — the rejection is of that prescription, not of Aristotle. 🔑 The transferable technique is to treat every normative argument as a two-legged structure and ask which leg is factual, because that leg is the one an empirically trained reader can actually attack.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not generalise the descriptive/prescriptive contrast into a rule that empirical work never prescribes and normative work never describes. ✅ The chapter supplies counter-traffic in both directions in the same paragraph, and hedges the contrast with **largely** and **mainly**. ❌ And do not reproduce "economy and efficiency" and "health and stability" as a single list; the chapter calls the first pair **obvious goals** and the second **specified goals**, and it does not explain the difference — so inventing one is adding to the source.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How can empirical inquiry bear on a normative argument? Illustrate from the chapter. *(How — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** It bears on the factual premises such arguments contain. The chapter's claim is that **empirical approach can render immense help in examining the grounds of a normative argument**, and its worked case is exact. Aristotle **prescribed harsher punishment to a slave than to a freeman for the same crime, on the ground that a slave is less sensitive to punishment**. Empirical inquiry **established beyond doubt that men are not more or less sensitive to punishment because of their status as freemen or slaves**, which is **a sufficient reason to reject Aristotle's prescription in this behalf**. ⚠️ What fell was the premise, not the moral principle that punishment should answer to sensitivity. ⚠️ The transferable technique is to treat every prescription as two-legged and test the factual leg, since that is the only one empirical work can reach.

**Rubric [10]:** the general claim about examining grounds **2** · the three stages of the Aristotle case reproduced **4** · the precise statement of what fell **2** · the transferable technique **2**.

---

## Lesson 8 — Strauss's charge and the bridge the chapter demands

Progress: 8 / 34 | Stage: Foundation | Part I — conceptual hygiene

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§6 and 9 with the Advanced companion's §§3, 5, 7 and 12, read against the chapter's closing paragraphs of the empirical/normative section and the boxed summary table (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.112 = printed 98), which supply the empiricists' charge, the normativists' counter-charge, Strauss's quoted sentence with its citation, the estrangement warning and the four-row comparison table.
CA search: "Leo Strauss value free social science criticism 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem

Part I closes with the two camps stating their objections to each other. The asymmetry between the two objections is itself examinable, and the chapter's own verdict — which refuses both extremes — is the sentence that governs the rest of the module.

### 2. The charges, and their asymmetry

| | What is attacked | The chapter's wording |
|---|---|---|
| **Empiricists attack normativists** | The **whole approach** | ✅ **there is no 'scientifically valid' or reliable method of determining what is morally right or wrong** |
| **Normativists attack empiricists** | ⚠️ **Not** the approach — only an attitude | ✅ they **do not condemn the empirical approach as such, but they criticize its indifference toward values, particularly its ignorance of discrimination between higher and lower values** |

🔑 ⚠️ **The asymmetry is the point and it is routinely missed.** The empiricists' objection is total; the normativists' is targeted. ⚠️ This asymmetry is what makes the post-behavioural settlement at Lesson 24 possible at all: if the normative side had condemned empirical inquiry **as such**, no synthesis could have been offered.

### 3. Strauss's sentence

✅ The chapter gives the normativist objection its most forceful voice:

> By teaching the equality of values, by denying that there are things which are intrinsically high and others which are intrinsically low as well as by denying that there is an essential difference between men and brutes, it unwittingly contributes to the victory of the gutter
>
> — Leo Strauss, *Essays on the Scientific Study of Politics*, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.112 (printed 98)

⚠️ Three notes on the citation, all of which belong in the Source Ledger and all of which an examiner-proof answer should be able to handle.
- ⚠️ The extraction prints **diffrence**, normalised here to **difference** and catalogued.
- ⚠️ The chapter gives the volume's editor as **Herbert Strong**. An independent check confirms the 1962 volume *Essays on the Scientific Study of Politics* was edited by **Herbert J. Storing**. ✅ The chapter's form is reproduced where the chapter is quoted; the correction is recorded as an open uncertainty.
- 🔑 The word **unwittingly** is doing heavy work. Strauss is not accusing value-free scholars of wanting the gutter to win; he is accusing the method of having that effect regardless of intent.

### 4. The chapter's own verdict

✅ And then, in two sentences, the chapter refuses the quarrel:

> The state of estrangement between empirical and normative approaches, wherever it exists, will prove disastrous. There is an urgent need to build a bridge between the two approaches which will be beneficial not only for their respective upholders, but also for the human civilization itself.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.112 (printed 98)

🔑 ⚠️ **This is the chapter's thesis, and it should open or close any 15- or 20-mark answer on this topic.** ⚠️ Note **wherever it exists** — the chapter does not assert that the estrangement is universal, only that it is disastrous where found.

⚠️ Note also where this sentence sits: it is written **before** behaviouralism has even been introduced. The post-behavioural revolution at Lesson 24 is therefore not a surprise in this chapter's architecture; it is the predicted consequence of a bridge not being built.

### 5. The summary table

✅ The section closes with a boxed table, reproduced here in its printed rows:

> | The Issue | Empirical Approach | Normative Approach |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Chief Concern** | **Facts** *(It is so.)* | **Values** *(It ought to be so.)* |
> | **Nature** | **Scientific and Descriptive** | **Critical and Prescriptive** |
> | **Based on** | **Sense-Experience and Logic** | **Speculation and Logic** |
> | **Criterion of Validity** | **True or False** | **Right or Wrong** |
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.112 (printed 98), boxed table "Distinction between Empirical and Normative Approaches"

⚠️ ⚠️ **Two observations about this table that most answers miss.**

1. ⚠️ **Logic appears on both sides.** **Sense-Experience and Logic** against **Speculation and Logic**. The shared term is the chapter's own structural concession that normative inquiry is not arbitrary — exactly the Weldon reply of Lesson 6, compressed into a table cell.
2. ⚠️ **The "Nature" row calls the normative approach Critical, not merely prescriptive.** Criticism presupposes standards that can be stated and contested, which again cuts against the taste analogy.

### 6. The visual

```text
   THE TWO CHARGES -- NOTE THE ASYMMETRY

   EMPIRICISTS  ---> NORMATIVISTS
   "no 'SCIENTIFICALLY VALID' or RELIABLE METHOD of determining
    what is morally right or wrong"
   => attacks the APPROACH ITSELF.  TOTAL.

   NORMATIVISTS ---> EMPIRICISTS
   "do NOT CONDEMN the empirical approach AS SUCH, but they
    criticize its INDIFFERENCE TOWARD VALUES, particularly its
    IGNORANCE OF DISCRIMINATION between HIGHER and LOWER values"
   => attacks an ATTITUDE, not the approach.  TARGETED.

        STRAUSS, at full force:
        "By teaching the EQUALITY OF VALUES ... it UNWITTINGLY
         contributes to the VICTORY OF THE GUTTER"

                        |
            THE CHAPTER'S OWN VERDICT
                        |
   "The state of ESTRANGEMENT between empirical and normative
    approaches, WHEREVER IT EXISTS, will prove DISASTROUS.
    There is an URGENT NEED TO BUILD A BRIDGE between the two
    approaches ..."
                        |
   >>> and this is written BEFORE behaviouralism is introduced.
       The POST-BEHAVIOURAL REVOLUTION (Lesson 24) is what
       happens when the bridge is NOT built.
```

*Caption: the asymmetry in the top half explains the possibility of the bottom half. A total objection admits no bridge; a targeted objection asks for one.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ Empiricists' charge: **there is no 'scientifically valid' or reliable method of determining what is morally right or wrong**.
- 🔑 Normativists' counter-charge is **targeted, not total**: they **do not condemn the empirical approach as such, but they criticize its indifference toward values**.
- ✅ The specific complaint is its **ignorance of discrimination between higher and lower values**.
- ✅ **Leo Strauss**: value-free teaching, by **teaching the equality of values** and **denying that there are things which are intrinsically high and others which are intrinsically low**, **unwittingly contributes to the victory of the gutter**.
- ⚠️ **Unwittingly** matters: the charge is about effect, not intent.
- ⚠️ Volume editor printed as **Herbert Strong**; independently verified as **Herbert J. Storing** — recorded as an open uncertainty.
- 🔑 The chapter's thesis: **the state of estrangement … wherever it exists, will prove disastrous**; there is **an urgent need to build a bridge between the two approaches**.
- ✅ The bridge is said to benefit **not only … their respective upholders, but also … the human civilization itself**.
- ✅ Table rows: **Chief Concern** — **Facts** / **Values**; **Nature** — **Scientific and Descriptive** / **Critical and Prescriptive**; **Based on** — **Sense-Experience and Logic** / **Speculation and Logic**; **Criterion of Validity** — **True or False** / **Right or Wrong**.
- ⚠️ **Logic** appears on **both** sides of the table; the normative approach is called **Critical**, not merely prescriptive.
- ⚠️ The thesis is stated **before** behaviouralism is introduced, so Lesson 24 reads as its predicted consequence.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter's table lists the normative approach as based on "Speculation and Logic". Does that concede Weldon's charge from Lesson 6? Argue both ways and settle it.

**Model answer:** It looks like a concession and is not one, but the appearance has to be taken seriously first. ⚠️ **For the concession:** **speculation** is a dismissive word, suggesting unanchored conjecture; set against the empirical approach's **sense-experience**, it seems to grant that normative claims rest on nothing observable, which is the premise Weldon needs for his conclusion that one can only **state one's taste and go away**. ⚠️ The **Criterion of Validity** row deepens the worry, since **right or wrong** is exactly the vocabulary Weldon says cannot be adjudicated. ⚠️ **Against the concession, and decisively:** the same cell contains **Logic**, and so does the empirical cell. ⚠️ The shared term denies that the two differ in rational discipline; they differ in **what they take as given**, not in whether reasoning constrains them. ⚠️ That is the table's compressed restatement of the Lesson 6 reply, which held that parties **can always come together to evolve or arrive at certain basic principles of reasoning by which they can prove or disprove different points**. ⚠️ The **Nature** row reinforces it by calling the normative approach **Critical and Prescriptive**: criticism requires standards that can be stated, applied and contested, which a taste cannot be. ⚠️ Settling it: the table concedes that normative inquiry begins from speculation rather than observation, and denies that it proceeds without logic — which is exactly the position the chapter argued for, and precisely not Weldon's. 🔑 The strongest version of this answer quotes **Speculation and Logic** and **Sense-Experience and Logic** side by side and lets the repetition make the point.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not present Strauss's sentence as the chapter's own view. ✅ It is quoted as the most forceful statement of the normativists' criticism, and the chapter's own verdict is the bridge-building paragraph that follows — which refuses both the empiricists' total rejection and any symmetrical rejection of empirical inquiry. ❌ And do not cite the volume's editor as "Herbert Strong" in your own voice as though it were settled; the chapter prints that name, the standard attribution is **Herbert J. Storing**, and the safe course is to cite the work and the author without the editor.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What does the chapter mean by the estrangement of the empirical and normative approaches, and why does it matter? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** It means the condition in which each approach proceeds without the other, which the chapter judges dangerous: **the state of estrangement between empirical and normative approaches, wherever it exists, will prove disastrous**, so there is **an urgent need to build a bridge between the two approaches**. ⚠️ The danger is stated asymmetrically. Empiricists deny there is any **'scientifically valid' or reliable method of determining what is morally right or wrong**; normativists **do not condemn the empirical approach as such** but criticise **its indifference toward values**, with Strauss charging that teaching **the equality of values** **unwittingly contributes to the victory of the gutter**. ⚠️ It matters because the thesis is stated before behaviouralism is introduced, so the post-behavioural revolution reads as the predicted cost of a bridge not built.

**Rubric [10]:** the estrangement sentence and the bridge demand **3** · the asymmetry of the two charges **3** · Strauss's formulation **2** · the placement of the thesis before behaviouralism **2**.

### 1. The roster, stated once

✅ **Philosophical, historical, legal and institutional approaches to the study of politics may be taken to represent the main traditional approaches.**

⚠️ **May be taken to** — the hedge from Lesson 3 is still operating. This is what is **usually identified**, not a closed list.

### 2. The first task: clarification

✅ **In the first place, the philosophical approach is concerned with the clarification of concepts used in a particular discipline.** ✅ And **Vernon Van Dyke** again:

> A philosophical analysis is an effort to clarify thought about the nature of the subject and about ends and means in studying it. Put more generally, a person who takes a philosophical approach to a subject aims to enhance linguistic clarity and to reduce linguistic confusion; he assumes that the language used in descriptions reflects conceptions of reality, and he wants to make conceptions of reality as clear, consistent, coherent, and helpful as possible.
>
> — Van Dyke, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.113 (printed 99)

🔑 Four adjectives govern the target state: **clear, consistent, coherent, and helpful**. ⚠️ The last is the surprising one. **Helpful** is a pragmatic criterion smuggled into a list of logical ones, and it is worth noticing because it quietly licenses the later post-behavioural demand for relevance.

⚠️ Note the embedded assumption: **the language used in descriptions reflects conceptions of reality**. That is why linguistic tidying is not mere pedantry on this account — confused words are evidence of confused conceptions.

### 3. The second task: evaluation

✅ **Secondly, the philosophical approach aims at evolving "standards of right and wrong" for the purpose of a critical evaluation of the existing institutions, laws and policies.** ✅ Van Dyke again:

> It may denote efforts to arrive at truth through the use of reason. The truth sought may be normative, descriptive, or prescriptive. The object of philosophic inquiry in this sense is to establish standards of the good, the right, and the just, and to appraise or prescribe political institutions and practices in the light of these standards.
>
> — Van Dyke, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.113 (printed 99)

🔑 **The truth sought may be normative, descriptive, or prescriptive** — three kinds, not one. ⚠️ This is a direct continuation of Lesson 5: philosophical inquiry is not confined to the normative, which is exactly why "traditional = normative" failed.

✅ The three standards named: **the good, the right, and the just**. ✅ The two operations: **appraise or prescribe**.

### 4. The qualification that saves the approach from antiquarianism

✅ **Most of the classical political theory represents philosophical approach.** Its themes are **generally concerned with moral reasoning which cannot be subjected to scientific test** — ✅ **although the empirical aspect of such reasoning can always be questioned**.

🔑 That final clause is the Lesson 7 technique, restated: the factual premises inside classical arguments remain open to attack.

✅ And a second line of attack is added: **the moral aspect of such reasoning can also be questioned from the viewpoint of our 'modern consciousness'.** ✅ The chapter's instance: **Kant's concept of 'human dignity' which rules out any type of slavery, is closer to modern consciousness than Aristotle's defence of slavery.**

✅ A third: **most of the political thinkers proceeded on some notion of 'human nature' which can now be questioned in the light of the findings of the contemporary psychology and social sciences.**

✅ Therefore: **the philosophical approach does not simply rely on the political thought of the past; it is a subject of current and continuous debate.**

### 5. The visual

```text
   THE PHILOSOPHICAL APPROACH HAS TWO TASKS, NOT ONE

   TASK 1 -- CLARIFICATION
   "concerned with the CLARIFICATION OF CONCEPTS used in a
    particular discipline"
        |
        +-- "enhance LINGUISTIC CLARITY and REDUCE LINGUISTIC
             CONFUSION"
        +-- assumption: "the LANGUAGE used in descriptions
             REFLECTS CONCEPTIONS OF REALITY"
        +-- target state: CLEAR | CONSISTENT | COHERENT | HELPFUL
                                                          ^
                                          a PRAGMATIC criterion
                                          hiding in a logical list

   TASK 2 -- EVALUATION
   "evolving 'STANDARDS OF RIGHT AND WRONG' for the purpose of a
    CRITICAL EVALUATION of the existing INSTITUTIONS, LAWS and
    POLICIES"
        |
        +-- truth sought may be NORMATIVE | DESCRIPTIVE |
            PRESCRIPTIVE   (three kinds -- note!)
        +-- standards: THE GOOD | THE RIGHT | THE JUST
        +-- operations: APPRAISE or PRESCRIBE

   AND THREE WAYS CLASSICAL THEORY REMAINS OPEN TO ATTACK
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   (a) its EMPIRICAL aspect "can ALWAYS be questioned"
   (b) its MORAL aspect can be questioned "from the viewpoint of
       our 'MODERN CONSCIOUSNESS'"
            e.g. KANT's 'human dignity' -- rules out any type of
            slavery -- vs ARISTOTLE's defence of slavery
   (c) its notion of 'HUMAN NATURE' can be questioned "in the light
       of the findings of CONTEMPORARY PSYCHOLOGY and SOCIAL
       SCIENCES"
            |
   => "the philosophical approach DOES NOT SIMPLY RELY on the
       political thought of the past; it is a subject of CURRENT
       AND CONTINUOUS DEBATE"
```

*Caption: the three lines of attack at the bottom are what prevent this approach from being merely the history of ideas — and they are also the precise difference between the philosophical and the historical approach, which Lesson 11 takes up.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ The roster: **philosophical, historical, legal and institutional approaches … may be taken to represent the main traditional approaches**.
- ✅ Task 1 — **the clarification of concepts used in a particular discipline**.
- ✅ Van Dyke: philosophical analysis is **an effort to clarify thought about the nature of the subject and about ends and means in studying it**.
- ✅ It aims **to enhance linguistic clarity and to reduce linguistic confusion**, assuming **the language used in descriptions reflects conceptions of reality**.
- ✅ Target state: **as clear, consistent, coherent, and helpful as possible** — ⚠️ **helpful** is the pragmatic outlier.
- ✅ Task 2 — **evolving "standards of right and wrong" for the purpose of a critical evaluation of the existing institutions, laws and policies**.
- ✅ Van Dyke: **the truth sought may be normative, descriptive, or prescriptive**; the object is to establish **standards of the good, the right, and the just** and to **appraise or prescribe**.
- ✅ **Most of the classical political theory represents philosophical approach**; its moral reasoning **cannot be subjected to scientific test**.
- ⚠️ But **the empirical aspect of such reasoning can always be questioned**.
- ✅ Second challenge: the moral aspect **from the viewpoint of our 'modern consciousness'** — **Kant's** human dignity against **Aristotle's defence of slavery**.
- ✅ Third challenge: notions of **human nature** tested against **contemporary psychology and social sciences**.
- 🔑 **The philosophical approach does not simply rely on the political thought of the past; it is a subject of current and continuous debate.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Van Dyke says the truth sought by philosophical inquiry "may be normative, descriptive, or prescriptive". Why is that three-way split important for this chapter's larger argument, and what would be lost if it were reduced to two?

**Model answer:** It is important because it supplies the textual basis for the chapter's refusal, at Lesson 4, to identify the traditional with the normative. ✅ If philosophical inquiry could seek only normative truth, the equation the chapter discards — **traditional approaches focus on values** — would be very hard to resist, since the chapter itself says **most of the classical political theory represents philosophical approach**. ⚠️ Van Dyke's three-way split blocks that inference from the inside: the philosophical approach itself makes **descriptive** claims, and so its products cannot be classified as normative by genre. ⚠️ The split also matters for the two tasks. Clarification is largely **descriptive** work — tidying the conceptions of reality that language reflects — while evaluation yields **normative** standards and **prescriptive** appraisals of institutions, so the three kinds map onto the approach's own internal division of labour. ⚠️ What would be lost by reducing it to two depends on which is dropped. ⚠️ Drop **descriptive**, and the clarification task loses its product, leaving philosophy as pure evaluation and reinstating the equation the chapter rejects. ⚠️ Drop **prescriptive**, and the approach can appraise institutions but not recommend, which contradicts Van Dyke's own **appraise or prescribe**. ⚠️ Either reduction also weakens the Lesson 7 technique, since attacking the **empirical aspect** of a classical argument presupposes that classical arguments contain descriptive content in the first place. 🔑 The three-way split is thus not a flourish; it is load-bearing for the chapter's central refusal.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not reduce the philosophical approach to "studying the great thinkers". ✅ The chapter's first task is **clarification of concepts**, which is work on present usage rather than on past texts, and it insists the approach **does not simply rely on the political thought of the past**. ❌ Reading the philosophical approach as the history of ideas also collapses it into the historical approach's second sense at Lesson 12, which the chapter keeps distinct. ❌ And do not quote the four adjectives as three — **helpful** is in the list, and it is the one that does unexpected work.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What are the two tasks of the philosophical approach? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** The first is clarification. The approach is **concerned with the clarification of concepts used in a particular discipline**, and Van Dyke describes philosophical analysis as **an effort to clarify thought about the nature of the subject and about ends and means in studying it**, aiming **to enhance linguistic clarity and to reduce linguistic confusion** on the assumption that **the language used in descriptions reflects conceptions of reality** — the target being conceptions **as clear, consistent, coherent, and helpful as possible**. ⚠️ The second is evaluation: it aims at **evolving "standards of right and wrong" for the purpose of a critical evaluation of the existing institutions, laws and policies**, establishing **standards of the good, the right, and the just**, where **the truth sought may be normative, descriptive, or prescriptive**. ⚠️ Hence it **does not simply rely on the political thought of the past**.

**Rubric [10]:** the clarification task with Van Dyke's formulation **3** · the four target adjectives including **helpful** **2** · the evaluative task with its three standards **3** · the three kinds of truth sought **2**.

## Lesson 10 — Philosophical approach II: the spine of classical theory

---

### 1. The problem

The chapter now does something unusual for a methodology chapter: it compresses the entire history of political philosophy into a single page, organised around **one question**. If you can reproduce that compression, you have a ready-made opening paragraph for a very wide range of questions.

### 2. The two themes

✅ **Most of the classical thinkers, proceeding from a hypothesis about human nature, dwelled on two main themes: 'art of government' and 'grounds of political obligation'.**

🔑 Everything that follows is organised by which of the two a thinker took up.

### 3. The chain, as the chapter gives it

| Thinker | The chapter's claim |
|---|---|
| **Aristotle** | ✅ Postulated **man is by nature a political animal** and **then elaborated his views on these two subjects** |
| **Machiavelli** | ✅ **Mainly dwelled on 'art of government'** on the assumption of **the very selfish and ungrateful nature of man** |
| — | 🔑 ✅ **Thereafter, 'art of government' ceased to be a part of mainstream political theory** |
| **Hobbes** | ✅ **Mainly focused on the grounds of political obligation**; his **absolutist view of political obligation** |
| **Rousseau and Hegel** | ✅ Carried on the absolutist view **on different grounds** |
| **Locke** | ✅ **Probably the first thinker to repudiate this absolutist view and to postulate 'rights' of the individual against the state**; hence **the pioneer of individualism which later developed into liberalism** |
| **Kant** | ✅ **Proceeding from different premises evolved the concept of 'human dignity'** |
| **J.S. Mill** | ✅ **Sought to explore the limits of political obligation by defining the conditions of state intervention** |
| **T.H. Green** | ✅ **Developed his theory of rights on moral grounds and sought to limit the authority of the state** |
| **Laski** | ✅ **Similarly tried to build an elaborate system of individual rights** |
| **John Rawls** | ✅ **Revived Kant's notion of 'rational negotiators' to build his theory of justice** |
| **Marx** | ✅ **Sought to demolish political obligation by identifying man's position in a social class**, and **projected the working class as an instrument of revolution** |
| **Neo-Marxists** | ✅ **Have been looking for alternative instruments as well as alternative strategies of revolution** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **One line deserves separate attention.** ✅ **Thereafter, 'art of government' ceased to be a part of mainstream political theory.** ⚠️ That is a strong historical claim, offered without argument, and it has an obvious counter-pressure: the whole second half of this very chapter — systems, structural-functional, communications and decision-making analysis — is arguably the art of government returning under a scientific name. ⚠️ The chapter never stages that tension. Noticing it is a legitimate and distinctive observation; attributing it to the chapter is not.

### 4. The derivation paragraph

✅ The compression, in the chapter's own words:

> In a nutshell, political philosophy mainly dwelled on the logic of the grounds and limits of political obligation. Concepts of individual rights and revolution are by-products of this debate. Conceptions of freedom, equality and justice are extensions of the theories of rights. The concept of democracy denotes an effort to translate the concept of individual rights into concrete political institutions. Again, concepts of authoritarianism, totalitarianism and fascism, etc. largely represent the negative side of this effort.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.114 (printed 100)

🔑 ⚠️ This is the most compressed map of the subject anywhere in the book, and it is examinable as a derivation chain, not as a list.

### 5. The visual

```text

   "the LOGIC OF THE GROUNDS AND LIMITS OF POLITICAL OBLIGATION"

          +---------------+---------------+

   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   'ART OF GOVERNMENT'          'GROUNDS OF POLITICAL OBLIGATION'
   ARISTOTLE (both)             ARISTOTLE (both)
   MACHIAVELLI                  HOBBES -- absolutist
      |                            ROUSSEAU, HEGEL -- same, on
   "THEREAFTER, 'art of              DIFFERENT GROUNDS
    government' CEASED to be      LOCKE -- first to REPUDIATE;

    political theory"                pioneer of INDIVIDUALISM
      |                              -> LIBERALISM
   (?) but see the second         KANT -- 'HUMAN DIGNITY'
   half of this very chapter      J.S. MILL -- LIMITS; conditions
   -- the chapter never              of state intervention
   raises the question            T.H. GREEN -- rights on MORAL
                                     grounds; LIMIT the state
                                  LASKI -- elaborate system of
                                     individual rights
                                  RAWLS -- revived KANT's
                                     'rational negotiators'
                                  MARX -- DEMOLISH obligation via
                                     man's position in a SOCIAL
                                     CLASS; working class as

                                  NEO-MARXISTS -- ALTERNATIVE
                                     instruments and strategies
```

*Caption: the top half is the derivation and the bottom half is the roster. In an answer, give the derivation first — it shows you can organise the history rather than merely list it.*

### 6. Strauss as the contemporary champion

✅ **Of the contemporary champions of the philosophical approach to the study of politics, Leo Strauss is the most outstanding.** The chapter's summary of his position:

- ✅ **Political science and political philosophy are coterminous.** They denote **an attempt to obtain true knowledge of political things as well as the standards of the right and the good**.
- ✅ **Political philosophy is a product of our quest for good life and good society.**
- ✅ **Values as well as facts are indispensable part of political philosophy** which enable **a critical and coherent analysis of political institutions and activities**.
- ✅ **Without such analysis, assumptions regarding the political things take the character of opinions.**
- ✅ **Political philosophy seeks to replace opinion by knowledge, as originally postulated by Socrates.**
- ✅ **Strauss has severely criticized the contemporary behavioural approach which insists on 'value-free analysis' and thus destroys the essence of true knowledge of politics.**

🔑 ⚠️ Note the structural role of the word **coterminous** here. At Lesson 3 the chapter denied that two distinctions were **coterminous**; here Strauss asserts that two disciplines are. ⚠️ The repetition is almost certainly incidental, but the contrast is instructive — Strauss's claim is precisely that the separation the behaviouralists want is not available.

### Revision notes

- ✅ Two classical themes, from **a hypothesis about human nature**: **'art of government'** and **'grounds of political obligation'**.
- ✅ **Aristotle**: **man is by nature a political animal**, then both themes. **Machiavelli**: art of government, on **the very selfish and ungrateful nature of man**.
- 🔑 **Thereafter, 'art of government' ceased to be a part of mainstream political theory.**
- ✅ **Hobbes** absolutist; **Rousseau** and **Hegel** carried it on **on different grounds**.
- ✅ **Locke** was **probably the first thinker to repudiate this absolutist view** and postulated **'rights' of the individual against the state** — **the pioneer of individualism which later developed into liberalism**.
- ✅ **Kant** evolved **'human dignity'**; **J.S. Mill** explored **the limits of political obligation by defining the conditions of state intervention**.
- ✅ **T.H. Green** built rights **on moral grounds**; **Laski** an **elaborate system of individual rights**; **Rawls** revived **Kant's notion of 'rational negotiators'**.
- ✅ **Marx** sought **to demolish political obligation by identifying man's position in a social class**; **neo-Marxists** seek **alternative instruments as well as alternative strategies of revolution**.
- 🔑 Derivation: obligation → **individual rights and revolution** as **by-products** → **freedom, equality and justice** as **extensions of the theories of rights** → **democracy** as the **translation** into institutions → **authoritarianism, totalitarianism and fascism** as **the negative side**.
- ✅ **Leo Strauss is the most outstanding** contemporary champion; **political science and political philosophy are coterminous**.
- ✅ **Values as well as facts are indispensable part of political philosophy**; it **seeks to replace opinion by knowledge, as originally postulated by Socrates**.
- ✅ He **severely criticized** the behavioural insistence on **'value-free analysis'**, which **destroys the essence of true knowledge of politics**.
- ⚠️ Unstaged tension: the chapter says the art of government left mainstream theory, yet its own second half reads as its scientific return.

### Concept check

**Question:** Use the chapter's derivation paragraph to answer, in outline, why a question about democracy is ultimately a question about political obligation.

**Model answer:** Because the chapter places democracy at the end of a four-step derivation whose first step is obligation. ✅ It states that **political philosophy mainly dwelled on the logic of the grounds and limits of political obligation** — the question of why anyone should obey, and how far. ✅ From that debate, **concepts of individual rights and revolution are by-products**: rights name what obligation does **not** cover, and revolution names what follows when the grounds fail altogether. ✅ Next, **conceptions of freedom, equality and justice are extensions of the theories of rights**, specifying what the protected sphere contains and how it is distributed. ✅ Then the decisive step: **the concept of democracy denotes an effort to translate the concept of individual rights into concrete political institutions**. ⚠️ So democracy, on this account, is not a free-standing ideal but an **institutional technology** for a prior normative claim; its justification therefore runs back through rights to the obligation question, and a defence of democracy that never reaches that question has not finished. ✅ The chapter completes the map by adding that **authoritarianism, totalitarianism and fascism** largely represent **the negative side of this effort** — the same translation attempted or refused in the other direction. ⚠️ A practical consequence worth stating is that the derivation gives an answer its spine: begin from obligation, pass through rights, and present democracy as the institutional answer rather than as the premise. 🔑 It also explains why **Marx** appears in the chain as seeking to **demolish political obligation** rather than to answer it — a move that detaches the whole chain from its anchor, which is exactly why Lesson 32's Marxian analysis denies the autonomy of politics.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the chain of thinkers as a chronology of influence in which each figure reacts to the one before. ✅ The chapter sorts them by **theme**, not by succession — Aristotle appears under both themes, Kant proceeds **from different premises**, and Rawls reaches back past three centuries to revive **Kant's notion of 'rational negotiators'**. ❌ And do not use the claim that **'art of government' ceased to be a part of mainstream political theory** as a settled historical fact; the chapter offers it without argument, and the chapter's own later sections on decision-making and systems analysis sit awkwardly beside it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Reconstruct the chapter's derivation of democracy from political obligation. *(Reconstruct — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** The derivation has four steps. Political philosophy **mainly dwelled on the logic of the grounds and limits of political obligation** — why anyone should obey, and how far. From that debate, **concepts of individual rights and revolution are by-products**: rights mark what obligation does not cover, revolution what follows when its grounds fail. Next, **conceptions of freedom, equality and justice are extensions of the theories of rights**, specifying the protected sphere and its distribution. Finally, **the concept of democracy denotes an effort to translate the concept of individual rights into concrete political institutions**, while **authoritarianism, totalitarianism and fascism** **largely represent the negative side of this effort**. ⚠️ On this account democracy is an institutional technology for a prior normative claim, so a defence of democracy that never reaches obligation is incomplete.

**Rubric [10]:** obligation identified as the root question **2** · rights and revolution as by-products **2** · freedom, equality and justice as extensions **2** · democracy as translation into institutions **2** · the negative side and the resulting consequence **2**.

## Lesson 11 — Historical approach I: historicism and its critic

Progress: 11 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part II — the traditional approaches

CA search: "historicism Popper political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### 1. The problem

"Historical approach" sounds like one thing. The chapter opens by saying it is two, and that they are so different that one of them is a theory of inevitability while the other is a way of reading books. Collapsing them is the standard error here.

### 2. The two senses

✅ **The term 'historical approach' to politics may be used in two senses.**

| | Sense 1 | Sense 2 |
|---|---|---|
| What it does | ✅ **the process of arriving at the laws governing politics through an analysis of historical events, that is events of the past** | ✅ **an attempt at understanding politics through a historical account of political thought of the past** |
| Instances | ✅ **the theories propounded by Hegel and Marx** | ✅ **George H. Sabine's 'A History of Political Theory'** |
| Popper's label | ✅ **'historicism'** | ⚠️ not applied |
| Object | Events | Texts |
| Output | Laws | Understanding in context |

🔑 ⚠️ The two senses differ in **what they take as data** — events in the first, writings in the second. That is a difference of **approach** in Van Dyke's precise sense from Lesson 1, which is why calling them one approach is sloppy.

### 3. Sense 1: historicism, and what it implies

✅ **Karl Popper has described this approach as 'historicism'.**

✅ Its content: **It implies that historical processes are determined by their inherent necessity which are beyond the control of human ingenuity.**

🔑 Two claims packed together: historical processes have an **inherent necessity**, and that necessity is **beyond the control of human ingenuity**. The second is what makes the doctrine politically consequential.

### 4. Popper's criticism — two steps, and the second is the sting

✅ **Popper has criticized historicism because it insists on discovering what is inevitable, and then advocates totalitarian methods for its realization, as Hegel and Marx have done for the realization of their respective visions of future society.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Notice the internal oddity, because it is examinable.** If a historical outcome is genuinely **inevitable** and **beyond the control of human ingenuity**, then advocating **methods for its realization** is strictly redundant — one does not need to force what cannot be avoided. ⚠️ The criticism is therefore partly a charge of **inconsistency** within historicism, not only a charge of authoritarian consequence. ⚠️ The chapter does not spell this out; the observation is a reconstruction and must be labelled as one. ⚠️ But it is a genuinely strong examination point, because it shows that the criticism bites from inside the doctrine.

❌ **Boundary.** The chapter gives Popper one sentence here and one mention at Lesson 4 as a contemporary thinker **rich in normative content** for his **advocacy of incremental change**. ❌ *The Open Society and Its Enemies*, *The Poverty of Historicism*, falsificationism and piecemeal social engineering appear **nowhere** in this chapter. ➡️ Route them to their own owners.

### 5. The visual

```text
   "HISTORICAL APPROACH" -- ONE NAME, TWO APPROACHES

   SENSE 1: LAWS FROM EVENTS            SENSE 2: THOUGHT IN CONTEXT
   ---------------------------          --------------------------
   data = EVENTS OF THE PAST            data = POLITICAL THOUGHT
                                               OF THE PAST
   aim  = "arriving at the LAWS         aim  = "UNDERSTANDING
          GOVERNING POLITICS"                  politics" through a
                                               historical ACCOUNT
   instances: HEGEL, MARX               exemplar: SABINE,
                                        'A History of Political
   Popper's name for it:                 Theory'
   >>> 'HISTORICISM' <<<
        |
   WHAT IT IMPLIES
   "historical processes are DETERMINED by their INHERENT
    NECESSITY which are BEYOND THE CONTROL of human ingenuity"
        |
   POPPER'S CRITICISM, IN TWO STEPS
   step 1: "it insists on DISCOVERING WHAT IS INEVITABLE"
   step 2: "and then ADVOCATES TOTALITARIAN METHODS for its
            REALIZATION, as HEGEL and MARX have done for the
            realization of their respective VISIONS OF FUTURE
            SOCIETY"
        |
   (!) THE INTERNAL ODDITY -- a reconstruction, not the chapter's:
       if it is INEVITABLE and BEYOND CONTROL, why would it need
       METHODS FOR ITS REALIZATION at all?  The criticism reveals
       a TENSION INSIDE historicism, not only a bad consequence.
```

*Caption: separating the two senses in the first line of an answer on the historical approach is worth a mark on its own, because almost everyone treats the term as single.*

### 6. Why this connects forward

⚠️ Sense 1 is where the historical approach touches the **Marxian analysis** of Lesson 32 — Marx appears in both places, as a historicist here and as the source of base-superstructure analysis there. ⚠️ Sense 2 is where the historical approach touches the **philosophical approach** of Lessons 9-10, since both work on past texts; the difference is that the philosophical approach evaluates them against **standards of the good, the right, and the just**, while the historical approach in this sense restores their **context**. 🔑 Being able to state that difference cleanly is the payoff of having done Lessons 9-10 first.

### Revision notes

- ✅ **The term 'historical approach' to politics may be used in two senses.**
- ✅ Sense 1: **arriving at the laws governing politics through an analysis of historical events, that is events of the past**.
- ✅ Its instances: **the theories propounded by Hegel and Marx**.
- ✅ **Karl Popper has described this approach as 'historicism'.**
- ✅ Historicism **implies that historical processes are determined by their inherent necessity which are beyond the control of human ingenuity**.
- ✅ Popper's criticism, step one: it **insists on discovering what is inevitable**.
- ✅ Step two: it **advocates totalitarian methods for its realization**, as **Hegel and Marx have done for the realization of their respective visions of future society**.
- ⚠️ Reconstruction, clearly labelled: inevitability and the need for methods of realisation sit uneasily together, so the criticism exposes an internal tension as well as a consequence.
- ✅ Sense 2: **understanding politics through a historical account of political thought of the past**, **as exemplified by George H. Sabine's 'A History of Political Theory'**.
- 🔑 The two senses differ in their **data** — events against texts — which is what makes them two approaches in Van Dyke's sense.
- ❌ No *Open Society*, no *Poverty of Historicism*, no falsificationism in this chapter. ➡️ Philosophy of science.
- ⚠️ Sense 1 links forward to Lesson 32's Marxian analysis; sense 2 sits beside the philosophical approach but restores context rather than applying standards.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it a methodological mistake, in Van Dyke's precise sense, to treat the historical approach's two senses as one approach?

**Model answer:** Because on Van Dyke's definition an approach is constituted by **criteria of selection** — **standards governing the inclusion and exclusion of questions and data** — and the two senses select different data to answer different questions. ⚠️ Sense 1 takes **events of the past** as its data and asks what **laws governing politics** can be derived from them; sense 2 takes **political thought of the past** as its data and asks how politics can be **understood** through a historical account of that thought. ⚠️ Since the data sets barely overlap and the questions are of different kinds — one seeks generalisations, the other seeks understanding in context — the inclusion-and-exclusion standards differ, which is precisely what makes two approaches two rather than one. ⚠️ The practical consequence is sharp. A criticism that lands on one sense may be irrelevant to the other: Popper's charge that the approach **insists on discovering what is inevitable** and **advocates totalitarian methods for its realization** is aimed squarely at sense 1 and has no purchase on Sabine's enterprise, which claims no inevitability at all. ⚠️ Conversely, the criticism Lesson 12 records — that **it is not possible to understand ideas of the past ages in terms of the contemporary ideas and concepts** — bears on sense 2 and leaves historicism untouched. ⚠️ Treating the two as one therefore guarantees that any evaluation will be partly off-target, which is the classic symptom of a mis-specified approach. 🔑 The examinable discipline is simply to name both senses before evaluating either.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not report Popper's criticism as the claim that Hegel and Marx were totalitarians. ✅ The chapter's sentence is that historicism **advocates totalitarian methods for its realization, as Hegel and Marx have done for the realization of their respective visions of future society** — a claim about the methods their doctrines license. ❌ And do not import Popper's wider philosophy of science here; in this chapter he appears only as the namer and critic of historicism and, at Lesson 4, as an advocate of **incremental change** rich in normative content.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Distinguish the two senses of the historical approach and state Popper's criticism of the first. *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** The chapter states that **the term 'historical approach' to politics may be used in two senses**. The first is **the process of arriving at the laws governing politics through an analysis of historical events, that is events of the past**, **as exemplified by the theories propounded by Hegel and Marx**; the second is **an attempt at understanding politics through a historical account of political thought of the past**, exemplified by Sabine. ⚠️ The data differ — events against texts — so these are two approaches, not one. **Karl Popper has described** the first **as 'historicism'**, which **implies that historical processes are determined by their inherent necessity which are beyond the control of human ingenuity**. His criticism is that it **insists on discovering what is inevitable, and then advocates totalitarian methods for its realization**. ⚠️ Inevitability and compulsory realisation sit uneasily together.

**Rubric [10]:** the two senses stated as the chapter does **3** · the different data as the ground of the distinction **2** · the definition of historicism **2** · Popper's two-step criticism **2** · the internal tension noted **1**.

## Lesson 12 — Historical approach II: Sabine, the critics and the revival

Progress: 12 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part II — the traditional approaches

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§6 and 9 with the Advanced companion's §§6 and 7, read against the chapter's account of Sabine, its criticisms of the historical approach and its record of the revival of interest in values (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.115 = printed 101), which supply the coincidence of subject-matter with the great philosophers, the sample questions, the context thesis, the two-way relation between theory and history, the critics' two objections, Easton's warning and the three worked instances of revival.
CA search: "revival of political theory values Rawls Macpherson 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Sabine's claim about subject-matter

✅ **According to Sabine, the subject-matter of political science coincides with the major themes of discussion in the writings of the well-known political philosophers**, and the chapter names them: **Plato, Aristotle, Hobbes, Locke, Rousseau, Bentham, Mill, Green, Marx and others**.

✅ The **leading examples** of their questions:
- **what ideals are sought to be realized through the state**
- **what is the meaning of freedom and equality**
- **what are the grounds and limits of political obligation**

🔑 The third is the chapter's own organising question from Lesson 10, now attributed to the tradition itself.

### 2. The context thesis, and its reciprocal

✅ **Sabine points out that each political theory is advanced in response to some specific situation.** ✅ Therefore: **It is necessary to recapitulate the circumstances under which a particular theory was produced, for understanding its relevance to the present situation.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Read the purpose clause.** Recapitulating the circumstances is **for understanding its relevance to the present situation** — not for antiquarian accuracy. ⚠️ The historical approach, in this second sense, is justified by a **present** payoff.

✅ And the relation runs both ways: **any political theory is not only a product of history, it also served as an instrument of moulding history by its ideological force.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **A text-integrity note that an answer must handle carefully.** ✅ The chapter's next sentence, as the local scan prints it, reads: **However, all great political theories are valid for all times.** ⚠️ The connective **However** signals a contrast with the preceding sentences, which a plain affirmative does not deliver, and the claim sits oddly beside the criticisms the chapter itself endorses in the very next paragraph. ⚠️ This file reproduces the sentence as printed and flags the apparent inconsistency rather than silently repairing it into a negative. ❌ Do **not** build an argument on this sentence in either direction; the safe course is to use Sabine's context thesis, which the chapter states unambiguously.

### 3. The criticisms

✅ Two, stated together:

> Critics of the historical approach point out that it is not possible to understand ideas of the past ages in terms of the contemporary ideas and concepts. Moreover, ideas of the past are hardly any guide for resolving the crises of the present-day world which were beyond comprehension of the past thinkers.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.115 (printed 101)

⚠️ The two are distinct and should be separated. The first is a **hermeneutic** objection — we cannot read them. The second is a **relevance** objection — even if we could, they would not help.

### 4. Easton's warning

✅ **David Easton has warned against living 'parasitically on ideas a century old' and failing to develop a 'new political synthesis'.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Mark this carefully: it is Easton's first appearance in the chapter, and he appears as a critic of the traditional approach.** ⚠️ The same figure will define behaviouralism's eight tenets at Lesson 21 and then lead the post-behavioural revolution at Lesson 23. ⚠️ Tracking Easton across those three appearances — critic, codifier, self-corrector — is one of the most effective organising devices available for a 20-mark answer on this chapter.

✅ And the chapter records the effect: **This challenge to historical approach of course encouraged the development of the 'behavioural approach'.**

### 5. The revival, with three worked instances

✅ **However, the recent revival of interest in values has led to a renewed interest in the rich heritage of political thought for evolving guiding principles for our own age.**

| Thinker | What was drawn on | What was rejected |
|---|---|---|
| **John Rawls** | ✅ **the methodology of Locke and Kant** | ✅ **the utilitarian philosophy of Bentham and Mill** |
| **Herbert Marcuse** | ✅ **Hegel's concept of 'civil society'**, to build **his neo-Marxist theory of freedom** | ⚠️ none stated |
| **C.B. Macpherson** | ✅ **Aristotle and J.S. Mill**, to build **his theory of democracy** | ✅ **Bentham's utilitarianism and the contemporary elitism of Schumpeter and Dahl** |

⚠️ 📰 **Recent** is relative to the **2009** edition. ❌ Do not present the revival as a current disciplinary trend; it is the chapter's description of its own period.

⚠️ Note the structural elegance: each instance is a **retrieval plus a rejection**, which is exactly what a defensible use of the historical approach looks like. You do not inherit a tradition whole; you mine it.

### 6. The visual

```text
   SENSE 2 OF THE HISTORICAL APPROACH -- THE FULL ARC

   SABINE'S CLAIM
   subject-matter of political science "COINCIDES WITH the major
   themes of discussion in the writings of the WELL-KNOWN POLITICAL
   PHILOSOPHERS" -- Plato, Aristotle, Hobbes, Locke, Rousseau,
   Bentham, Mill, Green, Marx and others
        |
   THE CONTEXT THESIS, AND ITS PURPOSE
   "each political theory is advanced IN RESPONSE TO SOME SPECIFIC
    SITUATION" -> "RECAPITULATE THE CIRCUMSTANCES ... FOR
    UNDERSTANDING ITS RELEVANCE TO THE PRESENT SITUATION"
        |
   AND THE RECIPROCAL
   theory is "NOT ONLY a PRODUCT of history, it ALSO served as an
   INSTRUMENT OF MOULDING HISTORY by its IDEOLOGICAL FORCE"
        |
        v
   TWO CRITICISMS -- SEPARATE THEM
   (1) HERMENEUTIC: "NOT POSSIBLE to understand ideas of the past
       ages IN TERMS OF the contemporary ideas and concepts"
   (2) RELEVANCE:  ideas of the past are "HARDLY ANY GUIDE for
       resolving the CRISES OF THE PRESENT-DAY WORLD which were
       BEYOND COMPREHENSION of the past thinkers"
        |
   EASTON'S WARNING  [his FIRST of THREE appearances]
   against living "PARASITICALLY ON IDEAS A CENTURY OLD" and
   failing to develop a "NEW POLITICAL SYNTHESIS"
        |
   "This challenge ... ENCOURAGED THE DEVELOPMENT OF THE
    'BEHAVIOURAL APPROACH'"   --> Lessons 16-22
        |
        v
   THE REVIVAL -- each case is RETRIEVE + REJECT
   RAWLS      : takes LOCKE + KANT methodology | rejects
                BENTHAM + MILL utilitarianism
   MARCUSE    : takes HEGEL's 'civil society'  | (none stated)
   MACPHERSON : takes ARISTOTLE + J.S. MILL    | rejects BENTHAM's
                                                 utilitarianism and
                                                 the elitism of
                                                 SCHUMPETER + DAHL

   EASTON'S THREE APPEARANCES IN THIS CHAPTER -- TRACK HIM
   p.115 CRITIC of the historical approach
   p.120 CODIFIER of behaviouralism's eight tenets
   p.121 SELF-CORRECTOR, leading the post-behavioural revolution
```

*Caption: the Easton strip at the bottom is the organising spine for a 20-mark answer. One man appears as the destroyer of the old approach, the architect of the new one, and its first serious critic — and the chapter lets that happen without comment.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Sabine**: the subject-matter of political science **coincides with the major themes of discussion in the writings of the well-known political philosophers** — **Plato, Aristotle, Hobbes, Locke, Rousseau, Bentham, Mill, Green, Marx and others**.
- ✅ Sample questions: **what ideals are sought to be realized through the state**; **what is the meaning of freedom and equality**; **what are the grounds and limits of political obligation**.
- 🔑 **Each political theory is advanced in response to some specific situation.**
- 🔑 Recapitulate the circumstances **for understanding its relevance to the present situation** — the purpose clause is the justification.
- ✅ Reciprocal: theory is **not only a product of history, it also served as an instrument of moulding history by its ideological force**.
- ⚠️ The sentence beginning **However, all great political theories are valid for all times** is reproduced as printed and flagged. ❌ Do not argue from it.
- ✅ Criticism one, **hermeneutic**: **it is not possible to understand ideas of the past ages in terms of the contemporary ideas and concepts**.
- ✅ Criticism two, **relevance**: such ideas are **hardly any guide for resolving the crises of the present-day world which were beyond comprehension of the past thinkers**.
- 🔑 **Easton** warns against living **'parasitically on ideas a century old'** and failing to develop a **'new political synthesis'**.
- ✅ This challenge **encouraged the development of the 'behavioural approach'**.
- 📰 The **recent revival of interest in values** — as of **2009** — produced three cases: **Rawls** (Locke and Kant; rejecting Bentham and Mill), **Marcuse** (Hegel's **'civil society'**), **Macpherson** (Aristotle and J.S. Mill; rejecting Bentham's utilitarianism and **the contemporary elitism of Schumpeter and Dahl**).
- ⚠️ Each revival case is a **retrieval plus a rejection** — the defensible form of the historical approach.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter records both a serious attack on the historical approach and a revival of interest in it. Are these consistent, and what do the three revival cases show about the form a defensible historical approach must take?

**Model answer:** They are consistent, because the attack and the revival target different uses of the past. ⚠️ The attack is on **inheritance**: the critics say **it is not possible to understand ideas of the past ages in terms of the contemporary ideas and concepts** and that those ideas are **hardly any guide for resolving the crises of the present-day world**, while Easton condemns living **'parasitically on ideas a century old'** and failing to develop a **'new political synthesis'**. ⚠️ Every one of those objections presupposes that the theorist is **receiving** a tradition rather than using it. ⚠️ The revival cases show the alternative form, and they are structurally identical: each is a **retrieval plus a rejection**. ✅ **Rawls** draws on **the methodology of Locke and Kant** while **rejecting the utilitarian philosophy of Bentham and Mill**; ✅ **Macpherson** reverts **to Aristotle and J.S. Mill** while **rejecting Bentham's utilitarianism and the contemporary elitism of Schumpeter and Dahl**; ✅ **Marcuse** reverts to **Hegel's concept of 'civil society'** to build a theory that is avowedly his own. ⚠️ In each case the past supplies a component for a **new** construction aimed at a present problem, which is precisely the **'new political synthesis'** Easton demanded — so the revival answers his objection rather than defying it. ⚠️ Sabine's own formulation already licensed this, since the circumstances are recapitulated **for understanding its relevance to the present situation**. 🔑 The defensible form is therefore selective and constructive: name what you take, name what you reject, and point the result at a contemporary question.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not present the revival as a refutation of the critics. ✅ The chapter's connective is **However**, marking a counter-movement, not a rebuttal, and it never says the hermeneutic objection was answered. 📰 Also do not describe the revival in the present tense as a feature of the discipline today — **recent** is relative to the **2009** edition, and no claim about the state of political theory after that date is available from this chapter. ❌ Finally, do not resolve the printed sentence about all great political theories being valid for all times by silently inserting a negative; reproduce it as printed, flag it, and argue from Sabine's context thesis instead.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why, on Sabine's account, must the circumstances of a political theory be recovered? *(Why — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Because relevance to the present cannot be assessed without them. Sabine holds that **each political theory is advanced in response to some specific situation**, so it is **necessary to recapitulate the circumstances under which a particular theory was produced, for understanding its relevance to the present situation**. ⚠️ The purpose clause matters: recovery is for a present payoff, not antiquarian accuracy. ⚠️ The relation is reciprocal, since a theory is **not only a product of history, it also served as an instrument of moulding history by its ideological force**. ⚠️ The method survives two objections — that **it is not possible to understand ideas of the past ages in terms of the contemporary ideas and concepts**, and Easton's warning against living **'parasitically on ideas a century old'** — because the revival cases retrieve selectively and reject explicitly rather than inherit whole.

**Rubric [10]:** the response-to-a-situation thesis **2** · the purpose clause about present relevance **2** · the reciprocal moulding claim **2** · the two objections **2** · the selective-retrieval answer **2**.
