## Lesson 13 — Legal approach: politics read through law

Progress: 13 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part II — the traditional approaches

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§6 and 7 with the Advanced companion's §§6, 8 and 12, read against the chapter's section headed "Legal Approach" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.115-116 = printed 101-102), which supplies the approach's definition, its full Indian illustration, the international extension, the inadequacy clause, Van Dyke's defence quoted in full, the culmination-in-law claim and the closing pivotal-role sentence.
CA search: "legal approach constitutional analysis political science 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no current-affairs item is concept-linked to a methodological approach without smuggling in post-2009 constitutional content; **no** live anchor carried. ❌ All Indian constitutional detail routes to Polity.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What the approach is

✅ **Legal approach stands for an attempt to understand politics in terms of law.**

✅ Its focus, stated as a list of three operations: it **focuses its attention on the legal and constitutional framework in which different organs of government have to function**, and **inquires into their respective legal position, their powers and the procedure which makes their actions legally valid**.

🔑 The third item is the distinctive one. **The procedure which makes their actions legally valid** — validity, not effectiveness, is this approach's central question.

### 2. The Indian illustration, reproduced as the chapter gives it

✅ The chapter spells out what a legal approach to Indian politics **will proceed to analyse**:

- ✅ **legal implications of various provisions of the Indian Constitution, duly documented by the decisions of the Supreme Court of India as well as by the opinions of legal luminaries**
- ✅ **procedure of formation and legal position of the two Houses of the Indian Parliament and State legislatures**
- ✅ **procedure of election or appointment, powers and position of the President, Prime Minister, Governors, Chief Ministers, Central and State Cabinets, etc.**
- ✅ **role and powers of the Supreme Court of India and High Courts**
- ✅ **full legal implications of the federal set up**
- ✅ **position of Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles of State Policy**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Read this list for what it is.** ❌ It is a list of **what such an inquiry would examine**, not a statement of Indian constitutional law. ❌ The chapter supplies **no** article number, no case name, no doctrine and no date. ➡️ Every substantive claim about any item on this list belongs to Polity. 📰 And the text is **2009**.

✅ The international extension: **legal approach to international politics will largely tend to analyse it in terms of the requirements of international law.**

### 3. The limitation, stated first

✅ **The legal approach may prove inadequate in understanding the complex political forces, processes and behaviour which might operate outside legal-formal framework, yet it is not entirely insignificant.**

⚠️ Note the shape of the sentence: the limitation comes first and the defence is appended with **yet**. The chapter is conceding before it defends.

### 4. Van Dyke's defence

✅ Quoted in full:

> Nor is a legal approach to be disparaged. After all, both the procedures and the substance of political action at every level are often controlled by law. In the field of both domestic and international politics, law frequently prescribes the action to be taken in given contingencies; it also forbids action or fixes the limits of permissible action.
>
> — Van Dyke, *Political Science: A Philosophical Analysis*; 1960, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.116 (printed 102)

🔑 Three verbs do the work: law **prescribes**, **forbids**, and **fixes the limits of permissible action**. ⚠️ And note the hedges — **often** controlled, **frequently** prescribes. Van Dyke is defending the approach's importance, not its sufficiency.

### 5. Two further defences

✅ **Moreover, all political processes to become effective and stable must culminate in legal provisions whether it is an independence movement in a colonized country or an agitation for civil rights or certain concessions for any sections of society.**

🔑 ⚠️ This is the strongest claim in the section and deserves attention. The argument is not that law **explains** politics but that law is where politics **ends up** if it is to last. ⚠️ Three instances are given, and all three are of movements **outside** the legal-formal framework — an independence movement, a civil-rights agitation, a demand for concessions. ⚠️ So the chapter's best defence of the legal approach is drawn from exactly the cases its critics cite against it, which is a genuinely elegant move worth reproducing.

✅ And finally: **the study of constitutional law and international law, etc. in spite of its limited use in understanding politics, continues to play a pivotal role in the social and political life of almost every country.**

### 6. The visual

```text
   LEGAL APPROACH: "to understand politics IN TERMS OF LAW"

   WHAT IT ASKS                      WHAT IT CANNOT REACH
   ----------------------------      --------------------------
   legal and constitutional          "the COMPLEX POLITICAL
   FRAMEWORK in which organs         FORCES, PROCESSES and
   have to function                  BEHAVIOUR which might
   their LEGAL POSITION              OPERATE OUTSIDE
   their POWERS                      LEGAL-FORMAL FRAMEWORK"
   the PROCEDURE which makes
   their actions LEGALLY VALID
        |
   INDIA -- what such an inquiry WOULD EXAMINE (not what it finds):
     provisions of the Constitution + Supreme Court decisions +
     opinions of legal luminaries | two Houses + State legislatures
     | President, PM, Governors, CMs, Cabinets | Supreme Court and
     High Courts | the federal set up | Fundamental Rights and
     Directive Principles
     >>> NO article, NO case, NO doctrine, NO date is supplied.
         2009 text. All substance -> POLITY.

   THE DEFENCE, IN THREE STRANDS
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   STRAND A  VAN DYKE: law PRESCRIBES the action to be taken |
             FORBIDS action | FIXES THE LIMITS of permissible action
             -- "often", "frequently": IMPORTANCE, not SUFFICIENCY

   STRAND B  THE CULMINATION CLAIM -- the strongest one:
             "ALL political processes TO BECOME EFFECTIVE AND STABLE
              MUST CULMINATE IN LEGAL PROVISIONS"
             instances: INDEPENDENCE MOVEMENT | CIVIL RIGHTS
                        AGITATION | CONCESSIONS for sections
             (!) all three are EXTRA-LEGAL movements -- the defence
                 is built from the critics' OWN examples

   STRAND C  "continues to play a PIVOTAL ROLE in the social and
              political life of ALMOST EVERY COUNTRY"
```

*Caption: strand B is the part to carry. It converts the standard objection — "law misses the real political forces" — into a point in the approach's favour, because those forces must end in law to endure.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Legal approach stands for an attempt to understand politics in terms of law.**
- ✅ It examines the **legal and constitutional framework in which different organs of government have to function**.
- ✅ It inquires into **their respective legal position, their powers and the procedure which makes their actions legally valid**.
- 🔑 **Validity**, not effectiveness, is the approach's central question.
- ✅ The Indian illustration lists Constitution and Supreme Court decisions and **the opinions of legal luminaries**, both Houses and State legislatures, the executive offices, the Supreme Court and High Courts, **the federal set up**, and **Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles of State Policy**.
- ❌ That list is an agenda of inquiry only — no article, case or doctrine is supplied, and the text is **2009**. ➡️ Polity.
- ✅ International extension: analysis **in terms of the requirements of international law**.
- ✅ Limitation: **may prove inadequate in understanding the complex political forces, processes and behaviour which might operate outside legal-formal framework**, **yet it is not entirely insignificant**.
- ✅ **Van Dyke**: **Nor is a legal approach to be disparaged**; **both the procedures and the substance of political action at every level are often controlled by law**.
- ✅ Law **prescribes the action to be taken in given contingencies**, **forbids action** and **fixes the limits of permissible action**.
- 🔑 The culmination claim: **all political processes to become effective and stable must culminate in legal provisions** — instanced by an **independence movement**, a **civil rights** agitation, and **concessions for any sections of society**.
- ⚠️ All three instances are extra-legal movements, so the defence is built from the critics' own cases.
- ✅ Constitutional and international law **continues to play a pivotal role in the social and political life of almost every country**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A critic says the legal approach is useless for understanding movements and agitations, since those operate outside the legal-formal framework. Using only this section, construct the chapter's best reply and state its residual.

**Model answer:** The chapter concedes the premise and then turns it. ✅ The concession is explicit: the approach **may prove inadequate in understanding the complex political forces, processes and behaviour which might operate outside legal-formal framework**. ⚠️ So the critic's description of movements is accepted. ✅ The turn is the culmination claim: **all political processes to become effective and stable must culminate in legal provisions whether it is an independence movement in a colonized country or an agitation for civil rights or certain concessions for any sections of society**. ⚠️ The elegance is that all three instances are drawn from precisely the class of cases the critic invoked. ⚠️ The reply therefore runs: a movement's **course** is indeed outside the legal approach's reach, but its **settlement** is not, because a movement that never issues in legal provisions has not become **effective and stable** — and a study of its outcome is unavoidably a study of law. ✅ Van Dyke's defence supplies the supporting mechanism, since law **prescribes**, **forbids** and **fixes the limits of permissible action**, so even an extra-legal movement is operating against a legally defined boundary. ⚠️ The residual, which an honest answer must state, is that this defends the legal approach's **indispensability**, not its **sufficiency**: it establishes that no account of a movement is complete without law, and leaves entirely untouched the question of why the movement arose, who mobilised it and why it succeeded. 🔑 Van Dyke's own hedges — law is **often** controlling and **frequently** prescriptive — make the same point from the other side, and the chapter's final word is only that the study of law **continues to play a pivotal role**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the chapter's Indian list as a statement of Indian constitutional law. ✅ It is a list of what a legal inquiry **will proceed to analyse**, and the chapter supplies no article number, no judgment and no doctrine for any item on it. ❌ Writing "as Gauba notes, Article 32 provides…" would be a fabrication. 📰 And the text is **2009**, so even the institutional picture must not be asserted as current. ➡️ Every substantive constitutional claim belongs to Polity.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Can the legal approach be defended against the charge that it misses the real political forces? *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** It can be defended as necessary without being defended as sufficient. The chapter concedes the charge: the approach **may prove inadequate in understanding the complex political forces, processes and behaviour which might operate outside legal-formal framework**. ⚠️ The defence then turns the concession. Van Dyke holds that **both the procedures and the substance of political action at every level are often controlled by law**, which **prescribes the action to be taken in given contingencies**, **forbids action** and **fixes the limits of permissible action**. ⚠️ More strongly, **all political processes to become effective and stable must culminate in legal provisions**, instanced by an independence movement, a civil-rights agitation and concessions for sections of society — cases drawn from the critic's own class. ⚠️ The residual is that this establishes indispensability, not explanatory adequacy.

**Rubric [10]:** the conceded limitation quoted **2** · Van Dyke's three verbs **3** · the culmination claim with at least one instance **3** · the indispensability-not-sufficiency residual **2**.

## Lesson 14 — Institutional approach I: what an institution is

Progress: 14 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part II — the traditional approaches

### 1. The claim that sets this approach apart

✅ **Institutional approach is closely related to legal approach, yet it is different.** And then the sentence that matters:

> Significantly, this approach does not solely bank on other disciplines—philosophy, history or law—for understanding politics. Amongst the traditional approaches it alone gives independent identity to the systematic study of politics.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.116 (printed 102)

🔑 ⚠️ **This is the structural key to Part II.** The other three traditional approaches each borrow their method from a parent discipline — philosophy, history, law. The institutional approach does not. ⚠️ And recall from Lesson 3 that contemporary approaches **are faced with the problem of the identity of the discipline**: the institutional approach is the chapter's reference point for what having an identity would mean.

### 2. Why institutions

✅ **Traditionally politics has been defined as the study of the state and government.** ✅ **Government itself is an institution**, and its organs — **Parliament (legislature), Cabinet (executive), and Supreme Court (judiciary), etc.** — **may also be recognized as institutions**. ✅ **Political parties—which exist separately—are also institutions in their own right.**

✅ And the selection rule: **there are lots of other institutions in society, such as family, school, church, or club. A student of politics will be interested only in those institutions which have a direct bearing on politics.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **That selection rule is a criterion of inclusion and exclusion in Van Dyke's exact sense from Lesson 1** — which is what makes this an approach rather than a mere subject list. ⚠️ Note also that the excluded institutions — **family, school, church** — are exactly the structures that Almond will later assign the function of **political socialization** at Lesson 28. The institutional approach rules out in advance what structural-functional analysis will rule back in.

### 3. The definition, in two forms

✅ The chapter's own working definition:

> In short, an institution is a set of offices and agencies arranged in a hierarchy, where each office or agency has certain functions and powers. Each office or agency is manned by persons with definite status and role; other persons also expect them to perform this role.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.116 (printed 102)

🔑 Four elements: **offices and agencies** · **arranged in a hierarchy** · **functions and powers** · **status and role**, with **expectation** by others.

✅ And an important extension: **the activities of an institution are not confined to its office-holders**. ✅ The instance: **ordinary voters who participate in the process of setting up a legislature through election are not themselves office-holders therein**.

✅ Then Van Dyke's compressed version:

> An institution is any persistent system of activities and expectations, or any stable pattern of group behaviour.
>
> — Van Dyke, *Political Science: A Philosophical Analysis*; 1960, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.116 (printed 102)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Compare the two definitions; the difference is examinable.** The chapter's own definition is **structural** — offices, hierarchy, powers. Van Dyke's is **behavioural** — **activities and expectations**, **stable pattern of group behaviour**. ⚠️ Van Dyke's version contains no offices at all, and would admit an institution with no formal structure whatever. 🔑 The behavioural turn is already latent inside the definition the traditional approach uses, which is part of why the institutional approach is the one that **exemplifies a shift from normative to empirical approach** at Lesson 15.

### 4. The visual

```text
   WHY THIS ONE IS DIFFERENT FROM THE OTHER THREE

   PHILOSOPHICAL --> banks on PHILOSOPHY
   HISTORICAL    --> banks on HISTORY
   LEGAL         --> banks on LAW
   INSTITUTIONAL --> banks on NOTHING ELSE
       "AMONGST THE TRADITIONAL APPROACHES IT ALONE GIVES
        INDEPENDENT IDENTITY to the systematic study of politics"

   WHICH INSTITUTIONS COUNT?
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   IN :  government | Parliament (legislature) | Cabinet
         (executive) | Supreme Court (judiciary) | POLITICAL
         PARTIES -- "institutions IN THEIR OWN RIGHT"
   OUT:  family | school | church | club
         rule: "interested ONLY in those institutions which have a
                DIRECT BEARING ON POLITICS"
   (!)   the OUT list is exactly what ALMOND will put back IN as
         structures performing POLITICAL SOCIALIZATION (Lesson 28)

   TWO DEFINITIONS -- AND THEY ARE NOT THE SAME KIND
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   THE CHAPTER'S (STRUCTURAL)        VAN DYKE'S (BEHAVIOURAL)
   "a SET OF OFFICES AND AGENCIES    "any PERSISTENT SYSTEM OF
    arranged in a HIERARCHY"          ACTIVITIES AND EXPECTATIONS,
   each has "certain FUNCTIONS        or any STABLE PATTERN OF
    AND POWERS"                       GROUP BEHAVIOUR"
   manned by persons with
    "definite STATUS AND ROLE"       >>> NO OFFICES AT ALL.
   "OTHER PERSONS ALSO EXPECT             The behavioural turn is
    them to perform this role"            already inside the
        |                                 traditional definition.
   and: "the activities of an
    institution are NOT CONFINED
    TO ITS OFFICE-HOLDERS"
    e.g. ORDINARY VOTERS setting
    up a legislature
```

*Caption: the bottom-right box is the high-value observation. Van Dyke's definition of an institution is already a behavioural definition, which is why this approach, alone among the traditional four, is described as having shifted toward the empirical.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Closely related to legal approach, yet it is different**; it **does not solely bank on other disciplines—philosophy, history or law**.
- 🔑 **Amongst the traditional approaches it alone gives independent identity to the systematic study of politics.**
- ✅ **Traditionally politics has been defined as the study of the state and government.**
- ✅ Institutions in view: **government**, **Parliament (legislature)**, **Cabinet (executive)**, **Supreme Court (judiciary)**, and **political parties—which exist separately—are also institutions in their own right**.
- ✅ Institutions of society not in view: **family, school, church, or club**.
- ✅ The selection rule: interested **only in those institutions which have a direct bearing on politics**.
- ✅ Chapter's definition: **a set of offices and agencies arranged in a hierarchy, where each office or agency has certain functions and powers**.
- ✅ Each is **manned by persons with definite status and role; other persons also expect them to perform this role**.
- ✅ **The activities of an institution are not confined to its office-holders** — e.g. **ordinary voters** setting up a legislature.
- ✅ **Van Dyke**: **an institution is any persistent system of activities and expectations, or any stable pattern of group behaviour.**
- ⚠️ The two definitions differ in kind: **structural** against **behavioural**, and Van Dyke's contains no offices.
- ⚠️ The excluded family, school and church are exactly what Almond readmits at Lesson 28 as socialising structures.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter say this approach alone gives the discipline independent identity, and what does Van Dyke's definition of an institution threaten to do to that claim?

**Model answer:** The claim rests on where each approach gets its method. ⚠️ The philosophical, historical and legal approaches each **bank on** a parent discipline, so a political scientist practising them is doing philosophy, history or law with political material; the discipline supplies the subject matter but not the mode of inquiry. ✅ The institutional approach **does not solely bank on other disciplines—philosophy, history or law—for understanding politics**, because its object — the organisation and functioning of government and parties — is distinctively political and is studied in its own terms. ✅ Hence **amongst the traditional approaches it alone gives independent identity to the systematic study of politics**. ⚠️ Van Dyke's definition threatens this because it dissolves the distinctive object. ✅ If an institution is **any persistent system of activities and expectations, or any stable pattern of group behaviour**, then the defining feature is stability of behaviour, not politicalness, and nothing in the definition confines the inquiry to government. ⚠️ A queue, a market and a caste network all satisfy it. ⚠️ The identity would then have to be restored by the chapter's separate selection rule — study **only those institutions which have a direct bearing on politics** — which is a stipulation imposed from outside the definition rather than something the definition secures. ⚠️ That is exactly the **problem of the identity of the discipline** which Lesson 3 attributes to the contemporary approaches, appearing here in embryo. 🔑 And it explains why Lesson 15 can say this approach **exemplifies a shift from normative to empirical approach** while still failing to **qualify as a contemporary approach**: the behavioural definition is present, but the restriction to formal institutions is still holding it in place.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read "it alone gives independent identity" as a claim of superiority. ✅ The chapter's verdict at Lesson 15 is that the approach **relies heavily on description rather than explanation** and **fails to qualify as a contemporary approach**, and it lists four drawbacks. ❌ The independence claim is about **disciplinary autonomy**, not about explanatory power. ❌ And do not merge the two definitions of an institution into one composite; the chapter's own is structural and Van Dyke's is behavioural, and the gap between them is the examinable point.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why is the institutional approach said to give political science an independent identity? *(Why — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Because it alone does not borrow its mode of inquiry. The chapter states that it **does not solely bank on other disciplines—philosophy, history or law—for understanding politics**, and that **amongst the traditional approaches it alone gives independent identity to the systematic study of politics**. ⚠️ Its object is distinctively political: **traditionally politics has been defined as the study of the state and government**, and the student attends **only to those institutions which have a direct bearing on politics**. ⚠️ An institution is **a set of offices and agencies arranged in a hierarchy**, each with **certain functions and powers**, manned by persons with **definite status and role**. ⚠️ The identity is nonetheless fragile, because Van Dyke's rival definition — **any stable pattern of group behaviour** — contains nothing political.

**Rubric [10]:** the non-borrowing claim **3** · the independent-identity sentence **2** · the selection rule limiting which institutions count **2** · the chapter's structural definition **2** · the fragility shown by Van Dyke's definition **1**.

## Lesson 15 — Institutional approach II: four drawbacks and a defence

Progress: 15 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part II — the traditional approaches

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What the approach actually studies

✅ Its practitioners **proceed to study the organization and functioning of government, its various organs, political parties and other institutions affecting politics**. ✅ The chief concerns are listed:

- ✅ **Classification of governments**, from **Aristotle (monarchy, tyranny, aristocracy, oligarchy, polity and democracy)** to **modern classification (democracy and dictatorship, parliamentary and presidential, unitary and federal, etc.)**
- ✅ **identification of levels of government (federal, state and local)**
- ✅ **branches of government (executive, legislative, judicial)**
- ✅ **composition and powers of each of these and their interrelationships (largely in legal terms)**

⚠️ Note the parenthesis **(largely in legal terms)** — the chapter is quietly recording how close this approach still stands to the legal one.

❌ **Boundary.** Aristotle's six-fold classification is named here and nowhere developed. ➡️ The forms-of-government owners hold its criteria, its rationale and its critique.

### 2. The verdict: description against explanation

✅ **It aims at giving an elaborate description of facts.** Therefore:

> Hence it exemplifies a shift from normative to empirical approach, and from a historical to a contemporary concern within the sphere of traditional approaches. However, it relies heavily on description rather than explanation. Hence it fails to qualify as a contemporary approach.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.117 (printed 103)

🔑 ⚠️ **This is the most precisely balanced verdict in Part II and it should be reproduced whole.** The approach moves **two steps** toward the contemporary — normative→empirical, historical→contemporary concern — and is then stopped by a third test it fails: **description rather than explanation**.

⚠️ So the criterion of a contemporary approach is being stated for the first time, and it is **explanation**. Hold it; Lesson 16 states the criterion formally and Lesson 21 makes explanation a tenet.

### 3. The four drawbacks, lettered as in the chapter

| | Drawback | The chapter's wording |
|---|---|---|
| **(a)** | Neglect of the individual | ✅ **with its preoccupation with the institutions, it neglected the individual; hence during the ascendancy of this approach, the study of voting behaviour and political attitudes of the individual was left to sociologists** |
| **(b)** | Neglect of international politics | ✅ **in the absence of overarching institutions governing international politics, it practically neglected the study of international politics**; it **confined its attention to international relations and description of the United Nations and its associated agencies** and **left the study of international politics to historians and students of international law** |
| **(c)** | Neglect of disruption | ✅ **being concerned with the established institutions alone, it neglected the role of violence or threat of violence, political movements and agitations, war and revolutions, etc.** |
| **(d)** | Neglect of the informal | ✅ **it neglected the role of informal groups and processes in shaping politics** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **Read drawbacks (a) and (d) together.** ✅ Voting behaviour and political attitudes were **left to sociologists**, and informal groups and processes were neglected. ⚠️ Those two abandoned territories are precisely what **Wallas** and **Bentley** will claim at Lesson 17 — Wallas on the psychology of actual behaviour, Bentley on informal groups. 🔑 The behavioural revolution is, on the chapter's own account, a reoccupation of ground the institutional approach vacated.

⚠️ And drawback (c) is the one that connects to Topic 07: the institutional approach neglected **violence or threat of violence, political movements and agitations, war and revolutions** — the same cluster Topic 07's chapter placed at the boundary of the political.

### 4. The defence

✅ The section closes with a genuine defence, not a formality:

> However, it should not be forgotten that institutions form a very important part of politics. Any discussion of politics without reference to the corresponding institutions will lead us nowhere. Moreover, in the present-day turmoil, particularly in the developing countries, constitution-making and institution-building is the order of the day. Institutional approach is inadequate in itself. But any other approach will also be incomplete without paying due attention to institutions.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.117 (printed 103)

🔑 The final pair of sentences is the formula to memorise: **inadequate in itself**, yet every other approach is **incomplete** without it. ⚠️ That is a claim of **necessity without sufficiency**, and it is the chapter's standard verdict shape — the same shape it gave the legal approach at Lesson 13.

📰 ⚠️ **The developing-countries sentence is dated.** **In the present-day turmoil … constitution-making and institution-building is the order of the day** is a claim about the chapter's own period, i.e. up to **2009**. ❌ Do not present it as a description of the present.

### 5. The visual

```text
   THE INSTITUTIONAL APPROACH: TWO STEPS FORWARD, ONE TEST FAILED

   STEP 1  from NORMATIVE --> EMPIRICAL
   STEP 2  from HISTORICAL --> CONTEMPORARY CONCERN
                    "within the sphere of TRADITIONAL approaches"
   TEST    DESCRIPTION or EXPLANATION?
           "relies HEAVILY on DESCRIPTION rather than EXPLANATION"
                    |
           >>> "HENCE IT FAILS TO QUALIFY AS A CONTEMPORARY
                APPROACH" <<<

   THE FOUR DRAWBACKS -- AND WHERE EACH ONE GOES NEXT
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   (a) neglected THE INDIVIDUAL; voting behaviour and political
       attitudes "LEFT TO SOCIOLOGISTS"
             --> reclaimed by WALLAS, Lesson 17
   (b) neglected INTERNATIONAL POLITICS; confined to IR and
       description of the UN; left to HISTORIANS and students of
       INTERNATIONAL LAW
             --> not reclaimed in this chapter
   (c) neglected VIOLENCE or THREAT of violence, POLITICAL
       MOVEMENTS and AGITATIONS, WAR and REVOLUTIONS
             --> the boundary cases of Topic 07
   (d) neglected INFORMAL GROUPS AND PROCESSES
             --> reclaimed by BENTLEY, Lesson 17

   THE DEFENCE -- NECESSITY WITHOUT SUFFICIENCY
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   "Any discussion of politics WITHOUT reference to the
    corresponding institutions will LEAD US NOWHERE"
   "Institutional approach is INADEQUATE IN ITSELF.
    But ANY OTHER APPROACH WILL ALSO BE INCOMPLETE without paying
    due attention to institutions."
```

*Caption: the arrows in the drawbacks block are the best single preparation for Part III. Two of the four gaps are filled by named thinkers within one page of the chapter, which is why the behavioural movement looks inevitable in the chapter's telling.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ Practitioners study **the organization and functioning of government, its various organs, political parties and other institutions affecting politics**.
- ✅ **Classification of governments** runs from **Aristotle (monarchy, tyranny, aristocracy, oligarchy, polity and democracy)** to **modern classification (democracy and dictatorship, parliamentary and presidential, unitary and federal, etc.)**.
- ✅ Also **levels (federal, state and local)**, **branches (executive, legislative, judicial)**, and **composition and powers … and their interrelationships (largely in legal terms)**.
- ✅ **It aims at giving an elaborate description of facts.**
- 🔑 The balanced verdict: it **exemplifies a shift from normative to empirical approach, and from a historical to a contemporary concern within the sphere of traditional approaches**.
- 🔑 But it **relies heavily on description rather than explanation. Hence it fails to qualify as a contemporary approach.**
- ✅ Drawback **(a)**: it **neglected the individual**, so voting behaviour and political attitudes **was left to sociologists**.
- ✅ Drawback **(b)**: it **practically neglected the study of international politics**, leaving it **to historians and students of international law**.
- ✅ Drawback **(c)**: it **neglected the role of violence or threat of violence, political movements and agitations, war and revolutions**.
- ✅ Drawback **(d)**: it **neglected the role of informal groups and processes in shaping politics**.
- ✅ The defence: **any discussion of politics without reference to the corresponding institutions will lead us nowhere**.
- 🔑 **Institutional approach is inadequate in itself. But any other approach will also be incomplete without paying due attention to institutions.**
- 📰 **Constitution-making and institution-building is the order of the day** in **developing countries** is a **2009** observation.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says this approach moved from normative to empirical and from historical to contemporary concern, yet still "fails to qualify as a contemporary approach". What exactly is the test it fails, and why is that test the right one?

**Model answer:** The test is **explanation**. ✅ The chapter grants both movements — **a shift from normative to empirical approach, and from a historical to a contemporary concern within the sphere of traditional approaches** — and then withholds the qualification on one ground: the approach **relies heavily on description rather than explanation**. ⚠️ So being empirical is not sufficient; a catalogue of facts about offices, powers and interrelationships is empirical in every respect and explains nothing. ⚠️ The test is the right one because of what Lesson 16 states as the mark of a contemporary approach — understanding politics **in totality, transcending its formal aspects** and looking for **those aspects of social life which influence and are influenced by it** — which is an explanatory demand about relations between variables, not a descriptive demand about accuracy. ⚠️ It is also the right test because it is the one the approach's own drawbacks point to: each of the four names something that would be needed to explain rather than describe — **the individual** and the study of **voting behaviour and political attitudes**, **international politics**, **violence or threat of violence, political movements and agitations, war and revolutions**, and **informal groups and processes**. ⚠️ An account confined to established institutions cannot explain why those institutions produce the outcomes they do, because the causes lie in the material it has excluded. 🔑 The test also sets up the behavioural programme precisely, since **regularities** at Lesson 21 are expressly for **explanation and prediction** — the thing the institutional approach was found to lack.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the four drawbacks as a verdict that the approach was worthless. ✅ The chapter's own closing is that **any discussion of politics without reference to the corresponding institutions will lead us nowhere** and that **any other approach will also be incomplete without paying due attention to institutions**. ❌ The finding is **inadequate in itself**, not useless. 📰 And do not quote the claim about **constitution-making and institution-building** being **the order of the day** in developing countries as a present-day observation; it belongs to the **2009** edition.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why does the institutional approach fail to qualify as contemporary despite moving toward the empirical? *(Why — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Because it fails a third test. The chapter grants two movements — it **exemplifies a shift from normative to empirical approach, and from a historical to a contemporary concern within the sphere of traditional approaches** — and then withholds the qualification: it **relies heavily on description rather than explanation. Hence it fails to qualify as a contemporary approach.** ⚠️ Being empirical is insufficient; **it aims at giving an elaborate description of facts**, and a catalogue of offices, powers and interrelationships explains nothing. ⚠️ The four drawbacks show what explanation would need: it **neglected the individual**, leaving voting behaviour **to sociologists**; it neglected **international politics**; it neglected **violence or threat of violence, political movements and agitations, war and revolutions**; and it neglected **informal groups and processes**. ⚠️ Yet it remains **inadequate in itself** rather than dispensable.

**Rubric [10]:** the two conceded movements **2** · the description-not-explanation verdict quoted **3** · at least three of the four drawbacks **3** · the closing qualification that it is inadequate rather than useless **2**.

## Lesson 16 — What makes an approach contemporary

Progress: 16 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part III — the behavioural movement

Book context: Canonical Core owner §§4 and 5 with the Advanced companion's §§1 and 3, read against the chapter's section headed "Contemporary Approaches" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.117 = printed 103), which supplies the two respects in which contemporary approaches depart from traditional ones, the caution that not all of them fulfil both conditions, and the three headings under which the rest of the chapter proceeds.
CA search: "contemporary approaches political science behaviouralism models 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The two departures, stated formally

✅ The chapter supplies a two-part criterion:

> Broadly speaking, contemporary approaches to the study of politics signify a departure from traditional approaches in two respects: (a) they attempt to establish a separate identity of political science by focusing on the real character of politics; and (b) they try to understand politics in totality, transcending its formal aspects and looking for those aspects of social life which influence and are influenced by it.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.117 (printed 103)

🔑 ⚠️ **Each clause answers a specific failure recorded earlier**, and seeing the correspondence is what turns a list into an argument.

| Departure | What it answers |
|---|---|
| **(a)** establish a **separate identity** by focusing on **the real character of politics** | ⚠️ Answers the dependence of the philosophical, historical and legal approaches on parent disciplines, noted at Lesson 14 |
| **(b)** understand politics **in totality, transcending its formal aspects** and tracing **those aspects of social life which influence and are influenced by it** | ⚠️ Answers the institutional approach's four drawbacks at Lesson 15 — especially the neglect of the individual and of informal groups and processes |

⚠️ ⚠️ Note the **reciprocal** phrasing in (b): **influence and are influenced by**. The relation is two-way, which is why feedback becomes central once systems analysis arrives at Lesson 26.

### 2. The caution

✅ **Contemporary approaches are legion, and all of them may not fulfil these conditions.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is the Lesson 3 hedge again, and it is important.** The two departures are **not** a definition that all contemporary approaches satisfy; they are the chapter's characterisation of the broad movement. ⚠️ An answer that presents them as necessary and sufficient conditions overstates the source.

### 3. The three headings

✅ **The following may be regarded as the most important: (a) behavioural approach; (b) post-behavioural approach; and (c) some important models of political analysis.**

🔑 That is the structure of the remaining eighteen lessons: Lessons 17-22 on (a), Lessons 23-24 on (b), Lessons 25-32 on (c).

⚠️ Note that the **interdisciplinary approach**, named as a contemporary approach at Lesson 3, is **not** among the three headings. ⚠️ It reappears not as a heading but as a tenet — **Integration** at Lesson 21 — and as a feature of decision-making analysis at Lesson 31. ⚠️ So the chapter demotes it from approach to component without comment.

### 4. The visual

```text

   (a) SEPARATE IDENTITY                (b) TOTALITY
   "establish a SEPARATE IDENTITY       "understand politics IN
    of political science by              TOTALITY, TRANSCENDING its
```

*Caption: the two upward arrows are the argument. The contemporary programme is defined by the two failures it is built to repair, which is why it is a response rather than a fashion.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ Departure **(a)**: contemporary approaches **attempt to establish a separate identity of political science by focusing on the real character of politics**.
- ✅ Departure **(b)**: they **try to understand politics in totality, transcending its formal aspects and looking for those aspects of social life which influence and are influenced by it**.
- ⚠️ (a) answers the parent-discipline dependence of the philosophical, historical and legal approaches.
- ⚠️ (b) answers the institutional approach's four drawbacks, especially the neglect of the individual and of informal groups.
- ⚠️ **Influence and are influenced by** is reciprocal, which anticipates feedback at Lesson 26.
- ✅ **Contemporary approaches are legion, and all of them may not fulfil these conditions** — ⚠️ a characterisation, not a definition.
- ✅ The three headings: **(a) behavioural approach; (b) post-behavioural approach; and (c) some important models of political analysis**.
- ⚠️ The **interdisciplinary approach** is named at p.109 but is not one of the three; it returns as the tenet **Integration** and in decision-making analysis.
- ⚠️ The criterion of a contemporary approach — **explanation** — was first stated negatively at Lesson 15.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how each of the two departures is a direct answer to a specific failure the chapter has already diagnosed, and state what follows for how the behavioural approach should be evaluated.

**Model answer:** Departure (a) answers the dependence problem. ⚠️ Three of the four traditional approaches **bank on** philosophy, history or law, so the political scientist practising them borrows the mode of inquiry and the discipline has no method of its own; the chapter noted that the institutional approach **alone gives independent identity to the systematic study of politics**, which concedes that the other three do not. ✅ Departure (a) proposes to secure that identity differently — by **focusing on the real character of politics** rather than by restricting attention to formal institutions. ⚠️ Departure (b) answers the institutional approach's drawbacks. ✅ Each of the four — the neglect of **the individual**, of **international politics**, of **violence or threat of violence, political movements and agitations, war and revolutions**, and of **informal groups and processes** — is a case of stopping at the formal, which is exactly what (b) sets out to transcend in **looking for those aspects of social life which influence and are influenced by it**. ⚠️ What follows for evaluation is a criterion rather than a preference: the behavioural approach should be judged by whether it actually delivers these two things. ⚠️ On the first it has a strong case, since political behaviour is a distinctively political object; on the second the chapter itself records a failure at Lesson 22, where behaviouralism **focused on micro-level situations rather than attempting macro-level generalizations** — which is a retreat from **totality**, not an achievement of it. 🔑 So the chapter supplies, in advance and in its own terms, the standard by which it will later find behaviouralism wanting, and an answer that uses the Lesson 16 criterion to assess Lesson 22 is doing the chapter's own argument rather than reporting it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not use the two departures as a definition and then exclude approaches that fail them. ✅ The chapter says contemporary approaches **are legion, and all of them may not fulfil these conditions**, so the two respects characterise the movement rather than police its membership. ❌ And do not list the interdisciplinary approach as a fourth heading; it is named earlier as a contemporary approach but does not appear among the three, returning instead as the tenet **Integration**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What makes an approach contemporary on the chapter's account? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Two departures from the traditional cluster. First, contemporary approaches **attempt to establish a separate identity of political science by focusing on the real character of politics** — answering the dependence of the philosophical, historical and legal approaches on parent disciplines. Second, they **try to understand politics in totality, transcending its formal aspects and looking for those aspects of social life which influence and are influenced by it** — answering the institutional approach's neglect of the individual and of informal groups and processes. ⚠️ The reciprocal phrasing **influence and are influenced by** anticipates feedback in the later models. ⚠️ The criterion is hedged: **contemporary approaches are legion, and all of them may not fulfil these conditions**, so it characterises the movement rather than defining membership. ⚠️ Three headings follow: behavioural, post-behavioural, and models of political analysis.

**Rubric [10]:** departure (a) quoted **2** · departure (b) quoted **2** · each departure matched to the failure it answers **2** · the **legion** hedge **2** · the three headings **2**.

## Lesson 17 — Behaviouralism's origins: Wallas and Bentley, 1908

Progress: 17 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part III — the behavioural movement

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §6 with the Advanced companion's §§3 and 5, read against the opening of the chapter's section headed "Behavioural Approach" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.118 = printed 104), which supplies the movement's association with post-war American political science, the two 1908 works, the shared emphasis on informal processes, Wallas's use of the new psychology against the rational-man assumption and his chief message, and Bentley's group approach with its stated excess.
CA search: "Graham Wallas Human Nature in Politics Arthur Bentley 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The placement, and a date that matters

✅ **Behaviouralism, or the behavioural approach to the analysis and explanation of political phenomena, is particularly associated with the work of American political scientists after the Second World War (1939-45)**, ✅ **but its origins may be traced back to the works of Graham Wallas (Human Nature in Politics) and Arthur Bentley (The Process of Government), both published as early as 1908.**

🔑 ⚠️ **1908 is examinable and frequently got wrong.** Both works, same year, nearly four decades before the movement itself. ⚠️ The chapter's **as early as** is doing rhetorical work: it is insisting that behaviouralism was not an invention of the post-war American academy but a resumption.

⚠️ The extraction prints the war dates as `(1939^15)` here, rendered **(1939-45)** and catalogued.

### 2. What the two shared

✅ **Both Wallas and Bentley were inclined to lay greater emphasis on the informal processes of politics and less on political institutions in isolation.**

⚠️ ⚠️ The phrase **in isolation** matters. The objection is not to studying institutions but to studying them **alone** — which is precisely the institutional approach's drawback (d) from Lesson 15, and precisely the defence the chapter mounted there.

### 3. Wallas

✅ **Wallas sought to introduce a new realism in political studies in the light of the new findings of contemporary psychology.**

✅ The target:

> While classical economists, the champions of the laissez-faire doctrine, had treated man as a rational creature following his self-interest, the new psychology had revealed that man was not a rational creature in this sense and that his political actions were not totally guided by reason and self-interest.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.118 (printed 104)

✅ **Human nature was too complex to be explained by simplistic utilitarian propositions.** ✅ **Wallas, therefore, insisted on exploring facts and evidence for understanding human nature and its manifestations in human behaviour.**

✅ And his chief message, which is the sentence to carry:

> His chief message was that the political process could be understood only by analysing as to how people actually behaved in a political situation, not merely by speculating on how they should or would behave.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.118 (printed 104)

🔑 ⚠️ **Three verbs, and the contrast is doubled.** How people **actually behaved**, against how they **should** behave — the normative — and how they **would** behave — the deductive model of rational self-interest. ⚠️ Wallas is rejecting both the moralist and the economist in the same clause, and an answer that notices the second rejection is distinguishing itself.

⚠️ Note also the precise scope of the psychological claim: man is not a rational creature **in this sense** — that is, in the laissez-faire economists' sense of consistently following self-interest. ❌ The chapter does not claim that human beings are irrational, and neither should you.

### 4. Bentley

✅ **Bentley, on the other hand, a pioneer of 'group approach' to politics, primarily sought not to describe political activity, but to provide for new tools of investigation.**

🔑 ⚠️ **That is a different kind of contribution from Wallas's, and the contrast is the lesson's payoff.** Wallas supplied a **corrected picture of the actor**; Bentley supplied **instruments**. ⚠️ One changed what behaviouralism believed; the other changed what it could do.

✅ The excess is recorded, not concealed: **He was so much fascinated by the study of informal groups that he tended almost completely to ignore the formal political institutions.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Mark this.** The chapter criticises a founder of the movement for the mirror-image of the institutional approach's error. ⚠️ The institutional approach neglected **informal groups and processes**; Bentley **almost completely** ignored **the formal political institutions**. 🔑 That symmetry is the best available evidence for the chapter's own pluralist verdict at Lesson 34 — each approach's characteristic vice is the other's characteristic subject.

✅ And what he did instead: **Greatly inspired by sociology, he proceeded to undertake a study of the roles of pressure groups, political parties, elections and public opinion in the political process.**

### 5. The visual

```text
   1908 -- TWO BOOKS, ONE YEAR, THIRTY-SEVEN YEARS EARLY

   GRAHAM WALLAS                      ARTHUR BENTLEY
   'Human Nature in Politics'         'The Process of Government'
         |                                  |
   SHARED: "GREATER EMPHASIS on the INFORMAL PROCESSES of politics
            and LESS on political institutions IN ISOLATION"
         |                                  |
   CONTRIBUTION TYPE:                 CONTRIBUTION TYPE:
   a CORRECTED PICTURE OF             NEW TOOLS
   THE ACTOR                          "primarily sought NOT TO
   "a NEW REALISM ... in the           DESCRIBE political activity,
    light of the NEW FINDINGS          but to PROVIDE FOR NEW TOOLS
    of contemporary PSYCHOLOGY"        OF INVESTIGATION"
         |                                  |
   TARGET: the classical              SOURCE: "GREATLY INSPIRED BY
   economists' rational,              SOCIOLOGY"
   self-interested man                OBJECTS: pressure groups |
   -- "man was NOT a rational         political parties |
      creature IN THIS SENSE"         elections | public opinion
         |                                  |
   CHIEF MESSAGE:                     RECORDED EXCESS:
   the political process can be       "SO MUCH FASCINATED by the
   understood "ONLY by analysing      study of INFORMAL GROUPS that
    as to HOW PEOPLE ACTUALLY         he tended ALMOST COMPLETELY
    BEHAVED in a political            TO IGNORE THE FORMAL
    situation, NOT merely by          POLITICAL INSTITUTIONS"
    SPECULATING on how they                 |
    SHOULD or WOULD behave"           (!) THE MIRROR IMAGE of the
         |                            institutional approach's
   rejects the MORALIST ("should")    drawback (d). Each approach's
   AND the ECONOMIST ("would")        vice is the other's subject.
```

*Caption: the two right-hand boxes are the examinable contrast. Wallas and Bentley are routinely run together as "early behaviouralists"; the chapter distinguishes them by the kind of contribution each made, and records a fault in one of them.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ Behaviouralism is **particularly associated with the work of American political scientists after the Second World War (1939-45)**.
- ✅ Its origins trace to **Graham Wallas** (*Human Nature in Politics*) and **Arthur Bentley** (*The Process of Government*), **both published as early as 1908**.
- ✅ Both laid **greater emphasis on the informal processes of politics and less on political institutions in isolation**.
- ✅ **Wallas** brought **a new realism … in the light of the new findings of contemporary psychology**.
- ✅ His target: classical economists, **the champions of the laissez-faire doctrine**, who **had treated man as a rational creature following his self-interest**.
- ⚠️ The psychological claim is bounded: man is not rational **in this sense**, and his actions **were not totally guided by reason and self-interest** — not irrational as such.
- ✅ **Human nature was too complex to be explained by simplistic utilitarian propositions.**
- 🔑 Wallas's chief message: the political process can be understood **only by analysing as to how people actually behaved in a political situation, not merely by speculating on how they should or would behave**.
- ⚠️ **Should** rejects the moralist; **would** rejects the deductive rational-actor model.
- ✅ **Bentley**, pioneer of the **'group approach'**, sought **not to describe political activity, but to provide for new tools of investigation**.
- ✅ **Greatly inspired by sociology**, he studied **pressure groups, political parties, elections and public opinion in the political process**.
- ⚠️ Recorded excess: he **tended almost completely to ignore the formal political institutions** — the mirror image of the institutional approach's drawback (d).

### Concept check

**Question:** Wallas's message rejects two things at once. Identify both, and explain why rejecting only one would have left the behavioural programme incomplete.

**Model answer:** The two rejections sit in a single clause. ✅ The political process is to be understood **only by analysing as to how people actually behaved in a political situation, not merely by speculating on how they should or would behave**. ⚠️ **Should** names the **normative** account — the moralist's picture of the good citizen — and rejecting it is the familiar half of the message. ⚠️ **Would** names something quite different: the **deductive** account, in which behaviour is predicted from an assumed model of the actor rather than observed. ✅ The chapter identifies the model precisely — **classical economists, the champions of the laissez-faire doctrine, had treated man as a rational creature following his self-interest** — and reports that **the new psychology had revealed that man was not a rational creature in this sense**. ⚠️ Rejecting only the normative half would have left the programme incomplete because the deductive model is also non-observational, and it is far more seductive, since it looks scientific. ⚠️ A political science that abandoned moral prescription but retained rational-actor deduction would still be **speculating**, merely with better manners, and would still be unable to discover the regularities Lesson 21 makes its first tenet — because those regularities have to be found in behaviour, not derived from an assumption about it. ⚠️ Rejecting only the deductive half would be equally defective, since it would leave the discipline free to lapse back into prescription. 🔑 The two rejections together produce a single positive requirement, which is the whole of behaviouralism in embryo: go and look.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not report Wallas as having shown that human beings are irrational. ✅ The chapter's careful phrasing is that man was **not a rational creature in this sense** — the laissez-faire economists' sense of consistently pursuing self-interest — and that **his political actions were not totally guided by reason and self-interest**. ❌ **Not totally guided** is a limitation, not a denial. ❌ And do not treat Bentley's neglect of formal institutions as the chapter's endorsement; it is reported as an excess, using the same vocabulary of neglect the chapter applied to the institutional approach.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What did Wallas and Bentley each contribute to the behavioural impulse? *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Both published in **1908** and both laid **greater emphasis on the informal processes of politics and less on political institutions in isolation**, but their contributions differ in kind. Wallas supplied a corrected picture of the actor: **a new realism … in the light of the new findings of contemporary psychology**, against classical economists who **had treated man as a rational creature following his self-interest**, since **human nature was too complex to be explained by simplistic utilitarian propositions**. His message was that the political process is understood **only by analysing as to how people actually behaved in a political situation, not merely by speculating on how they should or would behave**. ⚠️ Bentley supplied instruments: he sought **not to describe political activity, but to provide for new tools of investigation**, though he **tended almost completely to ignore the formal political institutions**.

**Rubric [10]:** the shared 1908 dating and shared emphasis **2** · Wallas's psychological correction **2** · the **should or would** double rejection **3** · Bentley's tools contribution **2** · his recorded excess **1**.

## Lesson 18 — Merriam and the Chicago School

Progress: 18 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part III — the behavioural movement

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §6 with the Advanced companion's §§3 and 5, read against the chapter's paragraphs on Charles E. Merriam (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.118-119 = printed 104-105), which supply his dates, his founding of the Chicago School, the 1921 article and the 1925 book with their criticism, his charge against the historians, his programme of interdisciplinary borrowing, the call for a policy science, the 1925 presidential address and his commitment to democracy.
CA search: "Charles Merriam Chicago School political science 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Who he is in this chapter

✅ **Charles E. Merriam (1874-1953) was another pioneer of the behavioural approach.** ✅ **He is also famous as the founder of the Chicago School which made substantial contribution to the behavioural movement.**

### 2. The two texts, and what they attacked

| Text | Year and place | Content |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **'The Present State of the Study of Politics'** | ✅ **American Political Science Review (1921)** | ✅ **criticized contemporary political science for its lack of scientific rigour** |
| ✅ ***New Aspects of Politics*** | ✅ **1925** | ✅ same criticism |

✅ The specific charge against a neighbouring discipline: **He deprecated the work of historians as they had ignored the role of psychological, sociological and economic factors in human affairs.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Place that charge against Lesson 12.** There, the historical approach was criticised for hermeneutic impossibility and present irrelevance, and **Easton** warned against living **parasitically on ideas a century old**. ⚠️ Merriam's complaint is different and more specific: not that history is useless, but that historians had **ignored** three classes of causal factor. 🔑 Two distinct attacks on the same approach, from two directions — and an answer that separates them is reading closely.

### 3. The programme

✅ **He argued that students of politics should make full use of recent advances in social sciences in order to develop interdisciplinary and scientific character of political science.**

✅ **He called for renewed scientific endeavour and emphasized the need for a 'policy science' by using quantitative techniques already developed in the fields of psychology and sociology.**

🔑 Three elements, and all three become tenets at Lesson 21:

| Merriam's call | The tenet it becomes |
|---|---|
| **interdisciplinary … character** | **Integration** |
| **quantitative techniques** | **Quantification** and **Techniques** |
| **'policy science'** | ⚠️ **Pure Science** — but note the tension below |

⚠️ ⚠️ **The policy-science point deserves care, because it contains a genuine tension the chapter never resolves.** ✅ Merriam demanded a **'policy science'**, which is knowledge oriented to use. ✅ Behaviouralism's seventh tenet at Lesson 21 is **Pure Science**, and Lesson 23 records that its **strict adherence to 'pure science' was responsible for its failure to attend to the pressing social and political issues of the period**. ⚠️ So the movement's own pioneer asked for the very thing the movement was later criticised for lacking, and the post-behavioural revolution at Lesson 24 restores it. ⚠️ The chapter does not stage this; the observation is a reconstruction and must be labelled. 🔑 But it is one of the strongest single points available in this topic, because it shows the post-behavioural correction as a **return** to the movement's founding intention rather than an external attack.

### 4. The presidential address

✅ **In his presidential address to the American Political Science Association (1925) Merriam exhorted political scientists to look at political behaviour as one of the essential objects of inquiry.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note the institution and remember it.** The APSA presidential address is the same vehicle **Easton** will use in **1969** to announce the post-behavioural revolution at Lesson 23. 🔑 The movement is announced and corrected from the same podium, forty-four years apart.

⚠️ Note too the modesty of the formulation: political behaviour as **one of the essential objects of inquiry**, not the only one.

### 5. Merriam and democracy

✅ **Apart from being an exponent of the scientific method for the study of politics, Merriam was also an ardent champion of democracy.** ✅ **In fact he vigorously sought to put science into the service of democratic principles.** ✅ **He saw no inconsistency in promoting science and democracy together.**

🔑 ⚠️ **That last sentence is the hinge of the next lesson.** Merriam **saw no inconsistency**; **Munro**, at Lesson 19, will take the opposite view, and **Catlin** will demand a **value-free** science. ⚠️ So the value question is contested **inside** the founding generation, before behaviouralism exists as a movement — which is why Lesson 24's post-behavioural settlement is best read as the resolution of an old family quarrel.

### 6. The visual

```text
   CHARLES E. MERRIAM (1874-1953) -- FOUNDER, CHICAGO SCHOOL

   THE TWO TEXTS                      THE CHARGE
   'The Present State of the          "criticized contemporary
    Study of Politics', APSR 1921      political science for its
   'New Aspects of Politics', 1925     LACK OF SCIENTIFIC RIGOUR"
                                      "DEPRECATED the work of
                                       HISTORIANS as they had
                                       IGNORED the role of
                                       PSYCHOLOGICAL, SOCIOLOGICAL
                                       and ECONOMIC factors"
         |
   THE PROGRAMME -- and where each piece lands
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   "make FULL USE of recent advances in social sciences"
        --> becomes the tenet INTEGRATION          (Lesson 21)
   "QUANTITATIVE TECHNIQUES already developed in the fields of
    PSYCHOLOGY and SOCIOLOGY"
        --> becomes the tenets TECHNIQUES + QUANTIFICATION
   "the need for a 'POLICY SCIENCE'"
        --> (!) collides with the tenet PURE SCIENCE
            and is RESTORED by POST-BEHAVIOURALISM (Lesson 24)

   APSA PRESIDENTIAL ADDRESS, 1925
   "exhorted political scientists to look at POLITICAL BEHAVIOUR
    as ONE OF THE ESSENTIAL OBJECTS of inquiry"
        |
        |  ... SAME PODIUM, 44 YEARS LATER ...
        v
   APSA PRESIDENTIAL ADDRESS, 1969 -- DAVID EASTON announces
   "A POST-BEHAVIOURAL REVOLUTION"                 (Lesson 23)

   AND ON VALUES
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   MERRIAM : "an ARDENT CHAMPION OF DEMOCRACY"; "sought to put
             SCIENCE INTO THE SERVICE OF DEMOCRATIC PRINCIPLES";
             "saw NO INCONSISTENCY in promoting science and
              democracy together"
   MUNRO   : NOT a proper function of political scientists to
             teach democratic citizenship        (Lesson 19)
   CATLIN  : the case for a 'VALUE-FREE' pure science (Lesson 19)
   >>> the value quarrel is INSIDE the founding generation.
```

*Caption: the two APSA addresses, 1925 and 1969, are the most economical way to frame the whole of Part III in an answer — one podium, one discipline, two revolutions, and the second one undoing the excess of the first.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Charles E. Merriam (1874-1953)** was **another pioneer of the behavioural approach**.
- ✅ He is **the founder of the Chicago School which made substantial contribution to the behavioural movement**.
- ✅ **'The Present State of the Study of Politics'**, **American Political Science Review (1921)**.
- ✅ ***New Aspects of Politics*** (**1925**) — both **criticized contemporary political science for its lack of scientific rigour**.
- ✅ He **deprecated the work of historians as they had ignored the role of psychological, sociological and economic factors in human affairs**.
- ✅ Programme: **make full use of recent advances in social sciences in order to develop interdisciplinary and scientific character of political science**.
- ✅ He emphasised **the need for a 'policy science' by using quantitative techniques already developed in the fields of psychology and sociology**.
- ✅ **APSA presidential address (1925)**: political behaviour as **one of the essential objects of inquiry** — ⚠️ one of several, not the only one.
- ✅ **An ardent champion of democracy**, he **vigorously sought to put science into the service of democratic principles**.
- 🔑 He **saw no inconsistency in promoting science and democracy together**.
- ⚠️ Labelled reconstruction: Merriam's **'policy science'** sits in tension with the later tenet of **Pure Science**, and post-behaviouralism restores it.
- ⚠️ The same APSA podium carries Merriam's 1925 call and Easton's 1969 correction.

### Concept check

**Question:** Merriam demanded a "policy science"; behaviouralism's seventh tenet is "Pure Science". Reconstruct this as a tension, say what it explains, and state clearly what the chapter does and does not assert about it.

**Model answer:** The tension is real and worth stating precisely. ✅ Merriam **emphasized the need for a 'policy science' by using quantitative techniques already developed in the fields of psychology and sociology** — knowledge oriented toward use. ✅ The movement he helped found later adopted **Pure Science** as a tenet, on which **the understanding and explanation of political behaviour is essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems of society**, and Lesson 23 records that its **strict adherence to 'pure science' was responsible for its failure to attend to the pressing social and political issues of the period**. ⚠️ So a founder's demand for applied knowledge became, in the movement's hands, a doctrine of deferral — understanding first, application later — and the deferral proved indefinite. ⚠️ What it explains is the character of the post-behavioural revolution. ✅ Easton's correction insists on **relevance** and **action** while stating that it **represented no complete departure from behaviouralism** and stood for **consolidating its gains**, which is exactly what a return to a founding intention would look like rather than an external assault. ⚠️ It also explains why the correction could be announced from the APSA podium by the movement's own leading theorist: nothing foreign was being imported. ⚠️ What the chapter asserts is each element separately — Merriam's call, the tenet, the failure, the correction. ❌ What the chapter does **not** assert is the connection between them; it never sets Merriam against the seventh tenet, and never describes post-behaviouralism as a recovery of Merriam. 🔑 The reconstruction is therefore legitimate and distinctive, and must be presented as the reader's inference, not the author's claim.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not present Merriam as a value-free scientist. ✅ The chapter is explicit that he was **an ardent champion of democracy**, that he **sought to put science into the service of democratic principles**, and that he **saw no inconsistency in promoting science and democracy together**. ❌ The value-free position belongs to **Catlin** at Lesson 19, and **Munro** explicitly disagreed with Merriam about teaching democratic citizenship. ❌ Treating the founding generation as unanimous on values erases the disagreement that makes the post-behavioural settlement intelligible.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Assess Merriam's contribution to the behavioural movement. *(Assess — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** He converted an impulse into a programme. As **founder of the Chicago School**, in **'The Present State of the Study of Politics'** (APSR, **1921**) and ***New Aspects of Politics*** (**1925**), he **criticized contemporary political science for its lack of scientific rigour** and **deprecated the work of historians as they had ignored the role of psychological, sociological and economic factors**. ⚠️ His positive programme supplied three later tenets: **full use of recent advances in social sciences** became Integration; **quantitative techniques already developed in the fields of psychology and sociology** became Techniques and Quantification; and his **APSA presidential address (1925)** made political behaviour **one of the essential objects of inquiry**. ⚠️ The assessment must record one unresolved strand: he demanded a **'policy science'**, which the later tenet of Pure Science deferred and post-behaviouralism restored. ⚠️ He also **saw no inconsistency in promoting science and democracy together**.

**Rubric [10]:** the two texts with years and the rigour charge **3** · the charge against historians **1** · the three programme elements mapped to later tenets **3** · the 1925 APSA address **1** · the policy-science tension, labelled as a reading **1** · the democracy commitment **1**.

## Lesson 19 — Munro, Catlin, Lasswell and the post-war series

Progress: 19 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part III — the behavioural movement

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §6 with the Advanced companion's §5, read against the chapter's paragraphs on Munro, Catlin, Lasswell and the sequence of post-war papers (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.119 = printed 105), which supply Munro's dates and dissent, Catlin's work and the case for a value-free pure science, Lasswell's dates and celebrated title, the five named papers with their authors and years, and the chapter's summary of what behaviouralism stood for.
CA search: "Harold Lasswell politics who gets what when how 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Munro: the first dissent

✅ **William B. Munro (1875-1957), another exponent of scientific method, however, argued that it was not a proper function of political scientists to teach democratic citizenship.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Read the sentence structure.** Munro is **another exponent of scientific method** — he agrees with Merriam about method — and dissents only about **function**. 🔑 The disagreement is therefore not scientific but about the scholar's **role**, which is exactly the terrain on which the post-behavioural revolution will be fought.

### 2. Catlin: the case for value-freedom

✅ **Then G.E.G. Catlin, in his Science and Method of Politics (1927) advanced the case for a 'value-free' pure science.**

✅ Two moves are attributed to him:
- **He treated 'power' as essence of politics**
- **and argued that analysis of 'power' should not be inclined in favour of any particular value-system.**

🔑 ⚠️ **The two moves are connected, and seeing the connection is the point of this lesson.** If politics is essentially **power**, then its study can be value-free, because power can be measured, traced and compared without approving of its uses. ⚠️ Value-freedom is not a free-standing methodological preference here; it is a **consequence** of choosing power as the object.

⚠️ That connection also makes the vulnerability visible. ⚠️ If the object were chosen differently — justice, say, or the common good — value-freedom would be unobtainable, so Catlin's position stands or falls with the claim that power is **the essence** of politics. ⚠️ That reconstruction is this session's, not the chapter's.

### 3. Lasswell

✅ **This view of politics as the science of power as well as a case for treating politics as a policy science was later developed thoroughly by Harold D. Lasswell (1902-78).** ✅ **His celebrated work Politics: Who Gets What, When, How (1936) proved to be a landmark in the empirical approach to politics as the study and analysis of power.**

⚠️ ⚠️ Note that **both** strands are attributed to Lasswell — **the science of power** and **politics as a policy science**. ⚠️ The second is Merriam's demand from Lesson 18, which Lasswell carries forward alongside the value-free power analysis. 🔑 So the two incompatible-looking commitments are held together in one body of work, which is further evidence that the pure-science/relevance quarrel is internal to the movement.

### 4. The post-war series

✅ **Despite these early attempts, behaviouralism in political science was systematically developed only after the Second World War, particularly through the writings of American political scientists.**

| Year | Author | Paper |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **1955** | ✅ **David B. Truman** | ✅ **'The Impact on Political Science of the Revolution in the Behavioural Sciences'** |
| ✅ **1961** | ✅ **Robert Dahl** | ✅ **'The Behavioural Approach in Political Science: Epitaph for a Monument to a Successful Protest'**, in the ✅ **American Political Science Review** |
| ✅ **1962** | ✅ **Evron M. Kirkpatrick** | ✅ **'The Impact of the Behavioural Approach on Traditional Political Science'** |
| ✅ **1967** | ✅ **David Easton** | ✅ **"The Current Meaning of 'Behaviouralism' in Political Science"** |
| ✅ **1968** | ✅ **Heinz Eulau** | ✅ article on **'Political Behaviour'** in the **International Encyclopedia of the Social Sciences** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **Dahl's title repays a moment.** **Epitaph for a Monument to a Successful Protest** — an epitaph is written for something that has died, and the thing that died is a **protest** that **succeeded**. ⚠️ The claim embedded in the title is that behaviouralism ceased to be a movement precisely because it won: once everyone accepts the protest's demands, there is nothing left to protest. ⚠️ That the chapter reproduces the title in full, eight years before Easton's revolution, is worth noticing. ❌ The chapter does not gloss the title; this reading is a labelled inference.

### 5. What behaviouralism stood for

✅ The chapter's summary:

> In a nutshell, behaviouralism stood for a shift of focus in the study of politics, from the formalism and normative orientations of the legalistic and philosophic schools to political behaviour, that is the behaviour of actual actors in the political field, such as power-holders, power-seekers as well as voters.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.119 (printed 105)

🔑 Three categories of actor are named: **power-holders, power-seekers as well as voters**. ⚠️ Note that two of the three are defined by their relation to power — Catlin's and Lasswell's object, still in place.

✅ And a scope note: **Behaviouralism as such came to be understood as something wider than the study of political behaviour, yet political behaviour was its main focus.**

### 6. The visual

```text

   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   MERRIAM (1874-1953)   "saw NO INCONSISTENCY in promoting
                          SCIENCE and DEMOCRACY together"
   MUNRO   (1875-1957)   "ANOTHER EXPONENT of scientific method,
                          HOWEVER" -- "NOT A PROPER FUNCTION of
                          political scientists to TEACH DEMOCRATIC
                          CITIZENSHIP"
   CATLIN  (1927)        the case for a "'VALUE-FREE' PURE SCIENCE"
                             |
                    HOW CATLIN GETS THERE -- two linked moves
                    (1) "treated 'POWER' as ESSENCE of politics"
                    (2) "analysis of 'power' should NOT BE INCLINED
                         IN FAVOUR OF any particular VALUE-SYSTEM"
                    (!) value-freedom is a CONSEQUENCE of the
                        choice of object, not an independent
                        methodological taste
                             |
   LASSWELL (1902-78)    carries BOTH strands:
                         "politics as the SCIENCE OF POWER"
```
