*Caption: the five-paper strip is pure recall and cheap to carry; the Catlin box is the analytical content. Most answers give the strip and miss the box.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **William B. Munro (1875-1957)**, **another exponent of scientific method**, argued it was **not a proper function of political scientists to teach democratic citizenship**.
- ⚠️ Munro's dissent is about the scholar's **role**, not about method — the terrain of the later revolution.
- ✅ **G.E.G. Catlin**, *Science and Method of Politics* (**1927**), **advanced the case for a 'value-free' pure science**.
- ✅ He **treated 'power' as essence of politics**.
- ✅ He held that analysis of power **should not be inclined in favour of any particular value-system**.
- ⚠️ Labelled reconstruction: value-freedom follows from the choice of **power** as the object; a different object would not permit it.
- ✅ **Harold D. Lasswell (1902-78)** developed **this view of politics as the science of power as well as a case for treating politics as a policy science**.
- ✅ ***Politics: Who Gets What, When, How*** (**1936**) was **a landmark in the empirical approach to politics as the study and analysis of power**.
- ✅ Behaviouralism **was systematically developed only after the Second World War, particularly through the writings of American political scientists**.
- ✅ The five papers: **Truman 1955**, **Dahl 1961** (APSR, **Epitaph for a Monument to a Successful Protest**), **Kirkpatrick 1962**, **Easton 1967**, **Eulau 1968** (*International Encyclopedia of the Social Sciences*).
- 🔑 Behaviouralism **stood for a shift of focus … from the formalism and normative orientations of the legalistic and philosophic schools to political behaviour**.
- ✅ The actors named: **power-holders, power-seekers as well as voters**.
- ✅ It **came to be understood as something wider than the study of political behaviour, yet political behaviour was its main focus**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why Catlin's two moves — power as the essence of politics, and value-free analysis — have to be taken together, and what the pairing implies about how value-freedom could be challenged.

**Model answer:** They have to be taken together because the second is supported by the first. ✅ Catlin **treated 'power' as essence of politics** and argued that **analysis of 'power' should not be inclined in favour of any particular value-system**. ⚠️ Power is a feature of relations between actors — who holds it, who seeks it, how it is distributed and exercised — and all of those are in principle observable, comparable and measurable without the analyst approving or condemning any use of it. ⚠️ So on this choice of object, value-freedom is not an austere self-denial but simply what the subject matter permits; the analyst has nothing to be partisan about. ⚠️ That is also why the programme is coherent rather than naive: it is not claiming that political life is value-free, only that an account of power-relations need not take sides. ⚠️ The pairing implies, however, that value-freedom is only as secure as the definition of the object. ⚠️ If one denied that power is **the essence** of politics — if, with **Strauss**, political science and political philosophy were **coterminous** and the discipline's business included **the standards of the right and the good** — then value-freedom would be unobtainable, because the object itself would be evaluative. ⚠️ The challenge to value-freedom therefore has to be mounted one level down, at the definition of politics, and not at the level of method. 🔑 This is exactly what Strauss does at Lesson 8, and it explains why the chapter presents his criticism as destroying **the essence of true knowledge of politics** rather than as a complaint about technique. ❌ The linkage itself is a reconstruction; the chapter states the two moves without connecting them.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not describe the founding generation as uniformly committed to value-freedom. ✅ **Merriam** **saw no inconsistency in promoting science and democracy together**, **Munro** dissented about the scholar's function while agreeing about method, and **Catlin** argued for value-freedom — three positions, not one. ❌ And do not read Dahl's 1961 title as hostile to behaviouralism; an **epitaph** for a **successful protest** records a victory, not a defeat, and the chapter offers no gloss on the title at all.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How did power become the object of a value-free political science? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** By a choice of object that made value-freedom available. **G.E.G. Catlin**, in *Science and Method of Politics* (**1927**), **advanced the case for a 'value-free' pure science**; he **treated 'power' as essence of politics** and argued that **analysis of 'power' should not be inclined in favour of any particular value-system**. ⚠️ The two moves are linked: power-relations can be traced and compared without the analyst approving any use of them, so value-freedom follows from the object rather than from austerity. **Harold D. Lasswell (1902-78)** then developed **this view of politics as the science of power as well as a case for treating politics as a policy science**, his ***Politics: Who Gets What, When, How*** (**1936**) being **a landmark in the empirical approach to politics as the study and analysis of power**. ⚠️ Hence the summary names **power-holders, power-seekers as well as voters**.

**Rubric [10]:** Catlin's work, year and value-free claim **3** · power as the essence of politics **2** · the link between the two moves, labelled as a reading **2** · Lasswell with work and year **2** · the actors named in the chapter's summary **1**.

## Lesson 20 — What "political behaviour" actually names

Progress: 20 / 34 | Stage: Core | Part III — the behavioural movement

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§3 and 6 with the Advanced companion's §§5 and 6, read against the chapter's definition of political behaviour and its account of what behavioural research studied (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.119-120 = printed 105-106), which supply Roberts's full definition with its three qualifications, the list of processes and factors studied, the interdisciplinary requirement, and the statement that behaviouralism grew into a set of orientations, procedures and methods.
CA search: "political behaviour voting attitudes study 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Why a definition is needed here

✅ The chapter pauses: **It would, therefore, be profitable to define 'political behaviour' at the outset.** ⚠️ The pause is warranted, because the term looks self-explanatory and is not. Roberts's definition contains three qualifications that between them answer almost every objection to the behavioural programme.

### 2. Roberts's definition, in full

✅ **Geoffrey K. Roberts** (*A Dictionary of Political Analysis*; 1971):

> Political behaviour, as an area of study within political science, is concerned with those aspects of human behaviour that take place within political contexts, that is within a state or other political community, for political purposes or with political motivation. Its focus is the individual person—as voter, leader, revolutionary, party member, opinion leader, etc.—rather than the group or the political system, but it necessarily takes account of the influences of the group on the individual's behaviour, the constraints of the system on the individual's opportunities for action, and the effects of the political culture on his attitudes and political habits.
>
> — Roberts, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.119 (printed 105)

⚠️ ⚠️ Roberts is the same authority quoted in Topic 07 for the definition of politics; ➡️ that material belongs there and is not reproduced here.

### 3. Taking the definition apart

**Three filters on what counts as political behaviour:**

| Filter | Wording | What it excludes |
|---|---|---|
| **Context** | ✅ **within a state or other political community** | ⚠️ Behaviour in purely private settings |
| **Purpose** | ✅ **for political purposes** | ⚠️ Incidental behaviour in a political setting |
| **Motivation** | ✅ **or with political motivation** | ⚠️ ⚠️ Note the **or** — motivation is an **alternative** to purpose, not an addition |

**Then the focus, and six named roles:**

✅ **Its focus is the individual person—as voter, leader, revolutionary, party member, opinion leader, etc.—rather than the group or the political system.**

🔑 ⚠️ **The inclusion of revolutionary is the single most valuable word in this definition for exam purposes.** ⚠️ Recall Lesson 15: the institutional approach **neglected the role of violence or threat of violence, political movements and agitations, war and revolutions**. ⚠️ Roberts's list puts the revolutionary back in as a standard object of study, which shows how directly behaviouralism was built against the institutional approach's gaps.

**And three compensating clauses, each conceding a limit of individualism:**

✅ It **necessarily takes account of**:
1. **the influences of the group on the individual's behaviour**
2. **the constraints of the system on the individual's opportunities for action**
3. **the effects of the political culture on his attitudes and political habits**

⚠️ ⚠️ **The word necessarily is doing defensive work.** The standard charge against behaviouralism is methodological individualism — that it reduces politics to individuals and loses structure. ⚠️ Roberts's definition concedes the individual **focus** and denies the reduction in the same sentence, naming **group**, **system** and **political culture** as unavoidable considerations. 🔑 Quoting the three compensating clauses is the most economical reply available to the individualism charge.

### 4. What was actually studied

✅ The researchers **sought to account for the psychological and social influences on behaviour of the individual in a political situation**. ✅ The processes and factors listed:

- ✅ **political socialization**
- ✅ **political ideologies**
- ✅ **political culture**
- ✅ **political participation**
- ✅ **political communication**
- ✅ **leadership**
- ✅ **decision-making**
- ✅ **and even political violence**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Three of these become chapter headings later.** **Political socialization** and **political communication** are Almond's input functions at Lesson 28; **decision-making** becomes a whole model at Lesson 31; **political culture** is derived in Topic 07's chapter and reappears at Lesson 29 as half of Almond's measure of political development. 🔑 The research agenda of behaviouralism becomes the architecture of the models.

✅ **An understanding of most of these processes involved interdisciplinary and cross-disciplinary research.**

### 5. The widening

✅ **In any case, behaviouralism as a movement in political science did not remain confined to the study of individual-based political behaviour, but developed into a set of orientations, procedures and methods of analysis.** ✅ **In practice it embraced all that lends a scientific character to the modern political science.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **That sentence licenses the eight tenets.** Behaviouralism is about to be defined not by its object — individual behaviour — but by a set of **orientations, procedures and methods**. ⚠️ Which is also why the movement could later be criticised for method-obsession: once the definition shifts from object to procedure, procedure is what gets perfected.

### 6. The visual

```text
   ROBERTS'S DEFINITION, DISSECTED

   WHAT COUNTS? -- three filters
   +---------------------------------------------------------+
   | CONTEXT  : "within a STATE or other POLITICAL COMMUNITY" |
   | PURPOSE  : "for POLITICAL PURPOSES"                      |
   | MOTIVATION: "OR with POLITICAL MOTIVATION"               |
   |              ^^ note the OR -- an ALTERNATIVE route in   |
   +---------------------------------------------------------+
                          |
   WHOSE BEHAVIOUR? -- the focus
   "the INDIVIDUAL PERSON -- as VOTER | LEADER | REVOLUTIONARY |
    PARTY MEMBER | OPINION LEADER, etc. -- RATHER THAN the GROUP
    or the POLITICAL SYSTEM"
        (!) REVOLUTIONARY is on the list -- exactly the actor the
            INSTITUTIONAL approach neglected (Lesson 15, drawback c)
                          |
   BUT -- three compensating clauses, introduced by "NECESSARILY"
   +---------------------------------------------------------+
   | 1. "the INFLUENCES OF THE GROUP on the individual's      |
   |     behaviour"                                           |
   | 2. "the CONSTRAINTS OF THE SYSTEM on the individual's    |
   |     opportunities for action"                            |
   | 3. "the effects of the POLITICAL CULTURE on his          |
   |     ATTITUDES and POLITICAL HABITS"                      |
   +---------------------------------------------------------+
        >>> the ANSWER to the methodological-individualism charge
            is INSIDE the definition itself.

   WHAT GOT STUDIED -- and where it goes next
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   political SOCIALIZATION ....... Almond input function (L28)
   political IDEOLOGIES
   political CULTURE ............. half of political development (L29)
   political PARTICIPATION
   political COMMUNICATION ....... Almond input function (L28)
   LEADERSHIP
   DECISION-MAKING ............... a whole model (L31)
   "and even POLITICAL VIOLENCE"

   THE WIDENING -- and its cost
   "did NOT remain confined to the study of individual-based
    political behaviour, but developed into a SET OF ORIENTATIONS,
    PROCEDURES AND METHODS OF ANALYSIS"
   "In practice it EMBRACED ALL THAT LENDS A SCIENTIFIC CHARACTER
    to the modern political science"
        (!) once the definition shifts from OBJECT to PROCEDURE,
            PROCEDURE is what gets perfected -- and that is the
            charge at Lesson 23.
```

*Caption: the three compensating clauses in the middle box are the most under-used quotation in this topic. They turn the standard objection into a non-starter, in the definition's own words.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ The chapter pauses to say **it would … be profitable to define 'political behaviour' at the outset**.
- ✅ **Roberts** (*A Dictionary of Political Analysis*; 1971): political behaviour concerns **those aspects of human behaviour that take place within political contexts**.
- ✅ The context filter: **within a state or other political community**.
- ✅ The purpose-or-motivation filter: **for political purposes or with political motivation** — ⚠️ note the **or**.
- ✅ Focus: **the individual person—as voter, leader, revolutionary, party member, opinion leader, etc.—rather than the group or the political system**.
- ⚠️ **Revolutionary** is on the list — the actor the institutional approach had abandoned.
- 🔑 It **necessarily takes account of** **the influences of the group on the individual's behaviour**, **the constraints of the system on the individual's opportunities for action**, and **the effects of the political culture on his attitudes and political habits**.
- ⚠️ Those three clauses are the answer to the methodological-individualism charge, inside the definition itself.
- ✅ Researchers sought to account for **the psychological and social influences on behaviour of the individual in a political situation**.
- ✅ Studied: **political socialization, political ideologies, political culture, political participation, political communication, leadership, decision-making, and even political violence**.
- ✅ Most of these **involved interdisciplinary and cross-disciplinary research**.
- ✅ The movement **developed into a set of orientations, procedures and methods of analysis**, embracing **all that lends a scientific character to the modern political science**.
- ⚠️ Once the definition shifts from object to procedure, method-perfection becomes the risk Lesson 23 names.

### Concept check

**Question:** Behaviouralism is routinely charged with methodological individualism — reducing politics to individual behaviour and losing structure. Using Roberts's definition alone, assess the charge.

**Model answer:** The definition concedes the premise and denies the conclusion, in one sentence. ✅ It concedes that the focus **is the individual person—as voter, leader, revolutionary, party member, opinion leader, etc.—rather than the group or the political system**, so the charge correctly identifies where the attention falls. ⚠️ But it denies reduction, because the same sentence continues that the study **necessarily takes account of the influences of the group on the individual's behaviour, the constraints of the system on the individual's opportunities for action, and the effects of the political culture on his attitudes and political habits**. ⚠️ The word **necessarily** is not decorative: the three considerations are presented as conditions of doing the work properly, not as optional enrichments. ⚠️ So group, system and culture enter the account as **explanatory variables acting on the individual**, which is a different thing from being ignored. ⚠️ The honest residual is that they enter only in that role. ⚠️ Structure is admitted as a cause of individual behaviour and not as a subject in its own right, so questions about why a system has the constraints it has, or how a political culture came to be as it is, fall outside this definition's scope. ⚠️ That residual is real, and it is the precise form the charge should take. 🔑 It is also why Lesson 22 finds behaviouralism focusing on **micro-level situations rather than attempting macro-level generalizations**, and why the models of Part V — which take the **system** itself as the unit — are presented as a separate development rather than as more behaviouralism.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat "political behaviour" as meaning any behaviour that happens to occur in a political setting. ✅ Roberts imposes a purpose-or-motivation filter — **for political purposes or with political motivation** — so the setting alone is insufficient. ❌ And do not drop **revolutionary** from the list of roles; it is there, and it is the word that shows behaviouralism reclaiming an object the institutional approach had abandoned.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Define political behaviour as the chapter does, and state what the definition concedes. *(Define and comment — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Roberts defines it as concerned with **those aspects of human behaviour that take place within political contexts, that is within a state or other political community, for political purposes or with political motivation** — three filters, the last two joined by **or**. Its focus is **the individual person—as voter, leader, revolutionary, party member, opinion leader, etc.—rather than the group or the political system**. ⚠️ What it concedes is decisive for the standard objection: the study **necessarily takes account of the influences of the group on the individual's behaviour, the constraints of the system on the individual's opportunities for action, and the effects of the political culture on his attitudes and political habits**. ⚠️ So structure enters as a cause acting on the individual, though not as a subject in its own right — which is the residual the charge of methodological individualism should actually target.

**Rubric [10]:** the three filters with the **or** noted **3** · the individual focus with at least three roles including **revolutionary** **2** · the three compensating clauses **3** · the precise residual about structure as cause rather than subject **2**.

### 1. The attribution

✅ **According to David Easton, the intellectual foundations of behaviouralism consist of eight major tenets.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Attribution discipline.** ✅ The chapter attributes the eight to Easton and lists them as bullet points, with a sentence of content for each. ⚠️ The canonical owner records that these are **commonly attributed, via Gauba's exposition**, to Easton's own listing, and advises treating the list as **a widely used pedagogical summary rather than a verbatim quotation of any single sentence**. ✅ This file follows the chapter's wording for each tenet and attributes the set to Easton, as the chapter does. ❌ It does not claim that the chapter's glosses are Easton's own sentences.

### 2. The eight, in the chapter's order and words

| # | Tenet | The chapter's content |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Regularities** | ✅ **there are discoverable uniformities in political behaviour which can be expressed in theory-like statements so as to provide for explanation and prediction of political phenomena** |
| **2** | **Verification** | ✅ **the validity of such theory-like statements must be testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour** |
| **3** | **Techniques** | ✅ **the means for acquiring and interpreting data should be examined self-consciously, refined and validated for the purpose of observing, recording and analysing behaviour** |
| **4** | **Quantification** | ✅ **precision in the recording of data and statement of findings requires measurement which should be expressed in terms of actual quantities to facilitate proper analysis** |
| **5** | **Values** | ✅ **The behaviouralists drew a clear distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation, which were concerned with values and facts respectively. They insisted that objective scientific inquiry has to be value-free or value-neutral** |
| **6** | **Systematization** | ✅ **establishing close interrelationship between theory and research, because research untutored by theory may prove trivial while theory unsupportable by data may turn out to be futile** |
| **7** | **Pure Science** | ✅ **the understanding and explanation of political behaviour is essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems of society** |
| **8** | **Integration** | ✅ **integration of political science with other social sciences in order to evolve a comprehensive view of human affairs, to strengthen its validity and the generality of its own results** |

### 3. Five readings that separate a good answer from a listing

⚠️ **(i) Tenet 1 is about explanation and prediction, not description.** ✅ The uniformities exist **so as to provide for explanation and prediction of political phenomena** — which is precisely the test the institutional approach failed at Lesson 15.

⚠️ **(ii) Tenet 2 contains a crucial hedge: in principle.** ✅ Statements must be **testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour**. ⚠️ Not actually tested; testable in principle. That is a far weaker and more defensible requirement, and it answers the lazy objection that most political-science claims have never been checked.

⚠️ **(iii) Tenet 4 is not an instruction to count everything.** ✅ **Precision … requires measurement which should be expressed in terms of actual quantities to facilitate proper analysis.** ⚠️ The demand is for precision; quantification is the means. ❌ The chapter does not say that unquantifiable questions are inadmissible.

⚠️ **(iv) Tenet 6 is the best-balanced sentence in the list.** ✅ **Research untutored by theory may prove trivial while theory unsupportable by data may turn out to be futile.** 🔑 Two failure modes, symmetrically stated, and the only tenet that includes a warning against the movement's own characteristic excess — trivial research.

⚠️ ⚠️ **(v) Tenet 7 is not what its name suggests, and this is the single highest-value observation in the lesson.** ✅ Read the content again: **the understanding and explanation of political behaviour is essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems of society**. ⚠️ That sentence makes understanding **essential to** solving urgent practical problems — it asserts a relation to use, not a renunciation of it. ⚠️ **Pure science**, on this gloss, means that understanding comes **first**, not that application is no business of the discipline. 🔑 Which is exactly why Easton's post-behavioural correction at Lesson 24 can be presented as **no complete departure**: the seventh tenet already pointed at application, and the complaint in 1969 was that the second half of the sentence had been forgotten.

### 4. The verdict on the eight

✅ **Any political inquiry conducted according to these guidelines would be most conducive to generate reliable theory and scientific explanations.** ✅ **The behavioural movement had such a profound effect on political science that these tests became the rule of political inquiry.**

⚠️ ⚠️ Note **the rule** — singular and definite. ⚠️ The movement did not merely add an option; it set the standard, which is what made the later correction necessarily internal. ⚠️ Note also **reliable** returning, as in the definition of method at Lesson 1, and now joined by **generality** in tenet 8 — the two values whose trade-off Lesson 22 is about.

### 5. The visual

```text
   THE EIGHT INTELLECTUAL FOUNDATIONS -- grouped by what each does

   WHAT THE WORLD IS LIKE
   [1] REGULARITIES ..... "DISCOVERABLE UNIFORMITIES in political
                           behaviour" -> "EXPLANATION AND
                           PREDICTION"

   HOW CLAIMS ARE CHECKED
   [2] VERIFICATION ..... "TESTABLE, IN PRINCIPLE, by reference to
                           RELEVANT BEHAVIOUR"
                           ^^ "in principle" -- a crucial hedge

   HOW DATA ARE HANDLED
   [3] TECHNIQUES ....... "examined SELF-CONSCIOUSLY, REFINED and
                           VALIDATED"
   [4] QUANTIFICATION ... "PRECISION ... requires MEASUREMENT ...
                           expressed in terms of ACTUAL QUANTITIES"
                           ^^ the demand is PRECISION; quantity is
                              the MEANS

   WHAT IS KEPT OUT
   [5] VALUES ........... "a CLEAR DISTINCTION between ETHICAL
                           EVALUATION and EMPIRICAL EXPLANATION";
                           inquiry "has to be VALUE-FREE or
                           VALUE-NEUTRAL"

   HOW THEORY AND DATA RELATE
   [6] SYSTEMATIZATION .. "research UNTUTORED BY THEORY may prove
                           TRIVIAL while theory UNSUPPORTABLE BY

                           ^^ TWO failure modes, symmetrical

   WHAT COMES FIRST

                           political behaviour is ESSENTIAL TO

                                (L24) restores the second half of
```

*Caption: the parenthetical on tenet 7 is the highest-value single point in Part III. Reproducing the tenet's full sentence pre-empts the error of describing behaviouralism as indifferent to practical problems.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **According to David Easton, the intellectual foundations of behaviouralism consist of eight major tenets.**
- ⚠️ Attribute the **set** to Easton as the chapter does; ❌ do not claim the glosses are his verbatim sentences.
- ✅ **Regularities** — **discoverable uniformities in political behaviour** expressible in **theory-like statements** for **explanation and prediction of political phenomena**.
- ✅ **Verification** — validity **must be testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour**.
- ⚠️ **In principle** is the hedge that makes the requirement defensible.
- ✅ **Techniques** — the means of acquiring and interpreting data **examined self-consciously, refined and validated** for **observing, recording and analysing behaviour**.
- ✅ **Quantification** — **precision … requires measurement which should be expressed in terms of actual quantities to facilitate proper analysis**.
- ✅ **Values** — **a clear distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation**; inquiry **has to be value-free or value-neutral**.
- ✅ **Systematization** — **research untutored by theory may prove trivial while theory unsupportable by data may turn out to be futile**.
- 🔑 **Pure Science** — **the understanding and explanation of political behaviour is essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems of society** — ⚠️ it points **at** application, with understanding first.
- ✅ **Integration** — with **other social sciences** for **a comprehensive view of human affairs**, strengthening **validity** and **generality**.
- ✅ Inquiry so conducted would be **most conducive to generate reliable theory and scientific explanations**.
- 🔑 **These tests became the rule of political inquiry** — definite article, so the standard, not an option.

### Concept check

**Question:** The seventh tenet is called "Pure Science", yet its stated content mentions solving urgent practical problems. Resolve the apparent contradiction and say what the resolution does to the usual account of post-behaviouralism.

**Model answer:** There is no contradiction once the tenet is read as a claim about **order** rather than **exclusion**. ✅ The content is that **the understanding and explanation of political behaviour is essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems of society** — so application is not denied but made **conditional** on prior understanding. ⚠️ **Pure** therefore marks a sequencing commitment: the discipline must secure explanation before it presumes to prescribe, because premature application would rest on unreliable knowledge. ⚠️ That is a defensible position and not an indifferent one. ⚠️ What it does to the usual account of post-behaviouralism is considerable. ⚠️ If the seventh tenet had excluded application, Easton's 1969 demand for **relevance** and **action** would have been a reversal, and the standard exam line that post-behaviouralism rejected behaviouralism would be right. ✅ But the chapter says the opposite: post-behaviouralism **represented no complete departure from behaviouralism** and stood for **consolidating its gains and applying them for problem-solving and crisis-management**. ⚠️ On this reading, what failed was not the tenet but its execution — Lesson 23's finding that **strict adherence to 'pure science' was responsible for its failure to attend to the pressing social and political issues of the period** describes a deferral that became permanent. ⚠️ Easton was therefore enforcing the tenet's own second clause against practitioners who had stopped at the first. 🔑 The accurate formulation is that post-behaviouralism corrected a **practice**, not a **principle**, and the seventh tenet is the textual evidence for saying so.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not present tenet 5 as a claim that behaviouralists had no values or thought values unimportant. ✅ The tenet states a **distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation** and a requirement that **objective scientific inquiry has to be value-free or value-neutral** — a constraint on inquiry, not a personal creed. ❌ Merriam at Lesson 18 was **an ardent champion of democracy** while demanding scientific rigour. ❌ And do not convert tenet 4 into a rule that only quantifiable questions are admissible; the stated aim is **precision**, with quantification as its means.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Name and explain any four of behaviouralism's eight intellectual foundations. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** **Regularities**: there are **discoverable uniformities in political behaviour which can be expressed in theory-like statements so as to provide for explanation and prediction of political phenomena** — note that the aim is explanation, the test the institutional approach failed. **Verification**: such statements **must be testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour**; ⚠️ **in principle** is a deliberate hedge, demanding testability rather than completed testing. **Systematization**: theory and research are interdependent, since **research untutored by theory may prove trivial while theory unsupportable by data may turn out to be futile** — two symmetrical failure modes. **Pure Science**: **the understanding and explanation of political behaviour is essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems of society**; ⚠️ this points at application and orders it after understanding. ⚠️ Together, **these tests became the rule of political inquiry**.

**Rubric [10]:** four tenets correctly named **2** · accurate content for each **4** · at least two of the three qualifications — explanation-not-description, **in principle**, the two failure modes, or Pure Science as ordering **3** · the closing verdict **1**.

## Lesson 22 — Reliability bought at the price of generality

Progress: 22 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part III — the behavioural movement

### 1. The trade-off, stated as a choice

✅ **Behaviouralism came to accord primacy to higher degree of reliability vis-a-vis higher degree of generality.** ✅ **It, therefore, focused on questions that could be answered reliably on the basis of the methods available.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Read this as the chapter writes it: a trade-off, deliberately resolved.** ⚠️ Reliability and generality are both virtues from Lesson 21 — **reliable theory** in the verdict, **generality of its own results** in tenet 8 — and behaviouralism could not maximise both. ⚠️ It chose reliability. That is not a mistake; it is a decision with a stated cost.

⚠️ ⚠️ And note the second sentence's qualifier: **on the basis of the methods available**. ⚠️ The restriction of subject matter is **method-relative**, which means it is contingent — improve the methods and the admissible questions widen. ⚠️ That is an important defence of behaviouralism and the chapter supplies it in passing.

### 2. Van Dyke's illustration

✅ **As Vernon Van Dyke has aptly illustrated:**

> The student who takes a behavioural approach is not likely to ask broad and vague questions like what caused the decline and fall of the Roman Empire . . . Nor is he likely to focus on ideologies or constitutions or laws or upon the organizational structure of institutions.
>
> — Van Dyke, *Political Science: A Philosophical Analysis*; 1960, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.121 (printed 107)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Two halves, and they are not the same kind of exclusion.**

| Half | What is excluded | Why |
|---|---|---|
| First | ✅ **broad and vague questions like what caused the decline and fall of the Roman Empire** | ⚠️ Because the question is **broad and vague** — unanswerable reliably by any method |
| Second | ✅ **ideologies or constitutions or laws or … the organizational structure of institutions** | ⚠️ Because these are **not behaviour** — the objects of the philosophical, legal and institutional approaches |

🔑 The first exclusion is about **answerability**; the second is about **object**. ⚠️ Treating them as one loses the point that behaviouralism was not merely cautious — it had also changed the subject.

⚠️ ⚠️ Note the irony the chapter does not comment on: the second list — **ideologies**, **constitutions**, **laws**, **organizational structure of institutions** — is an almost exact inventory of Part II of this very chapter. ⚠️ Van Dyke, whose definitions have carried every traditional approach, is here describing what the new approach will not look at.

⚠️ Note also the hedge **not likely to**, twice. Van Dyke describes a tendency, not a prohibition.

### 3. The summary

✅ **In short, behaviouralism focused on micro-level situations rather than attempting macro-level generalizations.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is the sentence that sets up the whole of Parts IV and V, and it should be seen as doing double work.**
- ⚠️ It states the **limitation** that the post-behavioural critics at Lesson 23 will attack — the discipline was absorbed in small, tractable questions while large crises went unaddressed.
- ⚠️ It also states the **gap** that the models of political analysis fill. ✅ Systems analysis takes the **political system** as its unit; structural-functional analysis compares **whole polities**; Marxian analysis works at the level of **modes of production** and **historical stages**. 🔑 Every one of them is macro.

⚠️ So the chapter has arranged its own sequel: having shown that behaviouralism went micro, it must produce the macro tools, and Part V is that production.

### 4. The visual

```text
   THE TRADE-OFF, AND WHAT IT COST

        RELIABILITY  <------------------->  GENERALITY
              ^                                  ^
              |                                  |
      behaviouralism "ACCORDED           tenet 8 wanted "the
      PRIMACY to higher degree of        GENERALITY of its own
      RELIABILITY vis-a-vis higher       results"
      degree of GENERALITY"
              |
      "focused on questions that could be ANSWERED RELIABLY
       ON THE BASIS OF THE METHODS AVAILABLE"
              ^^ method-relative -- so the restriction is
                 CONTINGENT, not permanent

   VAN DYKE'S ILLUSTRATION -- TWO DIFFERENT EXCLUSIONS
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   EXCLUSION BY ANSWERABILITY        EXCLUSION BY OBJECT
   "NOT LIKELY to ask BROAD AND      "NOR is he likely to focus on
    VAGUE questions like WHAT         IDEOLOGIES or CONSTITUTIONS
    CAUSED THE DECLINE AND FALL       or LAWS or upon the
    OF THE ROMAN EMPIRE"              ORGANIZATIONAL STRUCTURE OF
                                      INSTITUTIONS"
   reason: unanswerable by ANY       reason: NOT BEHAVIOUR
   available method                          |
                                     (!) this list IS Part II of

                                         philosophical, legal and
                                         institutional objects

   THE RESULT -- and what it sets up
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   "behaviouralism focused on MICRO-LEVEL SITUATIONS rather than
    attempting MACRO-LEVEL GENERALIZATIONS"
                |                           |
     the CHARGE at Lesson 23       the GAP filled by Part V
```

*Caption: the bottom block is the architecture of the rest of the chapter. Behaviouralism's micro-focus generates two separate sequels — a moral correction in Part IV and a methodological supplement in Part V — and knowing that both answer the same sentence is what lets an answer treat the chapter as one argument.*

### Revision notes

- 🔑 **Behaviouralism came to accord primacy to higher degree of reliability vis-a-vis higher degree of generality.**
- ✅ It **focused on questions that could be answered reliably on the basis of the methods available**.
- ⚠️ **On the basis of the methods available** makes the restriction method-relative and therefore contingent.
- ⚠️ Both virtues come from Lesson 21 — **reliable theory** in the verdict, **generality** in tenet 8 — and could not be maximised together.
- ✅ **Van Dyke**: the behavioural student is **not likely to ask broad and vague questions like what caused the decline and fall of the Roman Empire**.
- ✅ Nor **likely to focus on ideologies or constitutions or laws or upon the organizational structure of institutions**.
- ⚠️ Two different exclusions: by **answerability** and by **object**.
- ⚠️ The second list inventories the objects of Part II — the philosophical, legal and institutional approaches.
- ⚠️ **Not likely to**, twice: a tendency, not a prohibition.
- 🔑 **In short, behaviouralism focused on micro-level situations rather than attempting macro-level generalizations.**
- ⚠️ That one sentence generates both the post-behavioural charge (Lesson 23) and the macro models of Part V.

### Concept check

**Question:** Is behaviouralism's micro-focus a defect of the approach or a consequence of a defensible choice? Argue it out using the chapter's own terms, and state where the chapter itself comes down.

**Model answer:** It begins as a defensible choice and becomes a defect only through what the choice was allowed to exclude. ⚠️ **As a choice:** ✅ behaviouralism **came to accord primacy to higher degree of reliability vis-a-vis higher degree of generality** and therefore **focused on questions that could be answered reliably on the basis of the methods available**. ⚠️ That is a rational preference given the second tenet's demand that claims be **testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour** — a discipline that insists on verifiability must concentrate where verification is possible, and the alternative is theory **unsupportable by data** which tenet 6 calls **futile**. ⚠️ The restriction is also stated as method-relative, so it is contingent: better methods would widen the admissible range. ⚠️ **As a defect:** Van Dyke's second exclusion shows the cost was not only in scale but in **subject** — **ideologies or constitutions or laws or … the organizational structure of institutions** are not merely hard to study reliably; they were removed from view, and with them most of what political science had previously been about. ⚠️ The chapter's own verdict is that it **focused on micro-level situations rather than attempting macro-level generalizations**, and Lesson 23 records the consequence: **strict adherence to 'pure science' was responsible for its failure to attend to the pressing social and political issues of the period**. ⚠️ Where the chapter comes down is clear but not hostile. ✅ It calls the eight tenets **conducive to generate reliable theory and scientific explanations** and says they **became the rule of political inquiry**, and the correction it endorses is **no complete departure** but a **consolidating** of the gains. 🔑 So the chapter's position is that the choice was sound and the exclusion was overdrawn — which is exactly the judgment a strong answer should reproduce.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read Van Dyke's illustration as a prohibition. ✅ The wording is **not likely to** — twice — describing what a behavioural student tends to do, not a rule forbidding him. ❌ And do not collapse the two exclusions into "behaviouralism ignored big questions"; the Roman Empire is excluded as **broad and vague**, while ideologies, constitutions and laws are excluded because they are not behaviour. The first is a complaint about the question, the second a change of object, and only the second explains the loss of continuity with Part II.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why did behaviouralism concentrate on micro-level situations? *(Why — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Because of a trade-off it resolved deliberately. The chapter states that behaviouralism **came to accord primacy to higher degree of reliability vis-a-vis higher degree of generality**, and therefore **focused on questions that could be answered reliably on the basis of the methods available**. ⚠️ The qualifier matters: the restriction is method-relative, and therefore contingent rather than permanent. **Van Dyke** illustrates the result: such a student is **not likely to ask broad and vague questions like what caused the decline and fall of the Roman Empire**, nor **likely to focus on ideologies or constitutions or laws or upon the organizational structure of institutions**. ⚠️ Those are two different exclusions — one by answerability, one by object. 🔑 Hence the summary: behaviouralism **focused on micro-level situations rather than attempting macro-level generalizations** — the sentence that generates both the post-behavioural charge and the macro models.

**Rubric [10]:** the reliability-over-generality trade-off quoted **3** · the method-relative qualifier **2** · Van Dyke's two exclusions distinguished **3** · the micro-level summary and what it sets up **2**.

## Lesson 23 — The post-behavioural revolution: the critics and the address

Progress: 23 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part IV — the post-behavioural correction

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§6 and 14 with the Advanced companion's §§6, 7 and 12, read against the first half of the chapter's section headed "Post-Behavioural Revolution" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.121 = printed 107), which supplies the dating of behaviouralism's dominance, Strauss's and Wolin's criticisms with their papers and years, Kuhn's intervention from the philosophy of science, the internal realisation by the end of the 1960s, and Easton's 1969 presidential address.
CA search: "post-behavioural revolution Easton 1969 relevance action 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried. 📰 Every date in this lesson is the chapter's own.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The dominance, dated

✅ **By the mid-1960s behaviouralism gained a dominant position in the methodology of political science.**

📰 ⚠️ Keep the date attached. This is a claim about the mid-1960s in American political science, not a standing description of the discipline.

### 2. Three critics, from three directions

| Critic | Work, journal and year | The charge |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Leo Strauss** | ✅ **'What is Political Philosophy?', *Journal of Politics*; 1957** | ✅ **the rise of behaviouralism was symptomatic of a crisis in political theory because of its failure to come to grips with normative issues** |
| ✅ **Sheldon Wolin** | ✅ **'Political Theory as a Vocation', *American Political Science Review*; 1969** | ✅ **the preoccupation of political science with method signified an abdication of true vocation of political theory** |
| ✅ **Thomas Kuhn** | ✅ ***The Structure of Scientific Revolutions*** **(1962)**, **within the sphere of philosophy of science** | ✅ promoted the view that **significance of scientific method lies in its capacity of problem-solving and crisis-management, not in methodological sophistication** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **The three are not variations on one complaint, and separating them is the lesson's main analytical payoff.**

- ⚠️ **Strauss** attacks from **outside the method**: behaviouralism fails because it cannot handle normative issues at all. The charge is about **scope**.
- ⚠️ **Wolin** attacks from **inside the discipline**: method has displaced vocation. The charge is about **priorities** — note **abdication**, which implies a duty wrongly abandoned rather than an incapacity.
- ⚠️ ⚠️ **Kuhn** attacks from **inside science itself**, and this is the decisive one. ✅ His claim is about what makes **scientific method** significant — **problem-solving and crisis-management, not … methodological sophistication**. 🔑 Behaviouralism cannot answer Kuhn by appealing to scientific standards, because Kuhn is **stating** the scientific standard and finding behaviouralism short of it.

⚠️ Note the dating. Strauss's paper is **1957**, eight years before behaviouralism's mid-1960s dominance — the criticism precedes the triumph. Wolin's is **1969**, the same year as Easton's address.

❌ **Boundary.** The chapter gives Kuhn one sentence. ❌ Paradigms, normal science, incommensurability and the structure of scientific revolutions as a theory appear nowhere here. ➡️ Route them to philosophy of science.

### 3. The internal turn

✅ **By the end of 1960s even the exponents of behaviouralism realized that its strict adherence to 'pure science' was responsible for its failure to attend to the pressing social and political issues of the period.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Even the exponents.** The correction is **internal**; it is not imposed by defeated traditionalists. ⚠️ And the diagnosis names the seventh tenet by name — **strict adherence to 'pure science'** — which, as Lesson 21 showed, is the tenet whose own content pointed at **the solution of urgent practical problems of society**. ⚠️ The failure was of practice against principle.

### 4. The address

✅ **In 1969, David Easton himself in his presidential address to the American Political Science Association, announced a new revolution in political science—'a post-behavioural revolution'—that represented a shift of focus from strict methodological issues to a greater concern with public responsibilities of the discipline and with political problems.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Four things in that sentence deserve separate attention.**

1. ⚠️ **Easton himself.** The chapter's emphasis. The man who codified the eight tenets at Lesson 21 announces the correction.
2. ⚠️ **The same institution.** The APSA presidential address — the same vehicle **Merriam** used in **1925** to launch the behavioural call at Lesson 18. 🔑 Forty-four years, one podium, two revolutions.
3. ⚠️ **A shift of focus**, not a replacement. The vocabulary is already conciliatory before Lesson 24 makes it explicit.
4. ⚠️ **Two destinations**: **public responsibilities of the discipline** and **political problems**. The first is about the scholar's role — Munro's question from Lesson 19, reopened. The second is about subject matter.

### 5. The visual

```text
   THREE ATTACKS, THREE DIFFERENT PLACES TO STAND

   FROM OUTSIDE THE METHOD -- a charge about SCOPE
   STRAUSS, 'What is Political Philosophy?', Journal of Politics,
   1957  (!) EIGHT YEARS BEFORE the mid-1960s dominance
   "the RISE of behaviouralism was SYMPTOMATIC OF A CRISIS in
    political theory because of its FAILURE TO COME TO GRIPS WITH
    NORMATIVE ISSUES"

   FROM INSIDE THE DISCIPLINE -- a charge about PRIORITIES
   WOLIN, 'Political Theory as a Vocation', APSR, 1969
   "the PREOCCUPATION of political science WITH METHOD signified
    an ABDICATION of true VOCATION of political theory"
        ^^ ABDICATION = a duty wrongly abandoned

   FROM INSIDE SCIENCE ITSELF -- a charge about what SCIENCE IS
   KUHN, 'The Structure of Scientific Revolutions', 1962
   "significance of SCIENTIFIC METHOD lies in its capacity of
    PROBLEM-SOLVING and CRISIS-MANAGEMENT, NOT in METHODOLOGICAL
    SOPHISTICATION"
        (!!) behaviouralism CANNOT answer this by appealing to
             scientific standards -- Kuhn IS the standard here

                          |
   THE INTERNAL REALISATION
   "By the end of 1960s EVEN THE EXPONENTS of behaviouralism
    realized that its STRICT ADHERENCE TO 'PURE SCIENCE' was
    responsible for its FAILURE TO ATTEND to the PRESSING SOCIAL
    AND POLITICAL ISSUES of the period"
        ^^ names TENET 7 -- the tenet whose OWN CONTENT pointed at
           "the solution of urgent practical problems of society"
                          |
   THE ADDRESS
   1969 | DAVID EASTON HIMSELF | APSA PRESIDENTIAL ADDRESS
   announces "A POST-BEHAVIOURAL REVOLUTION" --
   "a SHIFT OF FOCUS from STRICT METHODOLOGICAL ISSUES to a
    GREATER CONCERN with (1) PUBLIC RESPONSIBILITIES OF THE
    DISCIPLINE and (2) POLITICAL PROBLEMS"

        1925 MERRIAM, APSA presidential address --> behaviouralism
        1969 EASTON,  APSA presidential address --> correction
```

*Caption: Kuhn is the critic most often omitted and the one hardest to answer. An answer that places him third, and says why his standpoint is different, is doing more than listing objections.*

### Revision notes

- 📰 **By the mid-1960s behaviouralism gained a dominant position in the methodology of political science.**
- ✅ **Leo Strauss**, **'What is Political Philosophy?'**, *Journal of Politics*; **1957**: behaviouralism's rise was **symptomatic of a crisis in political theory**.
- ✅ The reason: **its failure to come to grips with normative issues** — a charge about **scope**.
- ✅ **Sheldon Wolin**, **'Political Theory as a Vocation'**, *American Political Science Review*; **1969**: preoccupation with method **signified an abdication of true vocation of political theory**.
- ⚠️ **Abdication** implies a duty wrongly abandoned — a charge about **priorities**.
- ✅ **Thomas Kuhn**, ***The Structure of Scientific Revolutions*** (**1962**), from **within the sphere of philosophy of science**.
- 🔑 Kuhn's claim: the **significance of scientific method lies in its capacity of problem-solving and crisis-management, not in methodological sophistication**.
- ⚠️ Kuhn is the hardest to answer because he states the scientific standard rather than opposing it.
- ⚠️ Strauss's paper **precedes** the mid-1960s dominance by eight years; Wolin's is contemporaneous with Easton's address.
- 🔑 **By the end of 1960s even the exponents of behaviouralism realized that its strict adherence to 'pure science' was responsible for its failure to attend to the pressing social and political issues of the period.**
- ✅ **In 1969, David Easton himself**, in his **presidential address to the American Political Science Association**, announced **'a post-behavioural revolution'**.
- ✅ It **represented a shift of focus from strict methodological issues to a greater concern with public responsibilities of the discipline and with political problems**.
- ❌ No paradigms or normal science from Kuhn in this chapter. ➡️ Philosophy of science.

### Concept check

**Question:** Of the three critics named, whose objection is hardest for a committed behaviouralist to answer, and why?

**Model answer:** Kuhn's, and the reason lies in where he stands rather than in how severe the charge is. ⚠️ **Strauss** can be answered by a division of labour. ✅ His charge is that behaviouralism's rise was **symptomatic of a crisis in political theory because of its failure to come to grips with normative issues**, and a behaviouralist may reply that normative issues are not the business of empirical inquiry — which is precisely what tenet 5 asserts in drawing **a clear distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation**. ⚠️ The two then disagree about the discipline's scope, with neither compelled by the other. ⚠️ **Wolin** can be answered by denying the premise about duty. ✅ His charge is that preoccupation with method **signified an abdication of true vocation of political theory**, and the reply is that the vocation has been redefined, not abandoned; **abdication** presupposes an obligation the behaviouralist does not acknowledge. ⚠️ ⚠️ **Kuhn** is different because he does not stand outside science. ✅ His claim is that **significance of scientific method lies in its capacity of problem-solving and crisis-management, not in methodological sophistication** — a claim about what makes scientific method valuable **as science**. ⚠️ A behaviouralist who answers by invoking rigour, verification and technique is invoking exactly the **methodological sophistication** Kuhn has just denied is the source of significance, so the usual defence is unavailable. ⚠️ To answer Kuhn one would have to show that the behavioural programme **was** solving problems and managing crises, which Lesson 22's finding about **micro-level situations** makes difficult, or to dispute Kuhn's account of science, which requires leaving political science altogether. 🔑 That is why the chapter places Kuhn immediately before the internal realisation: his is the objection that the exponents themselves could not deflect.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not present the post-behavioural revolution as a victory of traditionalists over behaviouralists. ✅ The chapter records that **even the exponents of behaviouralism** reached the diagnosis, and that the announcement was made by **David Easton himself** — the codifier of the eight tenets. ❌ And do not expand Kuhn beyond his single sentence; paradigms and normal science are not in this chapter, and importing them adds unsourced content to a topic where provenance is the examined skill.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Who attacked behaviouralism before 1969, and on what different grounds? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Three critics, from three standpoints. **Leo Strauss**, in **'What is Political Philosophy?'** (*Journal of Politics*; **1957**), held that the rise of behaviouralism was **symptomatic of a crisis in political theory because of its failure to come to grips with normative issues** — a charge about **scope**, and one that precedes the movement's mid-1960s dominance. **Sheldon Wolin**, in **'Political Theory as a Vocation'** (APSR; **1969**), held that preoccupation with method **signified an abdication of true vocation of political theory** — a charge about **priorities**, since **abdication** presupposes a duty. ⚠️ **Thomas Kuhn**, in ***The Structure of Scientific Revolutions*** (**1962**), argued from within the philosophy of science that the **significance of scientific method lies in its capacity of problem-solving and crisis-management, not in methodological sophistication** — the hardest charge, because it states the scientific standard rather than opposing it.

**Rubric [10]:** three critics with correct works, places and years **4** · the distinct ground of each charge **3** · Strauss's priority in time **1** · why Kuhn's standpoint is the hardest **2**.

## Lesson 24 — Relevance and action, and what they did not mean

Progress: 24 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part IV — the post-behavioural correction

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§6, 9 and 14 with the Advanced companion's §§6, 7 and 12, read against the second half of the chapter's section headed "Post-Behavioural Revolution" and the boxed comparison table that follows (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.121-122 = printed 107-108), which supply the twin slogans, the no-departure clause, the three crises Easton named, the ivory-tower charge, the technicians warning, the value-construction conclusion, the absorption claim and the five-row comparison table.
CA search: "relevance and action political science post-behaviouralism 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried. 📰 Easton's named crises are **1969** references and are carried with that date.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The twin slogans, and the clause that frames them

✅ **Relevance and action were the twin slogans of post-behaviouralism.**

✅ And immediately:

> It represented no complete departure from behaviouralism. Rather it stood for consolidating its gains and applying them for problem-solving and crisis-management.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.121 (printed 107)

🔑 ⚠️ **If you memorise one sentence from Part IV, memorise this one.** ⚠️ **No complete departure** · **consolidating its gains** · **applying them**. ⚠️ Three phrases, all conciliatory, all in one sentence. ❌ Any answer that describes post-behaviouralism as a rejection of behaviouralism contradicts the chapter's own words.

⚠️ Note also **crisis-management** — the exact phrase attributed to Kuhn at Lesson 23. The settlement is written in the vocabulary of the objection that forced it.

### 2. The three crises Easton named

✅ Easton **emphatically drew the attention of contemporary political scientists** to:

1. ✅ **the impending threat of the nuclear bomb**
2. ✅ **inner conflicts within the US which might lead to civil war or dictatorship**
3. ✅ **undeclared war in Vietnam which was perturbing moral consciousness the world over**

📰 ⚠️ **These are 1969 references and must be carried with that date.** ❌ Do not update them, generalise them into "global crises" or substitute contemporary examples; the force of the passage is that these were the specific things political science was not talking about while perfecting its methods.

### 3. The charges

✅ **He lamented that the behavioural political scientists were taking refuge in their 'ivory tower', seeking to perfect their methodology, as if they were not at all concerned with the outside world.**

✅ **Emphasizing the intellectuals' historical role in protecting the humane values of civilization, Easton warned that if they failed to play this role, they would be reduced to mere technicians or mechanics for tinkering with society.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Technicians or mechanics for tinkering with society** is the sharpest phrase in the chapter, and the threat it names is not incompetence but **servility** — a technician applies skill to ends set by others. ⚠️ Recall Dahl at Lesson 6: **in politics, 'refusing to decide' is simply deciding to allow others to decide for you.** 🔑 Easton's warning is the same argument at the level of a discipline.

### 4. The credo

✅ The conclusion, and the sentence that makes the settlement coherent:

> Reminding them of their responsibility to reshape society Easton concluded that scientists could adopt a rational interest in value construction and application without denying the validity of their science.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.121-122 (printed 107-108)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Take it apart, because every phrase is load-bearing.**

| Phrase | What it does |
|---|---|
| ✅ **a rational interest** | ⚠️ Not a passion or a commitment — values are to be approached **rationally**, which keeps them inside the discipline's competence |
| ✅ **value construction** | ⚠️ Values are to be **built**, not merely held — compare Lesson 6's claim that parties can **evolve** basic principles of reasoning |
| ✅ **and application** | ⚠️ Construction is not enough; the values must be put to work |
| ✅ **without denying the validity of their science** | 🔑 The guarantee. Nothing in the eight tenets is withdrawn |

✅ **Accordingly, post-behaviouralism seeks to reintroduce a concern for values in the behavioural approach itself.**

⚠️ **In the behavioural approach itself** — the correction is housed inside, not built alongside.

### 5. The absorption claim

✅ **In the contemporary social science the behavioural approach has shown increasing concern with solving the prevailing problems of society. In this way it has largely absorbed the 'post-behavioural' orientation within its scope.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is a strong and under-noticed claim, and it is dated.** 📰 **Contemporary** here means the chapter's own period, up to **2009**. ⚠️ The claim is that post-behaviouralism no longer exists as a separate position because behaviouralism has taken it in. ❌ Do not assert this as the present state of the discipline. ⚠️ And note the hedge **largely**.

⚠️ ⚠️ A sharp observation worth making: if the absorption claim is right, then the standard exam framing of "behaviouralism versus post-behaviouralism" describes a quarrel that the chapter says is over. ⚠️ The comparison table that follows therefore sets out two positions the same page has just said have merged — which is not a contradiction, since a table can record a historical distinction, but it is worth handling explicitly rather than ignoring.

### 6. The comparison table

✅ Reproduced in its printed rows:

> | The Issue | Behavioural Approach | Post-Behavioural Approach |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Nature of Inquiry** | **Search for Pure Knowledge and Theory** | **Search for Applied Knowledge and Practice** |
> | **Purpose of Inquiry** | **'Knowledge for Knowledge Sake'; Not Interested in Action** | **Relevance of Knowledge to satisfy social needs and Action for Problem-Solving** |
> | **Focus of Study** | **Micro-level Analysis; focus on small units** · **Process of Decision-Making** | **Macro-level Analysis; focus on the role of big units** · **Content of the Decision** |
> | **Attitude toward Values** | **Value-Neutral** | **Interested in the Choice of Values** |
> | **Attitude toward Social Change** | **Interested in Status Quo; Not Interested in Social Change** | **Interested in Social Change for Solving Social Problems** |
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.122 (printed 108), boxed table "Distinction between Behavioural and Post-Behavioural Approaches"

⚠️ ⚠️ **Three notes on the table.**

1. ⚠️ **The third row has two sub-rows**, and the second of them is the subtler: **Process of Decision-Making** against **Content of the Decision**. ⚠️ Behaviouralism asks how a decision was reached; post-behaviouralism asks what was decided and whether it was any good.
2. ⚠️ ⚠️ **The fifth row is harsher than the prose.** **Interested in Status Quo** goes beyond anything the narrative says; the prose charges behaviouralists with taking refuge in an **ivory tower**, which is detachment, not endorsement of the existing order. ⚠️ The table states a stronger position than the text supports, and an answer that reproduces the table should note the gap rather than inherit it.
3. ⚠️ The extraction prints **Atitude** for the fifth row's label, normalised and catalogued.

### 7. The visual

```text
   THE SETTLEMENT, IN ONE SENTENCE
   "scientists could adopt a RATIONAL INTEREST in VALUE
    CONSTRUCTION AND APPLICATION *WITHOUT DENYING THE VALIDITY OF
    THEIR SCIENCE*"
        |            |              |            |
     rational    construction   application   nothing withdrawn
     -- inside   -- values are  -- put to     from the eight
     competence  BUILT          work          tenets

   WHAT IT IS NOT
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   X  a rejection of behaviouralism
      >>> "It represented NO COMPLETE DEPARTURE from
           behaviouralism."
   X  an abandonment of rigour
      >>> "Rather it stood for CONSOLIDATING ITS GAINS and
           APPLYING THEM for problem-solving and crisis-management."
   X  a separate school alongside behaviouralism
      >>> "seeks to REINTRODUCE a concern for values IN THE
           BEHAVIOURAL APPROACH ITSELF"
      >>> and, as of 2009: behaviouralism "has LARGELY ABSORBED
           the 'post-behavioural' orientation WITHIN ITS SCOPE"

   WHAT EASTON ACTUALLY POINTED AT -- 1969, carry the date
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   [1] "the impending threat of the NUCLEAR BOMB"
   [2] "INNER CONFLICTS WITHIN THE US which might lead to CIVIL WAR
        or DICTATORSHIP"
   [3] "UNDECLARED WAR IN VIETNAM which was perturbing MORAL
        CONSCIOUSNESS the world over"
        |
   THE CHARGE: taking refuge in their "IVORY TOWER", seeking to
   PERFECT THEIR METHODOLOGY, "AS IF THEY WERE NOT AT ALL CONCERNED
   WITH THE OUTSIDE WORLD"
        |
   THE THREAT: "reduced to MERE TECHNICIANS OR MECHANICS FOR
   TINKERING WITH SOCIETY"
        ^^ the danger is SERVILITY, not incompetence --
           a technician serves ends SET BY OTHERS
           cf. DAHL (L6): "'refusing to decide' is simply deciding
           to allow OTHERS to decide FOR YOU"
```

*Caption: the "what it is not" block is the most useful thing on this page. Three of the commonest exam errors are listed there with the chapter's own refutation beside each.*

### Revision notes

- 🔑 **Relevance and action were the twin slogans of post-behaviouralism.**
- 🔑 **It represented no complete departure from behaviouralism. Rather it stood for consolidating its gains and applying them for problem-solving and crisis-management.**
- ⚠️ **Crisis-management** is Kuhn's own phrase — the settlement is written in the vocabulary of the objection.
- 📰 Easton's three **1969** references: **the impending threat of the nuclear bomb**; **inner conflicts within the US which might lead to civil war or dictatorship**; **undeclared war in Vietnam which was perturbing moral consciousness the world over**.
- ✅ The charge: behavioural political scientists were **taking refuge in their 'ivory tower', seeking to perfect their methodology, as if they were not at all concerned with the outside world**.
- ✅ The warning, emphasising **the intellectuals' historical role in protecting the humane values of civilization**: they would be **reduced to mere technicians or mechanics for tinkering with society**.
- ⚠️ The threat is **servility**, not incompetence — the same argument as Dahl's at Lesson 6.
- 🔑 The credo: **scientists could adopt a rational interest in value construction and application without denying the validity of their science**.
- ✅ **Post-behaviouralism seeks to reintroduce a concern for values in the behavioural approach itself.**
- 📰 ⚠️ Absorption claim, as of **2009**: the behavioural approach **has largely absorbed the 'post-behavioural' orientation within its scope**.
- ✅ Table rows: **Nature of Inquiry** — pure knowledge and theory / applied knowledge and practice; **Purpose** — **'Knowledge for Knowledge Sake'; Not Interested in Action** / **Relevance … and Action for Problem-Solving**.
- ✅ **Focus** — micro, **Process of Decision-Making** / macro, **Content of the Decision**; **Values** — **Value-Neutral** / **Interested in the Choice of Values**; **Social Change** — **Interested in Status Quo** / **Interested in Social Change for Solving Social Problems**.
- ⚠️ The **Status Quo** cell is stronger than the prose supports; note the gap rather than inherit it.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Post-behaviouralism abandoned the scientific pretensions of behaviouralism in favour of political engagement." Dismantle this sentence using the chapter's own wording, and identify the one place in the chapter that might appear to support it.

**Model answer:** Three claims, and the chapter denies all three. ❌ **Abandoned**: ✅ the chapter states that post-behaviouralism **represented no complete departure from behaviouralism** and **stood for consolidating its gains**, which is retention, not abandonment. ❌ **Scientific pretensions**: ✅ Easton's own conclusion is that scientists could take a **rational interest in value construction and application without denying the validity of their science** — the final clause is an explicit guarantee that the scientific claims stand. ⚠️ The eight tenets are nowhere withdrawn, and the chapter's verdict that they **became the rule of political inquiry** is never retracted. ❌ **In favour of political engagement**: ✅ the demand is for **relevance** and **action**, but action is specified as **problem-solving and crisis-management** — Kuhn's own criterion for what makes scientific method significant — and not as partisanship; the interest in values is **rational**, and values are to be **constructed**, which is disciplined work rather than advocacy. ✅ The housing is also wrong in the sentence: the correction **seeks to reintroduce a concern for values in the behavioural approach itself**, so it is not a rival. ⚠️ The one place that might appear to support the sentence is the comparison table's fifth row, which sets **Interested in Status Quo; Not Interested in Social Change** against **Interested in Social Change for Solving Social Problems** — a contrast sharp enough to suggest two opposed political postures. ⚠️ But the table is a schematic summary, the prose charges only detachment in an **ivory tower**, and the same page states that behaviouralism **has largely absorbed the 'post-behavioural' orientation within its scope**. 🔑 The accurate formulation is that post-behaviouralism redirected a science it did not disown.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not update Easton's three crises. 📰 **The impending threat of the nuclear bomb**, **inner conflicts within the US** and the **undeclared war in Vietnam** are **1969** references, and replacing them with present-day examples destroys the passage's point, which is that those specific events were being ignored. ❌ And do not quote the table's **Interested in Status Quo** cell as the chapter's considered verdict on behaviouralism; the narrative charges detachment from the outside world, which is a different and lesser accusation.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Is post-behaviouralism a rejection of behaviouralism? *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** No, and the chapter says so in one sentence: it **represented no complete departure from behaviouralism. Rather it stood for consolidating its gains and applying them for problem-solving and crisis-management.** ⚠️ The slogans were **relevance and action**, and the charge was that behavioural scientists were **taking refuge in their 'ivory tower', seeking to perfect their methodology, as if they were not at all concerned with the outside world**, risking becoming **mere technicians or mechanics for tinkering with society**. ⚠️ But the settlement preserves the science: Easton concluded that scientists could adopt **a rational interest in value construction and application without denying the validity of their science**, and the correction **seeks to reintroduce a concern for values in the behavioural approach itself**. 📰 By **2009** the chapter records that behaviouralism **has largely absorbed the 'post-behavioural' orientation within its scope**.

**Rubric [10]:** the no-complete-departure sentence quoted **3** · the twin slogans and the ivory-tower charge **2** · the credo with its guarantee clause **3** · the housing-inside formulation or the dated absorption claim **2**.
