## Lesson 25 — What a model of political analysis is

Progress: 25 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part V — models of political analysis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§4 and 15 with the Advanced companion's §§3 and 6, read against the chapter's paragraphs introducing political analysis (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.122-123 = printed 108-109), which supply the origin of political analysis in the empirical-scientific orientation, the two classical precursors, the step-by-step construction of a model, the definition of a model, the reason there are several, the ideal-coincidence remark and the list of models with the standing of the Marxist addition.
CA search: "models of political analysis systems structural functional 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Where political analysis comes from

✅ **Political analysis is the product of the empirical-scientific orientation in the study of politics.**

✅ Two classical precursors are named: **In the classical tradition, Aristotle's classification of governments and Machiavelli's cost-benefit analysis of different techniques of leadership may be cited as earlier and rudimentary attempts of political analysis.**

⚠️ ⚠️ Note the adjective: **rudimentary**. ⚠️ And note that both precursors come from the two classical themes of Lesson 10 — Aristotle's classification sits under the art of government, and Machiavelli is the chapter's own exemplar of that theme. 🔑 The art of government, declared to have left mainstream theory at Lesson 10, reappears here as the ancestor of political analysis. ⚠️ The chapter does not say so; the connection is this session's observation.

✅ **However, political analysis in the real sense has flourished only in modern times, particularly with the adoption of scientific method in the study of politics.**

### 2. How a model is built — four steps

✅ The chapter walks through the construction:

| Step | The chapter's wording |
|---|---|
| **1** | ✅ **We start with determining the nature of politics.** **David Easton's concept of politics as 'authoritative allocation of values' will serve as a suitable example.** |
| **2** | ✅ **Politics is now everywhere recognized as a process.** **A process involves a set of interactions among its components.** **To proceed in the direction of political analysis, we must identify these components.** ✅ This **will guide us as to the relevant data which should be chosen** |
| **3** | ✅ **But how are these data interrelated? This will depend on our conception of the pattern of interaction of the components of the political process.** |
| **4** | ✅ **So the next step is to arrange our data according to their interrelationship as conceived by us so as to yield an explanation.** |

⚠️ ⚠️ The extraction prints **'authoritive allocation of values'** here; the chapter's own form elsewhere is **'authoritative allocation of values'**, and the correction is catalogued.

### 3. The definition

✅ **Now these two steps, i.e. determining the relevant data and evolving their suitable arrangement for yielding an explanation constitute a model of political analysis.**

🔑 ⚠️ **A model is therefore two things and only two: a selection of data, and an arrangement of it that explains.** ⚠️ Compare Van Dyke at Lesson 1: an approach is **criteria of selection**. ⚠️ A model adds the second component — **arrangement** — which is exactly what the institutional approach at Lesson 15 lacked when it was found to rely on **description rather than explanation**.

✅ **Since such model is evolved at a conceptual level, it is possible to build several models of political analysis, depending upon our points of inquiry.**

🔑 ⚠️ **At a conceptual level** is the licence for plurality. Models are constructions, so more than one can be built from the same material.

### 4. The honest admission

✅ **Ideally all explanations of a given situation derived through different models of political analysis should coincide, but this does not always happen.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This sentence deserves more attention than it usually gets.** ⚠️ If models were merely different presentations of the same findings, their explanations would always coincide. ⚠️ That they do not means the arrangement is doing substantive explanatory work — the model is not a neutral container. ⚠️ Which in turn means that choosing a model is a consequential decision, and the chapter's refusal at Lesson 34 to crown any one of them is not indecision but a consequence of this admission.

### 5. The list, and the standing of the fifth

✅ **Contemporary political science has evolved a large number of models of political analysis.** Of these, four **could be chosen for a detailed description**:

- ✅ **systems analysis**
- ✅ **structural-functional analysis**
- ✅ **communications theory**
- ✅ **decision-making analysis**

✅ And then, in a sentence that must be reproduced carefully:

> These models are associated with the liberal view of politics. Finally, Marxist analysis may be added to this list though it may lead to very different results.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.123 (printed 109)

🔑 ⚠️ **Two separate claims about the fifth model, and both matter.**
- ⚠️ The first four share an affiliation: they are **associated with the liberal view of politics**. ➡️ Topic 07 owns the three views of politics; here the point is only that the four models are not neutral between them.
- ⚠️ Marxist analysis is **added** — the verb is deliberate — and the warning is that it **may lead to very different results**.

⚠️ ⚠️ A sharp observation: at Lesson 2 the chapter said the models are **methods adopted under** the empirical approach. ⚠️ If all five sit under one approach, they share one selection of data; but if Marxist analysis leads to **very different results**, something more than arrangement differs. ⚠️ Lesson 32 resolves this — Marxian analysis denies that the political system is an autonomous object at all, which is a disagreement about selection, not arrangement. 🔑 So the fifth model is a different kind of thing from the other four, and the chapter's **may be added** quietly concedes it.

### 6. The visual

```text
   HOW A MODEL IS BUILT -- four steps, two components

   STEP 1  DETERMINE THE NATURE OF POLITICS
           e.g. Easton: "authoritative allocation of values"
                     |
   STEP 2  POLITICS IS A PROCESS -> "a set of INTERACTIONS AMONG
           ITS COMPONENTS" -> IDENTIFY THE COMPONENTS
                     |
           ==> this "will GUIDE US AS TO THE RELEVANT DATA
               which should be CHOSEN"        [COMPONENT 1]
                     |
   STEP 3  "HOW ARE THESE DATA INTERRELATED?" -- depends on "our
           CONCEPTION OF THE PATTERN OF INTERACTION"
                     |
   STEP 4  "ARRANGE our data according to their INTERRELATIONSHIP
           as conceived by us SO AS TO YIELD AN EXPLANATION"
                                              [COMPONENT 2]
                     |
   >>> A MODEL = (1) the RELEVANT DATA + (2) an ARRANGEMENT that
       YIELDS AN EXPLANATION.  Nothing else.

   WHY THERE ARE SEVERAL
   "Since such model is evolved AT A CONCEPTUAL LEVEL, it is
    POSSIBLE TO BUILD SEVERAL MODELS ... depending upon our POINTS
    OF INQUIRY"

   AND THE ADMISSION THAT MAKES THE CHOICE MATTER
   "IDEALLY all explanations ... should COINCIDE, BUT THIS DOES NOT
    ALWAYS HAPPEN."
        (!) so ARRANGEMENT does substantive explanatory work --
            a model is NOT a neutral container

   THE FIVE
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   SYSTEMS ANALYSIS           \
   STRUCTURAL-FUNCTIONAL       |  "associated with the LIBERAL
   COMMUNICATIONS THEORY       |   VIEW OF POLITICS"
   DECISION-MAKING ANALYSIS   /
   - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
   MARXIST ANALYSIS -- "MAY BE ADDED to this list THOUGH IT MAY
                        LEAD TO VERY DIFFERENT RESULTS"
        (!) a different KIND of entry: Lesson 32 shows it denies
            the autonomy of the political system itself, which is
            a disagreement about SELECTION, not ARRANGEMENT
```

*Caption: the two-component definition at the centre is the test to apply to each of the next seven lessons. For every model ask: what did it select, and how did it arrange? If you can answer both, you have the model.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Political analysis is the product of the empirical-scientific orientation in the study of politics.**
- ✅ Precursors, called **earlier and rudimentary attempts**: **Aristotle's classification of governments** and **Machiavelli's cost-benefit analysis of different techniques of leadership**.
- ✅ **Political analysis in the real sense has flourished only in modern times, particularly with the adoption of scientific method.**
- ✅ Step 1: determine **the nature of politics** — e.g. Easton's **authoritative allocation of values**.
- ✅ Step 2: politics is **a process**, which **involves a set of interactions among its components**; identifying them **will guide us as to the relevant data which should be chosen**.
- ✅ Step 3: how the data are interrelated **will depend on our conception of the pattern of interaction**.
- ✅ Step 4: **arrange our data according to their interrelationship as conceived by us so as to yield an explanation**.
- 🔑 **Determining the relevant data and evolving their suitable arrangement for yielding an explanation constitute a model of political analysis.**
- ✅ Models are **evolved at a conceptual level**, so **several** are possible **depending upon our points of inquiry**.
- 🔑 **Ideally all explanations … should coincide, but this does not always happen** — so arrangement does explanatory work.
- ✅ The four: **systems analysis; structural-functional analysis; communications theory; and decision-making analysis**.
- ✅ They are **associated with the liberal view of politics**.
- ✅ **Marxist analysis may be added to this list though it may lead to very different results.**
- ⚠️ The fifth is a different kind of entry, because its divergence is about selection rather than arrangement.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter admits that explanations derived from different models do not always coincide. What does that admission commit the chapter to, and how does it constrain the way the five models may be compared?

**Model answer:** It commits the chapter to treating a model as explanatorily substantive rather than presentational. ⚠️ If a model were only a way of displaying findings, then two models applied to one situation would differ in format and agree in content, and the **ideal** of coincidence would be automatically met. ✅ Since the chapter says it **does not always happen**, the second component of a model — **evolving their suitable arrangement for yielding an explanation** — must be contributing to the explanation itself, not merely organising it. ⚠️ That follows from the definition: a model is **the relevant data** plus an arrangement **as conceived by us**, and the dependence on conception is where the divergence enters. ⚠️ The constraint on comparison is therefore twofold. ⚠️ First, the models cannot be ranked by accuracy as if they were competing measurements of one quantity; they can be assessed only by what each is able to explain, which is why every later lesson ends with a **use** and a **limit** rather than a verdict. ⚠️ Second, comparison must be relativised to the question asked, since models are built **depending upon our points of inquiry** — a question about stability suits systems analysis, a comparative question suits structural-functional analysis, a question about why one option was chosen suits decision-making analysis. 🔑 That is exactly the pluralist conclusion Lesson 34 reaches, and the admission at p.123 is its textual foundation. ⚠️ It also explains why Marxist analysis sits apart: the warning that it **may lead to very different results** is a strong instance of the same admission, and Lesson 32 shows the divergence there runs deeper, into what is selected as data at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a model as a theory about politics. ✅ The chapter's definition contains no substantive claim about political life — only **the relevant data** and an **arrangement** that **yields an explanation**. ❌ A model can be adopted without accepting any thesis about what politics is for. ❌ And do not describe the first four models as politically neutral; the chapter says they are **associated with the liberal view of politics**, which is a statement of affiliation, not of bias, but it is not nothing. ➡️ The three views of politics belong to Topic 07.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What is a model of political analysis, and why are there several? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** A model is built in four steps and consists of two components. One determines **the nature of politics** — Easton's **authoritative allocation of values** serving as the chapter's example; recognises politics as **a process** involving **a set of interactions among its components**; identifies those components, which **will guide us as to the relevant data which should be chosen**; and then arranges **our data according to their interrelationship as conceived by us so as to yield an explanation**. ✅ Hence **determining the relevant data and evolving their suitable arrangement for yielding an explanation constitute a model of political analysis**. ⚠️ There are several because a model is **evolved at a conceptual level**, so more than one can be built **depending upon our points of inquiry**. ⚠️ And the choice matters, since **ideally all explanations … should coincide, but this does not always happen**.

**Rubric [10]:** the four construction steps **3** · the two-component definition quoted **3** · the conceptual-level licence for plurality **2** · the non-coincidence admission and its significance **2**.

## Lesson 26 — Systems analysis I: Easton's model

Progress: 26 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part V — models of political analysis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §15 with the Advanced companion's §§5, 6 and 8, read against the chapter's section headed "Systems Analysis" down to its classification of outputs (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.123-124 = printed 109-110), which supply Dahl's definition of a system, the abstraction requirement, Easton's 1957 paper, the open-system argument, the inputs-outputs-feedback vocabulary, the raw-materials and energy metaphors and the three four-fold classifications.
CA search: "Easton systems analysis inputs outputs feedback 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What a system is

✅ **This is the pioneering model of political analysis. It conceives politics in terms of a political system.** ✅ And **Robert Dahl** supplies the definition:

> Any collection of elements that interact in some way with one another can be considered a system: a galaxy, a football team, a legislature, a political party
>
> — Dahl, *Modern Political Analysis*; 1991, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.123 (printed 109)

⚠️ ⚠️ **The list is deliberately absurd in its range** — a galaxy and a football team — and that is the point. **System** is a purely formal notion: anything whose elements **interact** qualifies. ⚠️ It carries no political content whatever, which is both its power (universal applicability) and its weakness (it explains nothing by itself).

### 2. The abstraction requirement

✅ **For the purpose of analysis, elements of a system should be looked at in an abstract way rather than as concrete things.** ✅ Therefore: **elements of a political system should not be seen as a group of individuals constituting a government or its organs, but as abstract elements interacting with each other in the sphere of politics.**

🔑 ⚠️ **This sentence is the formal break with the institutional approach, and it should be quoted whenever the two are compared.** ⚠️ The institutional approach studied **a set of offices and agencies arranged in a hierarchy** manned by **persons with definite status and role** — concrete things. ⚠️ Systems analysis replaces them with **abstract elements**. 🔑 That is why the same polity can be described twice over with no overlap in vocabulary.

### 3. Easton's model

✅ **A model of political system in this sense was first developed by David Easton ('An Approach to the Analysis of Political Systems', World Politics; 1957) who is regarded to be the pioneer of systems analysis.**

📰 **1957** — the same year as Strauss's criticism at Lesson 23, and a decade before Easton's own post-behavioural turn.

✅ The construction, step by step:

- ✅ **Easton defined politics as the 'authoritative allocation of values' which broadly constitutes the political process.**
- ✅ **This does not take place in a closed circuit; its ends are connected with the social process. It is in this sense that the political system has been described as an open system.**
- ✅ **'Allocation of values' is made because there are corresponding 'demands' from the society or 'environment'; it becomes 'authoritative' because it gets 'supports' from the 'environment'.**

🔑 ⚠️ **That third bullet is the most elegant sentence in the model and is usually missed.** ⚠️ The two halves of Easton's definition of politics are mapped onto the two inputs: **allocation** answers to **demands**, and **authoritative** answers to **supports**. ⚠️ The model is not bolted onto the definition; it is derived from it. ➡️ The definition itself belongs to Topic 07.

✅ The vocabulary: **the 'political system' receives 'inputs' from the 'environment' in the form of 'demands' and 'supports'; it produces 'outputs' in the form of 'policies and decisions'.** ✅ **The 'outputs' flow back into the environment through a 'feedback' mechanism, giving rise to fresh 'demands', etc.**

### 4. The two metaphors

✅ **Easton has characterized demands as the raw materials out of which finished products called decisions are manufactured.** ✅ **He has described supports as the energy in the form of actions or orientations enabling the political system to convert the demands into authoritative decisions and policies.**

🔑 ⚠️ **One factory, two inputs of different kinds.** Demands are **raw materials** — the stuff worked on. Supports are **energy** — what powers the working. ⚠️ A factory with materials and no power produces nothing; a factory with power and no materials produces nothing. ⚠️ That is a genuinely useful way to hold the distinction, and it is the chapter's own.

✅ Sources and variability: **demands may arise from any source—the people, politicians, administrators, opinion leaders and so on—depending on the nature of the regime.** ✅ **The extent of support is bound to vary—depending on the expectations of the people from their political system.**

✅ And what varying support affects — three levels: **the political authorities (often called governments), the regime (democratic, authoritarian, and the like), and the political community.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **The three levels are examinable.** Loss of support at the first level changes a government; at the second, a regime; at the third, the political community itself dissolves. ⚠️ The model therefore has a built-in account of the **severity** of political crisis.

✅ **Outputs are produced by the political system through special processes that ensure their acceptance as binding by most members of the society most of the time.**

⚠️ Note the double hedge: **most members** … **most of the time**.

### 5. The three four-fold classifications

✅ **Easton has also given an elaborate classification of demands, supports and outputs which illustrates their nature thoroughly.**

| | **Demands** — four types | **Supports** — four types | **Outputs** — four categories |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | ✅ **demands for allocation of goods and services**, such as **wages and working conditions, educational opportunities, recreational facilities, roads and transportation** | ✅ **material supports**, such as **the payment of taxes and other levies, and rendering service in public interest, such as social work or military service** | ✅ **extractions**, which may take the form of **tribute, taxes or personal services** |
| **2** | ✅ **demands for the regulation of behaviour**, such as **provision of public safety, controls over markets and rules pertaining to marriage, health and sanitation** | ✅ **obedience to law, rules and regulations** | ✅ **regulations of behaviour**, covering **a wide range of human activities** |
| **3** | ✅ **demands for participation in the political system**, such as **the right to vote, to hold office, to petition government bodies and officials, and to form political associations** | ✅ **participatory supports**, such as **voting, political discussion and other forms of political activity** | ✅ **allocation or distribution of goods and services, opportunities, honours, statuses and the like** |
| **4** | ✅ **demands for communication and information**, such as **the affirmation of norms, the information regarding policy intent, and display of the majesty and power of the political system in times of foreign threats as well as on ceremonial occasions** | ✅ **paying attention to governmental communication, and display of deference or respect to public authority, symbols and ceremonials** | ✅ **symbolic outputs**, including **affirmation of values, display of political symbols and communication of policy intent** |

🔑 ⚠️ **The three columns are parallel, and that is the model's hidden achievement.** ⚠️ Read across any row and you find the same functional category appearing as a demand, as a support and as an output — regulation in row 2, participation in row 3, communication and symbolism in row 4. ⚠️ The parallelism is what makes the system **close**: what is asked for, what is contributed, and what is produced are commensurable.

✅ Note also the parenthesis defining policy: **a policy itself is a web of decisions** — the same formulation Topic 07's chapter used for Easton's definition.

### 6. The visual

```text
                          ENVIRONMENT
   ===============================================================
        INPUTS                                      OUTPUTS
   +-------------+                            +-----------------+
   |  DEMANDS    |---\                   /--->|  POLICIES and   |
   |  "RAW       |    \                 /     |  DECISIONS      |
   |   MATERIALS"|     \               /      |  ("a policy is  |
   +-------------+      \  +---------+/       |   a WEB OF      |
                         ->|POLITICAL|        |   DECISIONS")   |
   +-------------+      /  | SYSTEM  |\       +-----------------+
   |  SUPPORTS   |     /   +---------+ \                |
   |  "the       |    /    conversion   \               |
   |   ENERGY"   |---/                   \              |
   +-------------+                        \             |
          ^                                \            |
          |                                 \           v
          +------------- FEEDBACK <----------------------+
                    "giving rise to FRESH DEMANDS"
   ===============================================================
                          ENVIRONMENT

   THE DERIVATION -- why these two inputs and no others
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   politics = "AUTHORITATIVE   ALLOCATION   of values"
                    |              |
                    |              +--> made because there are
                    |                   corresponding DEMANDS
                    +-------------> becomes authoritative because
                                    it gets SUPPORTS

   THE FOUR-BY-THREE GRID -- read ACROSS, not just down
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
            DEMANDS              SUPPORTS            OUTPUTS
   1   goods and services    material (taxes,    EXTRACTIONS
       (wages, education,    levies, social      (tribute, taxes,
       roads, recreation)    work, military)     personal services)
   2   REGULATION of         OBEDIENCE to law,   REGULATIONS of
       behaviour (safety,    rules, regulations  behaviour
       markets, marriage,
       health, sanitation)
   3   PARTICIPATION         PARTICIPATORY       ALLOCATION of
       (vote, office,        (voting, political  goods, services,
       petition, associate)  discussion)         opportunities,
                                                 HONOURS, STATUSES
   4   COMMUNICATION and     ATTENTION to govt   SYMBOLIC outputs

       (norms, policy        DEFERENCE to        values, political
       intent, MAJESTY on    authority, symbols, symbols, policy
       ceremonial occasions) ceremonials         intent)

   WHAT VARYING SUPPORT THREATENS -- three levels of severity
   [1] the POLITICAL AUTHORITIES ("often called governments")
   [2] the REGIME ("democratic, authoritarian, and the like")
   [3] the POLITICAL COMMUNITY
```

*Caption: read the grid across. Row 4 is the striking one — the same communicative function appears as a demand for information, as deference to symbols, and as symbolic output, which is why the model can treat a ceremonial parade as a political act without strain.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **This is the pioneering model of political analysis**; it **conceives politics in terms of a political system**.
- ✅ **Dahl**: **any collection of elements that interact in some way with one another can be considered a system: a galaxy, a football team, a legislature, a political party.**
- 🔑 Elements must be **looked at in an abstract way rather than as concrete things** — **not … a group of individuals constituting a government or its organs, but as abstract elements**.
- ✅ **Easton**, **'An Approach to the Analysis of Political Systems'**, *World Politics*; **1957**, **the pioneer of systems analysis**.
- ✅ The political process **does not take place in a closed circuit; its ends are connected with the social process** — hence **an open system**.
- 🔑 The derivation: **'allocation of values' is made because there are corresponding 'demands'**; **it becomes 'authoritative' because it gets 'supports'**.
- ✅ **Inputs** = **demands** and **supports**; **outputs** = **policies and decisions**; outputs **flow back into the environment through a 'feedback' mechanism, giving rise to fresh 'demands'**.
- 🔑 **Demands** are **the raw materials out of which finished products called decisions are manufactured**.
- 🔑 **Supports** are **the energy in the form of actions or orientations enabling the political system to convert the demands into authoritative decisions and policies**.
- ✅ Demands **may arise from any source—the people, politicians, administrators, opinion leaders and so on—depending on the nature of the regime**.
- ✅ Varying support affects **the political authorities (often called governments), the regime (democratic, authoritarian, and the like), and the political community**.
- ✅ Outputs are accepted **as binding by most members of the society most of the time** — ⚠️ a double hedge.
- ✅ The three four-fold classifications are parallel; read across the rows.
- ✅ **A policy itself is a web of decisions.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the model insist that the elements of a political system be treated abstractly, and what does that insistence cost?

**Model answer:** It insists because the model's comparative ambition requires it. ✅ The chapter's rule is that elements **should not be seen as a group of individuals constituting a government or its organs, but as abstract elements interacting with each other in the sphere of politics**. ⚠️ If the elements were concrete offices, every polity would have a different list — a Cabinet here, a politburo there, a council of elders elsewhere — and no two systems could be described in the same vocabulary. ⚠️ Abstracting to **demands**, **supports**, **conversion** and **outputs** produces categories that any polity must instantiate somehow, which is precisely why the chapter later reports the framework **found very useful for the comparative analysis of diverse political units, such as modernized as well as developing polities**. ⚠️ Dahl's definition licenses the move, since **any collection of elements that interact in some way with one another** counts, from **a galaxy** to **a political party**. ⚠️ The cost is that the abstraction discards exactly what the institutional approach studied. ⚠️ Who occupies which office, with what powers and under what procedure, becomes invisible at this level of description, and so does the question of which actor prevailed over which. ⚠️ That is the structural reason for the criticism Lesson 27 records — the model's **inability in the analysis of political power** — because power is a relation between identifiable actors, and the model has abstracted the actors away. 🔑 So the same move that buys comparability across systems spends the capacity to explain conflict within one, and an answer that names both sides of that bargain is giving the model its due.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat "demands" and "supports" as two names for the same input. ✅ Easton distinguishes them by function: demands are **the raw materials out of which finished products called decisions are manufactured**, supports are **the energy … enabling the political system to convert** them. ❌ A system can be flooded with demands and starved of supports, which is precisely the stress condition Lesson 27 analyses. ❌ And do not forget that supports have their own four-fold classification including **obedience to law** and **deference or respect to public authority, symbols and ceremonials** — support is not merely approval.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Explain Easton's systems model of political analysis. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** It conceives politics as a **political system**, where, on Dahl's definition, **any collection of elements that interact in some way with one another can be considered a system**. Elements are treated **in an abstract way rather than as concrete things**. ✅ Easton (**'An Approach to the Analysis of Political Systems'**, *World Politics*; **1957**) defines politics as the **authoritative allocation of values**, and derives the model from the definition: **allocation** is made because there are corresponding **demands**, and it becomes **authoritative** because it gets **supports**. ⚠️ The system therefore receives **inputs** of demands and supports, produces **outputs** in the form of **policies and decisions**, and these **flow back into the environment through a 'feedback' mechanism, giving rise to fresh 'demands'**. ⚠️ Demands are **the raw materials**; supports are **the energy** enabling conversion.

**Rubric [10]:** Dahl's definition of a system **2** · the abstraction requirement **1** · Easton's work and year **1** · the derivation of the two inputs from the definition **3** · the full circuit including feedback **2** · the raw-materials/energy distinction **1**.

## Lesson 27 — Systems analysis II: stress, gatekeepers and the critics

Progress: 27 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part V — models of political analysis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§15 and 16 with the Advanced companion's §§6, 7 and 8, read against the chapter's account of feedback, equilibrium and stress and its closing verdict on systems analysis (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.125-126 = printed 111 and the unnumbered page), which supply the definition of feedback, the normal-circumstances expectation, the difficulty of equilibrium, demands lost in transit, the overload condition, the four regulatory mechanisms, the escalation to regime change and destruction, the comparative uses and the two stated criticisms.
CA search: "political system overload gatekeepers stress analysis 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Feedback, defined

✅ **Feedback is essentially a communication process which produces action in response to information about the state of the political system, or some part thereof, or its environment, to structures within the system in such a way that the future action of those structures is modified in consequence.** ✅ **The results of such modification may, in turn, produce further modifications, and so on.**

✅ What it achieves: **The feedback channel helps the political system in approaching its goals.** ✅ **It apprises the political system of the position of its goal, its own distance from it, and the changes in this distance brought about by its own performance, in response to information coming from the feedback process.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Three pieces of information, and they form a navigational set**: where the goal is, how far away it is, and whether the last action closed or widened the gap. ⚠️ That is already the logic Deutsch will formalise at Lesson 30 as **cybernetics**, and the chapter says as much when it notes that Easton's model **served as a basis for … Karl Deutsch's model of communications theory**.

### 2. The normal case, and why it is not guaranteed

✅ **Under the normal circumstances, demands would serve as guide to the political system for determining its policies and goals, and supports will enable it to achieve its goals.** ✅ **It may be hoped that if the political system is capable of processing the demand-inputs effectively, support-inputs are bound to come forth from its environment.** ✅ **Feedback mechanism is expected to adjust outputs to inputs.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Count the hedges: may be hoped, is expected to.** The chapter is describing an aspiration.

✅ And then the reversal: **However, maintaining a state of equilibrium in the political system is by no means a simple affair.** ✅ **Conversion of demands and supports into outputs in the shape of policies and decisions has to be regulated by a complex mechanism.**

### 3. Two failure conditions

| Condition | The chapter's wording |
|---|---|
| **Loss in transit** | ✅ **Of the many demands made on the political system, some may be lost in transit without reaching the output stage.** |
| **Overload** | ✅ **If there are too many demands, or too much insistence on particular type of demand, stress will arise and the channels will be overloaded.** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note that overload has two distinct causes**: **too many demands** — a volume problem — or **too much insistence on particular type of demand** — an intensity problem. ⚠️ A system can be overwhelmed by a single demand pressed hard enough, which is why mass movements around one issue strain polities that handle routine demand-traffic easily.

### 4. The four regulatory mechanisms

✅ **If so, various regulatory mechanisms will have to be applied to cope with the stress.**

| | Mechanism | The chapter's wording | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| **First** | **Structural** | ✅ **structural mechanisms, such as pressure groups, political parties, will assume the role of 'gatekeepers' who will allow only legitimate demands to enter the political system** | ⚠️ Filters at the **entry** |
| **Secondly** | **Cultural** | ✅ **cultural mechanisms will ensure that only right type of demands—which enjoy social approval—will be encouraged** | ⚠️ Filters **before** formation |
| **Thirdly** | **Communication** | ✅ **communication channels can be increased to ensure a smooth flow of excessive demands into the system** | ⚠️ Increases **capacity** |
| **Fourthly** | **Conversion** | ✅ **demands may be controlled in the conversion process itself by the legislative, executive and administrative organs of government who are responsible for processing all demands** | ⚠️ Filters at the **processing** stage |

🔑 ⚠️ **Three of the four reduce the load and one increases capacity.** ⚠️ Note also that the mechanisms are arranged by **where in the circuit they act** — before formation, at entry, in the channel, during conversion — which makes them easy to remember and easy to apply.

⚠️ ⚠️ **One observation the chapter does not make.** Three of these mechanisms work by **preventing demands from being heard**: gatekeepers admit only **legitimate** demands, cultural mechanisms encourage only those **which enjoy social approval**, and conversion-stage control filters during processing. ⚠️ A model whose account of stability runs largely through demand-suppression will find it hard to distinguish a well-functioning system from an effectively repressive one. ⚠️ That is a reconstruction, not the chapter's claim — but it connects directly to the criticism the chapter does make, about the **analysis of political power**.

### 5. Escalation, and the end

✅ **Should the stress reach a critical stage, and continue at that level for quite some time, changes in the authorities or even in the regime (such as through a revolution) might become inevitable.** ✅ **Failure to control the situation may even bring about eventual destruction of the system itself.**

⚠️ The three levels of Lesson 26 return as a scale of consequence: **authorities** → **regime** → **destruction of the system**. 🔑 So systems analysis does contain an account of revolution — as an outcome of sustained unmanaged stress. ⚠️ What it does not contain is an account of **who** makes the revolution or **why** they win, which is the criticism below.

### 6. Uses and criticisms

✅ **The framework of systems analysis has been found very useful for the comparative analysis of diverse political units, such as modernized as well as developing polities.** ✅ **It has also been extensively used for an analysis of the international political system.**

✅ Its intellectual descendants: **The model of political system has also served as a basis for Gabriel Almond's model of structural-functional analysis as also for Karl Deutsch's model of communications theory.**

🔑 That one sentence organises the next two lessons: both of the following models are built on this one.

✅ The criticisms, and there are exactly two:

> However, it has been criticized for its inability in the analysis of political power as well as in the analysis of mass political behaviour, such as voting. In any case, it represents a substantial advance in the direction of constructing a theoretical framework from within political science.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.125-126 (printed 111 and the unnumbered page; the sentence straddles a page on which the body is image-only in this scan except for one narrow text column, which is reassembled here and catalogued)

⚠️ ⚠️ **The second criticism is the striking one.** ⚠️ Systems analysis cannot analyse **mass political behaviour, such as voting** — which is the founding object of behaviouralism itself, the thing Wallas demanded at Lesson 17 and the institutional approach was faulted for leaving **to sociologists** at Lesson 15. 🔑 The leading model of empirical political analysis is weak at precisely the phenomenon the empirical turn was made to capture.

⚠️ ⚠️ And the explanation lies in Lesson 26: the model requires elements to be treated as **abstract elements** rather than **a group of individuals**. Voters are individuals. The abstraction that buys comparability forbids the micro-analysis.

⚠️ Note the concluding concession: **a substantial advance in the direction of constructing a theoretical framework from within political science** — ⚠️ **from within**, which answers the identity problem of Lesson 3.

### 7. The visual

```text
   THE SYSTEM UNDER LOAD

   DEMANDS arriving ................ |||||||||||||||||||||||
                                            |
   FAILURE CONDITION 1: LOSS IN TRANSIT
   "some may be LOST IN TRANSIT WITHOUT REACHING THE OUTPUT STAGE"
                                            |
   FAILURE CONDITION 2: OVERLOAD -- two different causes
   "if there are TOO MANY DEMANDS, or TOO MUCH INSISTENCE ON
    PARTICULAR TYPE OF DEMAND, STRESS will arise and the CHANNELS
    WILL BE OVERLOADED"
      (a) VOLUME        (b) INTENSITY -- one demand, pressed hard
                                            |
   THE FOUR REGULATORY MECHANISMS, BY WHERE THEY ACT
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   BEFORE FORMATION   [2] CULTURAL: "only RIGHT TYPE of demands --
                          WHICH ENJOY SOCIAL APPROVAL -- will be
                          ENCOURAGED"
   AT ENTRY           [1] STRUCTURAL: pressure groups, political
                          parties as "GATEKEEPERS who will allow
                          ONLY LEGITIMATE DEMANDS to enter"
   IN THE CHANNEL     [3] COMMUNICATION: "channels can be
                          INCREASED to ensure a SMOOTH FLOW of
                          EXCESSIVE DEMANDS into the system"
                          ^^ the ONLY one that adds CAPACITY
   DURING CONVERSION  [4] legislative, executive and
                          administrative organs "RESPONSIBLE FOR
                          PROCESSING ALL DEMANDS"
                                            |
   (!) THREE OF FOUR WORK BY NOT HEARING DEMANDS. A model that
       explains stability mainly through demand-suppression will
       struggle to tell a healthy system from a quiet one.
       [reconstruction -- not the chapter's claim]
                                            |
   IF STRESS PERSISTS -- escalation by level
   "changes in the AUTHORITIES" --> "or even in the REGIME (such as
   through a REVOLUTION)" --> "eventual DESTRUCTION OF THE SYSTEM
   ITSELF"

   VERDICT
   ---------------------------------------------------------------

          "MODERNIZED AS WELL AS DEVELOPING POLITIES";
          the INTERNATIONAL political system;
          BASIS for ALMOND (L28-29) and DEUTSCH (L30)
   FAULTS: "INABILITY in the analysis of POLITICAL POWER"
           "as well as in the analysis of MASS POLITICAL BEHAVIOUR,

           (!!) VOTING -- the founding object of behaviouralism
   STILL : "a SUBSTANTIAL ADVANCE in the direction of constructing
            a theoretical framework FROM WITHIN POLITICAL SCIENCE"
```

*Caption: the parenthetical in the middle and the double-flag on voting are the two observations that lift an answer above a description of the diagram. Note which is the chapter's and which is yours.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Feedback** is **a communication process which produces action in response to information about the state of the political system … in such a way that the future action of those structures is modified in consequence**.
- ✅ It **apprises the political system of the position of its goal, its own distance from it, and the changes in this distance brought about by its own performance**.
- ⚠️ Normal circumstances are described with hedges — **it may be hoped**, **is expected to**.
- ✅ **Maintaining a state of equilibrium in the political system is by no means a simple affair.**
- ✅ Failure one: demands **lost in transit without reaching the output stage**.
- ✅ Failure two: overload from **too many demands, or too much insistence on particular type of demand** — ⚠️ volume or intensity.
- ✅ **Structural** mechanisms — **pressure groups, political parties, will assume the role of 'gatekeepers' who will allow only legitimate demands to enter**.
- ✅ **Cultural** mechanisms ensure **only right type of demands—which enjoy social approval—will be encouraged**.
- ✅ **Communication channels can be increased to ensure a smooth flow of excessive demands into the system** — the only capacity-increasing mechanism.
- ✅ **Demands may be controlled in the conversion process itself by the legislative, executive and administrative organs of government.**
- ✅ Escalation: **changes in the authorities or even in the regime (such as through a revolution)**, and ultimately **eventual destruction of the system itself**.
- ✅ Uses: **comparative analysis of diverse political units, such as modernized as well as developing polities**, and **the international political system**.
- ✅ It **served as a basis for Gabriel Almond's model of structural-functional analysis as also for Karl Deutsch's model of communications theory**.
- 🔑 Two criticisms: **inability in the analysis of political power as well as in the analysis of mass political behaviour, such as voting**.
- ✅ Concession: **a substantial advance in the direction of constructing a theoretical framework from within political science**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter faults systems analysis for being unable to analyse mass political behaviour such as voting. Why is that criticism more damaging than it first appears, and what in the model produces it?

**Model answer:** It is more damaging because voting is not an incidental omission but the movement's founding object. ⚠️ **Wallas**'s chief message was that the political process can be understood **only by analysing as to how people actually behaved in a political situation**; the institutional approach was faulted at Lesson 15 precisely because **the study of voting behaviour and political attitudes of the individual was left to sociologists**; and **Roberts**'s definition puts the **voter** first among the roles that constitute political behaviour. ⚠️ So when the chapter reports that the **pioneering model of political analysis** suffers an **inability in the analysis of … mass political behaviour, such as voting**, it is reporting that the empirical turn's leading analytical framework cannot handle the phenomenon the empirical turn was made for. ⚠️ What produces it is the abstraction requirement from Lesson 26. ✅ Elements of a political system **should not be seen as a group of individuals constituting a government or its organs, but as abstract elements interacting with each other in the sphere of politics**. ⚠️ Voting is an aggregate of individual acts, and the model has abstracted individuals out of its vocabulary at the first step; voters can enter only as an undifferentiated source of **demands** and **participatory supports**, which says nothing about why any of them voted as they did. ⚠️ The same abstraction produces the companion criticism about **political power**, since power is a relation between identifiable actors. 🔑 The honest conclusion is that this is a cost of the model's design rather than a defect in its execution — the abstraction that makes **comparative analysis of diverse political units** possible is the same abstraction that forbids micro-analysis, which is why Lesson 34's pluralism is the chapter's conclusion and not a hedge.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the four regulatory mechanisms as a normative recommendation. ✅ The chapter presents them as what **will have to be applied to cope with the stress**, i.e. as a description of how systems in fact manage overload. ❌ Reading **gatekeepers who will allow only legitimate demands to enter** as advice would convert an analytical model into a theory of justified exclusion, which the chapter never does. ❌ And do not say systems analysis cannot explain revolution; it explicitly allows that sustained critical stress makes **changes in the authorities or even in the regime (such as through a revolution)** inevitable. What it cannot supply is the account of agency — who revolts, and why they succeed.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does Easton's model explain political stability and its breakdown? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Through the management of demand-load. Normally **demands would serve as guide to the political system for determining its policies and goals, and supports will enable it to achieve its goals**, with **feedback … expected to adjust outputs to inputs**. ⚠️ But **maintaining a state of equilibrium … is by no means a simple affair**: demands may be **lost in transit without reaching the output stage**, and **if there are too many demands, or too much insistence on particular type of demand, stress will arise and the channels will be overloaded**. ⚠️ Four regulatory mechanisms respond — **gatekeepers** admitting **only legitimate demands**, cultural filters encouraging those **which enjoy social approval**, increased communication channels, and control **in the conversion process itself**. ⚠️ If stress persists, **changes in the authorities or even in the regime (such as through a revolution) might become inevitable**, and ultimately **eventual destruction of the system itself**.

**Rubric [10]:** the normal circuit with its hedges **2** · the two failure conditions **2** · at least three regulatory mechanisms **3** · the three-stage escalation **2** · recognition that agency is not explained **1**.

## Lesson 28 — Structural-functional analysis I: functions and structures

Progress: 28 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part V — models of political analysis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §15 with the Advanced companion's §§5, 6 and 8, read against the first part of the chapter's section headed "Structural-Functional Analysis" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.127-128 = printed 112-113), which supplies the model's dependence on the concept of political system, its origin in social anthropology and sociology, Almond and Coleman's four characteristics, Almond and Powell's modification of Easton, the four input and three output functions, and the definition of each input function with its best-suited structure.
CA search: "Almond structural functional analysis input output functions 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Placement and pedigree

✅ **Like systems analysis, structural-functional analysis is also based on the concept of political system.** ✅ **This model of political analysis has been more widely used in the sphere of comparative politics because it provides for standard categories for different types of political systems.**

✅ Its lineage is explicitly borrowed:

| Discipline | Names |
|---|---|
| ✅ **social anthropology** — where it **originated** | ✅ **Radcliffe-Brown and B. Malinowski** |
| ✅ **sociology** — where it was **developed** | ✅ **Talcott Parsons, Robert Merton and Marion Levy** |
| ✅ **political analysis** — where it became **a tool** | ✅ **Gabriel Almond and his associates** |

⚠️ ⚠️ This is **Integration** — behaviouralism's eighth tenet — in action, and worth saying so. ❌ But the chapter gives these five names and nothing else: no work, no date, no doctrine. ➡️ Parsons, Merton and the sociological tradition belong elsewhere.

### 2. Almond and Coleman: four characteristics

✅ **Gabriel Almond and J.S. Coleman (The Politics of the Developing Areas; 1960) identified four characteristics of the political system:**

| | Characteristic |
|---|---|
| **(a)** | ✅ **all political systems have political structures** |
| **(b)** | ✅ **the same functions are performed in all political systems with different frequencies and by different kinds of structures** |
| **(c)** | ✅ **all political structures are multi-functional** |
| **(d)** | ✅ **all political systems are 'mixed' systems in the cultural sense, i.e. they are based in a culture which is always a mixture of the modern and the traditional** |

🔑 ⚠️ **Characteristic (b) is the engine of the whole model** and must be quoted precisely: the **same functions** everywhere, performed **with different frequencies and by different kinds of structures**. ⚠️ Functions are the constant; structures are the variable. That is what makes comparison possible between polities with nothing institutional in common.

⚠️ ⚠️ **Characteristic (d) is the one most often dropped, and it is the one that matters most for the later criticism.** ✅ **All** political systems are **'mixed'**, based in a culture **always a mixture of the modern and the traditional**. ⚠️ **All** — including Western ones. 🔑 So the model's own founding statement denies the modern/traditional dichotomy that Lesson 29's critics say it smuggles back in. ⚠️ An answer that notices this is identifying an internal inconsistency rather than merely repeating a complaint.

### 3. Almond and Powell: the modification of Easton

✅ **This approach was further developed by Gabriel Almond and G.B. Powell in Comparative Politics: A Developmental Approach (1966).**

✅ The premise: **all political systems, regardless of their type, must perform a specific set of tasks if they are to remain in existence as systems in working order or in equilibrium, i.e. as 'ongoing systems'.** ✅ **These are the functional requirements of the system.**

✅ The modification: they **sought to modify David Easton's model of the political system, suggesting that 'inputs' and 'outputs' recognized by Easton can best be understood as 'functions' or 'functional requisites' of political system.**

🔑 ⚠️ **State the modification precisely, because it is the exam-relevant difference between the two models.** ⚠️ Easton's inputs and outputs are **flows** — things that enter and leave. ⚠️ Almond reinterprets them as **functions** — things that must be **done** if the system is to persist. ⚠️ That shift is what lets him then ask **which structure does it**, a question Easton's abstract vocabulary could not pose.

✅ And the concession that makes the model comparative: they **conceded that in various political systems, these functions may be performed by different kinds of political structures and, sometimes, even by structures which are not overtly recognized as being, primarily, 'political'.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **That final clause is the single most usable sentence in this lesson.** ⚠️ It is the chapter's own authority for analysing caste associations, kinship networks or religious bodies as performers of political functions. ⚠️ Recall Lesson 14: the institutional approach excluded **family, school, church, or club**. 🔑 Almond lets them back in — not as institutions to be described, but as **structures performing functions**.

### 4. The seven functions

✅ **Almond and his associates discerned four input functions and three output functions.**

| | Function | Best-suited structure |
|---|---|---|
| **Input 1** | ✅ **Political socialization and recruitment** | ✅ **Family, school and other primary groups are most suited to perform this function** |
| **Input 2** | ✅ **Interest articulation** | ✅ **interest groups are most suited to perform this function** |
| **Input 3** | ✅ **Interest aggregation** | ✅ **Political parties are most suited to perform this function** |
| **Input 4** | ✅ **Political communication** | ✅ **Mass media or the organizations controlling the media of mass communication are most suited to perform this function** |
| **Output 5** | ✅ **Rule-making** | ✅ **legislature** |
| **Output 6** | ✅ **Rule-application** | ✅ **executive** |
| **Output 7** | ✅ **Rule-adjudication** | ✅ **judiciary** |

✅ The chapter's own note on the division: **output functions correspond to conventional governmental functions, which are performed by formal governmental organs**, whereas **Almond has paid special attention to input functions which are performed by non-governmental structures or institutions**.

✅ And the qualifier that prevents the table from becoming rigid: **Although all structures are multi-functional, yet some structures are especially suited for specific functions.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Most suited** is not **only**. The structure column records aptitude, not assignment.

### 5. The four input functions, defined

| Function | The chapter's definition |
|---|---|
| **Political socialization** | ✅ **the process whereby an individual acquires attitudes and orientations towards political phenomena**; also **the process whereby society transmits political norms and beliefs from one generation to the next** |
| **Recruitment** | ✅ **the process whereby political groups obtain members for various important roles in the political process, either in addition to the existing members or as replacement for other members** |
| **Interest articulation** | ✅ **the processes whereby opinions, attitudes, beliefs, preferences, etc. are converted into coherent demands on the political system** |
| **Interest aggregation** | ✅ **the process whereby various divergent interests are collated and translated into concrete demands of a very large section of society (preferably appealing to all sections of society), policy proposals and programmes of action** |
| **Political communication** | ✅ **the process whereby components of a political system, such as individuals, groups and institutions, transmit and receive information regarding the functioning of the political system** |

✅ The link between the first two: **since political socialization prepares the individuals to assume various important roles in the social structure, it is instrumental to recruitment also.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Articulation and aggregation are the pair most often confused and the pair most often examined.**
- ⚠️ **Articulation** turns **opinions, attitudes, beliefs, preferences** into **coherent demands** — it gives a want a voice.
- ⚠️ **Aggregation** takes **divergent interests** and collates them into demands **of a very large section of society**, **preferably appealing to all sections** — it gives many voices one programme.
- 🔑 The test is **scale and combination**: articulation makes one interest sayable; aggregation makes many interests compatible.

⚠️ Note that aggregation's output is explicitly threefold: **concrete demands of a very large section of society**, **policy proposals** and **programmes of action** — which is why parties, not interest groups, are its natural home.

### 6. The visual

```text
   EASTON'S FLOWS  --REINTERPRETED AS-->  ALMOND'S FUNCTIONS

    'FUNCTIONAL REQUISITES' of political system"
        a FLOW just happens | a FUNCTION must be PERFORMED
                            | -- so you can ask WHO PERFORMS IT

   THE FOUR CHARACTERISTICS (Almond + Coleman, 1960)

   (b) THE SAME FUNCTIONS are performed in ALL political systems

        STRUCTURES"              <<< THE ENGINE OF THE MODEL

       TRADITIONAL"              <<< note: ALL. Including the West.

       OUTPUTS by FORMAL GOVERNMENTAL ORGANS.

        structures are ESPECIALLY SUITED for specific functions"
        -- "most suited" is NOT "only".

   ARTICULATION vs AGGREGATION -- the examined pair

                   are CONVERTED INTO COHERENT DEMANDS on the
                   political system"
                   => gives ONE want A VOICE
   AGGREGATION  : "various DIVERGENT INTERESTS are COLLATED and
                   TRANSLATED INTO concrete demands of A VERY LARGE
                   SECTION OF SOCIETY (PREFERABLY APPEALING TO ALL
                   SECTIONS), POLICY PROPOSALS and PROGRAMMES OF
                   ACTION"
                   => gives MANY voices ONE PROGRAMME

   AND THE DOOR THAT OPENS COMPARATIVE POLITICS
   "these functions may be performed by DIFFERENT KINDS OF
    POLITICAL STRUCTURES and, SOMETIMES, EVEN BY STRUCTURES WHICH
    ARE NOT OVERTLY RECOGNIZED AS BEING, PRIMARILY, 'POLITICAL'"
        (!) exactly the family/school/church the INSTITUTIONAL

            FUNCTION-PERFORMERS rather than as institutions
```

*Caption: the articulation/aggregation box is worth memorising verbatim. Those two terms appear in exam questions more often than the rest of the model combined, and the distinction is scale plus combination, not importance.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Like systems analysis, structural-functional analysis is also based on the concept of political system.**
- ✅ It is **more widely used in the sphere of comparative politics because it provides for standard categories for different types of political systems**.
- ✅ Origin in **social anthropology** (**Radcliffe-Brown and B. Malinowski**), developed in **sociology** (**Talcott Parsons, Robert Merton and Marion Levy**), turned into a tool by **Gabriel Almond and his associates**.
- ✅ **Almond and J.S. Coleman** (*The Politics of the Developing Areas*; **1960**), characteristic (a): **all political systems have political structures**.
- 🔑 Characteristic (b): **the same functions are performed in all political systems with different frequencies and by different kinds of structures**.
- ✅ Characteristic (c): **all political structures are multi-functional**.
- ⚠️ Characteristic (d): **all political systems are 'mixed' systems in the cultural sense**, based in a culture **always a mixture of the modern and the traditional**.
- ✅ **Almond and G.B. Powell** (*Comparative Politics: A Developmental Approach*; **1966**): systems must perform **a specific set of tasks** to remain **'ongoing systems'** — their **functional requirements**.
- 🔑 The modification: Easton's **inputs** and **outputs** are best understood as **'functions' or 'functional requisites' of political system**.
- 🔑 Functions may be performed **even by structures which are not overtly recognized as being, primarily, 'political'**.
- ✅ Input functions: **political socialization and recruitment** (family, school, primary groups); **interest articulation** (interest groups); **interest aggregation** (political parties); **political communication** (mass media).
- ✅ Output functions: **rule-making** (legislature), **rule-application** (executive), **rule-adjudication** (judiciary) — these **correspond to conventional governmental functions**.
- 🔑 **Articulation** converts **opinions, attitudes, beliefs, preferences, etc.** into **coherent demands**; **aggregation** collates **divergent interests** into demands of **a very large section of society**, **policy proposals and programmes of action**.
- ⚠️ **Although all structures are multi-functional, yet some structures are especially suited for specific functions** — aptitude, not assignment.

### Concept check

**Question:** State precisely how Almond modifies Easton, and show what the modification makes possible that Easton's model could not do.

**Model answer:** The modification is a reinterpretation of category, not a change of content. ✅ Almond and Powell suggest that **'inputs' and 'outputs' recognized by Easton can best be understood as 'functions' or 'functional requisites' of political system**, grounded in the premise that all systems **must perform a specific set of tasks if they are to remain in existence as systems in working order or in equilibrium, i.e. as 'ongoing systems'**. ⚠️ The shift is from **flow** to **task**. ⚠️ In Easton's model demands and supports simply arrive and outputs simply emerge; the vocabulary describes movement through an abstract system and attributes the movement to no one. ⚠️ Recast as functions, the same items become things that **have to be done**, and a task that must be done admits the further question: **by what?** ⚠️ That question is what Almond's model adds, and it is answered by characteristic (b), that **the same functions are performed in all political systems with different frequencies and by different kinds of structures**. ⚠️ What this makes possible is comparison between polities with no institutions in common, since the functional grid is constant while the structural answers vary — which is why the chapter says the model is **more widely used in the sphere of comparative politics** because it **provides for standard categories for different types of political systems**. ⚠️ It also makes possible the analysis of informal and non-political structures, because Almond concedes that functions may be performed **even by structures which are not overtly recognized as being, primarily, 'political'**. 🔑 And it partly answers the criticism of Lesson 27, since a question about who performs interest articulation is at least adjacent to a question about power, which Easton's abstraction foreclosed entirely.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the structure column as a set of assignments. ✅ The chapter's qualifier is that **although all structures are multi-functional, yet some structures are especially suited for specific functions**, and Almond's whole point is that the same function is performed by **different kinds of structures** in different systems. ❌ Writing "in Almond's model, parties perform interest aggregation" as a universal claim contradicts the model. ❌ And do not drop characteristic (d) — **all political systems are 'mixed' systems in the cultural sense** — because it is the model's own denial of the modern/traditional dichotomy that Lesson 29's critics accuse it of importing.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Distinguish interest articulation from interest aggregation. *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** Both are input functions in Almond's framework, and they differ in scale and in what they do to interests. **Interest articulation** is **the processes whereby opinions, attitudes, beliefs, preferences, etc. are converted into coherent demands on the political system**; its best-suited structure is the **interest group**, and its work is to give a single want a sayable form. **Interest aggregation** is **the process whereby various divergent interests are collated and translated into concrete demands of a very large section of society (preferably appealing to all sections of society), policy proposals and programmes of action**; its best-suited structure is the **political party**. ⚠️ So articulation makes one interest expressible, while aggregation makes many interests compatible and converts them into a programme. ⚠️ The parenthesis matters: aggregation works by building breadth, which is why narrow interests may be articulated successfully and lost at aggregation.

**Rubric [10]:** articulation defined in the chapter's terms **3** · aggregation defined with its threefold output **3** · the correct best-suited structure for each **2** · the scale-and-combination contrast or the breadth consequence **2**.
