## Lesson 29 — Structural-functional analysis II: development and criticism

Progress: 29 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part V — models of political analysis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§15 and 16 with the Advanced companion's §§6, 7 and 8, read against the chapter's closing treatment of structural-functional analysis (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.128-129 = printed 113-114), which supplies the developed/less-developed contrast, Almond and Powell's two criteria of political development with both quoted definitions, and the three criticisms with the chapter's closing concession.
CA search: "political development structural differentiation secularization of culture 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried. ❌ No post-2009 development index or ranking is imported.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The comparative payoff

✅ **The structural-functional framework of political analysis has been particularly found useful for comparative politics.**

✅ The contrast it draws:

| Developed political system | Less developed political system |
|---|---|
| ✅ **characterized by differentiation of structures for the performance of specific functions** | ✅ functions of **interest articulation, interest aggregation and political communication might be performed by some structures which have not taken a definite shape** |
| ✅ **growth of interest groups, political parties and mass media would be clearly discernible** | ⚠️ Those structures are not separately discernible |

⚠️ The extraction prints **articulaion**, normalised and catalogued.

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note exactly which three functions are named** as the diagnostic ones: **interest articulation, interest aggregation and political communication**. ⚠️ All three are **input** functions. ⚠️ The output functions — rule-making, application, adjudication — are not used as the test, presumably because every system has some organ doing them. 🔑 Differentiation is diagnosed on the input side, which is also the side Almond said he had **paid special attention** to.

### 2. The two criteria of political development

✅ **Almond and Powell have identified two chief characteristics of development of political system, that is 'political development'. These are: (a) structural differentiation; and (b) secularization of culture.**

**(a) Structural differentiation**, in their own words:

> A principal aspect of the development or transformation of the political system is. . . role differentiation, or structural differentiation. By 'differentiation' we refer to the processes whereby roles change and become more specialized or more autonomous or whereby new types of roles are established or new structures and sub-systems emerge or are created.
>
> — Almond and Powell, *Comparative Politics: A Developmental Approach*; 1966, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.129 (printed 114)

⚠️ Four processes are listed, joined by **or**: roles become **more specialized**, or **more autonomous**, or **new types of roles are established**, or **new structures and sub-systems emerge or are created**. ⚠️ Any one of them counts.

**(b) Secularization of culture.** ✅ First the context: **the underlying propensities of a political system, representing its psychological dimension, have been described as political culture.** ✅ **Secularization of culture is concerned with this aspect of political system.** And then:

> The secularization of culture is the process whereby traditional orientations and attitudes give way to more dynamic decision-making processes involving the gathering of information, the evaluation of information, the laying out of alternative courses of action, the selection of a course of action from among these possible courses, and the means whereby one tests whether or not a given course of action is producing the consequences which were intended.
>
> — Almond and Powell, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.129 (printed 114)

🔑 ⚠️ ⚠️ **Read what secularization actually means here, because the word is badly misleading.** ⚠️ It has **nothing to do with religion and the state**. ⚠️ It is defined entirely as a **decision-making** property, and the definition contains a five-step procedure: **gathering of information** → **evaluation of information** → **laying out of alternative courses of action** → **selection of a course** → **testing whether the course is producing the consequences which were intended**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **That five-step list is exactly a rational decision cycle with a feedback test at the end** — and it is therefore the same logic as Easton's feedback at Lesson 27 and Deutsch's cybernetics at Lesson 30, now relocated into **culture**. 🔑 Almond's measure of political development is, in substance, the extent to which a political culture behaves like a well-functioning feedback system.

➡️ **Boundary.** ❌ Secularism as a doctrine about religion and the state is a different concept entirely and belongs to the Philosophy **Humanism, Secularism and Multiculturalism** owner. ❌ Do not let the shared word create a false link.

✅ **These two criteria would enable us to measure the level of development of any given political system.**

### 3. The three criticisms

✅ **Structural-functional analysis has been criticized on various grounds.**

| | Criticism | The chapter's wording |
|---|---|---|
| **Firstly** | Conservative bias | ✅ **it is alleged that this form of analysis is primarily concerned with system's survival; hence it is ideologically inclined towards conservatism** |
| **Secondly** | Power | ✅ **it is not suited to analysis of power relations in society** |
| **Finally** | Western benchmark | ✅ **it projects Western-type liberal-democratic system as a standard for institution-building in developing societies rather than encouraging them to build their institutions according to their own genius and specific requirements** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **Criticism one is an inference from the model's premise, and it is a fair one.** Almond's starting point is that systems **must perform a specific set of tasks if they are to remain in existence as systems in working order or in equilibrium** — the analytical question is therefore always "what keeps this going?", and a framework that only asks that question has no vocabulary for asking whether it should keep going.

⚠️ ⚠️ **Criticism two repeats the charge against systems analysis at Lesson 27** — unsurprising, since the chapter says this model is **based on the concept of political system** and that Easton's model **served as a basis for** it. 🔑 The power blindness is inherited, not independently acquired.

⚠️ ⚠️ **Criticism three is the one with an internal defence available**, and this is the lesson's sharpest point. ✅ Almond and Coleman's fourth characteristic holds that **all political systems are 'mixed' systems in the cultural sense**, based in a culture **always a mixture of the modern and the traditional** — which denies that any system is purely modern and therefore denies that any can serve as the finished template. ⚠️ So the Western-benchmark charge, if sound, convicts the model's **practitioners** of departing from the model's own stated premise. ⚠️ The chapter does not make this defence; it is a reconstruction and must be labelled. ⚠️ Nor does it settle the matter: the **structural differentiation** criterion is so phrased that the systems scoring highest on it are, in practice, those with the institutional profile the criticism names.

### 4. The concession

✅ **In any case, structural-functional analysis signifies a significant advance in the sphere of political analysis.**

⚠️ The chapter's standard shape again — criticism, then a concession that keeps the model in use.

### 5. The visual

```text
   DIAGNOSING DEVELOPMENT -- the three INPUT functions are the test

   LESS DEVELOPED                     DEVELOPED
   interest ARTICULATION  ........>   INTEREST GROUPS discernible
   interest AGGREGATION   ........>   POLITICAL PARTIES discernible
   political COMMUNICATION .......>   MASS MEDIA discernible
   "performed by some structures      "DIFFERENTIATION OF
    which have NOT TAKEN A             STRUCTURES for the
    DEFINITE SHAPE"                    performance of SPECIFIC
                                       FUNCTIONS"

   THE TWO CRITERIA OF 'POLITICAL DEVELOPMENT'
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   (a) STRUCTURAL DIFFERENTIATION -- any ONE of four processes:
         roles become MORE SPECIALIZED
         roles become MORE AUTONOMOUS
         NEW TYPES OF ROLES established
         NEW STRUCTURES and SUB-SYSTEMS emerge or are created

   (b) SECULARIZATION OF CULTURE
       (!!) NOTHING TO DO WITH RELIGION AND THE STATE
       "traditional orientations and attitudes GIVE WAY to more
        DYNAMIC DECISION-MAKING PROCESSES involving:"
            1. GATHERING of information
            2. EVALUATION of information
            3. LAYING OUT of alternative courses of action
            4. SELECTION of a course from among these
            5. "the means whereby one TESTS whether or not a given
                course of action is producing the CONSEQUENCES
                WHICH WERE INTENDED"
       >>> step 5 is a FEEDBACK TEST. This is Easton's feedback
           and Deutsch's cybernetics, relocated into CULTURE.

   THE THREE CRITICISMS
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   1  CONSERVATISM : "primarily concerned with SYSTEM'S SURVIVAL;
                      hence IDEOLOGICALLY INCLINED TOWARDS
                      CONSERVATISM"
                      ^ follows from the premise that systems must
                        perform tasks to remain 'ONGOING SYSTEMS'
   2  POWER        : "NOT SUITED to analysis of POWER RELATIONS in
                      society"
                      ^ INHERITED from systems analysis (L27)
   3  WESTERN      : "projects WESTERN-TYPE LIBERAL-DEMOCRATIC
      BENCHMARK      SYSTEM as a STANDARD for institution-building
                      in developing societies RATHER THAN
                      encouraging them to build their institutions
                      ACCORDING TO THEIR OWN GENIUS and SPECIFIC
                      REQUIREMENTS"
                      ^ (!) but characteristic (d) says ALL systems
                        are 'MIXED' -- so the charge, if sound,
                        convicts the PRACTITIONERS of departing
                        from the MODEL'S OWN PREMISE
                        [reconstruction -- not the chapter's]

   CONCESSION: "a SIGNIFICANT ADVANCE in the sphere of political
                analysis"
```

*Caption: the five numbered steps under secularization are the most under-used quotation in Part V, and the double-flag above them is the correction that prevents the commonest error in this topic — reading "secularization of culture" as a claim about religion.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ The framework **has been particularly found useful for comparative politics**.
- ✅ A developed system shows **differentiation of structures for the performance of specific functions**.
- ✅ In it, **growth of interest groups, political parties and mass media would be clearly discernible**.
- ✅ In less developed systems **interest articulation, interest aggregation and political communication might be performed by some structures which have not taken a definite shape**.
- ⚠️ All three diagnostic functions are **input** functions, not output ones.
- ✅ Two criteria of **political development**: **(a) structural differentiation; and (b) secularization of culture**.
- ✅ **Differentiation**: **roles change and become more specialized or more autonomous or … new types of roles are established or new structures and sub-systems emerge or are created** — any one suffices.
- ✅ **Political culture** = **the underlying propensities of a political system, representing its psychological dimension**.
- 🔑 **Secularization of culture** = **traditional orientations and attitudes give way to more dynamic decision-making processes** involving **gathering**, **evaluation**, **laying out of alternative courses of action**, **selection**, and the test of **whether or not a given course of action is producing the consequences which were intended**. ❌ Not about religion; ➡️ secularism belongs to the Humanism, Secularism and Multiculturalism owner.
- ✅ **These two criteria would enable us to measure the level of development of any given political system.**
- ✅ Criticism one: **primarily concerned with system's survival; hence it is ideologically inclined towards conservatism**.
- ✅ Criticism two: **not suited to analysis of power relations in society** — inherited from systems analysis.
- ✅ Criticism three: it **projects Western-type liberal-democratic system as a standard … rather than encouraging them to build their institutions according to their own genius and specific requirements**.
- ⚠️ Reconstruction: characteristic (d) — **all political systems are 'mixed'** — gives the model an internal defence against the third criticism.
- ✅ Concession: **structural-functional analysis signifies a significant advance in the sphere of political analysis**.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Secularization of culture" is Almond's second criterion of political development. Explain what it means here, why the term is misleading, and what the definition reveals about the model's underlying picture of a developed polity.

**Model answer:** It means a change in how decisions are made, not a change in the place of religion. ✅ The definition is that **traditional orientations and attitudes give way to more dynamic decision-making processes**, specified as **the gathering of information, the evaluation of information, the laying out of alternative courses of action, the selection of a course of action from among these possible courses, and the means whereby one tests whether or not a given course of action is producing the consequences which were intended**. ⚠️ Nothing in that sentence concerns belief, worship, or the relation between religious authority and the state; the contrast is between **traditional orientations** — understood as fixed dispositions — and procedures that are responsive to evidence. ⚠️ The term misleads because **secularization** carries a settled meaning in political theory that is wholly absent here, and importing it produces a serious category error, since the question of secularism belongs to a different owner entirely. ⚠️ What the definition reveals is that the model's picture of a developed polity is a **feedback system**. ⚠️ The five steps are a rational decision cycle and the fifth is explicitly a test of outcomes against intentions — which is Easton's feedback mechanism, that **apprises the political system of the position of its goal, its own distance from it, and the changes in this distance brought about by its own performance**, now predicated of a **culture** rather than of a structure. ⚠️ So the two criteria of development turn out to be complementary: **structural differentiation** supplies specialised organs, and **secularization** supplies a culture capable of using the information they generate. 🔑 It also shows why the model is open to the conservatism charge, since the whole apparatus is tuned to how well a system maintains and corrects itself, with no criterion for whether what it maintains is worth maintaining.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not connect this criterion to secularism as a doctrine about religion and the state. ✅ The chapter's definition is exhaustively about decision-making processes. ➡️ Secularism belongs to the Philosophy Humanism, Secularism and Multiculturalism owner, and the shared word creates no link. ❌ Do not present the Western-benchmark criticism as a view the chapter endorses without qualification either; the chapter reports it as an allegation among **various grounds** and closes by calling the model **a significant advance**, while the model's own fourth characteristic — **all political systems are 'mixed' systems in the cultural sense** — cuts against it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does structural-functional analysis measure political development, and on what grounds is it criticised? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** By two criteria. **Structural differentiation** covers the processes **whereby roles change and become more specialized or more autonomous or whereby new types of roles are established or new structures and sub-systems emerge or are created**; a developed system therefore shows **growth of interest groups, political parties and mass media … clearly discernible**. **Secularization of culture** is the process whereby **traditional orientations and attitudes give way to more dynamic decision-making processes** — gathering and evaluating information, laying out alternatives, selecting, and testing **whether or not a given course of action is producing the consequences which were intended**. ❌ It is not about religion. ⚠️ Three criticisms follow: the analysis is **primarily concerned with system's survival; hence … ideologically inclined towards conservatism**; it is **not suited to analysis of power relations in society**; and it **projects Western-type liberal-democratic system as a standard**.

**Rubric [10]:** structural differentiation with at least two of its four processes **2** · secularization defined as decision-making with at least three steps **3** · the explicit denial that it concerns religion **1** · the three criticisms **3** · the concession that it is a significant advance **1**.

## Lesson 30 — Communications theory: government as steering

Progress: 30 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part V — models of political analysis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §15 with the Advanced companion's §§5, 6 and 7, read against the chapter's section headed "Communications Theory" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.129-131 = printed 114-116), which supplies Deutsch's work and date, the nerves metaphor, the definition of cybernetics, the steering formulation, the network and self-regulation claims, the Davies and Lewis quotation, the three contexts and six components of analysis, Charlesworth's two quotations on distortion, and the stated uses and limits.
CA search: "Karl Deutsch nerves of government cybernetics political analysis 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Deutsch and the borrowed science

✅ **Communications theory represents another model of political analysis derived from the concept of political system.** ✅ **Karl Deutsch (The Nerves of Government: Models of Political Communication and Control; 1963), is the chief exponent of this approach.**

✅ What he borrowed: **Deutsch sought to apply the concepts and methods of modern information technology as well as physiology of nervous system to an analysis of the political system.** ✅ **He particularly introduced the techniques of cybernetics to the sphere of political analysis.**

✅ The definition: **Cybernetics is the study of the operation of control and communication systems; it deals both with biological systems and man-made machinery.**

⚠️ ⚠️ Note the dual reference — **biological systems and man-made machinery** — which is why the model can use a nerve metaphor and an engineering metaphor without inconsistency.

✅ And the image that gives the book its title: **Deutsch declared that his work was concerned less with the bones or muscles of the body-politic than with its nerves—its channels of communications and decision.**

🔑 ⚠️ **The three-term anatomy is the model's whole claim in one image.** **Bones** are structure — the institutional approach's subject. **Muscles** are force — power, the Marxist and realist subject. **Nerves** are information. ⚠️ Deutsch says the third has been neglected, and that is his entire case.

### 2. The central reorientation

✅ **Communications theory regards the function of communication as the centre of all political activity.** ✅ Therefore **an analysis of communications flowing from and flowing into political system would … be very helpful in the description, classification, analysis and explanation of the important aspects of political life.**

✅ The reorientation, in the chapter's own words:

> Deutsch argued that it might be profitable to look upon government somewhat less as a problem of power and somewhat more as a problem of steering, i.e. directing the course of its activity which is the main function of communication.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.130 (printed 115)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note the hedges: somewhat less … somewhat more … might be profitable.** ⚠️ Deutsch is proposing a shift of emphasis, not denying that power exists. ⚠️ An answer that reports him as replacing power with communication overstates him, and the chapter's own limitation clause at §6 below shows why the hedge was wise.

✅ The consequences: **he, therefore, regards political system as a 'network of communication channels'.** ✅ **It is largely a 'self-regulating' or 'self-controlling' system which evolves its own processes and mechanisms for the acquisition, collection, transmission, selection and the storage of information.**

⚠️ Five operations on information are named: **acquisition, collection, transmission, selection and the storage**.

### 3. Davies and Lewis on cohesion

✅ **Morton R. Davies and Vaughan A. Lewis** (*Models of Political Systems*; 1971):

> The members of the political system come to acquire mechanisms for the transmission of messages and for the coordination and control of the channels of communication. The cohesion of a political system can be analysed in terms of the degree to which these coordination and control mechanisms continue to function properly—to adapt themselves, in the context of the goals which they set, to the information which they receive from various sources; and even to modify the goals which they have set themselves.
>
> — Davies and Lewis, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.130 (printed 115)

🔑 ⚠️ **The final clause is the strongest thing in this lesson.** The mechanisms adapt **to the information which they receive**, and **even … modify the goals which they have set themselves**. ⚠️ That is **second-order** learning — not merely correcting course toward a fixed goal, but revising the goal in the light of what is learnt. ⚠️ Easton's feedback at Lesson 27 only closed the gap to a given goal; here the goal itself is in play. 🔑 Saying precisely that is how to distinguish Deutsch from Easton in one sentence.

### 4. What is actually analysed

✅ **The problem of communication may be studied in three contexts:**
- ✅ **(a) communication within the political system**
- ✅ **(b) communication between political system and its environment**
- ✅ **(c) communication between two or more political systems**

✅ And its analysis involves **several components, including**:

| | Component |
|---|---|
| **(a)** | ✅ **the structures meant for sending and receiving messages** |
| **(b)** | ✅ **the channels used for the purpose of communication (along with their capacities and rates of utilization, expressed in terms of their load and load capacity, rate of flow, amount of lag and gain, i.e. delay or promptness in responding to the information that is received)** |
| **(c)** | ✅ **processes of storage of information** |
| **(d)** | ✅ **feedback mechanisms** |
| **(e)** | ✅ **the codes and languages applied for the purpose of communication** |
| **(f)** | ✅ **the contents of the messages transmitted** |

🔑 ⚠️ **Component (b) is where the model earns its keep**, because it supplies measurable quantities: **load**, **load capacity**, **rate of flow**, **lag** and **gain** — with the chapter glossing the last pair as **delay or promptness in responding to the information that is received**. ⚠️ Note that this is the **Quantification** tenet from Lesson 21 finally finding a political object it can measure.

### 5. Distortion

✅ **Communication is by no means a smooth process. We must be very careful in detecting distortions.** ✅ **James C. Charlesworth** (*Contemporary Political Analysis*; 1967):

> The exponents of the communicational approach to political science emphasize the importance of distinguishing between what goes out from the source and what is exactly received at the other end. Which shall we examine—what a governmental agency says it says, or what the recipient says he hears?
>
> — Charlesworth, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.130 (printed 115)

✅ And his second remark, with its striking list:

> In order to correct the distortion we must study the determinants of distortion. Hence, communications is a legitimate methodological segment of political science. This study is unfortunately not well developed, nor is it standardized or unified. If it were, we should have fewer industrial strikes, slum riots, university sit-ins, and crackpot picket lines.
>
> — Charlesworth, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.130-131 (printed 115-116)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Handle the second quotation carefully.** 📰 The four phenomena — **industrial strikes, slum riots, university sit-ins, and crackpot picket lines** — are of their period and its idiom; **crackpot** is evaluative. ⚠️ More importantly, the claim that better communication study would reduce them attributes social conflict to **distortion** rather than to conflicting interests, which is exactly what the model's critics say it does. ⚠️ Quote it to show what the approach claims for itself; ❌ do not endorse it.

### 6. Uses and limits

✅ **In political science this approach is particularly useful for an analysis of the processes of bargaining, conflict-resolution, decision-making, evaluation of policies, estimating the impact of publicity and propaganda as well as for understanding the dynamics of international relations.**

✅ **However, this approach is hardly useful in the analysis of power structure of society, ideologies, allocation of resources, or in explaining the phenomena of violence and revolution, etc.**

✅ The verdict: **Communications theory can, therefore, be commended for application only in a limited sphere of political science.**

🔑 ⚠️ **This is the harshest verdict the chapter passes on any of the five models** — **only in a limited sphere** — and the list of exclusions is long: **power structure**, **ideologies**, **allocation of resources**, **violence and revolution**.

⚠️ ⚠️ And the exclusions are precisely what Deutsch's own metaphor predicted. He proposed to study **nerves** rather than **bones or muscles**; the criticism is that bones and muscles turn out to matter. 🔑 The limitation is not an accident of execution but the exact shape of the chosen object.

### 7. The visual

```text
   DEUTSCH'S ANATOMY OF THE BODY-POLITIC

        BONES  = structure .......... the INSTITUTIONAL approach
        MUSCLES = force ............. POWER; the Marxist subject
        NERVES  = "its CHANNELS OF COMMUNICATIONS AND DECISION"
                                      <<< DEUTSCH STUDIES THESE

   "look upon government SOMEWHAT LESS as a problem of POWER and
    SOMEWHAT MORE as a problem of STEERING"
        ^^ hedged. NOT a denial that power exists.

   WHAT GETS MEASURED -- the model's real contribution
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   (a) STRUCTURES for sending and receiving messages
   (b) CHANNELS -- and here come the QUANTITIES:
          LOAD | LOAD CAPACITY | RATE OF FLOW |
          LAG and GAIN = "DELAY or PROMPTNESS in responding to the
                          information that is received"
   (c) STORAGE of information
   (d) FEEDBACK mechanisms
   (e) CODES and LANGUAGES
   (f) CONTENTS of the messages

   THREE CONTEXTS: within the system | system <-> environment |
                   between two or more systems

   EASTON'S FEEDBACK vs DEUTSCH -- the one-sentence difference
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   EASTON  : feedback closes the gap to a GIVEN goal
   DEUTSCH : mechanisms "ADAPT THEMSELVES, in the context of the
             goals which they set, to the information which they
             receive ... AND EVEN TO MODIFY THE GOALS WHICH THEY
             HAVE SET THEMSELVES"      (Davies and Lewis, 1971)
             >>> SECOND-ORDER learning. The goal itself is in play.

   CHARLESWORTH ON DISTORTION
   "what a governmental agency SAYS IT SAYS, or what the RECIPIENT
    SAYS HE HEARS?"
   (!) and his claim that better communication study would mean
       "fewer INDUSTRIAL STRIKES, SLUM RIOTS, UNIVERSITY SIT-INS,
        and CRACKPOT PICKET LINES" -- period idiom, evaluative, and
       it attributes CONFLICT to DISTORTION. Quote; do not endorse.

   VERDICT -- the harshest in Part V
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   USEFUL FOR : bargaining | conflict-resolution | decision-making
                | evaluation of policies | impact of PUBLICITY and
                PROPAGANDA | dynamics of INTERNATIONAL RELATIONS
   HARDLY USEFUL FOR : POWER STRUCTURE of society | IDEOLOGIES |
                ALLOCATION OF RESOURCES | VIOLENCE and REVOLUTION
   >>> "commended for application ONLY IN A LIMITED SPHERE of
        political science"
        ^^ exactly the BONES and MUSCLES he set aside.
```

*Caption: the anatomy at the top and the verdict at the bottom are the same point, eight pages apart. Deutsch chose to study the nerves; the criticism is that the bones and muscles turned out to be where the politics was.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Communications theory represents another model of political analysis derived from the concept of political system.**
- ✅ **Karl Deutsch**, ***The Nerves of Government: Models of Political Communication and Control***; **1963**, **is the chief exponent of this approach**.
- ✅ He applied **modern information technology as well as physiology of nervous system** and introduced **the techniques of cybernetics**.
- ✅ **Cybernetics is the study of the operation of control and communication systems; it deals both with biological systems and man-made machinery.**
- 🔑 His work was **concerned less with the bones or muscles of the body-politic than with its nerves—its channels of communications and decision**.
- ✅ The theory **regards the function of communication as the centre of all political activity**.
- ✅ Government is to be seen **somewhat less as a problem of power and somewhat more as a problem of steering** — ⚠️ hedged three times.
- ✅ The political system is a **'network of communication channels'**, **largely 'self-regulating' or 'self-controlling'**, handling **acquisition, collection, transmission, selection and the storage of information**.
- 🔑 **Davies and Lewis** (*Models of Political Systems*; 1971): mechanisms **adapt themselves … to the information which they receive from various sources; and even to modify the goals which they have set themselves** — ⚠️ second-order learning, beyond Easton's feedback.
- ✅ Three contexts: **within**, **between system and environment**, **between two or more political systems**.
- ✅ Six components, including channels with **load and load capacity, rate of flow, amount of lag and gain, i.e. delay or promptness**.
- ✅ **Charlesworth** (*Contemporary Political Analysis*; 1967): distinguish **what a governmental agency says it says, or what the recipient says he hears?**
- 📰 ❌ His claim about **industrial strikes, slum riots, university sit-ins, and crackpot picket lines** is of its period and evaluative — quote, do not endorse.
- ✅ Useful for **bargaining, conflict-resolution, decision-making, evaluation of policies, estimating the impact of publicity and propaganda** and **the dynamics of international relations**.
- 🔑 **Hardly useful in the analysis of power structure of society, ideologies, allocation of resources, or in explaining the phenomena of violence and revolution**; commended **only in a limited sphere of political science**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Deutsch's model receives the chapter's harshest verdict. Show that the limitation follows from his own founding metaphor, and say what the model nonetheless adds that neither Easton nor Almond supplies.

**Model answer:** The limitation is the metaphor read back. ✅ Deutsch declared his work **concerned less with the bones or muscles of the body-politic than with its nerves—its channels of communications and decision**, and proposed to see government **somewhat less as a problem of power and somewhat more as a problem of steering**. ⚠️ **Bones** stand for structure and **muscles** for force, so the metaphor announces in advance that structure and force are being set aside. ✅ The chapter's verdict then names exactly those: the approach is **hardly useful in the analysis of power structure of society, ideologies, allocation of resources, or in explaining the phenomena of violence and revolution**, and is commended **only in a limited sphere of political science**. ⚠️ Power is muscle, resource allocation is a contest over substance rather than signal, and revolution is what happens when the muscles overpower the nerves — so each exclusion is the metaphor's own prediction. ⚠️ What the model nonetheless adds is twofold. ⚠️ First, **measurability**: component (b) supplies **load and load capacity, rate of flow, amount of lag and gain, i.e. delay or promptness in responding to the information that is received**, which gives the Quantification tenet of Lesson 21 a genuine political object, where Easton's demands and supports resist measurement and Almond's functions are categorical. ⚠️ Second, and more fundamentally, **goal revision**: Davies and Lewis state that the mechanisms adapt **to the information which they receive** and **even … modify the goals which they have set themselves**. 🔑 Easton's feedback measures distance from a fixed goal and Almond's functional requisites are fixed by what keeps a system ongoing; only Deutsch's model has a place for a polity that learns what it should be aiming at. ⚠️ That is a real addition, and it survives the limitation, because learning what to want is not the same as having the power to get it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not report Deutsch as replacing power with communication. ✅ His formulation is hedged twice over — **somewhat less** a problem of power, **somewhat more** a problem of steering, and only that it **might be profitable** to see it so. ❌ And do not reproduce Charlesworth's claim that better communication study would mean **fewer industrial strikes, slum riots, university sit-ins, and crackpot picket lines** as the chapter's view of social conflict; 📰 the list is of its period, **crackpot** is evaluative, and the claim presupposes that conflict is a distortion problem — which is precisely the assumption the model's critics reject.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What does cybernetics add to political analysis, and where does it fall short? *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** It adds a measurable object and a learning polity. **Karl Deutsch** (*The Nerves of Government*; **1963**) applied **the techniques of cybernetics** — **the study of the operation of control and communication systems** — and was **concerned less with the bones or muscles of the body-politic than with its nerves**, urging that government be seen **somewhat less as a problem of power and somewhat more as a problem of steering**. ⚠️ Its measurable terms are real: channels carry **load and load capacity, rate of flow, amount of lag and gain, i.e. delay or promptness**. ⚠️ Davies and Lewis add second-order learning, since mechanisms adapt and **even … modify the goals which they have set themselves**. ⚠️ It falls short exactly where the metaphor predicted: it is **hardly useful in the analysis of power structure of society, ideologies, allocation of resources, or in explaining the phenomena of violence and revolution**.

**Rubric [10]:** Deutsch with work, year and the definition of cybernetics **3** · the nerves/bones-and-muscles metaphor **2** · the measurable channel terms **2** · the goal-revision addition **1** · the four stated exclusions **2**.

## Lesson 31 — Decision-making analysis

Progress: 31 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part V — models of political analysis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §15 with the Advanced companion's §§5, 6 and 9, read against the chapter's section headed "Decision-Making Analysis" (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.131-132 = printed 116-117), which supplies the model's relation to Easton's outputs, the breadth of decision-making beyond politics, Van Dyke's illustration of actors as decision-makers, the seven lettered elements of analysis, the rationality assumption with its departure clause, the three named sub-models with their exponents, the interdisciplinary character and the extensions of scope.
CA search: "decision making analysis political science game theory 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Where it attaches

✅ **Decision-making analysis denotes an attempt to understand politics as a process of arriving at decisions.**

✅ Its link to the first model: **It will be recalled that David Easton's original model of political system treats decisions and actions as outputs of the political system. In this sense, this approach is closely related to the concept of political system.**

🔑 ⚠️ **State the relation precisely: this model takes Easton's output box and opens it.** ⚠️ Easton's system produced **policies and decisions** through a conversion process described only as **special processes**; decision-making analysis is the account of what happens inside that box.

### 2. The scope problem, and its solution

✅ **Decision-making is a wider phenomenon.** ✅ **Exercise of any form of authority involves decision-making whether it is in the sphere of business, university-administration or family.**

✅ The restriction: **In political science, however, we are concerned with those areas of decision-making which affect politics.** ✅ **In other words, here we are concerned with the process of arriving at public decisions.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Public** is the filter, and it is the same filter Topic 07's chapter applied to politics generally. ➡️ The theory of what makes an issue public belongs there.

✅ **However, the scope of decision-making in political sphere itself is very wide.** ✅ **Vernon Van Dyke** illustrates:

> Every actor is a decision-maker. Those acting for political parties decide which candidate to nominate. Voters decide whether to vote and for whom. Legislators decide which proposals to advance or support. Executives decide what legislation to seek, whether to sign or veto acts of the legislative body, precisely which steps to take in executing or administering the law, and what policies to pursue where action is left to their discretion.
>
> — Van Dyke, *Political Science: A Philosophical Analysis*; 1960, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.131 (printed 116)

🔑 ⚠️ **Note that voters appear in this list.** ⚠️ At Lesson 27 systems analysis was faulted for an **inability in the analysis of … mass political behaviour, such as voting**. ⚠️ Decision-making analysis recovers the voter by redescribing voting as a decision — **voters decide whether to vote and for whom**. 🔑 The fourth model repairs the first model's most conspicuous gap, and the chapter does not say so.

### 3. The seven elements

✅ **Decision-making analysis is concerned with analysis of political systems, processes and behaviour in terms of their decision-making mechanism and its functioning.** ✅ **It broadly involves the following:**

| | Element |
|---|---|
| **(a)** | ✅ **identification of the issues on which decisions are made** |
| **(b)** | ✅ **the structures involved in decision-making** |
| **(c)** | ✅ **the actors involved in decision-making (this may involve study of personality, if necessary)** |
| **(d)** | ✅ **the alternative courses of action or options that were considered before making a choice** |
| **(e)** | ✅ **the factors influencing the choice of the decision-makers, i.e. their range of preferences vis-a-vis the utilities attached to each of the alternatives** |
| **(f)** | ✅ **any external factors, pressures or constraints which influenced their decision** |
| **(g)** | ✅ **the outcome of the decision including its political costs** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **Three of these are distinctive and worth flagging.**
- ⚠️ **(c)** admits **study of personality, if necessary** — ⚠️ a licence no other model in this chapter grants. The individual returns as an individual, not as an abstract element.
- ⚠️ **(d)** requires the **alternatives that were considered** — ⚠️ which means reconstructing options that were **not** taken, a far harder evidential task than describing what happened.
- ⚠️ **(g)** includes **political costs** — ⚠️ so the model's account of an outcome is not confined to the policy's effects but includes what the decision cost the decider.

### 4. The rationality assumption and its exit clause

✅ **Under normal circumstances, it is assumed that the decision-makers are capable of rational calculation (i.e. cost-benefit analysis) and that they are fully conscious of their goals so that they select the most efficient or feasible means of attaining these goals.**

✅ And then the exit: **However, if there is any departure from this course, e.g. if a decision is a product of routinized behaviour or irrational attitude on the part of some actors, social-psychological reasons thereof must be investigated and recorded.**

🔑 ⚠️ **This is the chapter's most sophisticated methodological passage, and the structure deserves to be named.** ⚠️ Rationality is adopted as a **default**, not as a claim about human beings. ⚠️ Departures are expected, and when they occur the model does not fail — it **switches to a different explanatory register**, the **social-psychological**. ⚠️ So the rationality assumption functions as a **baseline against which deviation becomes visible and interesting**, which is exactly how a well-constructed model should use an idealisation.

⚠️ ⚠️ And note that this directly answers Wallas from Lesson 17. ✅ Wallas's objection was to treating man as **a rational creature following his self-interest**; here rationality is assumed provisionally and its failures are routed to psychology — the discipline Wallas wanted brought in. 🔑 The model has internalised the critique that founded behaviouralism.

### 5. The three sub-models

✅ **Decision-making analysis does not conform to a single pattern. It may follow one of the several prevalent models. Some important models are as follows:**

| | Sub-model | Named exponents |
|---|---|---|
| **(a)** | ✅ **models dealing with the identification of variables of stages involved in the decision-making processes** | ✅ **Richard Snyder and Harold Lasswell** |
| **(b)** | ✅ **models based on analogies between economic and political decision-making involving notions of maximizing advantage from the decision** | ✅ **Anthony Downs, J.M. Buchanan and G. Tullock** |
| **(c)** | ✅ **game theory which involves quantification of the results of decision strategies in certain types of competitive situation, i.e. a situation of conflict or bargaining in which it is assumed that each player will seek his maximum advantage under conditions of rationality** | ⚠️ none named |

⚠️ ⚠️ The chapter prints the first name as **Richard Synder**. An independent check confirms the standard form is **Richard C. Snyder**; the chapter's printed form is recorded in the Source Ledger and the standard form used in this session's prose.

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note that Lasswell reappears here.** He was the exponent of power analysis and policy science at Lesson 19; now he is a named source of stage-models of decision. 🔑 The policy-science strand that Merriam began runs continuously through Lasswell into this model.

⚠️ Note also that sub-model (c) is the one place in the chapter where **quantification** is applied to **strategy** rather than to behaviour, and that it imports a rationality assumption explicitly: **each player will seek his maximum advantage under conditions of rationality**.

❌ **Boundary.** ❌ Downs's median-voter theorem, Buchanan and Tullock's *Calculus of Consent*, and the content of game theory are **not** in this chapter — only the names and a one-line characterisation. ➡️ Route substantive public-choice and game-theoretic content elsewhere.

### 6. Interdisciplinarity and extensions

✅ **Decision-making analysis essentially follows interdisciplinary approach by drawing substantially on psychology, sociology, administrative theory and organization theory.** ✅ **It also seeks to relate its findings to the work of other social scientists in the field of decision-making in non-political situations, with a view to evolving general theory of decision-making.**

✅ And the honest limit: **However, such general theory is not yet adequately developed.**

📰 ⚠️ **Not yet adequately developed** is a **2009** judgment. ❌ Do not assert it as the present state of the field.

✅ Three extensions of practice are recorded: some exponents **proceed to undertake studies of socio-economic background of decision-makers, e.g. legislators, administrators, ministers and even of voters**; **some scholars tend to go into their styles of decision-making also**; and **some of them focus on the role of communications in decision-making**.

⚠️ The third extension joins this model to Lesson 30 — communications study inside decision-making.

✅ Scope: **Decision-making analysis can be applied to a wide range of situations involving local, regional, national as well as international politics**, covering **voters, elites, functionaries of political parties, legislators, judges, government officials as well as officials of international organizations**, and **may adopt different models of analysis according to the needs of each situation**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **No criticism section.** ⚠️ Unlike systems analysis, structural-functional analysis and communications theory, the chapter supplies **no list of criticisms** for decision-making analysis — only the admission that a general theory is **not yet adequately developed**. 🔑 An answer that states this is being accurate about the source; ❌ inventing criticisms and attributing them to Gauba is not. ⚠️ The canonical owner's independent assessment — narrow focus, elite bias, neglect of structural forces — is available but belongs to Lesson 33's owner-extension material.

### 7. The visual

```text
   WHERE THIS MODEL SITS: INSIDE EASTON'S OUTPUT BOX

   DEMANDS  \                                 /  POLICIES
             >--> [ POLITICAL SYSTEM ] ------>     and
   SUPPORTS /       "special processes"       \  DECISIONS
                           |
                           v
            +-------------------------------+
            |  DECISION-MAKING ANALYSIS     |
            |  opens this box               |
            +-------------------------------+

   THE SEVEN ELEMENTS
   (a) the ISSUES on which decisions are made
   (b) the STRUCTURES involved
   (c) the ACTORS involved -- "(this may involve study of
       PERSONALITY, IF NECESSARY)"      <<< the individual returns
   (d) the ALTERNATIVE COURSES OF ACTION "that WERE CONSIDERED
       before making a choice"          <<< the roads not taken
   (e) the FACTORS influencing choice -- "their RANGE OF
       PREFERENCES vis-a-vis the UTILITIES attached to each"
   (f) EXTERNAL factors, pressures or constraints
   (g) the OUTCOME "including its POLITICAL COSTS"

   THE RATIONALITY ASSUMPTION -- a DEFAULT with an EXIT
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   DEFAULT : decision-makers "CAPABLE OF RATIONAL CALCULATION
             (i.e. COST-BENEFIT ANALYSIS)" and "FULLY CONSCIOUS OF
             THEIR GOALS", selecting "the MOST EFFICIENT OR
             FEASIBLE MEANS"
   EXIT    : "if a decision is a product of ROUTINIZED BEHAVIOUR or
             IRRATIONAL ATTITUDE on the part of some actors,
             SOCIAL-PSYCHOLOGICAL REASONS thereof MUST BE
             INVESTIGATED AND RECORDED"
   (!) this ANSWERS WALLAS (L17): rationality is a BASELINE that
       makes deviation VISIBLE, not a claim about human nature

   THE THREE SUB-MODELS
   (a) STAGES / VARIABLES ....... Richard Snyder, Harold Lasswell
       [chapter prints "Synder"; standard form is Snyder]
   (b) ECONOMIC ANALOGIES,
       "MAXIMIZING ADVANTAGE" ... Anthony Downs, J.M. Buchanan,
                                  G. Tullock
   (c) GAME THEORY .............. "QUANTIFICATION of the results of
       (no exponent named)        DECISION STRATEGIES ... each
                                  player will seek his MAXIMUM
                                  ADVANTAGE under conditions of
                                  RATIONALITY"

   (!!) NO CRITICISM SECTION. The chapter's only reservation is
        that a GENERAL THEORY of decision-making "is NOT YET
        ADEQUATELY DEVELOPED" (a 2009 judgment).
```

*Caption: the double-flag at the bottom is a source-discipline point. Three of the five models get an explicit list of criticisms; this one does not, and an answer should say so rather than manufacture one.*

### Revision notes

- ✅ **Decision-making analysis denotes an attempt to understand politics as a process of arriving at decisions.**
- ✅ It is **closely related to the concept of political system** because Easton **treats decisions and actions as outputs of the political system**.
- ✅ **Exercise of any form of authority involves decision-making whether it is in the sphere of business, university-administration or family.**
- ✅ The filter: **we are concerned with the process of arriving at public decisions**.
- ✅ **Van Dyke**: **Every actor is a decision-maker** — parties nominate, **voters decide whether to vote and for whom**, legislators advance proposals, executives seek legislation, sign or veto, execute and exercise discretion.
- ⚠️ The voter's reappearance repairs systems analysis's most conspicuous gap.
- ✅ Seven elements: **(a)** issues; **(b)** structures; **(c)** actors, with **study of personality, if necessary**; **(d)** the **alternative courses of action or options that were considered**; **(e)** **range of preferences vis-a-vis the utilities**; **(f)** external **factors, pressures or constraints**; **(g)** the outcome **including its political costs**.
- 🔑 Rationality is a default — **capable of rational calculation (i.e. cost-benefit analysis)**, **fully conscious of their goals**, selecting **the most efficient or feasible means**.
- 🔑 With an exit: if a decision is **a product of routinized behaviour or irrational attitude**, **social-psychological reasons thereof must be investigated and recorded**.
- ✅ Three sub-models: stage/variable models (**Richard Snyder and Harold Lasswell**); economic analogies of **maximizing advantage** (**Anthony Downs, J.M. Buchanan and G. Tullock**); and **game theory**.
- ⚠️ The chapter prints **Synder**; the standard form is **Richard C. Snyder**.
- ✅ It **essentially follows interdisciplinary approach by drawing substantially on psychology, sociology, administrative theory and organization theory**.
- 📰 A **general theory of decision-making** is **not yet adequately developed** as of **2009**.
- ⚠️ ❌ The chapter supplies **no** criticism section for this model.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the rationality assumption in decision-making analysis is constructed, and why it does not reinstate the picture of man that Wallas attacked.

**Model answer:** It is constructed as a defeasible default with a specified alternative, not as a description. ✅ The chapter's formulation is that **under normal circumstances, it is assumed that the decision-makers are capable of rational calculation (i.e. cost-benefit analysis) and that they are fully conscious of their goals so that they select the most efficient or feasible means of attaining these goals** — note **it is assumed** and **under normal circumstances**, both of which mark the status of the claim as methodological. ✅ The exit clause then states what happens when the assumption fails: **if a decision is a product of routinized behaviour or irrational attitude on the part of some actors, social-psychological reasons thereof must be investigated and recorded**. ⚠️ So failure of the assumption is not a failure of the model; it is a trigger that moves the analysis into a different explanatory register. ⚠️ That is why it does not reinstate the picture Wallas attacked. ✅ Wallas's target was the classical economists who **had treated man as a rational creature following his self-interest** — a substantive claim about human nature, offered as true and used to deduce behaviour without observation. ⚠️ Here the assumption is used in the opposite direction: it generates an expectation against which actual decisions are compared, and the interesting cases are the **departures**, which are then referred to psychology — exactly the discipline Wallas wanted brought into political studies. ⚠️ The model also requires, at element (c), the **study of personality, if necessary**, which no rational-actor theory in Wallas's sense would permit. 🔑 The accurate formulation is that decision-making analysis uses rationality as an **instrument of detection** rather than as a **premise of deduction**, and that is precisely the difference between an idealisation and a dogma.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not supply criticisms of decision-making analysis and attribute them to the chapter. ❌ Unlike systems analysis, structural-functional analysis and communications theory, this model receives **no** criticism section; the chapter's only reservation is that a general theory **is not yet adequately developed**, and that is a **2009** judgment. ⚠️ Independent criticisms exist and are carried at Lesson 33 as owner-extension material, clearly marked. ❌ And do not expand the three sub-models beyond their one-line characterisations — Downs, Buchanan and Tullock are names here, with no theory attached.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What does decision-making analysis examine, and what does it assume about decision-makers? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** It opens the box Easton left closed, since **David Easton's original model of political system treats decisions and actions as outputs**, and examines **the process of arriving at public decisions**. Its seven elements are the **issues**, the **structures**, the **actors involved in decision-making (this may involve study of personality, if necessary)**, the **alternative courses of action or options that were considered before making a choice**, the **factors influencing the choice … i.e. their range of preferences vis-a-vis the utilities attached to each of the alternatives**, any **external factors, pressures or constraints**, and the **outcome of the decision including its political costs**. ⚠️ It assumes decision-makers **capable of rational calculation (i.e. cost-benefit analysis)** and **fully conscious of their goals** — but as a default, since departures from **routinized behaviour or irrational attitude** send the analysis to **social-psychological reasons**.

**Rubric [10]:** the relation to Easton's outputs and the public filter **2** · at least five of the seven elements **4** · the rationality assumption stated as an assumption **2** · the exit clause to social-psychological explanation **2**.

---

## Lesson 32 — Marxian analysis: political analysis by proxy

Progress: 32 / 34 | Stage: Advanced | Part V — models of political analysis

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §15 with the Advanced companion's §§5, 6 and 11, read against the chapter's concluding section on Marxian analysis (Gauba, Ch.4, PDF pp.133-134 = printed 118-119), which supplies the proxy formulation, Callinicos's three claims with their source and date, the base-superstructure distinction, the 1859 Preface quotation, the refusal of analysis *per se*, the dominant/dependent class pair, the *Manifesto* sentence, the five-stage table and the hedged terminus.
⚠️ The section heading itself is **not recoverable by text extraction** on PDF p.133 — the page carries 108 image objects and a roughly 50-point gap below the running head where the heading sits, while every other Part V heading extracts as text. The section is identified by its content and by that gap, and the point is catalogued in the Source Ledger.
CA search: "Marxian class analysis political system comparison 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked; **no** live anchor carried.
❌ **Topic boundary.** ❌ Marxism as a doctrine — dialectics, alienation, surplus value, Gramsci, the Frankfurt School — belongs to **Topic 04**. ✅ What is owned here is only Marxism **as a model of political analysis**: what it tells an analyst to look at, and why.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The proxy formulation

✅ **Marxian analysis deals with a wide range of social phenomena—their past, present and future.** ✅ **Politics figures therein only incidentally.** ✅ **It may, therefore, be called political analysis by proxy.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Political analysis by proxy** is the single most useful phrase in this section. ⚠️ It says that Marxism is not a theory *of politics* that happens to be radical; it is a theory of **society and history** from which political conclusions fall out as a by-product. ⚠️ That is a claim about the **logical location** of politics in the theory, not about its political colour.

### 2. Callinicos's three escalating claims

✅ **Alex Callinicos has significantly observed:**

> Marxism denies that politics is a persisting feature of every form of society. Furthermore, it claims that politics, where it does exist, cannot be studied in isolation from the rest of society. Finally, Marxism, insofar as it is a practical programme as well as a body of theoretical analysis, seeks the abolition of politics. These claims are obviously incompatible with the notion of an autonomous discipline of Politics.
>
> — Callinicos, 'Marxism and Politics' in *What is Politics? The Activity and its Study*, edited by Adrian Leftwich; 1984, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.133 (printed 118)

🔑 ⚠️ **Read the three claims as an escalation, because that is how they are built.**

| | Claim | What it denies |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | ⚠️ Politics is **not a persisting feature of every form of society** | ⚠️ its **universality** — there were societies without it and there will be again |
| **2** | ⚠️ Where it exists it **cannot be studied in isolation from the rest of society** | ⚠️ its **separability** — hence no autonomous method |
| **3** | ⚠️ Marxism **seeks the abolition of politics** | ⚠️ its **permanence** — the theory is also a programme against its own object |

⚠️ And the conclusion the chapter quotes: **These claims are obviously incompatible with the notion of an autonomous discipline of Politics.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is why Marxian analysis sits last and sits uneasily in a chapter on approaches.** ⚠️ The other four models disagree about *how* to study the political system; this one disputes that there is a distinct object there to be studied. ✅ The chapter states the implication plainly: **politics is not a fundamental activity of human beings, and political system is not an autonomous structure as held by liberal models of political analysis. Politics is only a part of superstructure.**

### 3. Base and superstructure

✅ **Marxist analysis starts with a distinction between 'base' (or 'substructure') and 'superstructure'.** ✅ **This building-like metaphor is used to postulate that the economic structure of society (which represents the base) is responsible for creating and transforming its social structure, including its legal and political structure, religion and morals, etc. (which represents the superstructure).**

✅ **Marx expressed this idea in his Preface to A Contribution to the Critique of Political Economy (1859) as follows:**

> In the social production of their life, men enter into definite relations that are indispensable and independent of their will, relations of production which correspond to a definite stage of development of their material productive forces. The sum total of these relations of production constitutes the economic structure of society, the real foundation, on which rises a legal and political superstructure and to which correspond definite forms of social consciousness. The mode of production of material life conditions the social, political and intellectual life process in general.
>
> — Marx, Preface to *A Contribution to the Critique of Political Economy*; 1859, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.133 (printed 118)

🔑 ⚠️ **The last verb is the one to get right: the mode of production *conditions* the social, political and intellectual life process.** ❌ It does not say *determines*. ⚠️ Reproducing this sentence with **determines** is the commonest single error made with it, and it converts a claim about the setting of limits into a claim about mechanical causation — which is what Topic 04's debate on crude determinism turns on. ✅ Quote the verb as printed.

⚠️ Note also what the passage builds in before any politics appears: relations of production are **indispensable and independent of their will**, and they **correspond to a definite stage of development of their material productive forces**. 🔑 Will is excluded at the foundation, which is why the superstructure cannot be treated as the product of choice.

✅ The chapter's gloss: **the political system, as a part of superstructure, is not autonomous, that it does not grow out of itself, but emerges on the foundation of the economic structure of society.** ✅ **The economic structure or the mode of production itself changes with the development of forces of production (i.e. means of production and labour power).** ✅ **This would bring about corresponding changes in the political system and other aspects of superstructure.**

### 4. The methodological conclusion

✅ **Since the political system is neither independent nor autonomous, no useful purpose will be served by undertaking analysis of political system per se.**

🔑 ⚠️ **That sentence is the whole model in one line, and it is a methodological prohibition, not merely a theoretical claim.** ⚠️ It tells the analyst to stop doing what the other four models of this chapter do.

✅ What to do instead: **Marxism (particularly classical Marxism), therefore, insists on an analysis of the economic structure of society, historical stages of its development and the corresponding class structure prevailing at each of these stages (which is reflected in 'relations of production') in order to understand the character of the political system.**

⚠️ ⚠️ Note the qualifier **particularly classical Marxism** — ⚠️ the chapter marks that this is the classical position, leaving room for the neo-Marxist revisions that **Topic 04** owns.

### 5. Classes, and what politics then is

✅ **At each stage of historical development society is divided into two antagonistic classes.** ✅ **The owners of private property or the major means of production rise to the position of 'dominant class' whereas those living solely on their labour on the terms dictated by the former constitute 'dependent class'.**

✅ **As long as the major means of production continue to be privately owned, the division of society into dominant and dependent classes cannot be eliminated, nor their class conflict can ever be resolved.**

✅ **Politics must always be traced back to its 'hidden basis' in the class struggle.** ✅ **Since politics arises from class struggle, it is historically a transient phenomenon.** ✅ **Different states are simply different forms of class domination.**

✅ **As Marx and Engels observed:**

> Political power, properly so called, is merely the organized power of one class for oppressing another.
>
> — Marx and Engels, *Communist Manifesto*; 1848, quoted at Gauba, Ch.4, PDF p.134 (printed 119)

✅ **Politics is the process through which antagonistic classes struggle to obtain, retain or influence state power.** ✅ **Thus under capitalism, politics of the capitalist class involves their strategy for exploitation of the working class while politics of the working class implies organizing themselves to overthrow the capitalist system.**

⚠️ ⚠️ Observe that **both** classes have a politics here, and that the word means the same thing in both cases — a strategy with respect to state power. 🔑 The model is symmetrical about class even though it is not neutral about outcomes.

### 6. The five stages

✅ **In a nutshell, Marxism speaks of five stages of historical development, including one prehistorical stage.** ✅ The chapter prints the following table, headed **Marxian Analysis of Historical Stages**:

| Historical Stage | Mode of Production | Class Structure |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Primitive Communism** | ✅ **hunting, fishing, food-gathering, etc.** | ✅ **Classes not yet emerged** |
| ✅ **Slave System** | ✅ **animal husbandry, domestic agriculture and crafts** | ✅ **Masters and slaves** |
| ✅ **Feudal System** | ✅ **Large-scale agriculture** | ✅ **Landlords and serfs** |
| ✅ **Capitalist System** | ✅ **Large-scale industry** | ✅ **Capitalists and workers** |
| ✅ **Socialist System** | ✅ **-do-** *(ditto: large-scale industry)* | ✅ **Workers in power and the former capitalists** |

⚠️ The third column's heading is printed **C/oss Structure** in the source — an OCR artefact for **Class Structure**, catalogued in the Source Ledger. ⚠️ The ditto mark **-do-** is reproduced as printed with its expansion supplied in italics.

🔑 ⚠️ **The ditto mark is the most instructive thing in the table and it is almost always missed.** ⚠️ Socialism's mode of production is the *same* as capitalism's — **large-scale industry**. ⚠️ What changes between the fourth and fifth rows is **only the class structure**: from **Capitalists and workers** to **Workers in power and the former capitalists**. ⚠️ So the transition to socialism is, on this table, a transfer of **power over** an industrial base, not a change *of* that base — which is exactly why ownership rather than technology carries the whole explanatory weight.

⚠️ ⚠️ Note too that the fifth row still names **two** groups. ⚠️ Classlessness has not yet arrived at the socialist stage; that is the next lesson of the section.

✅ **Boundary.** ⚠️ The table lists **five** stages **including one prehistorical stage** — Primitive Communism. ❌ Communism proper is **not** a sixth row; it is described in the following paragraph as what the socialist stage is hoped to lead to.

### 7. The terminus, and its three hedges

✅ **It hopes that once genuine socialist system is established and there is no distortion in its working, common ownership of major means of production will eventually lead to abolition of classes; state and political power will become redundant; and politics itself will disappear.** ✅ **Society will then enter the phase of communism; it will become classless and stateless society where authority will be exercised without resort to power, and administration will be carried on without the state machinery.**

🔑 ⚠️ **Three hedges are built into that sentence and all three must be carried into any answer that uses it.**

| | Hedge as printed | What it concedes |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | ⚠️ **It *hopes*** | ⚠️ the terminus is an expectation of the theory, not a finding |
| **2** | ⚠️ **once *genuine* socialist system is established** | ⚠️ the outcome is conditional, and a failed case can always be called not genuine |
| **3** | ⚠️ **and there is *no distortion* in its working** | ⚠️ a second condition, equally unfalsifiable in practice |

⚠️ ⚠️ The second and third hedges together make the prediction very hard to refute, because any actual socialist state that did not wither can be said to have failed a condition. ⚠️ Saying this is not an objection smuggled in from outside; it is a reading of the printed qualifications. ⚠️ ➡️ The developed form of that criticism, and the neo-Marxist responses to it, belong to **Topic 04**.

✅ Note what the communist terminus asserts positively: **authority will be exercised without resort to power, and administration will be carried on without the state machinery**. ⚠️ Authority does **not** disappear; **power** and the **state machinery** do. 🔑 That distinction is precise and worth reproducing exactly.

### 8. The comparative rule — the model's usable output

✅ **Marx, Engels and Lenin argued that political systems should be compared and contrasted with reference to their respective class structures.** ✅ **We should not be misled by their superficial similarities or differences.**

✅ The illustration: **Illustrating his point from his contemporary experience, Marx pointed out that the laws and state system in North America and Prussia were based on similar system of property ownership,** …

⚠️ ⚠️ **The sentence breaks at the boundary of the owners' declared range.** ⚠️ PDF p.134 is the last page in scope; the continuation on the next page is deliberately **not** used, and no completion is supplied here. ⚠️ What is already on the page is sufficient for the point: two states that differ on the surface — a republic and a monarchy — are put in the **same** class if their property ownership is the same.

🔑 ⚠️ **This is the one place where Marxian analysis yields a concrete comparative instruction that rivals Almond's**, and it is worth stating as a contrast. ⚠️ Almond's structural-functional analysis compares systems by asking which structures perform the same **functions**, which lets a tribal society and an industrial state be compared. ⚠️ Marxian analysis compares them by asking who owns the **means of production**, which cuts across constitutional form. ⚠️ Both are genuine comparative rules; they will frequently classify the same two states differently, and that disagreement is a good thing to be able to display in an answer.

### 9. The visual

```text
   WHY THIS SECTION SITS LAST AND SITS UNEASILY

   Models 1-4 : disagree about HOW to study the political system
   Model 5    : disputes that there is a DISTINCT OBJECT there
                "political analysis BY PROXY"
                "Politics figures therein only INCIDENTALLY"

   CALLINICOS'S THREE ESCALATING CLAIMS (1984)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   1. politics is NOT "a persisting feature of every form of
      society"                      >>> denies its UNIVERSALITY
   2. where it exists it "CANNOT BE STUDIED IN ISOLATION from the
      rest of society"              >>> denies its SEPARABILITY
   3. Marxism "SEEKS THE ABOLITION OF POLITICS"
                                    >>> denies its PERMANENCE
   ===> "obviously INCOMPATIBLE with the notion of an AUTONOMOUS

   THE BUILDING METAPHOR

        driven by : FORCES OF PRODUCTION
                    "(i.e. MEANS OF PRODUCTION and LABOUR POWER)"

   1859 PREFACE -- THE VERB MATTERS
        "The mode of production of material life CONDITIONS the
         social, political and intellectual life process"
        (!) CONDITIONS. Not "determines". Do not substitute.

   THE METHODOLOGICAL PROHIBITION

         of political system PER SE"
        >>> study instead: ECONOMIC STRUCTURE -> HISTORICAL STAGES
            -> CLASS STRUCTURE ("reflected in RELATIONS OF

   THE FIVE STAGES -- WATCH THE DITTO MARK
   ---------------------------------------------------------------

                                                 and the FORMER

   (!) The fifth row still names TWO groups -- classlessness has

        (!) AUTHORITY survives. POWER and STATE MACHINERY do not.

   worked example (breaks at the edge of the declared range):
```
