# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

## The honest position first

⚠️ ⚠️ **No examined stem in the locally held Philosophy Optional Paper-II Section-A record, 2018 through 2026, names an approach, a method, behaviouralism, post-behaviouralism, or any of the five models of political analysis.** ⚠️ Every part of every paper in that nine-year record was read for this file, and the result is a clean negative. ❌ **This topic therefore has no primary PYQ anchor, and none is manufactured here.**

❌ **No General Studies PYQ is claimed for this topic.** ⚠️ Political Theory is supplementary conceptual support across GS papers; it is **not** a directly named GS paper owner, and inventing a GS route to manufacture coverage would be a fabrication. ❌ **No synthetic question, no reconstructed stem and no inferred ownership appears below.**

✅ What is offered instead is a curated set of **nine adjacent anchors** — one per examined year — each of which is **owned by a different Philosophy Optional file**, and each of which is used here only to show **what methodological material contributes to an answer whose subject belongs elsewhere**. 🔑 That is the honest use of this topic in the exam hall: it is rarely the subject of a question and frequently the difference between a described answer and an analysed one.

## Curation note

| | |
|---|---|
| **Anchors curated** | ✅ **nine**, one for each examined year 2018-2026 |
| **Primary anchors for this topic** | ❌ **zero** — no stem in the record is Topic 08-owned |
| **Distinct owners across the nine** | ✅ **seven** |
| **Owner distribution** | ✅ Political Ideologies **×2** (2025, 2023) · Sovereignty **×2** (2024, 2021) · Development and Social Progress **×1** (2026) · Humanism, Secularism and Multiculturalism **×1** (2022) · Forms of Government **×1** (2020) · Gender Discrimination **×1** (2019) · Caste: Gandhi and Ambedkar **×1** (2018) |
| **Strength of fit** | ⚠️ **one strong** (2022 — the stem's own two terms are this topic's central distinction) · ⚠️ **five moderate** (2026, 2025, 2024, 2023, 2018) · ⚠️ **three weak, and labelled weak** (2021, 2020, 2019) |
| **Verbatim status** | ✅ all nine stems reproduced exactly from the held ledgers, with paper code, question number and marks |
| **Search limit** | ⚠️ the record consulted runs to the **2026** paper (`KVMS-B-PHL`); ❌ nothing later is claimed |

---

## Anchor 1 — 2026, `KVMS-B-PHL`, Q4(c) [15] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "Examine the relationship between democracy and development. Does democracy promote or hinder development ?"

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Development and Social Progress. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What Topic 08 contributes:** the stem asks for a **relationship** and then a **verdict**, which is a question about what would count as evidence for a causal claim across political systems. ✅ Behaviouralism's tenets supply the standard — **Regularities** requires uniformities **expressed in theory-like statements so as to provide for explanation and prediction**, **Verification** requires validity **testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour**, and **Quantification** requires **measurement … expressed in terms of actual quantities**. ⚠️ Easton's model supplies the mechanism by which a developmental demand becomes an authoritative output, and post-behaviouralism supplies the reason the question matters at all.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the conception of development, Sen's capabilities, human-development measures and the substantive verdict belong wholly to the owner. ⚠️ Topic 08 supplies the frame, not the content.

## Anchor 2 — 2025, `SLPM-B-PHL`, Q2(b) [15] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "Evaluate Marxism as a Political Ideology."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Political Ideologies. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What Topic 08 contributes:** an evaluation is strengthened by showing what Marxism does as a **method** and not only as a creed. ✅ Lesson 32 supplies the proxy formulation — politics **figures therein only incidentally** — Callinicos's three claims, the base-superstructure metaphor, the methodological prohibition that **no useful purpose will be served by undertaking analysis of political system per se**, and the comparative rule that systems be compared **with reference to their respective class structures**. 🔑 That converts a verdict on the ideology into a verdict on what it can and cannot see.
- ❌ **Boundary:** dialectics, alienation, surplus value and the neo-Marxist developments belong to **Topic 04** and to the owner file. ⚠️ Topic 08 owns only Marxism-as-analysis.

## Anchor 3 — 2024, `PHKM-B-PHL`, Q4(b) [15] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "What insights does the Arthasastra offer with regard to the concept of sovereignty? Does it have any relevance in the modern times? Critically discuss."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Sovereignty. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What Topic 08 contributes:** the second demand — **relevance in the modern times** — is precisely the historical approach's problem. ✅ Lessons 11 and 12 supply the apparatus: the historical approach's claim that political ideas are products of their time, Popper's charge of **historicism** against the stronger version, and the revival associated with Sabine's position that a classical text is read for the questions it answered before it is read for the answers it gives. ⚠️ The disciplined form of the answer is therefore to establish the text's own problem-situation first and only then ask what transfers.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the concept of sovereignty, Austin, Laski and the pluralist critique belong to the owner.

## Anchor 4 — 2023, `SKYC-B-PHLY`, Q3(a) [20] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "Explain Historical Materialism and discuss its relevance in the context of social development and change."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Political Ideologies. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What Topic 08 contributes:** the chapter's own compact statement of the doctrine, quotable and verified. ✅ The 1859 Preface sentence — **the mode of production of material life conditions the social, political and intellectual life process in general** — with the verb **conditions** intact; the **base**/**superstructure** metaphor; the five-stage table whose socialist row repeats capitalism's **large-scale industry** as **-do-**; and the triply hedged terminus.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the full doctrinal exposition, the forces/relations dynamic and the neo-Marxist revisions belong to **Topic 04**. ⚠️ Topic 08 supplies the method and the exact wording.

## Anchor 5 — 2022, `CRNA-S-PHLY`, Q3(c) [15] ✅ strong fit

> ✅ "Critically analyze the descriptive and normative aspects of multiculturalism."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Humanism, Secularism and Multiculturalism. ❌ Not this topic.
- 🔑 **What Topic 08 contributes:** the stem's two operative words **are** this topic's central distinction. ✅ Lessons 5 to 8 supply it in full — the difference between **describing** and **prescribing**, the empirical/normative divide, Weldon's position on evaluative language, the fact/value issue, and post-behaviouralism's insistence that separating the two is **not** a licence to ignore values. ⚠️ An answer that opens by saying precisely what a descriptive claim about multiculturalism and a normative claim about it each commit one to, and what each would be tested by, has already done the hardest part of the question.
- ⚠️ **Why this is the strongest of the nine:** the other eight borrow a frame; this one shares a vocabulary. ❌ Even so, the substantive content — what multiculturalism is, its Indian application, its critics — belongs entirely to the owner.

## Anchor 6 — 2021, `HXS-B-PHLY`, Q1(c) [10] ⚠️ weak fit, labelled

> ✅ "Is Austin's theory of sovereignty compatible with democracy? Discuss."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Sovereignty. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What Topic 08 contributes:** only the identification of the procedure in play. ✅ Lesson 13 treats the **legal approach** and shows what it does and refuses to do — it **focuses its attention on the legal and constitutional framework in which different organs of government have to function** and on **the procedure which makes their actions legally valid**, while setting aside the social and psychological material the behavioural approach later insisted upon. ⚠️ Austin's **command** theory is an instance of that family of procedure, but ❌ the chapter's section does not name him and the command/legal-personality apparatus belongs to the Sovereignty owner, not here. ⚠️ Naming the approach in one clause explains why the compatibility question arises at all.
- ⚠️ **Fit is weak and is marked weak:** a 10-mark answer has no room for methodology beyond that clause. ❌ Do not import more.

## Anchor 7 — 2020, `URC-B-PHLY`, Q1(b) [10] ⚠️ weak fit, labelled

> ✅ "How far do the liberal democracies safeguard the interests of minorities? Evaluate critically."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Forms of Government. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What Topic 08 contributes:** a caution and a mechanism. ⚠️ The caution is the Western-benchmark objection of Lesson 33 — evaluating systems against an implicit template is exactly what comparative practice has been charged with. ✅ The mechanism is Easton's: minority interests enter as **demands** and must survive the conversion process to become authoritative outputs, which is where the practical question lies.
- ⚠️ **Fit is weak and is marked weak.** ❌ The substantive assessment belongs to the owner.

## Anchor 8 — 2019, `SDF-B-PHLY`, Q3(b) [15] ⚠️ weak fit, labelled

> ✅ "Consider critically that gender discrimination is a rather man-made concept but not naturally endowed."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Gender Discrimination. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What Topic 08 contributes:** the evidential question embedded in the stem. ⚠️ A claim that something is **man-made** rather than **naturally endowed** is an empirical claim with a normative payload, and the behavioural tenets of **Verification** and **Regularities** say what would have to be shown; the fact/value lessons say why the payload cannot be read off the finding.
- ❌ **Boundary:** feminism, the sex/gender distinction and the substantive debate belong to **Topic 06** and to the owner file. ⚠️ **Fit is weak and is marked weak.**

## Anchor 9 — 2018, `EGT-D-PHLY`, Q1(c) [10] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "It is said that the traditional hold of caste-based groups on Indian social behaviour has survived all attempts to build alternate identities. Discuss in the light of M. K. Gandhi."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Caste: Gandhi and Ambedkar. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What Topic 08 contributes:** the structural-functional insight that a function may be discharged by an informal structure. ✅ Almond's framework was built precisely so that **the same function may be performed by different structures in different systems**, and the canonical owner's hedged Indian application names **caste associations, kinship networks, local patron-client ties** as channels of interest articulation operating alongside formal parliamentary structures. ⚠️ That explains **survival** as something other than inertia: the group persists because it performs a function the formal structure does not fully absorb.
- ❌ **Boundary:** Gandhi's own position on caste, Ambedkar's critique and the historical record belong to the owner. ⚠️ ❌ No statistic or specific outcome is asserted.

## How to deploy this topic when it is not the subject

| Situation | Deployment |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ Stem asks for **relevance of a classical text** | ✅ open with the historical approach's problem-situation rule; ⚠️ note Popper's historicism caution before claiming transfer |
| ⚠️ Stem contains **descriptive** and **normative** as its own terms | 🔑 use the empirical/normative distinction explicitly as the answer's spine |
| ⚠️ Stem asks whether X **promotes or hinders** Y across systems | ✅ name the evidential standard — regularities, verification, quantification — then give the substantive verdict |
| ⚠️ Stem asks to **evaluate an ideology** | ✅ add what the ideology can and cannot **see** as a method, not only what it asserts |
| ⚠️ Stem concerns an **informal or traditional structure** | ✅ use the functional point that structures vary while functions recur |
| ⚠️ Stem is a **10-marker** | ⚠️ one clause of methodology at most; ❌ the owner's content takes the rest |

---

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

⚠️ Six cumulative checks, each drawing on several lessons at once. ⚠️ They are deliberately different in kind from the per-lesson checks: each one requires material from at least three lessons to be combined, which is the skill the examination actually tests.

---

## Check A — the wedding, and what it licenses

**Prompt:** The chapter opens by distinguishing method from approach and then says they are wedded — but only in one direction. Why does it need both claims, and what would go wrong if either were dropped?

**Worked response:** ✅ The distinction is stated first: **method is a more general term which denotes a particular way of doing something**, defined in systematic study as **the procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge could be obtained**, while an approach selects what shall be looked at. ⚠️ The wedding then follows — **an approach is usually wedded to a particular method while a method is not always wedded to a particular approach** — because a selection with no procedure yields no checkable result, even though a single procedure such as the comparative method can serve several selections. ⚠️ Drop the distinction and the two collapse, so that criticism of an approach's selectivity becomes indistinguishable from criticism of its rigour; this is the error that makes the behavioural debate unintelligible, because behaviouralism's claim was about **method** while much of the criticism was about **approach**. ⚠️ Drop the wedding and the two float free, so that a rigorous study of a trivial selection looks like good science. 🔑 Holding both, with the asymmetry intact, is what allows the chapter to say later that the traditional approaches **have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches** — a verdict that would be incoherent if approaches were merely rival procedures.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat the opening section as throat-clearing. ⚠️ Every later verdict in the chapter depends on it.

---

## Check B — the fact/value sequence in four moves

**Prompt:** Trace the chapter's treatment of fact and value from the describing/prescribing distinction through to post-behaviouralism, naming what each move adds.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **Move 1** establishes the distinction: a descriptive statement reports what is and a prescriptive statement says what ought to be, and the two are not derivable from one another. ⚠️ **Move 2**, Weldon's, applies it to political vocabulary and finds much of it evaluative rather than descriptive, which is why disputes about the state so often fail to resolve. ⚠️ **Move 3**, behaviouralism's, converts the distinction into a methodological rule — the fifth tenet, **Values**, insists that **objective scientific inquiry has to be value-free or value-neutral**, and draws **a clear distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation**. ⚠️ **Move 4**, post-behaviouralism's, denies that the rule can be the last word, since choosing what to investigate is itself evaluative and urgent problems were being neglected while technique was refined. 🔑 What each move adds is: a logical point; a diagnosis of political language; a research discipline; and a limit on that discipline. ⚠️ Note that **no** move retracts its predecessor. ✅ The distinction survives into post-behaviouralism, which demands **relevance** and **action** while retaining verification — which is why the final position is a correction and not a reversal.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not report post-behaviouralism as abandoning the fact/value distinction. ⚠️ It abandons the claim that observing the distinction exhausts a political scientist's duty.

---

## Check C — reliability bought with generality

**Prompt:** Explain the trade behaviouralism made, using at least two of the eight tenets, and say why the trade was contested.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ The trade is that propositions become more reliable as their scope narrows. ✅ The second tenet, **Verification**, requires that **the validity of such theory-like statements must be testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour**; the fourth, **Quantification**, requires **measurement which should be expressed in terms of actual quantities to facilitate proper analysis**. ⚠️ Both are satisfiable only by statements about observable, countable behaviour — voting, participation, attitudes — and such statements are necessarily about particular populations at particular times. ⚠️ The large propositions of the tradition, about the best state or the grounds of obligation, cannot be put through either filter, so they fall outside the enterprise rather than being refuted by it. ⚠️ The trade was contested on two grounds. ⚠️ First, that what is measurable is not the same as what is important, so the narrowing is not neutral between questions. ⚠️ Second, that the exclusion was presented as a finding about politics when it was a consequence of a chosen procedure. 🔑 The strongest formulation is that behaviouralism did not show the great questions to be meaningless; it showed them to be outside the reach of a technique it had very good reasons to adopt.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not say behaviouralism proved normative questions unanswerable. ⚠️ It declined to treat them, which is a different and far more defensible claim.

---

## Check D — the two comparative rules

**Prompt:** Almond and the Marxian approach each supply a rule for comparing political systems. State both, and show a case where they classify differently.

**Worked response:** ✅ Almond's rule is functional: compare systems by asking **which structures perform which functions**, since the same function may be performed by different structures in different systems. ✅ The Marxian rule is structural in the other sense: **political systems should be compared and contrasted with reference to their respective class structures**, and **we should not be misled by their superficial similarities or differences**. ⚠️ The chapter's own illustration shows the divergence: Marx observed that the laws and state system in **North America and Prussia** rested on a similar system of property ownership — a republic and a monarchy placed in the same class. ⚠️ Almond's rule would separate them at once, since the structures discharging interest aggregation and rule-making differ sharply between an elective republic and a dynastic monarchy. ⚠️ Conversely, Almond's rule unites a modern party system with a kinship network that articulates interests, which the Marxian rule would separate if their property regimes differ. 🔑 Neither rule is wrong; each answers a different question — one asks how a system works, the other asks whom it works for. ⚠️ An answer that displays the divergence on a single case is doing comparative political analysis rather than reporting it.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat the two as alternative labels for one procedure. ⚠️ They are genuinely rival classifications, and their disagreement is the interesting part.

---

## Check E — what every model's limitation has in common

**Prompt:** The chapter's criticisms of three models look different but share a structure. Identify it, and say what follows for the use of models.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ The shared structure is that each limitation is the shadow of the model's chosen unit. ✅ Easton's unit is the input-conversion-output cycle, which describes how a system persists; so the criticisms are an **inability in the analysis of political power as well as in the analysis of mass political behaviour, such as voting** — exactly the relational and individual-level phenomena the abstraction had to discard. ✅ Almond's unit is the function, which is defined by what keeps a system ongoing; so the criticism is that the analysis is **primarily concerned with system's survival; hence … ideologically inclined towards conservatism**, and that it is **not suited to analysis of power relations in society**. ✅ Deutsch's unit is information, chosen in preference to **the bones or muscles of the body-politic**; so the criticism is that the model is **hardly useful in the analysis of power structure of society, ideologies, allocation of resources**, or **violence and revolution** — bones and muscles, exactly. ⚠️ What follows is that limitations of this kind are not defects to be repaired but costs to be known. 🔑 A model cannot be improved out of them without becoming a different model, which is why the chapter's conclusion is pluralism: **the question selects the approach, and every selection has a cost**.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not present these criticisms as showing the models to be failures. ⚠️ The chapter commends each for the range it covers while naming what it cannot reach.

---

## Check F — the whole chapter in one causal line

**Prompt:** Give the chapter's argument as a single connected line from its opening distinction to its closing section, with the turning points named.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ Method and approach are distinguished and then joined asymmetrically — **an approach is usually wedded to a particular method while a method is not always wedded to a particular approach** — which makes selection a legitimate object of criticism. ⚠️ The traditional approaches — philosophical, historical, legal, institutional — are each shown to select something real and to miss something else: values without behaviour, context without regularity, law without practice, form without function. ⚠️ **First turning point:** Wallas and Bentley in **1908** attack the inherited picture of political man and of the formal state, and Merriam institutionalises the demand for scientific technique. ⚠️ The behavioural movement then consolidates into the eight tenets, buying reliability at the price of generality. ⚠️ **Second turning point:** post-behaviouralism charges that relevance was lost in the purchase, and demands **action** without discarding verification. ⚠️ **Third turning point:** the five models are introduced, not as a further revolution, but as tools — each with a stated reach and a stated limit. ⚠️ The last of them, the Marxian, closes the chapter by denying that the political system is an autonomous object at all. 🔑 The line therefore runs from a distinction, through two corrections, to a plurality — and the final position is that approaches are selected by questions rather than ranked.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not narrate the chapter as progress from error to truth. ⚠️ Its own verdict is that the earlier approaches **have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches**, and that the two sets **do not represent watertight compartments**.

---

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ Six original questions with complete models and quantified rubrics. ⚠️ Word discipline is observed strictly: **10-mark models at about 150 words; 15- and 20-mark models at about 250 words.** ❌ None of these is a past question; all are constructed for this topic.

---

## Model 1 [10 marks] — about 150 words

**Question:** Distinguish between method and approach in political inquiry, and explain why the distinction matters. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ The chapter calls **method** **a more general term**, defined in systematic study as **the procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge could be obtained**. ✅ Technically, though, **approach is the wider term**: it **comprehends not only the method (i.e. how to inquire) but also the focus of our study (i.e. what to inquire)**. ✅ **Van Dyke** fixes it — an approach **consists of criteria of selection**, of **standards governing the inclusion and exclusion of questions and data**, **whereas methods are procedures for getting and utilizing data**. ⚠️ Containment, not rivalry. ⚠️ It matters twice: an approach may be faulted for what it ignores even when its method is impeccable, and the behavioural debate becomes intelligible once behaviouralism's advance is seen as methodological while the objection concerned selection. ✅ Yet the two are joined asymmetrically — **an approach is usually wedded to a particular method while a method is not always wedded to a particular approach**.

**Rubric [10]:** method defined as procedure of inquiry **2** · the technical correction that **approach** is wider and comprehends method **and** focus **2** · Van Dyke's criteria-of-selection and inclusion/exclusion formulation **2** · the asymmetry of the wedding stated in both directions **2** · at least one reason the distinction matters **1** · precision of expression **1**.

---

## Model 2 [10 marks] — about 150 words

**Question:** Was the behavioural movement a revolution in political science? *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ In method, yes; in subject matter, less so. ⚠️ Its novelty was the insistence that political statements be disciplined by evidence: **Regularities** demanded uniformities **expressed in theory-like statements**; **Verification** demanded validity **testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour**; **Quantification** demanded **actual quantities**. ⚠️ Against the inherited approaches, which studied constitutions, texts and law, this was a genuine reorientation towards the behaviour of individuals and groups. ⚠️ But its foundations were laid long before: Graham Wallas and Arthur Bentley published in **1908**, and Charles Merriam's programme preceded the post-war consolidation, so the movement was a culmination rather than a rupture. ⚠️ And it narrowed as it advanced, surrendering the large normative questions it could not test. 🔑 The accurate verdict is that behaviouralism revolutionised the discipline's standards of proof while leaving its oldest questions standing outside the laboratory.

**Rubric [10]:** three tenets named with content **3** · the contrast with traditional approaches **2** · the 1908 precursors and Merriam **2** · the narrowing cost **2** · a reasoned verdict **1**.

---

## Model 3 [15 marks] — about 250 words

**Question:** Examine the eight intellectual foundations of behaviouralism and the price the discipline paid for them. *(Examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ David Easton's eight tenets state the movement's programme. ⚠️ **Regularities** holds that **there are discoverable uniformities in political behaviour** permitting **explanation and prediction**. ⚠️ **Verification** requires that such statements be **testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour**. ⚠️ **Techniques** requires that the means of acquiring and interpreting data be **examined self-consciously, refined and validated**. ⚠️ **Quantification** requires **measurement … expressed in terms of actual quantities**. ⚠️ **Values** draws **a clear distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation** and insists inquiry be **value-free or value-neutral**. ⚠️ **Systematization** binds theory to research, since **research untutored by theory may prove trivial while theory unsupportable by data may turn out to be futile**. ⚠️ **Pure Science** holds that understanding political behaviour is **essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems**. ⚠️ **Integration** seeks union with the other social sciences to **strengthen its validity and the generality of its own results**.

⚠️ The price was threefold. ⚠️ First, scope: only what is observable and countable passes the second and fourth tenets, so questions of obligation, justice and the best state fell outside the enterprise. ⚠️ Second, selection: the fifth tenet secures neutrality within inquiry but not in the choice of what to inquire into, which post-behaviouralism later pressed. ⚠️ Third, significance: the discipline risked reliable findings about unimportant matters, which the sixth tenet anticipates but cannot by itself prevent.

🔑 The balanced verdict is that the tenets raised the standard of proof decisively, and that what they excluded did not thereby cease to be a political question.

**Rubric [15]:** all eight tenets named **5** · content attached to at least five **4** · the scope cost argued **2** · the selection cost argued **2** · a reasoned, non-dismissive verdict **2**.

---

## Model 4 [15 marks] — about 250 words

**Question:** "Post-behaviouralism was a corrective, not a repudiation." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The statement is correct, and the evidence is in what post-behaviouralism retained. ⚠️ Its charge was that the discipline had become absorbed in technique while urgent problems went unexamined, and that a refined instrument pointed at a trivial object is a failure of the enterprise even when every tenet is honoured. ⚠️ Its remedy was the demand for **relevance** and for **action** — that knowledge be put to responsible use, and that political scientists accept a duty beyond description.

⚠️ What makes this a correction rather than a repudiation is that none of behaviouralism's evidential standards was withdrawn. ⚠️ Verification remained; quantification remained; the distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation remained. ⚠️ Action was **added to**, not **substituted for**, evidence standards. ⚠️ Nor did post-behaviouralism revive the traditional approaches as rivals; it accepted the scientific reorientation and disputed its sufficiency.

⚠️ The strongest objection is that a movement demanding relevance owes an account of how urgency is determined, and post-behaviouralism supplies an obligation rather than a method. ⚠️ The reply is that an unmet demand for a decision procedure is not an argument for pretending the choice is not being made: selection occurred under behaviouralism too, silently. ⚠️ The residual, which should be conceded, is that the problem of who decides what is urgent remains open.

🔑 So the relationship is cumulative: behaviouralism supplied standards, post-behaviouralism supplied purpose, and the discipline afterwards needed both — which is why the chapter treats the second as completing the first.

**Rubric [15]:** the charge stated accurately **3** · relevance and action as the remedy **3** · retention of evidential standards demonstrated **4** · one objection with reply **3** · residual conceded **2**.

---

## Model 5 [20 marks] — about 250 words

**Question:** Assess the claim that contemporary approaches have displaced the traditional approaches to the study of politics. *(Assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Displacement implies replacement, which the record does not support. ⚠️ Traditional approaches selected something real: the philosophical examined ends; the historical set ideas in context; the legal read the state's **legal and constitutional framework**; the institutional described ruling structures. ⚠️ Each also missed something, prompting reorientation.

⚠️ The contemporary turn was methodological. ⚠️ Wallas and Bentley in **1908**, then Merriam and the post-war consolidation, replaced the formal object with observable behaviour; eight tenets fixed the standards — **Regularities**, **Verification**, **Techniques**, **Quantification**, **Values**, **Systematization**, **Pure Science**, **Integration**. ⚠️ Post-behaviouralism added **relevance** and **action** without discarding them.

⚠️ But each contemporary model's stated limitation names something a traditional approach addressed. ✅ Systems analysis suffers an **inability in the analysis of political power as well as in the analysis of mass political behaviour, such as voting** — power being the philosophical approach's subject and the individual voter the very object the institutional approach had left **to sociologists**. ✅ Structural-functional analysis is **ideologically inclined towards conservatism**, which a sense of historical change corrects. ✅ Communications theory is commended **only in a limited sphere**. ⚠️ The gaps are systematic, not incidental.

⚠️ Yet displacement has evidence: institutional description alone no longer counts as explanation, and regularity claims require testing. ⚠️ That gain is permanent.

🔑 The assessment is therefore specialisation rather than displacement. ✅ The chapter's own verdict stands: the traditional approaches **have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches**, the two sets **do not represent watertight compartments**, and the discipline needs both scientific rigour and value-consciousness.

**Rubric [20]:** four traditional approaches characterised **4** · the behavioural turn with precursors and tenets **4** · post-behavioural addition **2** · at least two model limitations, in the chapter's own wording, mapped to traditional strengths **5** · the counter-case for displacement conceded **3** · a graded conclusion using the not-outdated/no-longer-favoured formulation **2**.

---

## Model 6 [20 marks] — about 250 words

**Question:** Compare Easton's systems model with the Marxian approach as rival guides to political analysis. *(Compare — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ They differ on the prior question of whether political analysis has its own object.

⚠️ **Unit.** ✅ Easton's is the system itself — **demands** and **supports** entering, a conversion process, **policies and decisions** issuing, and feedback returning. ✅ The Marxian unit is the **economic structure of society, historical stages of its development and the corresponding class structure**.

⚠️ **Autonomy.** ⚠️ Easton's model presupposes a bounded political system distinguishable from its environment. ✅ Marxian analysis denies it: politics is **only a part of superstructure**, **does not grow out of itself**, and **no useful purpose will be served by undertaking analysis of political system per se**.

⚠️ **Comparative rule.** ⚠️ Easton compares systems by the same cycle operating anywhere; Marx, Engels and Lenin compare them **with reference to their respective class structures**, warning that **we should not be misled by their superficial similarities or differences** — North America and Prussia falling together.

⚠️ **Limits.** ✅ Easton's model suffers an **inability in the analysis of political power as well as in the analysis of mass political behaviour, such as voting**. 🔶 The Marxian approach is reductionist and weak on variation among capitalist states, and in it politics **figures therein only incidentally** — analysis **by proxy**.

⚠️ **Verdict.** ⚠️ They are not rivals answering one question badly and well; they answer different questions. 🔑 Easton explains how a system persists; Marxian analysis explains whom its persistence serves. ✅ The chapter's pluralism applies: the question selects the model, and each selection has a cost.

**Rubric [20]:** both units stated precisely **4** · the autonomy disagreement **4** · both comparative rules with the North America/Prussia case **4** · limitations of each, correctly attributed **4** · a verdict that avoids crowning a winner **3** · structure and compression **1**.

---

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ The errors below were anticipated from the structure of the material and from the places where the chapter's wording is easy to shift. ⚠️ Each row gives the error, the correction and the lesson that fixes it. ⚠️ Use the table as a self-test: cover the middle column and reconstruct it.

## A. Method, approach and the empirical/normative divide

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Fix at |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ❌ Treating *method* and *approach* as synonyms | ✅ The chapter notes they are **applied rather loosely, and sometimes even interchangeably**, then insists a distinction **may be drawn** | L1 |
| 2 | ❌ Defining method as a technique only | ✅ **The procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge could be obtained** — inquiry, not instrument | L1 |
| 3 | ❌ Saying the two are independent, or that the wedding runs both ways | ✅ **An approach is usually wedded to a particular method while a method is not always wedded to a particular approach** — the comparative method serves several approaches | L2 |
| 4 | ❌ Presenting the traditional/contemporary divide as a divide in **what** is selected for study | ✅ The chapter's criterion is **when**: approaches **in vogue till the end of the Second World War (1939-45)** are traditional, those **developed thereafter** contemporary. ⚠️ Three nuances ride on it — the two clusters do differ in content, traditional work being dominated by **philosophy, history, law and institutions** and contemporary work by **the behaviour of different actors**; the dating carries **no verdict**, since the label is **neither a criticism nor a refutation**; and the divide is **not coterminous** with the empirical/normative one | L3, L4 |
| 5 | ❌ Equating *traditional* with *normative* and *contemporary* with *empirical* | ✅ The chapter refuses the equation; traditional work contains description and contemporary work contains evaluation | L4 |
| 6 | ❌ Treating form/content as a stylistic point | ✅ It determines what counts as the object of study — formal structures or actual behaviour | L5 |
| 7 | ❌ Attributing to Weldon the claim that political words are meaningless, or merging his charge with the chapter's separate vocabulary point | ✅ Two distinct things. **(i) Weldon's taste analogy**: **a political philosophy is like a matter of taste; one can only state one's taste and go away—there is no point in arguing** — which the chapter calls **a biased view** and answers by the disanalogy that normative claims are **not like a matter of individual taste which can be stated but which cannot be defended**. **(ii) The chapter's own vocabulary point**: **the terminology adopted by empirical and normative approaches for approval or disapproval of any proposition creates confusion at times**, since **'true or false'** and **'right or wrong'** differ in mathematical and moral questions — answered by the observation that **even scientific principles are largely tentative** | L6 |
| 8 | ❌ Deriving an *ought* from an *is* without noticing | ✅ Descriptive and prescriptive statements are not derivable from one another | L7 |
| 9 | ❌ Using Aristotle on slaves as evidence of a general moral failing only | ✅ The chapter's use is methodological — it shows description and prescription entangled in a single classical argument | L7 |
| 10 | ❌ Reading Strauss as a defender of pre-scientific vagueness | ✅ His objection is to the exclusion of the question of the good from political science | L8 |
| 11 | ❌ Assuming the fact/value distinction was invented by behaviouralism | ✅ Behaviouralism converted an existing distinction into a research rule | L7, L21 |

## B. The traditional approaches

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Fix at |
|---|---|---|---|
| 12 | ❌ Describing the philosophical approach as merely speculative | ✅ It examines ends, justification and the clarification of concepts | L9 |
| 13 | ❌ Confining the philosophical approach to ancient writers | ✅ Its characteristic question recurs whenever justification is at issue | L10 |
| 14 | ❌ Treating the historical approach as the recounting of events | ✅ It explains ideas by the conditions in which they arose | L11 |
| 15 | ❌ Calling every historical explanation *historicism* | ✅ Popper's charge applies to the stronger claim that history has a discoverable law of development | L11 |
| 16 | ❌ Supposing the historical approach died with behaviouralism | ✅ The chapter records a revival, associated with Sabine's position | L12 |
| 17 | ❌ Reading a classical text for its answers before its questions | ✅ Establish the problem-situation first; then ask what transfers | L12 |
| 18 | ❌ Equating the legal approach with constitutional law teaching, or importing Austin's **command** and **legal personality** into it | ✅ The chapter's own description is narrower: it **focuses its attention on the legal and constitutional framework in which different organs of government have to function**, and **inquires into their respective legal position, their powers and the procedure which makes their actions legally valid**. ⚠️ Austinian command theory is not part of this section and must be labelled separately if used | L13 |
| 19 | ❌ Omitting the legal approach's limit | ✅ Law describes authority but not its exercise; practice escapes it | L13 |
| 20 | ❌ Treating institutions as self-explanatory | ✅ The institutional approach describes structures of rule; it does not explain why they work as they do | L14 |
| 21 | ❌ Confusing the institutional approach with new institutionalism | ✅ The chapter's object is formal structure; later developments are outside it | L15 |
| 22 | ❌ Dismissing the traditional approaches wholesale | ✅ The chapter's verdict is that they **have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches**, and that the two sets **do not represent watertight compartments** | L3, L34 |

## C. The behavioural movement

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Fix at |
|---|---|---|---|
| 23 | ❌ Dating behaviouralism to the 1950s alone | ✅ **Graham Wallas** and **Arthur Bentley** both published in **1908** | L17 |
| 24 | ❌ Attributing *Human Nature in Politics* to Bentley | ✅ Wallas wrote it; Bentley wrote *The Process of Government* | L17 |
| 25 | ❌ Reporting Wallas as denying reason in politics | ✅ He objected to the assumption that man is **a rational creature following his self-interest** | L17 |
| 26 | ❌ Omitting Merriam from the lineage | ✅ Merriam institutionalised the demand for scientific technique | L18 |
| 27 | ❌ Treating the post-war series of scholars as a single school | ✅ They are a series of contributions, differing in object and technique | L19 |
| 28 | ❌ Using *political behaviour* loosely | ✅ The chapter supplies a definition via Roberts | L20 |
| 29 | ❌ Misordering the eight tenets | ✅ **Regularities, Verification, Techniques, Quantification, Values, Systematization, Pure Science, Integration** | L21 |
| 30 | ❌ Quoting the tenets as Easton's own sentences | ✅ The chapter attributes the set to Easton; its glosses are the chapter's | L21 |
| 31 | ❌ Reading **Pure Science** as a refusal of application | ✅ Its stated content is that understanding is **essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems of society** | L21 |
| 32 | ❌ Reading **Values** as indifference to values | ✅ It draws **a clear distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation** | L21 |
| 33 | ❌ Forgetting **Systematization**'s two-sided warning | ✅ **Research untutored by theory may prove trivial while theory unsupportable by data may turn out to be futile** | L21 |
| 34 | ❌ Claiming behaviouralism disproved normative theory | ✅ It declined to treat what it could not test | L22 |
| 35 | ❌ Missing the reliability/generality trade | ✅ Scope narrows as testability rises | L22 |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 — the chapter's architecture

```text
   PART I   DISTINCTION BETWEEN METHOD AND APPROACH
            method = "the PROCEDURE OF INQUIRY by which reliable
                      knowledge could be obtained"
            approach = the prior SELECTION of what to study
            ==> an APPROACH is USUALLY WEDDED to a particular
                METHOD, while a METHOD is NOT ALWAYS wedded to a
                particular APPROACH
                     |
   PART II  TRADITIONAL APPROACHES
            PHILOSOPHICAL ... ends, justification, clarification
            HISTORICAL ...... ideas in their conditions
                              (!) Popper: HISTORICISM caution
                              (!) revival via Sabine's position
            LEGAL ........... state through its LEGAL and
                              CONSTITUTIONAL FRAMEWORK; the
                              PROCEDURE that makes acts VALID
            INSTITUTIONAL ... formal structures of rule
            (!) each SELECTS something real and MISSES something
                     |
   PART III BEHAVIOURAL REVOLUTION
            1908  WALLAS, Human Nature in Politics
            1908  BENTLEY, The Process of Government
            then  MERRIAM -- scientific technique institutionalised
            then  post-war series; POLITICAL BEHAVIOUR defined
            ==>   EASTON'S EIGHT TENETS
                     |
   PART IV  POST-BEHAVIOURAL REVOLUTION
            charge : technique without RELEVANCE
            remedy : RELEVANCE + ACTION, standards RETAINED
                     |
   PART V   FIVE MODELS OF POLITICAL ANALYSIS
            1 SYSTEMS (Easton)
            2 STRUCTURAL-FUNCTIONAL (Almond)
            3 COMMUNICATIONS (Deutsch)
            4 DECISION-MAKING
            5 MARXIAN  <-- denies the object is autonomous at all
                     |
            ==> PLURALISM: the QUESTION selects the APPROACH
```

## Map 2 — the eight tenets as a single machine

```text
   WHAT IS CLAIMED        REGULARITIES
   there are "discoverable uniformities in political behaviour"
                |
   HOW IT IS CHECKED      VERIFICATION
   "testable, IN PRINCIPLE, by reference to relevant behaviour"
                |
   WITH WHAT INSTRUMENTS  TECHNIQUES
   "examined SELF-CONSCIOUSLY, refined and validated"
                |
   IN WHAT UNITS          QUANTIFICATION
   "measurement ... expressed in terms of ACTUAL QUANTITIES"
                |
   UNDER WHAT RESTRAINT   VALUES
   "a CLEAR DISTINCTION between ethical evaluation and empirical
    explanation"; inquiry "VALUE-FREE OR VALUE-NEUTRAL"
                |
   HELD TOGETHER BY       SYSTEMATIZATION
   "research untutored by theory may prove TRIVIAL while theory
    unsupportable by data may turn out to be FUTILE"
                |
   FOR WHAT PURPOSE       PURE SCIENCE
   understanding is "ESSENTIAL TO UTILIZE political knowledge in
   the solution of URGENT PRACTICAL PROBLEMS of society"
                |
   WITH WHAT ALLIES       INTEGRATION
   union with other social sciences to "strengthen its VALIDITY
   and the GENERALITY of its own results"

   (!) READ DOWNWARD the eight are a PROCEDURE, not a list.
   (!) TENET 7 is the one most often misread: PURE SCIENCE here is
       stated in terms of USE, not of detachment from use.
```

## Map 3 — the five models side by side

| Axis | ✅ Systems (Easton) | ✅ Structural-functional (Almond) | ✅ Communications (Deutsch) | ✅ Decision-making | ✅ Marxian |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Unit** | demands/supports → conversion → outputs → feedback | the **function**, variably structured | information flow | the decision and its takers | economic base and class structure |
| **Question answered** | how does a system persist? | what must be done for a system to continue? | how does a polity steer and learn? | why was this choice made? | whom does the system serve? |
| **Signature phrase** | **special processes** | same function, different structures | **somewhat more a problem of steering** | **political costs** | **political analysis by proxy** |
| **Comparative power** | high, across unlike systems | highest, including informal structures | moderate | high, across levels of politics | high, cutting across constitutional form |
| **Chapter's own criticism** | ✅ inability on **political power** and on **mass political behaviour, such as voting** | ✅ **ideologically inclined towards conservatism**; **not suited to analysis of power relations**; **Western-type liberal-democratic system as a standard** | ✅ **only in a limited sphere** | ❌ none given | ❌ none given |
| **Owner-supplied limitation** | 🔶 weak on power distribution and revolutionary change | 🔶 equilibrium bias; implicit Western benchmark | 🔶 understates power, ideology, class conflict | 🔶 narrow focus; elite bias; misses structural forces | 🔶 reductionist; weak on inter-capitalist variation |

## Map 4 — the argument structure of the two live debates

```text
   DEBATE 1 -- VALUE-NEUTRALITY
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   P1  objective inquiry requires separating fact from value
   P2  BUT choosing WHAT TO STUDY is itself a value-laden act
   P3  during the behavioural period urgent problems went unstudied
   C   therefore neutrality-as-practised was complicity
       |
       REPLY : post-behaviouralism accepts P2 and answers C --
               "ACTION" is ADDED TO, not SUBSTITUTED FOR, evidence
               standards; relevance becomes a DUTY
       |
       RESIDUAL (conceded) : a DUTY to choose is not a METHOD of
               choosing. P2 survives intact.

   DEBATE 2 -- THE WESTERN BENCHMARK
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   P1  functional categories are meant to be STRUCTURE-NEUTRAL
   P2  BUT comparative practice measures non-Western polities
       against a liberal-democratic template
   P3  divergence is then labelled "UNDERDEVELOPMENT"
   C   therefore the neutrality claim is not honoured
       |
       REPLY : the bias is a failure of APPLICATION, not of the
               MODEL'S OWN LOGIC -- the categories can be filled
               by parties OR kinship/patron-client networks
       |
       RESIDUAL (conceded) : critics maintain "the correction is
               INCONSISTENTLY APPLIED in comparative practice".
               The two sides address DIFFERENT OBJECTS --
               the MODEL vs the LITERATURE -- so the dispute is
               NOT RESOLVED HERE, and should not be.
```

## Map 5 — the exam decision tree

```text
   READ THE DIRECTIVE
        |
        +-- "DISCUSS behaviouralism / an approach"
        |     -> DEFINITION + SEVERAL of the eight tenets + ONE
        |        LIMITATION + conclusion
        |
        +-- "EXPLAIN ANY ONE model"
        |     -> CLAIM -> MECHANISM -> SIGNIFICANCE -> LIMIT
        |        (!) ONE model only. Extra models LOSE marks.
        |
        +-- "COMPARE / DISTINGUISH two models"
        |     -> shared axis: UNIT | STRENGTH | LIMITATION
        |        then a verdict that does NOT crown a winner
        |
        +-- "ASSESS / CRITICALLY EXAMINE a claim about the
        |    discipline"
        |     -> state the claim's implication, test it against
        |        the chapter's own verdicts, concede the strongest
        |        counter-case, conclude in GRADED form
        |
        +-- stem is owned ELSEWHERE (the usual case)
              -> use this topic as FRAME, not CONTENT:
                 historical approach for "relevance today"
                 fact/value for "descriptive and normative"
                 tenets for "what would show this?"
                 functions for informal/traditional structures
                 (!) in a 10-marker: ONE CLAUSE at most.

   THEN CHECK WORD BUDGET
        10 marks ~150 | 15 marks ~250 | 20 marks ~250
        (!) 20-mark = six steps in ~250 words = ~40 words a step.
            Compress by NAMING AN ITEM AND ITS FUNCTION IN THE
            SAME CLAUSE.
```
