# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ Twenty-five sections, one or two per lesson cluster, sized to the material rather than to a template. ⚠️ Everything page-anchored is the chapter's; everything marked 🔶 is owner-supplied; everything marked ⚠️ without a quotation is this session's reading.

---

## §1 — Method and approach (L1-L2 · PDF pp.108-109)

- ✅ The terms are used **rather loosely, and sometimes even interchangeably**; the chapter stipulates a distinction.
- ✅ **Method** = **the procedure of inquiry by which reliable knowledge could be obtained and reliable conclusions could be drawn**.
- ✅ Four methods named: **scientific, inductive, deductive, comparative**.
- ✅ **Approach** is **wider**: it covers **the method (i.e. how to inquire)** *and* **the focus of our study (i.e. what to inquire)**.
- ✅ **Van Dyke** (*Political Science: A Philosophical Analysis*; 1960): an approach consists of **criteria of selection** — **criteria employed in selecting the problems or questions to consider and in selecting the data to bring to bear**.
- 🔑 **An approach is usually wedded to a particular method while a method is not always wedded to a particular approach.**
- ✅ **That is why an approach suggests the relevant method also.**
- ✅ Behavioural approach → scientific method, **because behaviour of several actors in a political situation is capable of scientific study**.
- ✅ Normative approach → philosophical method, **because norms and values can only be determined philosophically, not through scientific method**.
- ⚠️ The asymmetry matters: a method may serve several approaches, but an approach that selects unobservable objects cannot borrow an observational method.

## §2 — Traditional and contemporary (L3 · PDF p.109)

- ✅ The criterion is a date: **in vogue till the end of the Second World War (1939-45)** = traditional; **developed thereafter** = contemporary.
- ✅ **Alan Ball** (*Modern Politics and Government*; 1988): the label **'traditional' is neither a criticism nor a refutation of the obvious fact that they still play important role in modern political analysis**.
- 🔑 They **have not become all outdated, but they are no longer favoured by the champions of the contemporary approaches**.
- ✅ **It is not possible to furnish any comprehensive lists**; the approaches **do not represent watertight compartments, although some of their distinctive features might be identified**.
- ⚠️ A date is not a verdict: the chapter uses chronology to sort and then refuses to let chronology grade.

## §3 — The equation the chapter refuses (L4 · PDF p.110)

- ✅ Concession: contemporary political science **gives prominence to empirical approach**; traditional study **was dominated by normative approach**.
- 🔑 Denial: it **cannot be assumed that the distinction between empirical and normative approaches reflects the distinction between contemporary and traditional approaches**.
- ✅ Ground: **some features of empirical as well as normative approaches are found both in the traditional and contemporary approaches**.
- ✅ Traditional yet empirical: **Aristotle** on causes of revolution or rebellion; **Montesquieu** on separation of powers; **Marx** on exploitation of the working classes.
- ✅ Contemporary yet normative: **Karl Popper** (incremental change); **F.A. Hayek** (libertarianism); **C.B. Macpherson** (creative freedom); **Rawls** (theory of justice).
- ⚠️ Two independent axes, not one: *when* a body of work was produced and *what kind of statement* it makes.

## §4 — Empirical and normative statements (L5 · PDF pp.110-111)

- ✅ An empirical statement **can be observed by our sense-experience**, **verified by repeated observation**, and its **accuracy can be tested**.
- ✅ A normative statement **expresses preference for a particular type of order as dictated by a sense of duty or universal need or by commitment to a moral principle or ideal**.
- 🔑 **Strong arguments may be advanced in support of a normative statement**, yet it is **not capable of being discovered, described or verified by our sense-experience**.
- ⚠️ Grammatical form misleads: statements of the form *justice is …* are normative despite the indicative mood.
- ✅ Such statements **express a variety of value preferences** and are **by no means empirical statements**.

## §5 — Weldon and the charge of taste (L6 · PDF p.111)

- ✅ The critics' framing: **the empirical approach is objective whereas the normative approach is subjective**.
- ✅ **T.D. Weldon**, *Vocabulary of Politics* (**1953**): a political philosophy is **like a matter of taste; one can only state one's taste and go away—there is no point in arguing**.
- 🔑 The chapter's verdict in four words: **This is a biased view.**
- ✅ Rebuttal step 1: **a dialogue between these schools can always be opened**, notwithstanding **several schools of thought upholding different interpretations of, say, freedom, equality and justice**.
- ✅ Rebuttal step 2: the parties **can always come together to evolve or arrive at certain basic principles of reasoning by which they can prove or disprove different points**.
- ⚠️ The reply does not promise agreement; it denies that disagreement is *mere* preference.

## §6 — Describing and prescribing (L7 · PDF pp.111-112)

- ✅ **The empirical approach remains largely descriptive while the normative approach is mainly prescriptive** — ⚠️ **largely** and **mainly** are the chapter's own hedges.
- ✅ The empirical approach seeks **laws that are unalterable**, such as the **law of gravitational force**, which are **beyond man's control; one can discover and describe them**.
- ✅ The normative approach deals with laws **largely created or adopted by human society, which are alterable**, such as **laws governing property and public order**.
- ✅ Of these one may **examine how far they are morally right or wrong and then prescribe the right course**.
- ⚠️ The division is by **alterability of the object**, which is a better criterion than the mood of the sentence.

## §7 — Value-freedom and Strauss's counter-charge (L8 · PDF p.112)

- ✅ Empiricists' charge: **there is no 'scientifically valid' or reliable method of determining what is morally right or wrong**.
- 🔑 The normativists' reply is **targeted, not total**: they **do not condemn the empirical approach as such, but they criticize its indifference toward values**.
- ✅ The specific complaint is its **ignorance of discrimination between higher and lower values**.
- ✅ **Leo Strauss**: value-free teaching, by **teaching the equality of values** and **denying that there are things which are intrinsically high and others which are intrinsically low**, has an effect it did not intend.
- ⚠️ **Unwittingly** is the operative word — the charge concerns effect, not motive.

## §8 — The philosophical approach (L9-L10 · PDF pp.112-114)

- ✅ The roster: **philosophical, historical, legal and institutional approaches … may be taken to represent the main traditional approaches**.
- ✅ Task 1 — **the clarification of concepts used in a particular discipline**.
- ✅ **Van Dyke**: philosophical analysis is **an effort to clarify thought about the nature of the subject and about ends and means in studying it**.
- ✅ It aims **to enhance linguistic clarity and to reduce linguistic confusion**, on the assumption that **the language used in descriptions reflects conceptions of reality**.
- ✅ Target state: **as clear, consistent, coherent, and helpful as possible** — ⚠️ **helpful** is a pragmatic criterion sitting beside three logical ones.
- ✅ Two classical themes derive from **a hypothesis about human nature**: the **'art of government'** and the **'grounds of political obligation'**.
- ✅ **Aristotle**: **man is by nature a political animal**, and both themes follow. **Machiavelli**: the art of government, grounded on **the very selfish and ungrateful nature of man**.
- 🔑 **Thereafter, 'art of government' ceased to be a part of mainstream political theory.**
- ✅ **Hobbes** was absolutist; **Rousseau** and **Hegel** continued the obligation theme **on different grounds**.
- ✅ **Locke** was **probably the first thinker to repudiate this absolutist view**, postulating **'rights' of the individual against the state**, and is **the pioneer of individualism**.

## §9 — The historical approach (L11-L12 · PDF pp.114-115)

- ✅ **The term 'historical approach' to politics may be used in two senses.**
- ✅ Sense 1: **arriving at the laws governing politics through an analysis of historical events, that is events of the past** — instanced by **the theories propounded by Hegel and Marx**.
- ✅ **Karl Popper has described this approach as 'historicism'**, which **implies that historical processes are determined by their inherent necessity which are beyond the control of human ingenuity**.
- ✅ Sense 2 studies political ideas in the circumstances that produced them.
- ✅ **Sabine**: the subject-matter of political science **coincides with the major themes of discussion in the writings of the well-known political philosophers** — **Plato, Aristotle, Hobbes** and the rest.
- ✅ Its questions: **what ideals are sought to be realized through the state**; **what is the meaning of freedom and equality**; **what are the grounds and limits of political obligation**.
- 🔑 **Each political theory is advanced in response to some specific situation**, and the circumstances are recapitulated **for understanding its relevance to the present situation**.
- ✅ The relation is reciprocal: a theory is **not only a product of history, it also served as an instrument of moulding history by its ideological force**.
- ⚠️ Sense 2 survives Popper's objection because it claims no law of development.

## §10 — The legal approach (L13 · PDF pp.115-116)

- ✅ **Legal approach stands for an attempt to understand politics in terms of law.**
- ✅ It examines the **legal and constitutional framework in which different organs of government have to function**.
- ✅ It inquires into **their respective legal position, their powers and the procedure which makes their actions legally valid**.
- 🔑 **Validity**, not effectiveness, is its central question.
- ✅ Its Indian illustration ranges over the Constitution, Supreme Court decisions and **the opinions of legal luminaries**, both Houses, the State legislatures and the executive offices.
- ⚠️ The limit follows from the object: law states what authority *may* do, not what it *does*.

## §11 — The institutional approach (L14-L15 · PDF pp.116-117)

- ✅ **Closely related to legal approach, yet it is different**; it **does not solely bank on other disciplines—philosophy, history or law**.
- 🔑 **Amongst the traditional approaches it alone gives independent identity to the systematic study of politics.**
- ✅ **Traditionally politics has been defined as the study of the state and government.**
- ✅ Institutions in view: **government**, **Parliament (legislature)**, **Cabinet (executive)**, **Supreme Court (judiciary)**, and **political parties—which exist separately—are also institutions**.
- ✅ Institutions **not** in view: **family, school, church, or club**.
- ✅ Practitioners study **the organization and functioning of government, its various organs, political parties and other institutions affecting politics**.
- ✅ **Classification of governments** runs from **Aristotle (monarchy, tyranny, aristocracy, oligarchy, polity and democracy)** to modern classifications, and extends to **levels (federal, state and local)** and **branches (executive, legislative, judicial)**, treated **largely in legal terms**.
- ✅ **It aims at giving an elaborate description of facts.**
- 🔑 The balanced verdict: it **exemplifies a shift from normative to empirical approach, and from a historical to a contemporary concern within the sphere of traditional approaches**.
- 🔑 But it **relies heavily on description rather than explanation. Hence it fails to qualify as a contemporary approach.**

## §12 — What makes an approach contemporary (L16 · PDF p.117)

- ✅ Departure **(a)**: contemporary approaches **attempt to establish a separate identity of political science by focusing on the real character of politics**.
- ✅ Departure **(b)**: they **try to understand politics in totality, transcending its formal aspects**, looking for **those aspects of social life which influence and are influenced by** politics.
- ⚠️ (a) answers the dependence of the philosophical, historical and legal approaches on parent disciplines; (b) answers the institutional approach's neglect of the individual and of informal groups.
- ⚠️ **Influence and are influenced by** is reciprocal, which anticipates feedback at §19.
- ✅ **Contemporary approaches are legion, and all of them may not fulfil these conditions** — ⚠️ a characterisation, not a definition.

## §13 — Origins of behaviouralism (L17-L19 · PDF pp.117-119)

- ✅ Behaviouralism is **particularly associated with the work of American political scientists after the Second World War (1939-45)**.
- ✅ Its origins lie with **Graham Wallas** (*Human Nature in Politics*) and **Arthur Bentley** (*The Process of Government*), **both published as early as 1908**.
- ✅ Both laid **greater emphasis on the informal processes of politics and less on political institutions in isolation**.
- ✅ **Wallas** brought **a new realism … in the light of the new findings of contemporary psychology**, against classical economists — **the champions of the laissez-faire doctrine** — who **had treated man as a rational creature following his self-interest**.
- ⚠️ The claim is bounded: his actions **were not totally guided by reason and self-interest**, which is not a charge of irrationality.
- ✅ **Charles E. Merriam (1874-1953)**, **another pioneer of the behavioural approach**, founded **the Chicago School**.
- ✅ **'The Present State of the Study of Politics'**, *American Political Science Review* (**1921**), and ***New Aspects of Politics*** (**1925**), both **criticized contemporary political science for its lack of scientific rigour**.
- ✅ He **deprecated the work of historians as they had ignored the role of psychological, sociological and economic factors in human affairs**, and urged **full use of recent advances in social sciences**.
- ✅ **William B. Munro (1875-1957)** held that it was **not a proper function of political scientists to teach democratic citizenship** — ⚠️ a dissent about the scholar's **role**.
- ✅ **G.E.G. Catlin**, *Science and Method of Politics* (**1927**), **advanced the case for a 'value-free' pure science** and **treated 'power' as essence of politics**, holding that its analysis **should not be inclined in favour of any particular value-system**.
- ⚠️ Labelled reconstruction: value-freedom is purchased by the choice of **power** as the object.

## §14 — What *political behaviour* names (L20 · PDF pp.119-120)

- ✅ The chapter pauses because **it would … be profitable to define 'political behaviour' at the outset**.
- ✅ **Roberts** (*A Dictionary of Political Analysis*; **1971**): it concerns **those aspects of human behaviour that take place within political contexts**.
- ✅ Context filter: **within a state or other political community**.
- ✅ Purpose filter: **for political purposes or with political motivation** — ⚠️ the **or** widens the definition considerably.
- ✅ Focus: **the individual person—as voter, leader, revolutionary, party member, opinion leader, etc.—rather than the group or the political system**.
- ⚠️ **Revolutionary** appears on that list, which is the actor the institutional approach had no place for.

## §15 — The eight intellectual foundations (L21 · PDF p.120)

- ✅ **According to David Easton, the intellectual foundations of behaviouralism consist of eight major tenets.**
- ⚠️ Attribute the **set** to Easton as the chapter does; ❌ do not present the glosses as his verbatim sentences.
- ✅ **Regularities** — **discoverable uniformities in political behaviour** expressible **in theory-like statements so as to provide for explanation and prediction of political phenomena**.
- ✅ **Verification** — validity **must be testable, in principle, by reference to relevant behaviour**; ⚠️ **in principle** is what makes the demand reasonable.
- ✅ **Techniques** — the means of acquiring and interpreting data must be **examined self-consciously, refined and validated**.
- ✅ **Quantification** — **precision … requires measurement which should be expressed in terms of actual quantities**.
- ✅ **Values** — **a clear distinction between ethical evaluation and empirical explanation**; inquiry **has to be value-free or value-neutral**.
- ✅ **Systematization** — **research untutored by theory may prove trivial while theory unsupportable by data may turn out to be futile**.
- ✅ **Pure Science** — understanding political behaviour is **essential to utilize political knowledge in the solution of urgent practical problems of society**.
- ✅ **Integration** — union with the other social sciences **to strengthen its validity and the generality of its own results**.
- 🔑 Read downwards the eight are a procedure, not a list: claim, check, instruments, units, restraint, cohesion, purpose, allies.

## §16 — Reliability bought with generality (L22 · PDF p.121)

- 🔑 **Behaviouralism came to accord primacy to higher degree of reliability vis-a-vis higher degree of generality.**
- ✅ It **focused on questions that could be answered reliably on the basis of the methods available**.
- ⚠️ **On the basis of the methods available** makes the restriction method-relative, hence contingent rather than permanent.
- ✅ **Van Dyke**: the behavioural student is **not likely to ask broad and vague questions like what caused the decline and fall of the Roman Empire**, nor **likely to focus on ideologies or constitutions or laws or upon the organizational structure of institutions**.
- ⚠️ The two prized virtues both come from §15 — reliability from **Verification**, generality from **Integration** — and could not be maximised together.

## §17 — The post-behavioural revolution (L23-L24 · PDF pp.121-122)

- 📰 **By the mid-1960s behaviouralism gained a dominant position in the methodology of political science.**
- ✅ **Leo Strauss**, **'What is Political Philosophy?'**, *Journal of Politics*; **1957**: its rise was **symptomatic of a crisis in political theory**, owing to **its failure to come to grips with normative issues** — ⚠️ a charge about **scope**.
- ✅ **Sheldon Wolin**, **'Political Theory as a Vocation'**, *American Political Science Review*; **1969**: preoccupation with method **signified an abdication of true vocation of political theory** — ⚠️ a charge about **priorities**.
- ✅ **Thomas Kuhn**, ***The Structure of Scientific Revolutions*** (**1962**), supplied a challenge **from within the sphere of philosophy of science**.
- 🔑 **Relevance and action were the twin slogans of post-behaviouralism.**
- 🔑 **It represented no complete departure from behaviouralism. Rather it stood for consolidating its gains and applying them for problem-solving and crisis-management.**
- ⚠️ **Crisis-management** is Kuhn's own vocabulary, which is why the settlement reads as written in the objector's terms.
- 📰 Easton's **1969** references: **the impending threat of the nuclear bomb**; **inner conflicts within the US which might lead to civil war or dictatorship**; and the **undeclared war** of that moment — ❌ all to be kept at their date.
- ✅ The charge: behavioural political scientists were **taking refuge in their 'ivory tower', seeking to perfect their methodology, as if they were not at all concerned with the outside** world.
- ✅ The warning, resting on **the intellectuals' historical role in protecting the humane values of civilization**: otherwise they would be **reduced to mere technicians or mechanics for tinkering** with society.

## §18 — What a model of political analysis is (L25 · PDF pp.122-123)

- ✅ **Political analysis is the product of the empirical-scientific orientation in the study of politics.**
- ✅ Precursors are called **earlier and rudimentary attempts**: **Aristotle's classification of governments**; **Machiavelli's cost-benefit analysis of different techniques of leadership**.
- ✅ **Political analysis in the real sense has flourished only in modern times, particularly with the adoption of scientific method.**
- ✅ Step 1 — determine **the nature of politics**, e.g. Easton's **authoritative allocation of values**.
- ✅ Step 2 — politics is **a process**, which **involves a set of interactions among its components**; identifying them **will guide us as to the relevant data which should be chosen**.
- ✅ Step 3 — how those data are interrelated **will depend on our conception of the pattern of interaction**.
- ✅ Step 4 — **arrange our data according to their interrelationship as conceived by us so as to yield an explanation**.
- 🔑 **Determining the relevant data and evolving their suitable arrangement for yielding an explanation constitute a model of political analysis.**
- ⚠️ A model is therefore a **selection plus an arrangement**, which is exactly the pairing of §1.

## §19 — Systems analysis (L26-L27 · PDF pp.123-126)

- ✅ **This is the pioneering model of political analysis**; it **conceives politics in terms of a political system**.
- ✅ **Dahl**: **any collection of elements that interact in some way with one another can be considered a system: a galaxy, a football team, a legislature, a political party.**
- 🔑 Elements are **looked at in an abstract way rather than as concrete things** — **not … a group of individuals constituting a government or its organs, but as abstract elements**.
- ✅ **Easton**, **'An Approach to the Analysis of Political Systems'**, *World Politics*; **1957**, is **the pioneer of systems analysis**.
- ✅ The political process **does not take place in a closed circuit; its ends are connected with the social process** — hence an **open system**.
- 🔑 The derivation: **'allocation of values' is made because there are corresponding 'demands'**, and **it becomes 'authoritative' because it gets 'supports'**.
- ✅ **Inputs** are **demands** and **supports**; **outputs** are **policies and decisions**; outputs **flow back into the environment through a 'feedback' mechanism, giving rise to fresh 'demands'**.
- 🔑 **Demands** are **the raw materials out of which finished products called decisions are manufactured**.
- ✅ **Feedback** is **a communication process which produces action in response to information about the state of the political system**, apprising it of **the position of its goal, its own distance from it, and the changes in this distance brought about by its own performance**.
- ⚠️ The normal case is hedged — **it may be hoped**, **is expected to**.
- ✅ **Maintaining a state of equilibrium in the political system is by no means a simple affair**: demands may be **lost in transit without reaching the output stage**, or the system may be overloaded by **too many demands, or too much insistence on particular type of demand**.
- ✅ **Structural** relief comes from **pressure groups, political parties**, which **assume the role of 'gatekeepers' who will allow only legitimate demands to enter**; **cultural** relief ensures **only right type of demands—which enjoy social approval—will be encouraged**.
- ✅ Criticisms recorded, and there are exactly two: an **inability in the analysis of political power as well as in the analysis of mass political behaviour, such as voting**. ⚠️ 🔶 **Revolutionary change** is owner shorthand, not the chapter's wording — ✅ the chapter allows revolution as a stress **outcome** and withholds only the account of agency.
- ⚠️ ❌ The conversion process is left as **special processes** — the model's deliberate blank, which §22 fills.

## §20 — Structural-functional analysis (L28-L29 · PDF pp.127-129)

- ✅ **Like systems analysis, structural-functional analysis is also based on the concept of political system.**
- ✅ It is **more widely used in the sphere of comparative politics because it provides for standard categories for different types of political systems**.
- ✅ Lineage: **social anthropology** (**Radcliffe-Brown and B. Malinowski**) → **sociology** (**Talcott Parsons, Robert Merton and Marion Levy**) → political science (**Gabriel Almond** and others).
- ✅ **Almond and J.S. Coleman** (*The Politics of the Developing Areas*; **1960**), characteristic (a): **all political systems have political structures**.
- 🔑 Characteristic (b): **the same functions are performed in all political systems with different frequencies and by different kinds of structures**.
- ✅ Characteristic (c): **all political structures are multi-functional**.
- ✅ Characteristic (d): **all political systems are 'mixed' systems in the cultural sense**, since the culture is **always a mixture of the modern and the traditional**.
- ✅ **Almond and G.B. Powell** (*Comparative Politics: A Developmental Approach*; **1966**): systems must perform **a specific set of tasks** to remain **'ongoing systems'**.
- ✅ A developed system shows **differentiation of structures for the performance of specific functions**, with a discernible **growth of interest groups, political parties and mass media**.
- ✅ In less developed systems **interest articulation, interest aggregation and political communication might be performed by some structures which have not taken a definite shape** — ⚠️ all three are **input** functions.
- ✅ Two criteria of political development: **(a) structural differentiation; and (b) secularization of culture**.
- ✅ **Differentiation** occurs where **roles change and become more specialized or more autonomous** or **new types of roles are established or new structures and sub-systems emerge or are created**.
- ✅ **Political culture** is **the underlying propensities of a political system, representing its psychological dimension**.
- ✅ Criticisms recorded, and there are three: **primarily concerned with system's survival; hence it is ideologically inclined towards conservatism**; **not suited to analysis of power relations in society**; and it **projects Western-type liberal-democratic system as a standard for institution-building in developing societies rather than encouraging them to build their institutions according to their own genius and specific requirements**. ⚠️ 🔶 **Conservative outlook** and **status quo-oriented** are owner shorthand for the first.
- ✅ Concession: **structural-functional analysis signifies a significant advance in the sphere of political analysis**.

## §21 — Communications theory (L30 · PDF pp.129-131)

- ✅ **Karl Deutsch** (*The Nerves of Government: Models of Political Communication and Control*; **1963**) **is the chief exponent of this approach**, applying **modern information technology as well as physiology of nervous system** and introducing **the techniques of cybernetics**.
- ✅ **Cybernetics is the study of the operation of control and communication systems; it deals both with biological systems and man-made machinery.**
- 🔑 His work was **concerned less with the bones or muscles of the body-politic than with its nerves—its channels of communications and decision**.
- ✅ Government is to be viewed **somewhat less as a problem of power and somewhat more as a problem of steering** — ⚠️ hedged, and not a denial of power.
- ✅ The political system is a **'network of communication channels'**, **largely 'self-regulating' or 'self-controlling'**.
- 🔑 **Davies and Lewis** (*Models of Political Systems*; **1971**): the mechanisms **adapt themselves … to the information which they receive from various sources; and even to modify the goals which they have set themselves** — ⚠️ second-order learning.
- ✅ Three contexts and six components, including channels with **load and load capacity, rate of flow, amount of lag and gain, i.e. delay or promptness**.
- ✅ **Charlesworth** (*Contemporary Political Analysis*; **1967**) on distortion: **what a governmental agency says it says, or what the recipient says he hears?**
- 📰 ❌ His list of **industrial strikes, slum riots, university sit-ins, and crackpot picket lines** is period idiom and evaluative — quote, do not endorse.
- ✅ Verdict: useful for **bargaining, conflict-resolution, decision-making, evaluation of policies**, publicity and **international relations**; **hardly useful** on **power structure of society, ideologies, allocation of resources** or **violence and revolution**; commended **only in a limited sphere of political science**.

## §22 — Decision-making analysis (L31 · PDF pp.131-132)

- ✅ **Decision-making analysis denotes an attempt to understand politics as a process of arriving at decisions**, closely related to Easton's model since he **treats decisions and actions as outputs**.
- ✅ **Exercise of any form of authority involves decision-making whether it is in the sphere of business, university-administration or family**; the filter is **the process of arriving at public decisions**.
- ✅ **Van Dyke**: **Every actor is a decision-maker** — including the voter, who **decide[s] whether to vote and for whom**.
- ✅ The seven elements: **issues**; **structures**; **actors** (with **study of personality, if necessary**); **alternative courses of action … that were considered**; **range of preferences vis-a-vis the utilities**; **external factors, pressures or constraints**; **the outcome … including its political costs**.
- 🔑 Rationality is a default — **capable of rational calculation (i.e. cost-benefit analysis)**, **fully conscious of their goals** — with an exit to **social-psychological reasons** where behaviour is **routinized** or shows an **irrational attitude**.
- ✅ Three sub-models: stage/variable models (**Richard Snyder and Harold Lasswell**); economic analogies of **maximizing advantage** (**Anthony Downs, J.M. Buchanan and G. Tullock**); **game theory**, in which **each player will seek his maximum advantage under conditions of rationality**.
- ⚠️ The chapter prints **Synder**; the standard form is **Richard C. Snyder**.
- ✅ It **essentially follows interdisciplinary approach by drawing substantially on psychology, sociology, administrative theory and organization theory**.
- 📰 A general theory is **not yet adequately developed** — a **2009** statement.
- ⚠️ ❌ The chapter supplies **no** criticism of this model.

## §23 — Marxian analysis (L32 · PDF pp.133-134)

- 🔑 **Politics figures therein only incidentally. It may, therefore, be called political analysis by proxy.**
- ✅ **Callinicos** (1984): Marxism **denies that politics is a persisting feature of every form of society**; politics **cannot be studied in isolation from the rest of society**; Marxism **seeks the abolition of politics** — **obviously incompatible with the notion of an autonomous discipline of Politics**.
- ✅ Politics is **only a part of superstructure**; the political system is **not an autonomous structure as held by liberal models of political analysis**.
- 🔑 The 1859 Preface: **the mode of production of material life conditions the social, political and intellectual life process in general** — ❌ **conditions**, never *determines*.
- 🔑 **No useful purpose will be served by undertaking analysis of political system per se.**
- ✅ Study instead **the economic structure of society, historical stages of its development and the corresponding class structure … reflected in 'relations of production'**.
- ✅ **Dominant class** and **dependent class**; **politics must always be traced back to its 'hidden basis' in the class struggle**; **different states are simply different forms of class domination**.
- ✅ ***Communist Manifesto*; 1848**: **Political power, properly so called, is merely the organized power of one class for oppressing another.**
- ✅ **Five stages … including one prehistorical stage**; ⚠️ socialism's mode of production is **-do-**, so only the class structure changes and the socialist row still names two groups.
- ⚠️ The terminus is triply hedged — **It hopes**, **once genuine socialist system is established**, **there is no distortion in its working**.
- ✅ Communism: **classless and stateless society where authority will be exercised without resort to power, and administration will be carried on without the state machinery**.
- ✅ **Marx, Engels and Lenin**: compare systems **with reference to their respective class structures**; **we should not be misled by their superficial similarities or differences**.
- ⚠️ ❌ The **North America and Prussia** illustration breaks at the range boundary and is not completed.

## §24 — Owner extensions (L33 · canonical owners only)

- 🔶 Limitations by model: Easton — **weak on power distribution and revolutionary change**; Almond — **conservative/equilibrium bias; implicit Western benchmark**; Deutsch — **understates power, ideology, class conflict**; decision-making — **narrow focus; elite bias; misses structural forces**; Marxian — **reductionist; weak on inter-capitalist variation**.
- ⚠️ The first three are corroborated by the chapter; 🔶 the last two are owner-supplied and must be marked.
- 🔶 **No single model is "correct"**; each **highlights different variables** and is **chosen according to the question asked, not treated as a universal master key**.
- 🔶 Chain 1: the value-neutrality objection and post-behaviouralism's reply, with the **selection** residual conceded.
- 🔶 Chain 2: the Western-benchmark objection and the application/logic reply — ⚠️ **present both sides; do not resolve the debate as settled**.
- 🔶 Three hedged Indian applications: a Cabinet-level decision; formal structures beside **caste associations, kinship networks, local patron-client ties**; a sustained demand through the systems cycle.
- ⚠️ The hedging rule: **name a mechanism, refuse a particular**.

## §25 — Answer architecture (L34)

- 🔑 The chapter **never crowns a winner**; every verdict is bounded, so pluralism is its own conclusion.
- ✅ **"Discuss behaviouralism"** → **definition + the eight tenets (at least several named) + a limitation**.
- ✅ **"Explain any one model"** → **claim, mechanism, significance and limitation for that one model only**.
- ✅ **"Compare/Distinguish"** → the shared axes **unit of analysis, strength, limitation**.
- ✅ **10 marks, about 150 words**: thesis → **either** tenets **or** one model → one limitation → one-line conclusion.
- ✅ **15 marks, about 200-250 words**: thesis and decoding → doctrine chain naming **at least four** tenets → one model in full → **one** objection-reply chain → reasoned conclusion.
- ✅ **20 marks, about 250 words**: as above with **two** models, **two** chains, **one** hedged Indian application and a **graded conclusion**.
- ⚠️ Roughly forty words per step at 20 marks: compress by naming an item and attaching its function in the same clause.
- ✅ **Prefer named propositions over unverified verbatim quotations** unless independently verified.
- ❌ A changed verb is worse than a paraphrase: **conditions** ≠ *determines*; **somewhat less a problem of power** ≠ a denial of power.

## Evidence-typing convention

| Marker | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ✅ | the chapter's own statement, reproduced or closely reported, with a page anchor available |
| ⚠️ | this session's reading, inference, caution or reconstruction — ❌ not a claim of the source |
| 🔶 | canonical owner material that is **not** in the chapter |
| 📰 | a dated statement, kept at its date and ❌ not presented as current |
| 🔑 | a high-yield point, flagged for revision rather than for provenance |
| ❌ | a prohibition, boundary or explicit denial |
| ➡️ | a routing instruction to another topic's owner |

## Subject boundary

| Belongs here | ➡️ Belongs elsewhere |
|---|---|
| ✅ Method and approach; the empirical/normative distinction as a **methodological** issue | ➡️ The substantive theory of justice, liberty and rights — **Topic 03** and the Philosophy owners |
| ✅ The traditional approaches as **ways of studying** politics | ➡️ The doctrines those approaches studied — Topics 01-07 |
| ✅ Behaviouralism, post-behaviouralism and the eight tenets | ➡️ The decline-and-revival debate about political theory as such — **Topic 01** |
| ✅ The five models as analytical devices | ➡️ The nature and scope of politics, and communitarianism — **Topic 07** |
| ✅ Marxism **as a method** of political analysis | ➡️ Marxism and neo-Marxism as doctrine — **Topic 04** |
| ✅ The fact/value issue as it bears on inquiry | ➡️ Normative ethics and applied ethics — the Ethics subject |
| ✅ Comparative method at the level of models | ➡️ Indian constitutional and governance detail — Polity and Governance |
| ✅ Philosophy Optional PYQs as **separately labelled application anchors** | ❌ Any claim that this topic owns a GS or Philosophy Optional stem |
